A truss is the equivalent slender box tube its section and length imply; parts are point masses at their mount frames; propellant is a point mass at the centroid of the volume holding it. **Edges are walked once, so a closed loop counts each edge's mass exactly once.** The three projected areas are **not** a sum over edges: a projection is the union of the edges' images, so along each principal axis the edges whose own axis is within 45° of that direction contribute their maximum and the rest their sum — but only the ones whose images actually overlap: two parallel edges stack behind one another only if the footprints they cast on the plane perpendicular to the view direction touch (each approximated as a disc of the edge's own transverse radius, centred on its midpoint), and touching is treated transitively so three edges overlapping in a chain read as one image. Two parallel booms offset laterally therefore sum, which taking the maximum over every parallel edge got wrong. **External parts are projected too** (§V-9/§X-5): a radiator, a solar paddle or a heat shield is a flat plate whose projected area along a direction is `area × |n̂·d̂|` for its mount frame's own outward normal, and those are summed with no overlap test — a plate juts out past the mount face, so what the hull's own image hides of it is small against what it adds. A heat shield carries no area of its own, so its plate takes the area of the section at the port it covers. **This is what makes a heat shield produce drag rather than only survive it** — the atmospheric-grazing manoeuvre stands on it. **The deploy state is not read here, and that is why the answer comes in two parts**: `principalAreas` is the fully-deployed total, and `deployableAreas` is the portion of it that folds away — a design-time value that depends on neither attitude nor operation, leaving the composition to whoever actually holds a deploy fraction (`Vessel.currentAreas` = `principalAreas − deployableAreas × (1 − radiator.deployedFraction())`, read by `bcInv`/`srpCoeff`/`predictionBcInv` alike). Only the radiator is treated as deployable: the solar paddle declares `deployable` in its data but no system carries a deploy fraction for it, so nothing could fold it. On the default crewed ship the 50 m² of radiators face fore/aft, so deployed they make the nose-on area the largest of the three (65.8 m² against 10.2/12.5) and stowed it drops to 15.8 — nose-on `bcInv` 0.136 → 0.033 m²/kg — which is what makes folding the panels in low orbit worth doing. **Edges are walked once, so a closed loop counts each edge's mass exactly once.** `structuralMasses(assembly)` is the same walk exposed on its own, answering the hull/truss/decoupler split rather than one total — `vessel/production.ts` charges the first two to different resources (`hull-panel`/`truss-member`), so a sum would not be enough to build the requirement from. The three projected areas are **not** a sum over edges: a projection is the union of the edges' images, so along each principal axis the edges whose own axis is within 45° of that direction (their images stack behind one another) contribute their maximum and the rest their sum. Summing them instead made the nose-on area of a slender hull the *largest* of the three, inverting §11-2's rule that holding the nose along the airstream minimises drag.
- `vessel-assemblies.ts` — the three default designs' shape trees and part placements (`crewedAssembly`/`orbitalBaseAssembly`/`hostileAssembly`), plus `crewedMassProperties()` (the crewed design's derived mass properties, resolved once and reused, which `tests/unit/vessel-parts.test.ts` pins the composed part totals against). Each builder places the parts `vessel-parts.ts` hands it and then finishes by tuning the actuators against the mass properties its own shape implies (`tuneAssemblyActuators`), so nothing outside this module can observe a half-built design. **A node's port takes exactly one thing** — one edge or one exterior part, which `blueprint-validation.ts`'s `checkPortExclusivity` enforces — so on the crewed ship the nose port belongs to the ablative heat shield alone (its covering direction *is* the port's outward normal, §11-3) and the gun sits on the forward hull's outer surface instead.
- `collision-shape.ts` — `HullCapsule` and `deriveCapsules(tree)`: the narrow-phase shape, one capsule per tree edge. A `hull` edge's radius is the mean of its two sections' circumradii and a `truss` edge's is half its section size; a `decoupler` edge gets none, since the capsules at either end already meet across it. `sweptWorldCapsule` moves one body-local capsule into the interval's world-space swept form, holding the attitude fixed across the interval — a substep's own rotation is too small to change whether a contact happens. `Vessel` derives its own set once at construction and exposes it as `collisionCapsules` (empty for a design with no assembly, which then keeps the bounding sphere).
- `assembly-editor.ts` / `dock-workbench.ts` / `dock-workbench-controller.ts` — editing a `VesselAssembly`, in three layers, all THREE/DOM-free. `assembly-editor.ts` is the command set — `addNode`/`moveNode`/`removeNode`/`addEdge`/`reconnectEdge`/`moveEdge`/`removeEdge`/`editSection`/`addPlacement`/`movePlacement`/`removePlacement` — each answering an `AssemblyEditResult` (the new assembly, whether it was accepted, and the errors and touched ids if not) rather than mutating in place, so a rejected edit is simply not adopted. `addPlacement` is the entry point for a part that is not on the vessel yet and `movePlacement` for one that already is; both validate through the same `validateMount` before committing, which is what makes "install from the warehouse" and "move to another port" agree on what a legal mount is. `occupiedPorts(assembly, ignoredEdgeId?)` is the single answer to which ports are taken — edges **and** external placements both occupy one — and it is exported precisely so `mount-candidates.ts` asks it rather than carrying a second rule. `dock-workbench.ts`'s `DockWorkbenchSession` is the editing session over several targets at once (a base, its docked vessels, new-ship drafts) plus the warehouse, with undo/redo and per-target validation; `dock-workbench-controller.ts`'s `DockWorkbenchController` is the drag state machine over it (`beginDrag`/`updateCandidate`/`drop`), holding what is held, where it came from and the current snap candidate — and `applyAssemblyEdit(targetId, result, label?)`, the generic one-command-per-edit channel onto the session's own undo history that `docking.ts`'s node/edge removal and section editing apply through, so a confirmed edit there is one history entry exactly like a placement. **Neither this class nor `assembly-drag-controller.ts` judges whether a mount is allowed — `assembly-editor.ts` is the sole judge**: a `SnapCandidate.verdict` is a `MateOutcome` (`{accepted, reason}`), carrying `AssemblyEditResult.accepted` and the first `AssemblyEditError.message` straight through rather than a separate vocabulary reduced from it. Neither knows how a candidate was found — that is `mount-candidates.ts` and `assembly-drag-controller.ts` above them.
- `mount-candidates.ts` — `nearestMountCandidate(assembly, localPoint, maxDistance, filter?, portKinds?)`: the hull-local point → nearest `MountPoint` lookup, a pure function beside `physics/`'s own. A node's free axial ports are candidates (skipping whatever `occupiedPorts` reports taken) and so is the outer surface of every `hull`/`truss` edge — and the surface candidate is **solved, not searched**: the point's offset from `edgeFrame` projects onto the edge axis for `along` (clamped to the edge) and its angle within that section gives `around` directly, so a continuous surface costs the same as a discrete port. A `decoupler` edge offers none, since it carries no skin. **`portKinds` (default `['axial']` alone) decides which of a node's ports enter the candidate set at all**, so a caller that never passes it — the part-drag path — sees exactly the axial-only candidates it always did; `assembly-drag-controller.ts`'s member drag is the one caller that adds `'lateral'`, enumerating `tree.ts`'s `lateralFaceCount(primitive)` faces per placed primitive (`placeSectionPrimitives`) so a member can also snap to a cross-section's own side faces, not just a node's two axial ends.
- `member.ts` — the structural-member shelf's own vocabulary: `MemberSpec` (`kind: 'hull' | 'truss' | 'decoupler'`, `length`/`radius`/`separationImpulse`) is a ready-made member picked before the drag even starts, because `addNode` ignores any length a caller supplies and re-derives it from the two ports' own `MountFrame`s (`tree.ts`) — a node dropped wherever the cursor happens to point would almost never land on `validateTree`'s required `DIMENSION_UNIT` multiple. **A member therefore carries its own length, quantized before the drag (`quantizeMemberLength`, rounded to `DIMENSION_UNIT` and floored at `MIN_EDGE_LENGTH`), so the far node placed at `origin + z × length` reproduces exactly that length once `addNode` reads it back** — quantization holds by construction rather than by validating the result. `memberAdditionAt(assembly, nearNodeId, nearPort, mountFrame, member)` builds the one-node/one-edge `AssemblyEditResult` that grows it — the far node's own cross-section is a single circle sized to `member.radius`, and `decoupler`/`truss`/`hull` map to `TreeEdge.kind`'s three variants — with `validateBlueprint` off, the same partial-design exemption every drag-time check uses. **Attaching both ends to existing ports is out of scope**: it would need the member's own length to match a port-to-port distance the drag itself doesn't know until the far end is also snapped. `memberGhostTree(member)` is the throwaway two-node tree `assembly-drag-controller.ts`'s `buildMemberGhost` runs through the real hull's own `hullShapeOf`/`buildLoftGeometry`, so a held member's ghost and the edge it actually grows are drawn by the exact same geometry code. THREE-independent.
- `assembly-drag-controller.ts` — `AssemblyDragController`: carrying a part or a structural member in the 3D world, and — for a member — building the edit that grows it. **It holds no `document` listener of its own**: `pickAt(camera, pointerScreen, viewport, root)` reads a raycast against `root` (`Vessel.renderObject` for the real hull, `AssemblyRenderObject.object` for a draft) and, walking each hit's ancestors, answers one `AssemblyPick` — `'part'` (`hull-mesh.ts`'s own `userData['partVisualRef']`), `'node'`/`'edge'` (`render/vessel-wireframe.ts`'s `userData['assemblyNodeId']`/`['assemblyEdgeId']`), or `'none'` — and `release(targetId)`/`drop(targetId)` are ordinary methods `docking.ts`'s own click queue calls, not a `pointerup` handler; a raycast reads a mesh whether or not it is `visible`, so the wireframe stays pickable while hidden. `beginDrag(workbench, part, sourceTargetId, sourceInventory)` records the grab in `DockWorkbenchController` and builds the held part's own mesh from `part-fittings.ts`'s table; `beginMemberDrag(workbench, member: MemberSpec)` is the structural-member sibling — a member has no warehouse slot to leave or return to, so it carries none of `DockWorkbenchController`'s `DragState` and is tracked only by this class's own `held` field. `update(camera, cameraPos, pointerScreen, viewport, target)` (update phase, no THREE state written) turns the cursor into an ECI ray, moves it into the target hull's local frame, narrows against that hull's `collision-shape.ts` capsules so a frame pointing nowhere near the ship costs no node/edge scan at all, and then splits by what is held: a part asks `nearestMountCandidate` for the nearest port/surface mount within `SNAP_DISTANCE` and then `assembly-editor.ts` whether putting it there would be accepted (`movePlacement` when the target already carries it, `addPlacement` otherwise, both with `validateBlueprint` off, since a design being edited is partial by definition and only the mount itself is in question here); a member asks the same lookup restricted to free ports alone (`portKinds: ['axial', 'lateral']`, `filter: m => m.kind === 'port'`, since a member attaches to a port rather than a surface) and, on a hit, hands the port's `MountFrame` to `member.ts`'s `memberAdditionAt` to build — and hold as `pendingMemberEdit`, unapplied — the edit that would grow a far node `member.length` along the port's own +Z. **It never judges the mount itself**; it only records the resulting pose and verdict (`valid`/`invalid`/`far`, colored from `const.ts`'s `COLOR_ASSEMBLY_GHOST_*`) for `sync(fo)` to push into the ghost. `drop(targetId)` commits: a part goes through `workbench.drop(targetId)` (in-warehouse move/placement, folding `DragState`), a member instead applies whatever `pendingMemberEdit` the last `update` produced via `workbench.applyAssemblyEdit` directly, bypassing `DragState` entirely. `release(targetId)` is the click-to-drop counterpart clicking a second time invokes: it commits the same way when the pose is accepted, and otherwise discards the grab — a part picked up off the hull (not the shelf) returns to the warehouse via `workbench.remove`, and a member with no accepted pose is simply thrown away, since it was never in the warehouse to return to. The held mesh is always a separate object from the hull's own (a part's `part-fittings.ts` shape, or — for a member — a throwaway two-node tree run through the real hull's own `hullShapeOf` → `buildLoftGeometry` so the previewed and the built shapes cannot look different): a committed placement rebuilds the hull mesh wholesale (WebGPU cannot swap a live mesh's geometry), so the ghost is disposed either way and never becomes part of what it landed on.
- `part-fittings.ts` — `FITTINGS`: which shape, at what fraction of the hull's reference size, each externally-mounted part type is drawn as. Read by `hull-mesh.ts` for the mounted look and by `assembly-drag-controller.ts` for the held one, so a part cannot look like one thing in hand and another once attached. It lives under `vessel/` rather than beside the builders it names in `render/hull/part-meshes.ts` because it is keyed by `PartType`, and `render/` reading a `game/` type would cross the render/game boundary the wrong way.
- `blueprint.ts` / `feed-network.ts` / `blueprint-validation.ts` / `blueprint-library.ts` / `blueprint-store.ts` / `blueprint-transfer.ts` — 設計データ(第2巻 §4)。`VesselBlueprint` は id・名前・`VesselTree`・`PartPlacement[]`・`FeedNetwork`・`PaintScheme`・分離順・作成/更新時刻を1つにまとめた値で、`assemblyOf` がそのまま `VesselAssembly` として読ませる — 質量特性も内容積の割り当ても、設計を別の形へ変換せずに既存の経路へ渡せる。**`FeedNetwork` が持つのは手動で敷いた区間だけ**(`FeedRoute.manual` は `true` しか取らない型で、自動敷設の結果はそこに入りようがない) — 自動敷設で導ける区間は保存せず設計から毎回導出する、という §4-1 の規則を型で言い切っている。`blueprint-validation.ts` の `validateBlueprint(bp, limits?)` は §4-2 の検証項目を `BlueprintIssue[]`(`severity`/`targetId`/`message`、空配列なら成り立つ)として返す**純関数**で、ドック空間の表示と生産の両方がこれを呼ぶ — DOM にも THREE にも依存せず、`tsconfig.test.json` がそのままコンパイルする。ツリーと断面が解けない設計はそこまで報告して打ち切る(以降の幾何がすべて例外になるため)、内容積が解けない設計では推力軸と総質量の判定を飛ばす(質量特性がその割り当ての上に立つため)。**予算の検証は3つ、いずれも `PartInventory` が既に持つ合計値の組み合わせで、二重に計算し直さない**: 発電が消費電力を上回るか(`totalPowerGeneration` vs `totalPowerDraw`)、外殻温度の上限(§11-3 の `MAX_HULL_TEMP`)で 廃熱(`totalWasteHeat`)を放熱しきれるか(thermal.ts と同じステファン・ボルツマンの式・定数をその1点だけで評価)、そして機体3軸それぞれへ姿勢制御トルクが出せるか — こちらは第二の解析を持たず、`physics/attitude-control.ts` の 既存の配分器(`allocateControl`)へ軸ごとに単位トルクの要求を通し、有限・非負の応答が返るかで判定する(磁気トルカは 設計時に磁場が定まらないため寄与から除く)。**軸を1本でも完全に失う設計だけが error** — error はここで `assembly-editor.ts` の確定を拒む severity なので、フライホイール・RCSスラスタを1基失っても3軸とも出せるかという 冗長性の判定(要素を1つずつ除いたアクチュエータ一式で同じ配分器を問い直す)は warning にとどめる。発電・廃熱の 不足も、即座に飛べなくなる/壊れるわけではなく蓄電・蓄熱の猶予があるので同じく warning。**生産に要る資源と設備が揃っているかはここでは扱わない** — それは `economy/producibility.ts` の問いであり、`VesselBlueprint` はその `ProducibilityBlueprint` を構造的に満たす形へ B6 が繋ぐ。保存は3層に分かれる: `blueprint-library.ts` の `BlueprintLibrary`(保存・読み込み・複製・削除・改名・取り込み。永続化は `BlueprintStore` インターフェース越しなので DOM を知らない)、`blueprint-store.ts` の `LocalStorageBlueprintStore`(**鍵は `tepui.blueprints` 一本で、既存のセーブの `tepui.saveIndex`/`tepui.snapshot.*` とは分かれている** — 設計を消してもセーブは、セーブを消しても設計は残る)、`blueprint-transfer.ts`(`File`/`Blob` の出し入れだけ)。外から来た JSON の検証は `blueprint.ts` の `parseBlueprintFile` が持ち、**識別子が合わない「設計ファイルではない」と、識別子は合うが構造が壊れている「壊れている」を言い分ける** — `save-transfer.ts` が `format` 識別子で同じことをしているのと同じ考え方で、判定が DOM を要さないので単体テストが直接叩ける。骨格の検査は配列の**要素**の型まで見る — ノード・エッジ・配置のいずれかが読めない値なら「壊れている」であって、後段で例外になる形のまま保管庫へ入ることはない。`blueprint-transfer.ts` は骨格を通ったものをさらに `validateBlueprint` へ通し、`error` の指摘が1つでもある設計、または検証そのものが例外になる設計を、保管庫に触れる前に拒む。取り込みは必ず新しい id を振るので、同じファイルを2度読んでも既存を上書きしない。
- `part-inventory.ts` — `PartInventory`: the parts array plus the type-indexed references, HP derivation (`hull` or `cockpit` gone ⇒ `hp = 0` regardless of the rest), damage application with the best armour's reduction, self-repair (radiator/solar panels excluded — those need a dock), and **every performance total, composed exactly as §6-5 states it**: `totalThrust` from the engines alone (`totalRcsThrust` is the separate translation-RCS sum), `totalTorque` from the flywheels' `maxTorque` (a plain , direction-independent sum — the direction-dependent answer is `physics/attitude-control.ts`'s allocation over the whole `ActuatorSet`, and this total is only the flywheel stage's own ceiling), `totalCoolingRate` from each radiator's area × efficiency (an effective radiating area [m²], which is what `radiator.ts` treats it as), `totalPowerGeneration` from the solar paddles (`SOLAR_CONSTANT × area × efficiency`, with incidence and sunlight left to `power.ts`) plus fuel cells plus RTGs, `totalEnergyStorage` from the batteries, `propellantVolume(id)` from the tanks of that propellant, and **`totalPowerDraw`/`totalWasteHeat`** — the electrical load, and that load plus the farm/life-support extra heat, since electricity all becomes heat in the end. `refresh()` is the one call after a part swap. sum until the attitude-control allocation of §14-2 lands, which is what makes the question direction-dependent), `totalCoolingRate` from each radiator's area × efficiency (an effective radiating area [m²], which is what `radiator.ts` treats it as), `totalPowerGeneration` from the solar paddles (`SOLAR_CONSTANT × area × efficiency`, with incidence and sunlight left to `power.ts`) plus fuel cells plus RTGs, `totalEnergyStorage` from the batteries, `propellantVolume(id)` from the tanks of that propellant, and **`totalPowerDraw`/`totalWasteHeat`** — the electrical load, and that load plus the farm/life-support extra heat, since electricity all becomes heat in the end. `refresh()` is the one call after a part swap.
- `base-module.ts` — the storage state a base module gives a vessel and the world coordinates of its ports: `BaseState` (money/inventory/`DockedVesselEntry[]`) and `portWorldPos`/`portWorldNormal`, which rotate a port's body-frame position/normal by the vessel's own attitude. A `DockedVesselEntry`'s `parts` is the *same array* as the stored ship's, so a repair in the dock lands on the ship itself and only the `hp`/`maxHp` totals the ship-list tab reads have to be written back. `baseFacilities(base)` (the production facilities this base can reach: the lunar base's own initial set plus whatever its base-module part declares) and `basePowerAvailable(base)` (**how much power the base can run at once, summed from those facilities' own `powerOutput`** — deliberately *not* the vessel's `totalPowerGeneration`, since a ship's solar paddles exist to run the ship's own systems and are orders of magnitude below what a production facility draws; charging production against the paddles made every design unbuildable on a station with none). Also the storage state a base module gives a vessel and the world coordinates of its ports: `BaseState` (money/inventory/`DockedVesselEntry[]`) and `portWorldPos`/`portWorldNormal`, which rotate a port's body-frame position/normal by the vessel's own attitude. A `DockedVesselEntry`'s `parts` is the *same array* as the stored ship's, so a repair in the dock lands on the ship itself and only the `hp`/`maxHp` totals the ship-list tab reads have to be written back.
- `vessel-parts.ts` — the parts each default design carries: `crewedParts`/`hostileParts`/`baseParts`, each splitting the vessel's nominal `maxHp` across its own fixed ratio table (summing to 1), plus `createDefaultBaseModule`. **The hatch position, the slot layout, the capacity (`Vessel.dockCapacity`, read from the part rather than from a base-wide constant) and the capture thresholds are the base-module part's own performance values** — `Vessel.canCapture(other)` and `Docking`'s own full-dock refusal both read them, so a different module gives a different station a different envelope with no other change. Radiators and solar panels come in two parts each, because the vessel physically has a left and a right panel, and **the crewed design carries `CREWED_RCS_THRUSTER_COUNT` (=12) separate `rcs_thruster` parts** rather than one — a thruster's position and firing direction come from where it is bolted on (`vessel-assemblies.ts`'s `CREWED_RCS_MOUNTS`), so a cluster is the only way to state a layout that can torque about all three axes in both directions; the `rcsThruster` HP share is split evenly across them. It also carries one `magnetorquer` (`C.MAGNETORQUER_MOMENT` = 400 A·m², §5-15's medium grade). **The actuators are sized from the vessel's own derived mass properties, not from a constant**: `tuneActuators(parts, mass, maxMoment)` — called by each of `vessel-assemblies.ts`'s builders as the last step of assembling, so a `VesselAssembly` never leaves that module carrying an untuned placeholder thrust — writes the engine's thrust as `mass × max(THROTTLE_LEVELS)`, each translation RCS thruster's as `mass × THROTTLE_LEVELS[0]`, and the flywheel's torque as `MAX_ANG_ACCEL × the largest principal moment`, so the acceleration and the angular acceleration a vessel can produce stay the numbers the HUD prints however the shape changes. **Every design runs it, including the ones that supply their mass properties directly** — `vessel-designs.ts`'s `hostileShipDesign` calls it from the mass properties it declares, so a design with no shape tree cannot be left carrying an untuned (or wrongly-united) placeholder the way the hostile flywheel's `maxTorque` once was. The rest are still pinned to `const.ts`: the tank to `CREWED_RCS_FUEL_CAPACITY`, the battery to `POWER_CAPACITY`, the paddles/radiators to `SOLAR_PANEL_AREA`/`RADIATOR_COOLING_AREA`, and the elements' own draws to `CREWED_POWER_DRAW`/`CREWED_WASTE_HEAT` — `tests/unit/vessel-parts.test.ts` asserts each composed total against the constant, so a part value that drifts from the vehicle it describes fails rather than silently re-tuning the ship. `COMM_MODULE_SPECS` is the communication modules' grade ladder (§13-2: small/medium/large/deepSpace, each a reach and a mass), `createCommModule(grade)` builds one, and `createCommStation()` builds the one a communication station carries — a plain `CommunicationPart` of a fixed spec, since a station is a base module plus a large communication module rather than a part kind of its own. Its `COMM_STATION_RANGE` (2.0e8 m) is deliberately shorter than the Earth-Moon distance, which is what leaves low Earth orbit outside the initial coverage (§13-4). **Each part also carries its own `weight`, and those weights are what the derived dry mass adds up to** — about 1,153 kg for the crewed ship, 3e6 kg for the base, 10,000 kg for the hostile design — with the `hull` part itself weighing nothing, since the primary structure *is* the skin whose mass §10-3 derives from the shape. `tests/unit/mass-properties.test.ts` pins each of the three, and pins the crewed design's whole derived tensor and area triple. Radiators and solar panels come in two parts each, because the vessel physically has a left and a right panel. **The actuators are sized from the vessel's own derived mass properties, not from a constant**: `tuneActuators(parts, mass, maxMoment)` — called by each of `vessel-assemblies.ts`'s builders as the last step of assembling, so a `VesselAssembly` never leaves that module carrying an untuned placeholder thrust — writes the engine's thrust as `mass × max(THROTTLE_LEVELS)`, each translation RCS thruster's as `mass × THROTTLE_LEVELS[0]`, the flywheel's torque as `MAX_ANG_ACCEL × the largest principal moment`, and — for an engine or thruster burning monopropellant hydrazine — that unit's own `catalystMass` from `economy/build-cost.ts`'s `catalystMassFor(thrust)` (the catalyst bed sits inside the combustion chamber and its mass scales with thrust, so it is decided wherever the thrust is), so the acceleration and the angular acceleration a vessel can produce stay the numbers the HUD prints however the shape changes. The rest are still pinned to `const.ts`: the tank to `CREWED_RCS_FUEL_CAPACITY`, the battery to `POWER_CAPACITY`, the paddles/radiators to `SOLAR_PANEL_AREA`/`RADIATOR_COOLING_AREA`, and the elements' own draws to `CREWED_POWER_DRAW`/`CREWED_WASTE_HEAT` — `tests/unit/vessel-parts.test.ts` asserts each composed total against the constant, so a part value that drifts from the vehicle it describes fails rather than silently re-tuning the ship. `COMM_MODULE_SPECS` is the communication modules' grade ladder (§13-2: small/medium/large/deepSpace, each a reach and a mass), `createCommModule(grade)` builds one, and `createCommStation()` builds the one a communication station carries — a plain `CommunicationPart` of a fixed spec, since a station is a base module plus a large communication module rather than a part kind of its own. Its `COMM_STATION_RANGE` (2.0e8 m) is deliberately shorter than the Earth-Moon distance, which is what leaves low Earth orbit outside the initial coverage (§13-4). **Each part also carries its own `weight`, and those weights are what the derived dry mass adds up to** — about 1,061 kg for the crewed ship, 3e6 kg for the base, 10,000 kg for the hostile design — with the `hull` part itself weighing nothing, since the primary structure *is* the skin whose mass §10-3 derives from the shape. `tests/unit/mass-properties.test.ts` pins each of the three, and pins the crewed design's whole derived tensor and area triple.
- `production.ts` — **設計から実機を得る経路の、資源の側(§6 B6)。DOM にも THREE にも依存しない** — 実機そのものを組み立てるのは `docking.ts` の `Docking.buildDraft`(下書きを実艦にして基地のドックへ格納する)であり、この層が扱うのは物の出入りだけである。`productionBlueprintOf(bp)` が `VesselBlueprint` を `economy/producibility.ts` の `ProducibilityBlueprint` へ写す(搭載要素は**1つずつ**数える — 同じ種別でも質量が違えば建造費も違うので種別ではまとめられない。殻の材料を推進剤が決める3種のタンクだけが `tanks` の枠へも入り、`structure` は `mass-properties.ts` の `structuralMasses` が返す外皮/トラス/分離機構の質量を `hull-panel`/`truss-member` へ割り振る。分離機構は外皮と同じ板金であり独自の資源を持たない)。**設計が直に名指す設備は `ASSEMBLY_FACILITY`(`assembly-dock`)ひとつだけである** — 搭載要素ごとの前提は、その要素が要求する資源を作る設備の側(`FACILITIES`)が持つので、部品の表に設備を重ねて書かない。`productionResourceDemand(bp, ledger)` は設計が在庫から引く資源で、判定と消費が同じ材料を選ぶよう(`chooseTankMaterial` を通して)両者がこれを共有する。`consumeProductionResources` は**1つでも足りなければ何も減らさず** false を返す — 途中まで引いた状態で失敗すると、失敗した生産が資源だけを食ったことになる。`productionTimeOf(bp, factor)` は搭載要素の合計質量 × 係数で、`DEFAULT_PRODUCTION_TIME_FACTOR` は 0(すべての設計が即座に完成する)。
- `default-blueprints.ts` — `crewedShipBlueprint(now)`: 実装が最初から持つ設計。組立モード(B7・B8)で設計を編集できるようになるまで、生産の対象にできる設計はこれと保管庫に保存されたものだけである。形は保管庫の設計と同じなので、生産の経路はどちらも区別しない。
- `enemy-ai.ts` — `EnemyKind`/`inertiaForEnemyKind` and `EnemyAi`: burst-fire scheduling and lead-aimed plasma against a target, held by hostile vessels only. Driven from `Stage.behaveAllHostiles`, not from `updateControls`.
- `hp-marker-svg.ts` — the three marker glyph builders (`notchedHpMarkerSvg` for combat view, `headingHpMarkerSvg` for the map, `baseMarkerSvg` for a station), pure string functions with no vessel reference.
- `throttle.ts` — `VesselThrottle`: translation input → thrust accel (`buildThrust`, all 6 body-frame directions), manual rotation + RCS damping, and the prograde-hold autopilot (target forward = velocity direction, target up = radial-out) built on `physics/attitude.ts`'s `attitudeAlignTorque` — the PD control itself (quaternion error → body-frame axis-angle → `PROGRADE_HOLD_KP`/`_KD` torque) is a pure function there, shared with `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s burn-direction alignment rather than living only here. While prograde-hold is on and no rotation key is held, `updateTorque` returns the alignment torque alone — the PD law carries its own derivative gain, so adding RCS damping on top of it would over-damp and slow the convergence it is meant to produce. `updateTorque` only *returns* the body torque — **and it is a request, not a command**: `Vessel.updateControls` hands the result to `attitudeControl.requestTorque(...)`, and `game/vessel/attitude-control-system.ts` is what turns it into `entity.torque` one simulation step later (see below) — and `updateThrustState` only returns the thrust vector — the actual attitude/orbit integration is `Simulator`'s. **It runs on two clocks, and which quantity takes which is not interchangeable**: the RCS output ramp (`rotationHoldTime`, over `RCS_MANUAL_RAMP_TIME`) advances on the wall `dt`, since "how many seconds has the key been held" is an operational-feel quantity a time warp must not accelerate; every fuel draw (`Vessel.consumeFuel`, in `buildThrust` and in `updateTorque` alike) advances on `simDt`, since the thrust and torque it pays for are integrated over exactly that span — drawing on the wall clock instead makes Δv-per-fuel depend on the warp level. `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` draws its own burn's fuel on the same `simDt` for the same reason, so a manual burn and an autopilot burn cost the same. Each of the 6 translation keys is independently double-tap latchable (public `updateThrustLatches`, consuming `Input`'s press edges via `takeKey`; `isThrustHeld` ORs the physical hold state with the latch set), timed against `performance.now()` rather than a `dt`-accumulated clock so a double-tap's interval judgment doesn't depend on whether every frame in between actually called it. `updateThrustLatches` is `Vessel.updateControls`'s own call — reached only when that frame handed the ship an `input` at all, so a double-tap at a warp level past the physical band neither latches nor consumes the key edge, and dropping back below the threshold can't detonate a latch that silently accumulated during the warp — not folded into `updateThrustState`, since the latter runs unconditionally whenever real thrust is being computed and the two questions ("did a key just double-tap" vs "what thrust does the current hold/latch state produce") are separate. Holding the *opposite* direction key also holds that latch cleared for as long as the opposite key stays down (not just on its press edge) — otherwise a physical hold on the opposing key while one side is latched would only cancel it out in `buildThrust`'s per-axis sum, and releasing the opposing key would make the still-latched side reappear unannounced. `stopThrust()` (called from `updateThrustState`'s no-thrust branch and from `clearTransientState`) only zeroes `thrustAccelVec` (the belt's own fictitious-force input, see `belt.ts` below) now — it does **not** touch SFX or the plume, since `thrust-effects.ts`'s `ThrustEffects.sync` reads `this.thrust` (the `GameEntity` field, whichever of `VesselThrottle`/`plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` last wrote it) directly every frame and owns the engine-sound toggle itself; a manual stop reaches the plume/SFX only because `ship.thrust` itself goes `null` on the same path, not through a second explicit call. `latchedThrustKeys`/`lastThrustPressTime` are both cleared by `clearTransientState`. Holding any of the 3 opposite-key pairs together (`isThrustKillSwitchActive`) is an emergency all-stop: `buildThrust` returns `null` unconditionally (bypassing the per-axis sum entirely, so it also zeroes any *other* axis's held/latched thrust, not just the pressed pair's own) and `updateThrustLatches` clears every latch instead of running its per-key toggle logic that frame — a single pair stops every axis, not just its own.
- `gunnery.ts` — `Gunnery`: gun state (mag/round/barrel counters, reload timer), `updateFireState`/`fireCycle`/`consume` (the ammo state machine, returning `ConsumeResult` = `empty`/`normal`/`mag-reload`/`barrel-reload`), `fireGun` (bullet spawn, recoil, casing eject, muzzle flash, `ScoreCounter.recordShot`), and `dropBarrel`/`spawnEjectedMagazineFrame` for reload debris.
- `belt.ts` / `belt-physics.ts` — the ammo belt display split from its physics: `belt-physics.ts`'s `BeltPhysics` is a pure Vec3/Quat Verlet-chain solver (position-based dynamics; otherwise no THREE/mesh dependency — see `BeltSection` below for the one exception) for the belt's sag/twist under the ship's thrust+spin pseudo-forces; `belt.ts`'s `Belt` owns the link meshes and derives each link's transform from `BeltPhysics`'s solved state via parallel transport. `belt-physics.ts` also defines `BeltSection extends GameEntity` — the per-link contact proxy `game/simulation/contact.ts` resolves against (`BeltPhysics.collisionSections`/`applyCollisionSections` convert to/from it each frame). It's declared here rather than in `game-entity/` because nothing outside the belt uses it, and its constructor needs only a placeholder `THREE.Object3D` to satisfy `GameEntity`'s constructor — it's never added to a scene or stepped, so that placeholder is the only place this otherwise THREE-free module touches THREE.
- `heat-shield.ts` — the heat shield's own geometry and bookkeeping (§11-3), a pure function over an assembly rather than a class: `heatShielding(tree, placements, flowDirBody)` walks the externally-mounted `heat_shield` parts, turns each one's `solidAngle` into a cone half-angle (`Ω = 2π(1 − cosθ`), and admits a shield only when the body-frame airspeed direction falls inside the cone its **mount frame's own outward axis** points along — so which way a shield protects is a fact about where it was bolted on, not a second field that could disagree with the model. A shield with no ablator left, or no HP, is skipped outright. The admitted fraction (`solidAngle / HEAT_SHIELD_FULL_SOLID_ANGLE`, a hemisphere counting as full coverage) scales both the fraction of incoming heat kept out and the two limits (`HEAT_SHIELD_TEMP_MULT`/`HEAT_SHIELD_DYN_PRESSURE_MULT`); `UNSHIELDED` is the bare-vessel answer. `ablate(placements, shieldedHeat)` spends the ablator. **Turning the nose away from the flow drops the whole protection**, which is what makes reentry attitude a thing the player has to hold.
- `thermal.ts` — `ThermalSystem`: **the single source of truth for the hull's heat budget** — aerodynamic heating, gun firing heat, bullet-impact heat and radiative cooling all land in `updateThermal`, plus dynamic pressure and the altitude-descent-warning EMA (`updateAltitudeAlarm`); returns a `ThermalLimit` verdict. **The temperature and dynamic-pressure limits it judges against are not constants** — `updateThermal` asks the vessel for that frame's `heatShielding()` (`vessel/heat-shield.ts`, §11-3) and keeps it, so the shielded fraction both keeps that much heat out of the hull and raises the two limits; a vessel with no heat shield, or one whose shield is pointed away from the flow, judges against the bare `MAX_HULL_TEMP`/`MAX_DYN_PRESSURE`. The heat the shield stopped is handed straight back through `ablateHeatShields`, so the ablator is spent by exactly what it blocked. `addGunHeat(rounds)`/`addImpactHeat()` both bank their energy in joules into the shared `pendingHeat` rather than writing `hullTemp` directly, because `updateThermal` runs once per substep and the deposited energy must not scale with how many times that happens — the actual destruction (`alive = false` + effects) happens in `Vessel.checkLoss`.
- `radiator.ts` — `RadiatorSystem`: the two deployable radiator panels (deploy fraction and hinge-angle `sync`). **It does not own the panels' damage** — `wear` (0=intact, 1=destroyed) is handed to `update(dt, wear)` every frame by `Vessel`, derived from the two radiator *parts*' remaining HP, so the thing the dock repairs and the thing that stops radiating are one and the same. Panels therefore never self-heal in flight; repair is the dock's job alone. It **does not know the hull temperature** — it answers only `radiatingArea()` [m²] and `solarLoad(sunlit, sunDir, att)` [W], which `Vessel` feeds to `ThermalSystem.setRadiatorLoad` each frame, so the Stefan-Boltzmann T⁴ law stays owned by `ThermalSystem` alone and no temperature round-trips between the two; `radiatingArea` multiplies `RADIATOR_PANEL_AREA` (the modeled dimensions) by the separate `RADIATOR_EFFICIENCY_MULT` performance multiplier, so tuning the panels' cooling power never has to fight the "model dimensions and physics stay one number" rule that `RADIATOR_PANEL_AREA` itself follows. `foldThetas(side)` is shared by `sync` and the panel-normal math so the drawn angle and the illuminated angle cannot diverge; because an accordion's even and odd folds face different ways, `solarLoad` evaluates both orientations and splits the area between them. A deployed panel is a real contact surface, not a hit-radius adjustment: `RadiatorFold extends GameEntity` is a one-fold-wide rigid collision proxy (`collides = true`, `attachedTo = owner`, so it never contacts the ship itself or the panel's own other folds/the belt's own nodes), and `collisionFolds(shipR, shipV, att, t)` re-derives each fold's world position/velocity from the ship's current state every frame — cheap enough to place fresh rather than track, since a fold's placement is a pure function of the ship's attitude and the panel's own deploy angle. Only sides deployed past `RADIATOR_CONTACT_DEPLOY` and not fully worn contribute proxies, so a stowed or destroyed panel can't be hit. `RadiatorFold.collideWith` forwards straight to `Vessel.collideAtRadiator(side, other, contact, activeStage)`, which special-cases a `Bullet` contact (routed to the same `attackedByBullet` a hull hit uses, tagged with `side` so the damage lands on that panel's part) and otherwise applies the contact's own impulse to the matching `radiatorParts[side]` — deploying a panel therefore both adds a real obstacle a bullet or another rigid body can strike and, once struck, funnels the damage to that specific part; `RadiatorSystem.tipWorldPosition` reads the destroyed panel's own fold mesh (post-`sync()`) for the one-shot break effect's spawn point, and `sync()` hides a fully-worn panel's fold meshes for good.
- `reentry-effects.ts` — `ReentryEffects`: the plasma glow ahead of the ship during reentry. **Stateless** — the intensity is derived from `ThermalSystem.qdyn` every frame (`REENTRY_GLOW_MIN_Q` → `REENTRY_GLOW_FULL_Q` mapped continuously onto scale and opacity), so there is nothing to advance in `update` and only a `sync`. It orients off the *absolute* ECI velocity rather than `fo.VtoThreeV3`, since the floating origin is the player itself and the relative velocity is therefore always zero here.
- `power.ts` — `PowerSystem`: the solar panels' charge, HUD-facing as `chargeRatio` (0..1). The panels are fixed (no deploy animation), so their normal is a constant body-frame `(0,1,0)` — `update(dt, sunlit, sunDir, att)` just rotates that one normal into world space, takes `max(0, dot(...))` (unlike `RadiatorSystem.solarLoad`'s `abs`, since a one-sided panel doesn't generate power off its back face), and banks the energy into `charge`, clamped to `POWER_CAPACITY`. Entirely numeric — no `sync()`, since there's no mesh state to push.
- `thrust-effects.ts` / `rcs-effects.ts` — billboard VFX (+ the shared engine/RCS loop SFX) for the main engine plume (positioned from `render/ships.ts`'s `MUZZLE_OFFSETS`) and for the attitude thrusters. `ThrustEffects.sync(fo, playerPos, thrust, maxAccel, visible, audible, camera)` reads straight off the `GameEntity` field the physics side already writes — it takes `this.thrust` itself (not a `VesselThrottle`-specific direction/throttle-index pair), so a burn commanded by manual input (`VesselThrottle.updateThrustState`) and one commanded by `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` (which writes `ship.thrust` directly, never touching `VesselThrottle`) render identically without either module needing to know about the other; `maxAccel` (`ship.totalThrust / ship.mass`, computed by the caller) turns the thrust vector's magnitude into a 0..1 output ratio for the plume size. **`RcsEffects` instead reads the frame's own allocation**: `sync(fo, playerPos, allocation, actuators, torque, att, …)` draws one plume per entry of the vessel's own `ActuatorSet.thrusters` (lazily minted, so a design's thruster count decides how many exist) at that thruster's own mount position, pointing along the reverse of its thrust direction and scaled by `allocation.thrusterForces[i] / maxThrust` — **there is no fixed nozzle table behind the plumes any more**, so where a plume appears and how hard it burns is exactly what `physics/attitude-control.ts`'s allocation decided, and a design with a different RCS layout shows a different pattern with no change here. `render/rcs-nozzles.ts`'s `RCS_NOZZLES` survives only as the *modelled nozzle geometry*'s own definition, read by `tools/export-models.mjs`. The `torque` argument is used for one thing only — comparing the frame's actual angular acceleration against `RCS_PUFF_TORQUE_EPS` to decide whether anything fires at all and whether the shared RCS loop sounds. Both take an `audible`/`isActive`-style flag since every self-flown ship's plume renders but only the operated ship's engine/RCS loop should be heard.

`src/game/game-entity/` — the entity base and derived classes, one file per class:
- `game-entity.ts` — `GameEntity` (base: mesh/HP/alive/attitude/AI-facing state, plus the fields that let an instance stand in for `physics/attractor.ts`'s `Attractor` with no conversion: `readonly id: string` (explicit constructor argument, or self-assigned by the base `EntityIdAllocator` when omitted), `name: string` (the display name every marker/list/window shows, defaulting to the id — `Ship`/`Base` overwrite it in their own constructors, and a rename writes it, so there is no second name field anywhere), `radius = 0` (physical radius [m]; 0 = point, the same value `Attractor.radius` reads), `collides = false` (rigid-contact participation — independent of `radius`, since a bullet or debris fragment has a physical size but must not bounce off the hull), `mu = 0` (gravity constant GM; 0 = no gravity — a body is a gravity source purely by `mu !== 0`, decided nowhere else), `degree2: Degree2Gravity | null = null` and `isStar = false` — both plain mutable fields (not `readonly`) precisely so a derived class can build its own field at construction, the way `Asteroid` does. `accel: Vec3 = v3()` (the entity's own ECI acceleration, the field `Attractor.accel` needs — see `attractor.ts` below) is never updated by any entity, since a gravity-bearing entity's mass is negligible next to a registered celestial body's and the resulting extrapolation error is worth accepting rather than tracking. `protected setGravitatingMass(mass)` is the one place a mass becomes a `mu` (`mu = GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT * mass`, also recording `mass` itself for rigid-contact use), so a subclass never derives `mu` by hand. `EntityManager.attractors()` filters on `mu !== 0 && alive` alone — `radius`/`collides` don't gate it, since a body's shape and its collision participation are separate questions from whether it pulls. The constructor's `addToScene` argument (default `true`) controls only whether `obj` is added to `scene` — `Bullet` and a `casing`/`fragment` `DebrisPiece` pass `false`, since those are drawn through `EntityManager`'s `InstancedPool`s instead (`render/instanced-pool.ts`, above); `obj` itself is unaffected and still receives its transform from `sync` every frame, since the pool reads it from there. Delegates the actual "time-stamped state + its sample history + advance itself" job to `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory`, held as the public `actual` field. `state`/`prevState`/`history` are thin forwarding getters to `actual` — the ~20 call sites across the codebase that only ever read `.state` are unaffected by this split. The `state` **setter** forwards to `actual.reset(...)`, the sole entry point for a discontinuous external overwrite (rigid contact, recoil); `stepActual(dt, attractors)` never goes through it — it self-gates on `alive` and hands `actual.step(...)` the caller-supplied gravity-source array plus the entity's own `bcInv`/`thrust`/`sampleInterval(attractors)`/`historyDuration` (a getter = `max(baseHistoryDuration, requestedHistoryDuration)` — the per-class floor raised by whatever past-display span `requestHistoryDuration(sec)` was last handed, capped at `HISTORY_DURATION_MAX`; the retained sample *count* stays bounded at `ARC_MAX_SAMPLES` regardless, since `sampleInterval` decimates against the same window, so a longer past costs coarser interpolation rather than more memory; an entity whose `baseHistoryDuration` is 0 keeps no history whatever is requested) (integration itself is `actual`'s job, not this class's; `Simulator.substep` decides `attractors` for this step — see `simulation/attractors.ts` below). `orbitalElementsOf(center)` is the only way to get osculating elements out of an entity — there is no bare `elements` getter, since which body an ellipse is centered on is a per-call choice (typically `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`), not a fixed property of the entity; it memoizes on `state`'s object identity and `center.id`, both of which only change together with an actual new orbit. `sampleInterval(attractors)` derives the history's push interval from `attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod(state.r, attractors)` fed into `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s exported `trajectorySampleInterval` (the period around whichever body pulls hardest right now, divided by `TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV`, falling back to a `SHIP_HISTORY_DURATION`-based value when no finite period exists, e.g. hyperbolic orbits — the same formula `PredictedArc.step` itself calls, so the real-state history and the predicted-future arc never disagree on it) and is skipped entirely while `historyDuration === 0` (bullets/casings/debris never need it), `torque`/`thrust` slots the owning system writes each frame, and a default `checkLoss(_dt, _simTime, _activeStage, _playerPos, attractors)` that calls `attractor.ts`'s `reachedBody(actual.prevState, state, attractors, 0)` — surface contact with *any* attractor, not just Earth, since a stray debris piece near the moon shouldn't pass through it; the `attractors` argument is whichever body window that substep already holds — `Simulator` hands `EntityManager.cleanup` the endpoint all-body window while rigid contact is being resolved (it is built for the contact resolution anyway, and covers the `mu = 0` display bodies), and the substep's own midpoint gravity window above that warp band, where the surface-reach check is the only consumer left. The second case trades the `mu = 0` bodies' reentry check for having the living gravity-bearing entities (an `Asteroid`) as surface-contact partners). `orbitLine: OrbitLine | null` (default `null`) is the entity's own Kepler-ellipse line around whichever body it currently orbits — the same show/hide-owns-the-line shape as `predictedLine`/`actualLine`: `showOrbitLine(style)`/`hideOrbitLine()` are what actually build/dispose it, so whether the field is non-`null` is exactly whether something has asked for the line to be shown. `syncOrbitLine(fo, camera, attractors)` is a no-op when `orbitLine` is `null` and otherwise resolves the center via `strongestAttractor(state.r, attractors)` and syncs it to `orbitalElementsAround(center)` — the entity resolves its own center rather than being handed one, so every subclass that owns an `orbitLine` shares this one implementation instead of each re-deriving the center logic. Whether to draw at all is decided by the caller — `entity-line-manager.ts`'s `EntityLineManager` (below) is that caller for `Enemy`/`Base`, for whichever `Player` currently holds the primary/secondary combat target, and for the operated ship in combat view (where the analytic ellipse stands in for the integrated `predictedLine`), calling `showOrbitLine(style)` or `hideOrbitLine()` in the update phase and `syncOrbitLine()` in the sync phase. `equatorNodes: EquatorNodeMarkerPair | null` (default `null`) is the same shape again for this entity's own EqAN/EqDN icons; `ensureEquatorNodes(markerManager)` lazily creates and returns it, so a module that decides this object needs them is the one that brings the pair into being, and `dispose()` takes it down with the mesh (see `marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts` below). `dispose()` is also **the one place a mesh's own GPU resources are released**, for every entity kind: it removes `renderObject` from the scene, drops the marker/equator-node pair, then walks `renderObject` and disposes each mesh's geometry/material **only where `userData.ownsGeometry`/`ownsMaterial` says this instance owns it**. Those flags are declared at build time by whoever mints the mesh (`render/ships.ts`'s `withDispose`, and `cloneIndependent` which marks the material it clones but never the geometry it shares with the parse cache), and an unflagged mesh is treated as sharing — the safe default, since failing to free a per-instance resource costs memory while freeing a module-cached one leaves every later entity of that kind drawing from a released buffer. No subclass overrides this traversal. `predictedLine: TrajectoryLine | null` (default `null`) is the same shape for the integrated future in place of the analytic ellipse, built by `showPredictedLine(style)`; `actualLine: TrajectoryLine | null` (default `null`) is its past-facing counterpart, drawn from `actual`'s own retained history rather than from the prediction. `syncTrajectoryLines(frame, simTime, displayTime, pastDuration, predictedTo, ephemeris, fo, camera, attractors)` takes no `show` flag — **whether a line is drawn is exactly whether the entity is holding one**, so it skips whichever of the two is `null` and otherwise calls `TrajectoryLine.syncGeometry(this.predicted, simTime, predictedTo, frame, ephemeris, attractors)`/`syncTransform`/`sync` — `predicted` itself is the argument, not a per-call spliced array, `simTime` is the line's own drawn lower bound, and `predictedTo` (the caller-resolved display-window end, or `null` for a permanently-truncated prediction that must not keep drawing past where it died) is the upper bound the line is asked to reach: when the held prediction falls short of it, `TrajectoryLine` itself continues the line with a two-body Kepler extrapolation from the tip rather than `GameEntity` or its caller switching to a second, analytic line (`trajectory-line.ts`, below). `TrajectoryLine` clamps the lower bound to its baked span and interpolates its sampler there for the leading vertex, so the line's leading end tracks the entity's current position (to within `discardPredictionIfDiverged`'s tolerance) without `GameEntity` needing to manufacture a first sample or splice it onto `predicted`'s own array. Because the array `TrajectoryLine` bakes from is `predicted.samplesOldestFirst()`, whose reference only changes when the prediction actually advances, `TrajectoryLine`'s reference-equality re-bake guard holds across ordinary frames — passing a fresh `simTime` only re-derives the sampler's time mapping, not the baked vertices.

`src/game/game-entity/` — the entity base and the non-vessel entity classes, one file per class:
- `parts.ts` — the搭載要素 vocabulary: `PartType` (split into `ExteriorPartType`/`InteriorPartType` after 第8版第1巻 §6-1's own two tables, plus `hull`/`armor` as the structural pair §6-5 names when it says the armour total drops to 0 once the hull or the cockpit is gone), one interface per element carrying exactly the performance values §6-1 lists for it, the `PART_DEFAULTS` table `createPart` layers a caller's overrides onto, `partFromSaveData` (one `createPart` call, so a newly registered element needs no second switch arm), and the two aggregation primitives every consumer of the heat budget shares: `powerDrawOf(part)` (the standing electrical draw — communication/flywheel/magnetorquer/autopilot/farm/life support, and a *movable* combat shield; a destroyed part draws nothing) and `extraWasteHeatOf(part)` (the heat a farm or a life-support unit gives off beyond its own draw). **An engine is a cycle × propellant pair** (`EngineCycle` × `economy/propellant-compatibility.ts`'s `PropellantId`) rather than a bare thrust number, and **the magazine and the ammunition are separate elements** (§6-2 — a magazine is a container, so ammunition can be resupplied rather than conjured by carrying one). Attitude torque comes from `flywheel`, translation from `rcs_thruster`, and the main-engine thrust from `engine`, which is why no single element carries both a thrust and a torque any more.
- `game-entity.ts` — `GameEntity` (base: mesh/HP/alive/attitude/AI-facing state, plus the fields that let an instance stand in for `physics/attractor.ts`'s `Attractor` with no conversion: `readonly id: string` (explicit constructor argument, or self-assigned by the base `EntityIdAllocator` when omitted), `name: string` (the display name every marker/list/window shows, defaulting to the id — `Vessel`/`Vessel` overwrite it in their own constructors, and a rename writes it, so there is no second name field anywhere), `radius = 0` (physical radius [m]; 0 = point, the same value `Attractor.radius` reads), `collides = false` (rigid-contact participation — independent of `radius`, since a bullet or debris fragment has a physical size but must not bounce off the hull), `mu = 0` (gravity constant GM; 0 = no gravity — a body is a gravity source purely by `mu !== 0`, decided nowhere else), `degree2: Degree2Gravity | null = null` and `isStar = false` — both plain mutable fields (not `readonly`) precisely so a derived class can build its own field at construction, the way `Asteroid` does. `protected setGravitatingMass(mass)` is the one place a mass becomes a `mu` (`mu = GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT * mass`, also recording `mass` itself for rigid-contact use), so a subclass never derives `mu` by hand. `EntityManager.attractors()` filters on `mu !== 0 && alive` alone — `radius`/`collides` don't gate it, since a body's shape and its collision participation are separate questions from whether it pulls. The constructor's `addToScene` argument (default `true`) controls only whether `obj` is added to `scene` — `Bullet` and a `casing`/`fragment` `DebrisPiece` pass `false`, since those are drawn through `EntityManager`'s `InstancedPool`s instead (`render/instanced-pool.ts`, above); `obj` itself is unaffected and still receives its transform from `sync` every frame, since the pool reads it from there. Delegates the actual "time-stamped state + its sample history + advance itself" job to `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory`, held as the public `actual` field. `state`/`prevState`/`history` are thin forwarding getters to `actual` — the ~20 call sites across the codebase that only ever read `.state` are unaffected by this split. The `state` **setter** forwards to `actual.reset(...)`, the sole entry point for a discontinuous external overwrite (rigid contact, recoil); `stepActual(dt, attractors)` never goes through it — it self-gates on `alive` and hands `actual.step(...)` the caller-supplied gravity-source array plus the entity's own `bcInv`/`thrust`/`sampleInterval(attractors)`/`historyDuration` (a getter = `max(baseHistoryDuration, requestedHistoryDuration)` — the per-class floor raised by whatever past-display span `requestHistoryDuration(sec)` was last handed, capped at `HISTORY_DURATION_MAX`; the retained sample *count* stays bounded at `ARC_MAX_SAMPLES` regardless, since `sampleInterval` decimates against the same window, so a longer past costs coarser interpolation rather than more memory; an entity whose `baseHistoryDuration` is 0 keeps no history whatever is requested) (integration itself is `actual`'s job, not this class's; `Simulator.substep` decides `attractors` for this step — see `simulation/attractors.ts` below). `orbitalElementsOf(center)` is the only way to get osculating elements out of an entity — there is no bare `elements` getter, since which body an ellipse is centered on is a per-call choice (typically `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`), not a fixed property of the entity; it memoizes on `state`'s object identity and `center.id`, both of which only change together with an actual new orbit. `sampleInterval(attractors)` derives the history's push interval from `attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod(state.r, attractors)` fed into `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s exported `trajectorySampleInterval` (the period around whichever body pulls hardest right now, divided by `TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV`, falling back to a `SHIP_HISTORY_DURATION`-based value when no finite period exists, e.g. hyperbolic orbits — the same formula `PredictedArc.step` itself calls, so the real-state history and the predicted-future arc never disagree on it) and is skipped entirely while `historyDuration === 0` (bullets/casings/debris never need it), `torque`/`thrust` slots the owning system writes each frame, and a default `checkLoss(_dt, _simTime, _activeStage, _playerPos, attractors)` that calls `attractor.ts`'s `reachedBody(actual.prevState, state, attractors, 0)` — surface contact with *any* attractor, not just Earth, since a stray debris piece near the moon shouldn't pass through it; the `attractors` argument is whichever body window that substep already holds — `Simulator` hands `EntityManager.cleanup` the endpoint all-body window while rigid contact is being resolved (it is built for the contact resolution anyway, and covers the `mu = 0` display bodies), and the substep's own midpoint gravity window above that warp band, where the surface-reach check is the only consumer left. The second case trades the `mu = 0` bodies' reentry check for having the living gravity-bearing entities (an `Asteroid`) as surface-contact partners). `orbitLine: OrbitLine | null` (default `null`) is the entity's own Kepler-ellipse line around whichever body it currently orbits — the same show/hide-owns-the-line shape as `predictedLine`/`actualLine`: `showOrbitLine(style)`/`hideOrbitLine()` are what actually build/dispose it, so whether the field is non-`null` is exactly whether something has asked for the line to be shown. `syncOrbitLine(fo, camera, attractors)` is a no-op when `orbitLine` is `null` and otherwise resolves the center via `strongestAttractor(state.r, attractors)` and syncs it to `orbitalElementsAround(center)` — the entity resolves its own center rather than being handed one, so every subclass that owns an `orbitLine` shares this one implementation instead of each re-deriving the center logic. Those flags are declared at build time by whoever mints the mesh (`render/ships.ts`'s `withDispose`, and `cloneIndependent` which marks the material it clones but never the geometry it shares with the parse cache), and an unflagged mesh is treated as sharing — the safe default, since failing to free a per-instance resource costs memory while freeing a module-cached one leaves every later entity of that kind drawing from a released buffer. No subclass overrides this traversal. `predictedLine: TrajectoryLine | null` (default `null`) is the same shape for the integrated future in place of the analytic ellipse, built by `showPredictedLine(style)`; `actualLine: TrajectoryLine | null` (default `null`) is its past-facing counterpart, drawn from `actual`'s own retained history rather than from the prediction. `syncTrajectoryLines(frame, simTime, displayTime, pastDuration, predictedTo, ephemeris, fo, camera, attractors)` takes no `show` flag — **whether a line is drawn is exactly whether the entity is holding one**, so it skips whichever of the two is `null` and otherwise calls `TrajectoryLine.syncGeometry(this.predicted, simTime, predictedTo, frame, ephemeris, attractors)`/`syncTransform`/`sync` — `predicted` itself is the argument, not a per-call spliced array, `simTime` is the line's own drawn lower bound, and `predictedTo` (the caller-resolved display-window end, or `null` for a permanently-truncated prediction that must not keep drawing past where it died) is the upper bound the line is asked to reach: when the held prediction falls short of it, `TrajectoryLine` itself continues the line with a two-body Kepler extrapolation from the tip rather than `GameEntity` or its caller switching to a second, analytic line (`trajectory-line.ts`, below). `TrajectoryLine` clamps the lower bound to its baked span and interpolates its sampler there for the leading vertex, so the line's leading end tracks the entity's current position (to within `discardPredictionIfDiverged`'s tolerance) without `GameEntity` needing to manufacture a first sample or splice it onto `predicted`'s own array. Because the array `TrajectoryLine` bakes from is `predicted.samplesOldestFirst()`, whose reference only changes when the prediction actually advances, `TrajectoryLine`'s reference-equality re-bake guard holds across ordinary frames — passing a fresh `simTime` only re-derives the sampler's time mapping, not the baked vertices.

- `src/game/display-window-manager.ts` / `hud/predict-panel.ts` — `DisplayWindowManager`: "when and in what coordinate frame to look" — resolves, once per frame, the `DisplayWindow` every future-aware consumer shares (`{frame, simTime, referencePeriod, duration, pastDuration, displayTime, tickLabelMode}`) and the merged attractor window (analytic bodies plus any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`), instead of each consumer re-deriving them. **"Window" is an overloaded word in this codebase: here it names the coordinate-frame-plus-displayTime bundle this class resolves — not a time span alone, and not a DOM window** — a line drawn in one coordinate frame and a marker projected from another would visibly disagree, and a mesh drawn at one displayTime and a marker positioned at another would point at different instants, so the two have to be settled together, once, per frame. The class owns six independent pieces of settable state: the display duration (private `durationKey: DisplayDurationKey` = `'orbit'` (default) /`'day'`/`'week'`/`'month'`/`'custom'` → `durationSec(referencePeriod)`), the *past* display duration (private `pastDurationKey: DisplayPastDurationKey`, the same set plus `'none'` (default) → `pastDurationSec(referencePeriod)`, 0 for `'none'` — the symmetric counterpart of `duration`, so the drawn span is `[simTime - pastDuration, simTime + duration]`; changing it deliberately does *not* touch `sliderT`, since the past is not a span the future-ghost cursor reaches into), the tick-label notation (`tickLabelMode`, a get/set `TickLabelMode` accessor — `'absolute'` (default) writes a time label as its UTC calendar form, `'relative'` as the signed elapsed time from `simTime`; carried in `DisplayWindow` so every future-aware consumer's labels read the same way), the future-ghost slider position (private `sliderT`), `forceCurrent` (a get/set accessor, starting `true`, flipped by `ViewManager`; while set, `resolve()`'s `displayTime` always returns the present, and the setter also zeroes `sliderT` — leaving combat view always resets the slider to the present rather than resuming wherever it was left, and `panel.onDurationSelect`/`onCustomDurationConfirm` zero it the same way, since switching how far the slider can reach mid-drag would otherwise leave it pointing at a now-meaningless fraction of the new span), and `frame` (a get/set `ReferenceFrame` accessor — the coordinate frame the future is drawn in: the planned trajectory's polyline (`PlanPath`), a ship's predicted-trajectory line, and the equatorial-node markers all bake into this one frame, independent of `MapCamera.cameraFrame` — see `frame-controls.ts` below for how a player selects the two separately, and `map-camera.ts` for why the camera's own frame is a distinct value). `durationSec` holds no orbital period of its own: for `'day'`/`'week'`/`'month'` it looks the constant up in a `Record<key, seconds>` table (a compile-time-exhaustive alternative to an if-chain — adding a preset without a table entry is a type error rather than a silent fallthrough), `'custom'` reads the private `customDurationSec` instead, and only `'orbit'` returns `referencePeriod` as-is (falling back to `C.APERIODIC_ARC_DURATION` when it isn't finite/positive) — every caller resolves its own notion of "one orbit" and passes it in, so this class never has to decide whose orbit or around which body. A private `currentOrbitPeriod(player, simTime)` supplies it for the game-wide display window (the active ship's osculating period around whichever body currently pulls hardest, `NaN` with no ship — it assembles the full gravity-source array and runs `strongestAttractor`); `plan.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom` supplies it separately for the plan's trailing segment and `Plan.nodeTimeRange`, from that segment's own start state — the two contexts can disagree about "one orbit" without either owning the other. `resolve(simTime, player): DisplayWindow` is the single per-frame entry point — it takes `simTime`/`player` as plain arguments rather than holding `Simulator`/`ActiveVesselController` references itself, and caches on those two (plus `player.state`'s identity) and a private `revision` counter (bumped by the `frame`/`forceCurrent`/`tickLabelMode` setters and every `PredictPanel` callback — duration-pill select, custom-duration confirm, tick-label-mode toggle, slider drag, reset-to-now, jump-to-time), so `currentOrbitPeriod()` only actually reruns when one of those has moved — `Game` calls `resolve` from several points a frame (see `game.ts` above), most of which are cache hits and return `current` as-is. `attractorsAt(simTime)` merges `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(simTime)` with `EntityManager.attractors()` (`simulation/attractors.ts`'s `mergeAttractors`), cached on reference equality of both inputs plus `simTime` so repeated same-frame calls return the identical array. `sync(player)` pushes the whole frame's state into the panel through a single `panel.render(state)` call — no per-field setters — built from `current` plus this class's own `durationKey`/`customDurationSec`/`sliderT`/a private `predictionCoverageRatio(player)` (the active ship's `predicted.state.t` expressed as a fraction of `current`'s `[simTime, simTime + duration]` span, clamped to `[0, 1]`, defaulting to `1` with no ship/prediction/duration — computed fresh inside `sync` itself, with no external push)/`sliderSteps()`, and also caches `current.duration` as `lastDuration` for a jump/edit input firing later that same tick outside the frame flow (`onJumpToTime`) to resolve `sliderT` against, and pushes the tick marks `hud/tick-scale.ts`'s `buildTicks(current.duration, TICK_MAX_COUNT)` returns (positions at round multiples of a chosen interval rather than equal divisions of the span, so the panel shows scale regardless of which duration is selected). `sliderSteps()` sizes the slider's step count from `lastDuration` at roughly `SLIDER_TARGET_STEP_SEC` per step, clamped to `[SLIDER_MIN_STEPS, SLIDER_MAX_STEPS]` — the panel's `sliderT` interface stays 0..1 regardless, so no caller needs to know the current step count. `PredictPanel` is its DOM: five rows — a future-duration pill row and a past-duration pill row, both built from the same module-private `DurationPillRow` (`1周`/`1日`/`7日`/`28日` fixed pills — the past row prefixes a `なし` pill — plus a `任意…` pill that swaps itself for an inline value+unit `DurationValueInput` when pressed, confirmed with ✓/Enter or cancelled with ✕/Escape/blur; a row being edited holds off its own re-render so a keystroke can't be overwritten), a `目盛りを相対表記` `ToggleSwitch` row (two named states rather than three-or-more, so it is a switch and not a `SegmentedControl`, per `DEVELOP/DESIGN-RULES.md` §3; ON reports `'relative'` back through `onTickLabelModeChange`), a scrubber row (a `⏮` reset-to-now icon button, the range slider itself — its track background linearly gradients from the normal color to a dimmed one at `predictionRatio`, visualizing how far the player's own `predicted` has actually reached versus how far the slider can be dragged — an always-visible absolute-UTC-date label, and a `T+`-elapsed label that swaps itself for the same kind of inline `DurationValueInput` on click, confirmed against the *current* `lastDuration` as the clamp range rather than a fixed year), and a tick row underneath the scrubber, which describes the future span alone (each tick's text-anchor picked from its own `t` position — near-0 left-aligned, near-1 right-aligned, otherwise centered — rather than a `:first-child`/`:last-child` CSS rule, since under `'custom'` the final tick is not guaranteed to land exactly at `t = 1`). `DurationValueInput` only clamps and reports on an explicit commit (Enter, blur, or its owner's `commit()`/`openWithSec()` call) — never on every keystroke — so a value being typed can't be silently mangled mid-edit; `cancel()` (Escape, or its owner's ✕) restores nothing itself, since the caller never applied the in-progress edit to begin with. The constructor wraps the panel in its own `#hud-predict-wrap` alongside a `hud/hud-root.ts`'s `buildCollapseToggle` button (`PREDICT_TOGGLE_LABELS`) — the same collapse mechanism `hud/hud-root.ts`'s left/right rail toggles use — so the PREDICT bar itself can be folded away independently of `render`'s own `visible` field: that field is view-driven (hidden outright in combat view) while the collapse toggle's `.collapsed` class is the player's own choice within map view, and the two compose rather than overriding each other. Owned by `Game` as a private field, constructed right after `EntityManager` and before `EffectsSystem` — its constructor takes only `Ephemeris`/`EntityManager` references (not `Simulator`/`ActiveVesselController`, which `resolve`'s `simTime`/`player` arguments replace instead), early enough to be handed onward to `PlanEditor`/`ViewManager`/`FrameControls`, each constructed only after it. Shared onward two ways rather than by handing out the whole class: `plan.ts`'s narrow `DisplayDurationSource` interface (`{durationSec(referencePeriod): number}`), which this class satisfies structurally — `plan-editor.ts`/`plan-display.ts`/`plan-path.ts` hold a `DisplayDurationSource`, never a `DisplayWindowManager` (they import only the `DisplayWindow` value type from it), so the plan/ folder's PREDICT-tracking dependency is exactly "how long is one display duration," nothing else on the concrete class; and the resolved `DisplayWindow` value itself, passed as a plain argument into `PlanEditor.update`/`PlanDisplay.update`/`EntityLineManager.update`/`.sync`/`MapPickables.refresh` each frame rather than any of those holding a `DisplayWindowManager` reference. The duration scales the future-ghost slider and, through `segmentDurationFrom`, the length of the plan's trailing segment and the range a node may be dragged across.
- `ammo-pickup.ts` — `AmmoPickup`: the orbital resupply pickup `stage-utils/logistics.ts` spawns and the player absorbs on approach (`bcInv = SMALL_DEBRIS_BCINV`, `predictedForGhost = true`, `radius = AMMO_PHYS_RADIUS`, `collides = true`). `id` is always set — the constructor takes an optional override (`CreativeStage` passes its own placement id, the `{saved}` init variant passes the saved id) and otherwise self-generates one through `entity-id.ts`'s shared `EntityIdAllocator('ammo-')` when omitted, so every spawn — including `Logistics`' auto-resupply, which never passes one — has an id a caller can key a `MapPickable`/window on without inventing one at the call site.
- `debris-piece.ts` — `DebrisPiece`/`DebrisKind` (`bcInv = SMALL_DEBRIS_BCINV`): the one class backing fragments/ejected barrels/magazine frames/casings — `DebrisKind` picks which `render/ships.ts` builder runs (`barrel`/`magazineFrame`/`casing`) or, for `fragment`, which extra `checkLoss` rule applies (only `casing` pieces expire on `CASING_LIFETIME`; the rest only via the shared any-attractor surface-contact check). `fragment` pieces get `collides = false` (pass through collision untouched, though they may still carry a nonzero `radius`); the others take the caller-supplied `radius` and `collides = true`. A `fragment`'s look is not built per instance: at construction it draws a `fragmentVariant` index (0..`DEBRIS_FRAGMENT_VARIANT_COUNT`) and a `fragmentColor` (30% chance of `COLOR_SHIP_DARK_HULL`, else the caller's `accent`) from `render/ships.ts`'s fixed, pre-baked variant geometries (`debrisFragmentResources`) — `obj` itself carries no mesh, only the transform (`obj.scale.setScalar(size)` at construction, position/attitude from `sync` same as any entity), since the geometry lives once per variant in `EntityManager`'s per-variant `InstancedPool`s and `fragmentColor` is pushed as that pool's per-instance color.
- `bullet.ts` — `Bullet extends GameEntity`: player/enemy bullets and enemy plasma bolts (`Shooter`/`BulletType`), `bcInv = BULLET_BCINV`, its own loss check (**distance from the player first — `BULLET_MAX_DIST` — with the lifetime only as a backstop**, since a runaway bullet count still costs one instance slot each even though they share a draw call), and a velocity-facing (not attitude-facing) `sync` override that uses `fo.VtoThreeV3`. it declares none of the three prediction reasons, so `predictsFuture` stays `false` and `displayState(displayTime)` is `null` for the entire time the future-ghost slider is active — bullets simply disappear rather than showing a nonsensical projected flight path, which is fine since their lifetime is a few seconds.
- `asteroid.ts` — `Asteroid extends GameEntity`: the one class that both exerts and receives gravity through the ordinary integration path — no new orbit classification, it rides `stepActual`/`DynamicTrajectory` exactly like every other entity. The constructor derives `radius`/`collides = true` (from the caller-supplied physical radius) and, via `GameEntity.setGravitatingMass(mass)`, `mu = GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT * mass`, so the pull it exerts and the weight it hits with can never disagree. Optional `j2`/`c22` (both default 0, i.e. a point mass) build a `degree2` field once at construction — pole = its own `att.q`-rotated model +Y, tesseral long axis = model +Z rotated the same way, the same convention `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinOrientation` uses — rather than a getter, since `Asteroid` sits outside `Simulator.stepAttitudes`' target arrays (vessels/casings/debris/ammos) and so never rotates after construction; a getter would rebuild the pole vector every RK4 stage for every entity for no reason. Its `mu !== 0` alone already makes `predictedAsGravitySource` true, and it additionally declares `predictedAsPlanCollider` — an asteroid grows its own future-ghost through `Predictor` exactly like a ship, so a gravity-bearing entity's predicted trajectory is available for `FutureAttractors.bodyAt` (`simulation/future-attractors.ts` below) to read from rather than needing a frozen stand-in. `bcInv`/`srpCoeff` stay at the `GameEntity` default of 0 (no drag/radiation-pressure model for a bare rock). Distinct from `game/celestial/point-field.ts`/`point-field-view.ts`'s point cloud, which is display-only and neither a `GameEntity` nor a gravity source — see that module's own note.

`src/game/simulation/` — the array that holds live entities, plus the systems that actually drive it each frame; split from `game-entity/` because these are updaters *over* the arrays, not entity classes themselves:
- `entity-id.ts` — `EntityIdAllocator`: the one implementation of "mint `${prefix}${counter}` unless a caller supplies an id to restore, in which case adopt it and advance the counter past it" — the pattern `AmmoPickup`/`Vessel`/`Asteroid` all need (a restored save must keep its old id, but the counter must never hand out that id again to a fresh spawn). Each class holds its own instance keyed by its own prefix; the allocator has no cross-class state.
- `entity-manager.ts` — `EntityManager`: owns the `vessels`/`bullets`/`casings`/`debris`/`ammoPickups`/`asteroids` arrays. **Every craft — a crewed ship, an orbital base, a hostile — is one element of `vessels`**; `ownShips()`/`hostileVessels()`/`baseVessels()` are cached derivations of that single array (faction plus `hasBaseModule`), rebuilt with the same `collectionRevision` guard `all()`/`attractors()` use, never separate stores. `addVessel`/`removeVessel`/`parkVessel` are the roster's only mutators (`parkVessel` splices without disposing — that is what "stored in a base" means, and it folds the parked vessel's equator-node markers since no per-frame sync will reach it again); `findVessel(id)` plus the role-narrowed `findOwnShip`/`findHostile`/`findBaseVessel` are the id lookups menu actions, `NavTarget`'s plane resolution and the equatorial-node source list read. `cleanup(dt, simTime, activeStage, playerPos, attractors)` runs `checkLoss` over `all()` uniformly and `prune`s the non-vessel arrays only — **a dead vessel is removed by `active-vessel-controller.ts`'s `ActiveVesselController.reclaimDead()`**, once per frame from `Game.advanceSimulation` right after `Simulator.advance` returns, because the reference cleanup (nav target, combat target, camera focus) and the hand-off to the next living ship do not belong inside the substep loop. `updateVessels(activeVessel, input, simSpeed, dt, activeStage, ephemeris)` is the single per-frame entry point for every craft's own behaviour: it loops `vessels` and hands the real `input` only to `vessel === activeVessel && simSpeed.canShipAct`, `null` to every other. **That one ternary is the whole of "which craft may be operated right now"** — being the operated one and the warp level staying inside the physical band are the same question. `syncVessels(...)` drives `Vessel.syncVessel` over every living vessel, telling each whether it is the operated one and resolving its display kind through `map-pickable.ts`'s `vesselMapKind`; `applyVisibility` folds the class toggles into every vessel/pickup mesh through that same derivation; `updateBaseEquatorNodes` walks `baseVessels()`; `syncEquatorNodes` walks `all()`. **It also owns the `render/instanced-pool.ts` `InstancedPool`s that draw bullets/casings/debris fragments** (`bulletBodyPool`/`bulletHaloPool`/`plasmaPool`/`casingPool`, plus one `debrisFragmentPools` entry per fragment variant), since those share one geometry/material per kind and instancing needs a single owner above the arrays; `sync(fo, displayTime)` walks the non-vessel entities, then `beginFrame`/`push`/`endFrame` on each pool. Vessels are excluded there because `Vessel.syncVessel` does its own richer sync. **It also owns `EffectsSystem`** (the public readonly `effects` field) rather than `Game` holding a sibling field: a spawned `DebrisPiece` is itself an entity, and nesting it here breaks what would otherwise be a construction-order cycle. The constructor's trailing `saved?: GameSaveData` is what the private `restoreFromSave` rebuilds the roster from — one `VesselDeps` built once, then `savedShip`/`savedHostile`/`savedBase` per saved record. **A fresh launch builds nothing here**: the starting world is the `Stage`'s own (`stages/stage.ts`'s `addOwnShip`). `attractors()` filters `all()` down to the living, gravity-bearing (`mu !== 0`) entities; `collectionRevision` is how an outside cache asks whether the roster changed, since the returned arrays keep their identity.
