Owned by `Game` and exposed as the public `entities` field.

  **The operated vessel is just `Game.player`'s current pointer into `vessels`**, not a separately-tracked identity — `vessels` holds every craft (a campaign stage's lone ship, a station, a hostile) on equal footing, and `active-vessel-controller.ts`'s `ActiveVesselController.set` only ever reassigns which element `player` (the getter it backs) points at. Nothing filters it out before processing: `Simulator.substep`/`stepAttitudes` step every element of `vessels` uniformly at the same per-substep `subDt`, `EntityManager.cleanup`'s `checkLoss` pass covers them via `all()`, and `ContactPhysics.resolveSubstep` takes `entities.all()` as its participant list outright. The two places that single the operated vessel out do so through the explicit `player` argument rather than by excluding it from an array: `Predictor.update` spends its first budget slice on it whenever it is itself one of the frame's growth targets, then round-robins the background target set skipping it there (`e !== interactiveShip`). `EntityManager.sync` is the one place `vessels` is genuinely skipped wholesale, since every vessel syncs itself via `Vessel.syncVessel` instead.
- `entity-line-manager.ts` — `EntityLineManager`: the single place that decides which of an entity's lines (`orbitLine`/`predictedLine`/`actualLine`) is shown, and in what `LineStyle`. Owned by `Game` as a private field, holding only an `EntityManager` reference (constructed right after `entities`, ahead of `DisplayWindowManager`) — `Targeter` is not held by reference either; which entity is the primary/secondary combat target arrives as a plain per-frame argument, the same way `Predictor`/`ContactPhysics` take `EntityManager` by reference and act over its arrays rather than owning any state of their own. Split across the two phases like every other per-frame owner: `update(activePlayer, primaryTarget, secondaryTarget, overviewMode, displayWindow, visibilityPolicy)` — called from `Game.update`'s tail, right after `handlePointerInput()`, so every input it reads (which ship is active, which target is locked, which view is showing) is already this frame's settled value — decides, per living player/enemy/base, whether that entity's line is shown at all and calls `show*Line(style)`/`hide*Line()` accordingly. **The combat target's highlight line is that entity's own `orbitLine`, not a line `Targeter` holds**: whichever player or enemy is the primary/secondary target has `showOrbitLine` called with a target style built fresh every call from `theme.ts`'s `currentThemePalette()` (primary = `palette.accent`, secondary = `palette.secondary`, opacity `TARGET_LINE_OPACITY`=0.9, `renderOrder` = `const.ts`'s `LINE_RENDER_ORDER.target`/`.secondaryTarget`) rather than a static `const.ts`'s `LINE_STYLE` entry, since the highlight has to track a live theme switch with no subscription of its own; it shows **regardless of view** (an ordinary enemy orbit line is map-only) and is admitted by `MapVisibilityPolicy`'s orbit toggle with the opposite fallback an ordinary line gets (`?? true` rather than `?? false`) whenever no policy exists at all. A targeted player's own `predictedLine`/`actualLine` are hidden rather than shown, so a highlighted ship shows only its orbit line, not its future/past trajectory. **Which line stands for the operated ship's own orbit is decided by view**: combat view draws it as the analytic ellipse (`showOrbitLine(LINE_STYLE.playerOrbit)`, with `predictedLine` hidden), the map draws the integrated `predictedLine` instead — the past line follows the ordinary rule in both, independent of that choice. Every other line's look still comes from `const.ts`'s `LINE_STYLE` table (`playerOrbit`/`playerPredicted`/`playerActual`/`enemyOrbit`/`baseOrbit`) — an untargeted enemy's line additionally layers its own `orbitLineColor` on top of `LINE_STYLE.enemyOrbit`. `sync(displayWindow, fo, camera, attractors, ephemeris)` — called from `Game.sync`, right after `editor.sync` (the same coordinate frame the planned trajectory's polyline bakes into, so the two read as comparable on screen) — never creates or destroys a line: it loops every player/enemy/base unconditionally and calls `ship.syncTrajectoryLines(...)`/`ship.syncOrbitLine(...)`/`enemy.syncOrbitLine(...)`/`base.syncOrbitLine(...)`, each already a no-op on an entity holding no line, so whether anything is actually drawn was settled entirely by `update`. Each `syncOrbitLine` is passed `entity.thrust !== null` as its `force` argument, since `OrbitLine` otherwise holds its baked ellipse until the osculating elements drift past a threshold and a burn moves them continuously.
- `simulator.ts` — `Simulator`: the actual simulation step. Owns **`simTime`/`lastSimDt`**, `ContactPhysics`, and takes the `EntityManager` reference in its constructor rather than holding any array itself — splitting "which entities exist" (`EntityManager`) from "how they're stepped" (`Simulator`) keeps each to one responsibility. `advance(dt, simDt, player, activeStage, simSpeed, nanWatchdog)` takes the frame's `SimSpeedManager` directly and asks it for `resolveCollision = simSpeed.canResolvePhysicalCollisions` itself — the caller never decides or simplifies this beforehand, and the substep ceiling is always `adaptiveSimulationMaxStep` regardless of that gate. `advance` splits the frame's `simDt` into substeps (count = `ceil(simDt / maxStep)` — depends only on the simulated time this frame, never on the real frame rate. `maxStep` is `SUBSTEP_MAX_DT` until the frame's own `simDt` would need more than `SUBSTEP_MAX_COUNT` (=64) substeps to cover at that width, past which the step widens instead so the per-frame cost stops tracking the warp multiplier; 64 is one revolution in 54 steps at the top warp in LEO, where the numerical orbit decay is 3% of what atmospheric drag removes at that altitude anyway); `advance` builds each substep's gravity window once, at that substep's own midpoint (`simTime + dt/2`), via `simulation/attractors.ts`'s `attractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` and hands it to `substep` — merging `Ephemeris.attractorsAt`'s `mu !== 0` subset with any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity` (`entities.attractors()`) into a single array — then `classifyAttractors` splits that array once into the `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` heaviest bodies (`always`) and a `SpatialGrid` the rest are binned into (`physics/spatial-grid.ts`, cell size derived from the split — see `attractors.ts` below), and finally every live entity's own `stepActual(dt, attractorsNearInto(entity.state.r, classified, scratch, entity.id))` reads only its own 27-neighbor cell block plus `always`, with its own id excluded so a gravity-bearing entity never sums a pull on itself (`game-entity/game-entity.ts`). Assembling the classified structure once and handing every entity a lookup against the same one (rather than each entity re-deriving its own) is a correctness requirement, not an optimization: two gravity-bearing entities integrated against structures built at different moments would see each other in slightly different positions, breaking the symmetry a mutual interaction must have — excluding an entity from its own lookup doesn't touch this, since the shared classified structure underneath is still the one built once for this substep, and only that entity's own view of it loses its own entry. The substep ceiling itself is `adaptiveSimulationMaxStep` (`simulation/time-step.ts`) over the living **ships** alone (`entities.players`/`entities.enemies`): a ship at or descending toward `REENTRY_SUBSTEP_ALT` pulls the whole frame's ceiling down to `REENTRY_SUBSTEP_MAX_DT`, since heating and dynamic pressure are non-linear in the step size and their integrated result is what decides whether that ship survives — an outcome the player observes. Every other kind (bullets/casings/debris/ammo/bases/asteroids) is left out: reaching the atmosphere only removes it, and *when* it is removed, to what precision, changes nothing about play — surface contact itself is caught at any step size by `attractor.ts`'s swept `reachedBody`. Each substep is also cut short at the next known event boundary (`nextEventTime`), which combines two sources on deliberately different cadences: the **stage's** own `nextSimulationEventTime` is asked every substep, since a `PlanExecutor` ignition time is recomputed from the ship's current Δv and acceleration on each call and can move between substeps; the **entities'** minimum is a fixed absolute deadline per entity, so it is resolved once and held (`cachedEventTime`), re-scanned only when `simTime` reaches it, when `EntityManager.collectionRevision` moves, or when the passive-warp LOD flips. The revision is what makes that safe in the direction that matters: a *removed* entity only leaves a boundary standing where nothing happens, but an *added* one carrying an earlier deadline would make a held minimum too late and let a substep step straight over the event, so "did the roster change" has to be answered by the manager's own generation counter rather than inferred from the arrays. While that LOD is on, the entities it batches into a single trailing integration (`Bullet`/`DebrisPiece`, the set `isPassiveWarpEntity` names) are left out of the scan as well — nothing is resolved at their deadlines during those substeps, so a boundary there would subdivide the step for no one. `Simulator` only iterates the arrays; the orbit integration itself is the entity's responsibility. It also owns the attitude integration for *all* entities including every self-flown ship (`stepAttitudes`). AI/input/spawn decisions live in `game.ts`/`stages/`/`Enemy.behave`, which add entities back in via `entities.addXxx`.
- `predictor.ts` — `Predictor`: a third updater over `EntityManager`'s arrays, symmetric with `Simulator` (①holds the arrays / ②steps them / ③predicts their future — `Predictor` is ③) rather than nested inside `Simulator`, so it doesn't inherit that class's `simTime` concerns or vice versa. Owned directly by `Game` and driven from `Game.update`'s main flow, unconditionally — after `PlanEditor.update` and before `Targeter.updateEquatorNodes`, no longer nested inside `advanceSimulation` — so it keeps growing while paused or after the stage has decided, since `simTime` holds still then and nothing can diverge. **The growth target set is derived from whether anything actually reads that entity's future, never from which view is showing**: `entities.all().filter((e) => e.hasFutureReader(canDisplayFuture))`, `canDisplayFuture` being `update`'s own fourth argument (`Game` passes `!displayWindowManager.forceCurrent`) — the one of `hasFutureReader`'s four reasons that isn't self-contained, since the future ghost only reads ahead while the display time may leave the present. In combat view that leaves the operated ship out (its orbit is drawn as the analytic ellipse so it carries no `predictedLine`, its ghost is pinned to now, and it has no mass), so on a stage with no gravity-bearing or plan-collider entity the target set is simply empty and the only arcs still costing anything are the plan's own — those arrive as `planArcs` rather than through the target set, and the planned polyline is drawn in combat view too. The divergence check is a separate pass and still runs over every predicting entity in `entities.all()` regardless, so a prediction nothing is growing can't sit frozen and keep feeding `FutureAttractors` a stale trajectory. There is one frame budget, `ARC_STEP_BUDGET`: a view with nothing to grow costs nothing because its target set is empty, not because it is handed a smaller number. The only state it holds is a round-robin `cursor` into that frame's growth target set, a `divergenceAttractorsScratch` for the per-frame divergence check, and the `tracked`/`finished`/`discarded` counters plus the entity-side/plan-side step tallies `lastSteps`/`lastPlanSteps` (`finished` counts an entity whose prediction has either reached the horizon or been permanently truncated — a truncated list can never grow again, so counting it as unfinished would leave the readout stuck) — all read via `Game.perfCounts()` as `predicted`/`predictComplete`/`predictDiscarded`/`predictorSteps`/`planSteps`. `update(simTime, player, horizon, canDisplayFuture, planArcs)` takes `horizon` from `Game`'s `displayWindowManager.current.duration` (the frame's already-resolved `DisplayWindowManager.resolve(...)` result — see `game.ts` above and `display-window-manager.ts` below — how far the future-ghost slider can currently reach) and `planArcs` from `plan/plan-editor.ts`'s `PlanEditor.growableArcs()` — the frame's owned plan-segment arcs (`plan/plan-path.ts`'s `PlanPath`, below), in chronological order, already carrying this frame's `requiredEnd`/`retainFrom` (`PlanPath.update` writes both, and runs first — see `game.ts`'s frame order above), so this class only has to grow them. **It is not gated on time warp at all**: the budget outruns real time at every warp level — `ARC_MIN_STEP_DT` floors the step at 40 s (a value pinned by the far-end shape error the floor itself causes wherever it overrides the period-derived step — LEO and an eccentric orbit's perigee passage — not by the simulator's own `SUBSTEP_MAX_DT`, which is a ceiling on a different integration), so an `ARC_STEP_BUDGET` slice advances the active ship 12,000 sim-s per frame (720,000 sim-s per real second at 60fps) against the 131,072 the top warp level asks for. A gate here would stop growth while `discardPredictionIfDiverged` kept discarding, leaving the list permanently absent for that warp level and silently falling back to `OrbitLine`'s analytic ellipse, whose radius disagrees with the integrated trajectory under J2/third-body/drag. It first calls `discardPredictionIfDiverged` on every **predicting** entity in `entities.all()` (the active ship included, once, since `all()` doesn't repeat it) and tallies the counters — cheap (one binary search each); `predictsFuture` is checked before the call, so a bullet/casing/fragment — the bulk of `entities.all()` — costs nothing at all here. The `Attractor[]` that check hands each entity is `attractorsNearInto(entity.state.r, …)` off a single `classifyAttractors` built once at the top of the frame, so the divergence tolerance's center/period lookup reads the `always` set plus that position's own grid neighbourhood instead of the whole registry. **Budget spending is a two-layer split, and plan arcs ride the same interactive layer the active ship already had**: at most `ARC_STEP_BUDGET × ARC_INTERACTIVE_RATIO` (=0.5, or the whole budget once the background round-robin below has nobody but the active ship to visit) is spent, in order, on the active ship *when it is itself a target* (`interactiveShip`, `null` otherwise — a ship whose future nothing reads gets no interactive slice either) and then on every entry of `planArcs` in turn (grown directly through the private `grow` helper below, since `requiredEnd`/`retainFrom` are already set) — an arc that finished growing earlier this frame costs nothing and leaves its share for the next one, which is what makes the nearer plan segments finish before a farther one is touched at all, and the drawn line read as growing outward from the ship rather than every segment inching forward together. The remaining budget (the interactive cap's own leftover included) then goes to a *background* round-robin over the growth target set starting at `cursor`, saving wherever it stopped as next frame's `cursor` — it **skips the active ship** (`e !== interactiveShip`), since the interactive layer already served it and it would otherwise draw budget twice in one frame. Each entity in that pass gets an even share of what is left — `max(ARC_MIN_ITEM_STEPS, floor(budget / remaining visits))`, itself capped by the remaining budget — and whatever it doesn't spend rolls on to the next one. Handing one entity the whole remainder is what produces a discard/regrow loop: an entity served fewer steps than it takes to push its first sample has an `at(t)` that answers `null` almost everywhere, so the next frame's divergence check discards the list and the work restarts from scratch. `ARC_MIN_ITEM_STEPS` (=16) is the floor that prevents it, sized above the ~10 steps one sample interval costs at the 28-day preset in LEO. The interactive cap exists for the same reason it always did, now covering the plan too: a prediction — or a plan segment — is also read as a gravity source and collision partner by other entities' own predictions (through `FutureAttractors`), so letting either consume the whole budget would stall the growth the rest of the scene's own shape depends on. **`advanceBudget(entity, budgetSteps, simTime, horizon)` and the plan-arc loop share one private `grow(arc, budgetSteps)`** (`while (consumed < budgetSteps && arc.step()) consumed++`) — neither holds any step-sizing logic of its own, since that whole job (window resolution, `dt`, extrapolation/apsis center, surface-reach/burn-up detection) belongs to `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc` (below). `advanceBudget` additionally calls `entity.ensurePredictedArc(this.futureAttractors)` (`null` for a non-`predictsFuture` entity, costing nothing) and writes that frame's `requiredEnd = simTime + horizon`/`retainFrom = simTime` onto the arc before handing it to `grow`; the plan-arc loop needs neither write, since `PlanPath.update` already made them. It never asks *why* a list is short (freshly spawned vs. just invalidated) — that indifference is what keeps discards from ever spiking frame time, since a discarded list just competes for the same budget as a new one next frame.

- `predicted-arc.ts` — `PredictedArc`: the unified integration arc behind both every `GameEntity`'s own future trajectory (`GameEntity.ensurePredictedArc`, above) and every owned segment of a maneuver plan (`plan/plan-path.ts`'s `PlanPath`, below) — "from a frozen `state0`, grow the tip toward a caller-set `requiredEnd` one RK4 step at a time." It knows nothing about who owns it or when to be discarded; the two mutable fields `requiredEnd`/`retainFrom` are the owner's whole interface for "how far" and "since when" (`keepDuration = requiredEnd - retainFrom` feeds both the step size's coarsening term and `DynamicTrajectory.step`'s own retained window), and `needsGrowth = !truncated && trajectory.state.t < requiredEnd` is what `Predictor` polls in its budget loop. The constructor's `keplerTail: boolean` decides whether a tip that has stopped growing may still be read past its own end via a two-body Kepler extrapolation (`DynamicTrajectory.step`'s trailing `extrapolationCenter` argument, below) — an entity's own prediction takes `true` (the existing behavior, unchanged), a plan segment's owned arc takes `false`, since a plan segment's whole job is to reach the *next* node exactly, and a node placed on an extrapolated tail would disagree with what a later re-integration of that same span actually finds. `readonly sourceRevision: number` freezes `sources.revision` at construction, and `represents(state0, end, sourceRevision): boolean` is the sole reuse test an owner (today only `PlanPath`) needs to ask before replacing an arc outright: `sourceRevision` must match exactly, the arc's own recorded `decimation` (the coarsest `span / ARC_MAX_SAMPLES` any `step()` call has required, tracked every step and deliberately excluding the orbital-period term — that one reproduces identically on a rebuild, so comparing against it would call a coarseness no rebuild could ever reduce a reason to rebuild anyway) must not exceed the newly-requested floor by more than `ARC_MAX_SAMPLE_COARSENING` (=8), and `state0` must be the exact same reference as the one fixed at construction — a plain identity check, since `state0` never moves once set. `step()` resolves the window (its own `simulation/arc-bodies.ts`'s `ArcBodies.resolve`, held as the private `bodies` field) once at the tip's own time on the very first call and thereafter **carries the previous step's own midpoint window forward** (`carriedSources: ArcBodyWindow | null`) rather than re-resolving at the tip every time — a half-step-to-one-frame-stale window is used only for the step size and the extrapolation/apsis center, both quantities tolerant of that staleness. `rawCenter` (`strongestAttractor` over the carried window's `gravity` — already excluding the arc's own `excludeId`, since `ArcBodies` drops that candidate outright rather than `strongestAttractor` filtering it per call) sizes the step; `analyticCenter` (`strongestAttractor` over the carried window's `analyticGravity` alone — never a dynamic entity, since one can't be looked up by the ephemeris) is what both the two-body Kepler extrapolation (passed to `DynamicTrajectory.step` only while `keplerTail` holds, `null` otherwise) and the periapsis/apoapsis track (`ApsisTrack`, created once on the first finite step and held for the arc's life — every arc tracks apsides unconditionally, `keplerTail` or not, since one `ApsisTrack.observe` call per step costs nothing worth gating) are centered on. The step size composes three terms — an orbital-period term (`keplerPeriod(…) / ARC_STEPS_PER_REV`), a coarsening floor (`span / ARC_MAX_STEPS`), and an approach term sized off the **radial** closing rate toward any collision-window body (not raw relative speed — a circular orbit's relative speed is its own orbital speed, which would otherwise always trip the term and override the coarsening floor) — floored at `ARC_MIN_STEP_DT` *outside* the `min()` of those three, which is what stops the approach term collapsing toward zero geometrically as a body is neared (a floor placed inside that same `min()` could still be driven arbitrarily low by the approach term). The RK4 step itself is handed a **freshly resolved** midpoint window (`this.bodies.resolve(tip.t + dt/2, tip, dt)`), matching `Simulator.substep`'s own midpoint policy — this is the one lookup never carried, since a fast-moving gravity source (the Moon) held stale across a step would visibly pull a lunar-orbit prediction off its real trajectory. Surface-reach/burn-up detection reads that same midpoint window's **whole `collision` set** (every analytic body, `mu = 0` ones included, plus every living `predictedAsPlanCollider` entity) via `reachedBody`/`burnUpBody` — the arc's own `excludeId`, when set, never reaches that set at all, since `ArcBodies` drops it from every candidate rather than this class filtering it back out, so a mass-bearing `predictedAsPlanCollider` entity (an `Asteroid`) can't register a collision against its own extrapolated position. `trajectorySampleInterval(period, keepDuration)` (exported) is the one shared "how coarsely to decimate a retained window" formula — `max(period-or-fallback / TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV, keepDuration / ARC_MAX_SAMPLES)` — called both by this class's own `step()` and by `game-entity/game-entity.ts`'s `GameEntity.sampleInterval` (the real-state history's own decimation), so the two formulas can never drift apart even though each decimates against its own, different `keepDuration`.

- `contact.ts` — `ContactPhysics`: rigid contact enumeration and resolution for every `collides` `GameEntity` plus the infinite-mass celestial bodies. `resolveSubstep(simTime, entities, attractors, activeStage)` runs once per substep (the warp gate `SimSpeedManager.canResolvePhysicalCollisions` is checked by `Simulator.advance` before calling, not here); `resolveBelt(dt, ...)` is separate because the belt is a ship-local simulation solved at the real frame `dt`, so it is resolved once per frame outside the substep loop. Candidate pairs are narrowed through one `physics/spatial-grid.ts` `SpatialGrid` per call — the same 27-neighbor pattern `simulation/attractors.ts` uses for gravity, applied to contact instead. **Its cell size is measured against the participants' *relative* displacement, not their absolute one**: what decides whether a pair touches is how they move with respect to each other, and in ECI every participant carries the same orbital velocity — kilometres per substep — on top of that. Subtracting the mean displacement Δ̄ over the call's participants leaves each one's reach at `radius + |Δ − Δ̄|`, and twice the largest such reach is the cell edge, since a pair that touches within the interval must end it no further apart than `radius sum + |Δa − Δ̄| + |Δb − Δ̄|`. Taking the raw displacement instead sizes every cell to the shared orbital motion, which no pair's contact depends on, and collapses the whole participant set into a handful of cells. `C.CONTACT_GRID_CELL_SIZE_FLOOR` (1 m) only guards the degenerate case where every radius and every relative displacement is zero — nothing can touch at all then, so any positive edge is correct, and the grid holds no empty cells, so a small one costs nothing. Within one substep the candidate pairs are enumerated **once** (`collectCandidates`, which is also where each pair's `contactsWith` filter runs), and a pair that does not currently touch is kept as a candidate with a `null` response rather than dropped — the parties' states change as earlier contacts resolve, so a pair that misses now may touch later. `earliestContact` then makes one pass over that array per resolution, picking the lowest-TOI unresolved candidate and re-deriving the response only for the candidates involving whichever two parties the previous resolution actually moved; a resolved candidate carries its own `resolved` flag. Any participant whose position/velocity/radius/mass is non-finite (or whose mass is not positive) is dropped before it can corrupt a grid cell index. **It resolves the impulse and hands both parties a `Contact`, and nothing else** — damage, sound, destruction and effects are each `GameEntity` subclass's own `collideWith`, so a bullet's damage and a hull's reaction are written on their own sides rather than branched on here. `Contact` is stated from the receiver's point of view (`self`/`other` swapped and `normal` flipped per side), so one resolution yields both parties' descriptions. **The narrow-phase shape is a capsule per tree edge for whichever party carries one** (`Vessel.collisionCapsules`, `vessel/collision-shape.ts`): `computeEntityResponse` branches base geometry first, then the capsule path, then the plain sphere path. The capsule path gates on the *swept* bounding spheres (`boundingSpheresMayTouch` — the static end-of-interval distance would reject exactly the one-step pass-through the capsules exist to catch), then takes the earliest `physics/capsule-contact.ts` `sweptCapsuleToi` over every capsule pair, falling back to the deepest `capsuleGapAt` overlap at the interval end since the sweep declines an already-touching start. The resulting impulse is the ordinary two-body one along that contact's normal, and **positions are left where the integrator put them** — a capsule's push-out direction depends on where along the axis the touch landed, so moving the centres would add a translation the mass ratio doesn't govern. `BeltSection` (the belt's contact proxy) is defined in `vessel/belt-physics.ts` and `RadiatorFold` in `vessel/radiator.ts`, not here. An `Asteroid` is not special-cased — it takes part on exactly the same footing as an `Vessel` or a pickup, and whether an object also pulls gravitationally (`mu`) plays no role in whether or how it bounces.
- `attractors.ts` — the gravity source array for one simulation step, and the spatial classification that keeps summing it cheap. `mergeAttractors(bodies, dynamic)` is the one array-spread primitive (never mutating the `Ephemeris` ring-cache array it read from) the rest of the module builds on. `gravityBodiesAt(ephemeris, t)` narrows `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` (the full analytic-body window) down to `mu !== 0` — a body's gravity-source status is decided by this one filter, nowhere else. `attractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` merges that with `EntityManager.attractors()` (the living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`s, most notably `Asteroid`); `Simulator.substep` calls it once per substep, at the substep's own midpoint, and hands the one resulting array to every entity in that substep, so two gravity-bearing entities integrated together see each other at the same instant — computing it separately per entity would let their mutual pull disagree by whichever one moved first. `attractorsNearInto` (below) is what filters a caller's own id out of a classified window — `Simulator.substep` before integrating against it, and `Predictor.update`'s own per-frame divergence check before comparing a predicted state to the actual one (its `excludeId` argument on the step window instead lets a gravity-bearing `GameEntity`'s own step exclude itself, matched by `strongestAttractor`'s own trailing `excludeId` argument on a center-only lookup, with no classification in between — see below for why). `gravityBodiesAt(ephemeris, t)` is read before the entity loop so a later dynamic-entity extrapolation's own `ephemeris.stateOf` calls land on the ring cache it just populated. `simulation/future-attractors.ts`'s `FutureAttractors` (below) — the predicted/planned-trajectory sibling that answers for an arbitrary future time via each dynamic entity's `displayState` rather than the current instant — never calls `classifyAttractors` at all: it hands out a plain candidate list plus a per-body-per-time lookup, and `simulation/arc-bodies.ts`'s `ArcBodies` (below) is what narrows that down per arc, tracking each candidate's own reach rather than sorting the whole array into `always`/grid every time; this module knows nothing about predictions or plans.

  `classifyAttractors(attractors)` sorts one such array into `{always, grid}`: the `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` heaviest bodies by `μ` go into `always`, the rest are inserted into a `physics/spatial-grid.ts` `SpatialGrid` keyed on their own position — a value comparison made once per body per step, not once per (body, query point) pair. `attractorsNearInto(pos, classified, out, excludeId?)` is what every entity actually integrates against: it writes `always` plus whatever `SpatialGrid.neighbors(pos)` returns for `pos`'s own cell and its 26 neighbors into a caller-owned scratch array in place, rather than allocating, with `excludeId` dropped from both. Every caller integrating a gravity-bearing entity (`Simulator.substep`, `Predictor.advanceBudget`) passes that entity's own id, so a gravity-bearing `GameEntity` (an `Asteroid`) can never end up pulling on itself. Dropping the whole `Attractor` entry this way also drops its ECI origin-correction term (`attractorAccel`'s second term, independent of the query position) along with the direct one — real but negligible at the masses a `GameEntity` can carry, e.g. a 1e12 kg asteroid at 2 AU corrects the origin by ~7e-25 m/s². A body sorted into the grid contributes only to query points within roughly one cell of it, which is safe because a query point farther than that has an even smaller ECI origin-correction term to lose (see `spatial-grid.ts`'s own comment).

  **Both the `μ` cut and the cell size are derived from the array itself each call** (module-private `alwaysThresholdMu`/`gridCellSize`), so the two tuning constants are a *count* and an *accuracy*, not two lengths: `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` fixes how many bodies every entity sums unconditionally, and the cell size follows as `sqrt(heaviest gridded μ / GRAVITY_NEGLIGIBLE_ACCEL)` — the distance at which that body's pull has fallen to the tolerance. The approximation rests on mass in a star system being Zipf-distributed: almost all of it sits in the star and a handful of bodies, so a cut a few ranks deep leaves a remainder small enough that its reach is short. The threshold is a `μ` **value** rather than a rank so equal-`μ` bodies (a field of identical rocks) can never be split arbitrarily by sort order; the count may therefore exceed the target on ties. Because `μ` is constant per body, the `always` set is stable frame to frame.

  Two constraints on the count. **It must cover the perturbations the game depends on** — under the default registry the Moon is only the 14th heaviest body (the Galilean moons and Titan all outweigh it), so a count below that drops the lunar term for ships outside cislunar space; at 15 there is one rank of margin. And **the `μ` order is not the order of importance**: a body's own contribution splits into a direct pull `μ/d²` and the ECI origin correction `μ/D²` (`D` = its distance from the origin body), the latter being *independent of the query point* — the Moon's is 3.8e-5 m/s², the largest non-central term in the system, which is why it must never be gridded. Any criterion keyed on the **entity's** distance from the origin is forbidden however tempting the pruning: the simulation must behave the same wherever the origin body sits, floating-point error aside. Every gravity-integration call site that actually narrows runs its own `classifyAttractors` → `attractorsNearInto` pair rather than sharing a `ClassifiedAttractors`, since each call site's array (and often its evaluation time) differs — today that's `Simulator.substep` alone; `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc.step` — the one unified stepping site used identically for an entity's own prediction and for an owned plan segment — takes a different path entirely: rather than classifying the whole registry every step, it reads its own `simulation/arc-bodies.ts`'s `ArcBodies` (below), which keeps only the bodies close enough to matter as members and revisits the rest only once each candidate's own estimated time-to-relevance has passed. `Predictor.advanceBudget`'s own tip-time center/`dt` lookup is the one gravity-integration site that skips classification altogether: `strongestAttractor` scans the window directly (its own `excludeId` argument dropping the entity itself), since one query point spent on a single `strongestAttractor` call never earns back what a classification costs — that reasoning is `classifyAttractors`'s own comment, not a rule this module states twice.

- `future-attractors.ts` — `FutureAttractors`: **計画・予測の積分が引きうる天体の候補一覧と、そのうち1体を時刻ごとに解決する窓口**。`Game` が1つだけ持ち、`PlanEditor`/`Predictor` が参照を共有する。`candidates(): readonly FutureBodyCandidate[]` は積分が引きうる天体の候補一覧 — レジストリの全解析天体(`mu = 0` の表示天体も表面到達の相手として含む)と、生存する動的重力源、生存する `predictedAsPlanCollider` entity(現状 `Asteroid` のみ。計画の起点になっている艦は除外集合で外す)を束ねたもの。`bodyAt(id, t): Attractor | null` は候補1体の時刻 t での状態 — 解析天体は `Ephemeris.attractorAt(id, t)` を、動的個体は `displayState(t, ephemeris)` を読む(予測が t にまだ届いていなくても先端からの二体ケプラー外挿で答えるので、外挿もできない個体だけ null になる)。候補一覧を毎回まるごと解決するのではなく、実際に何体を・どの時刻で引くかは読み手(`simulation/arc-bodies.ts`'s `ArcBodies`、below)が弧ごとに `bodyAt` を1体ずつ問い合わせて決める。候補一覧自体は `resolve(...)` の中で `refreshCandidates` が組み直す — `EntityManager.collectionRevision` か除外集合の顔ぶれが前回と同じなら何もせず、`candidateRevision` はそのとき進む世代値。`resolve(...)` はこのフレームの入力(除外集合・`Plan.revision`・`planEnd`)を受け取って `futureSourceRevision` を組み直し、値が動いたときにだけ `revision` を進める — 呼び出し側は入力を渡すだけで、何が答えを変えるかを知らない。`Predictor` は `resolve` を呼ばず、育てる弧(`ArcBodies` 経由)が `candidates()`/`bodyAt` を読むだけ。

  **`revision` is what tells an owned plan-segment arc (`game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc.represents`) that it must be rebuilt rather than reused**, and it is a *semantic* generation value: `futureSourceRevision(entities, excludedEntityIds, planRevision, planEnd)` folds exactly the inputs that can change what `at()` returns — `Plan.revision` (the plan's own edit counter), the excluded-id set (the operated ship changing), and, per entity, that entity's `id` and its prediction tip. `Ephemeris`'s own phase offsets are fixed at construction and never move under a running `Game` (loading a snapshot discards the running `Game` and constructs a fresh one — with its own `Ephemeris` — rather than moving bodies under the current one), so there is no "save load moved every body" term left to fold in. **Only the entities that can answer for a future time appear in `at()`'s output at all** — `displayState(t)` reads `predicted` past the entity's own real state, so a `predictsFuture === false` entity (a bullet, a casing, a fragment) never enters either set and is never folded; the ones that do fold their `id`, which is what makes an entity joining or leaving the set (an asteroid placed or removed, a ship added, an enemy destroyed) change the revision. `displayState(t)` is `null` past an entity's prediction horizon, so a growing prediction makes that body *appear* in both sets at times it was absent from; the raw tip would therefore change every frame and defeat the cache, so each entity contributes only **whether its prediction reaches `planEnd`** — a value that holds still for the whole of the growth and changes exactly once, at the moment coverage is achieved (monotone, so it cannot oscillate; a discarded and regrown one moves covered→short→covered and re-integrates correctly). A prediction that falls short is split further by whether it was truncated, since a truncated one can no longer be extrapolated past its tip and so drops out of both sets entirely. An entity with no prediction at all contributes a fourth value distinct from all three. The deliberate cost is that a body growing into the plan's span is not reflected in the drawn arc until its prediction covers the span outright — the final shape is right, the intermediate frames are slightly stale. A quantized tip instead makes the growth itself visible to the cache, and at a 28-day display duration coverage takes many frames to reach, so every one of those frames would otherwise rebuild the whole plan's arcs and restart their budget-driven growth from nothing. The fold is a 32-bit `Math.imul` mix with `| 0` on every input, so the result is always a finite integer — a `NaN` revision would compare unequal to itself and re-integrate forever, silently. `planEnd` is **`PlanPath.plannedEnd` — the segment list's own terminus, un-clipped by the display window** (`PlanEditor`'s own private `lastPlanEnd`), read from the *previous* frame since `PlanEditor.update` resolves the provider before the same call's `planDisplay.update` decides this frame's segments; a `planEnd` that is not finite (nothing has been segmented yet) yields a value that differs on every call, so the first frame always integrates. **It must not be the display-clipped `timeRange()` end**: that one is `min(the segments' own end, currentTime + duration)` and so tracks `simTime`, which at a warp level whose per-frame `simTime` advance exceeds the display duration lets `simTime` overtake it — and once the whole polyline falls behind `currentTime`, `timeRange()` answers null and `planEnd` pins at `NaN` with no path back. Either way the revision then changes on every single frame, and every segment's arc is rebuilt and its growth restarted from scratch forever, for a polyline with not one point inside the display window. `plannedEnd` is a pure function of the anchor, the nodes, the ephemeris and the duration preset, so with a node placed it holds still and the held integration survives; a plan whose end has fallen into the past is simply one every prediction covers, which is a stable answer rather than an unresolvable one.
- `arc-bodies.ts` — `ArcBodies`: **1本の積分弧が引く天体の一覧と、その維持**。seam 型 `FutureAttractorProvider`/`FutureBodyCandidate`(`future-attractors.ts`'s `FutureAttractors` が実装する、above)と、`resolve` の返り値型 `ArcBodyWindow` をここに持つ。候補(レジストリの全天体 + 生存する動的個体)を毎歩ぜんぶ解決する代わりに、**いま効きうる天体だけを成員として保持する** — 成員は解決するついでに抜ける条件も見て、成員でない候補は「最短でもこの時刻までは効き得ない」期限(`nextVisitT`)を持ち、その時刻が来たときだけ解決して入る条件を見る。`resolve(t, from, stepDt): ArcBodyWindow` が唯一の入口(`from` が判定の基準にする弧の先端状態、`stepDt` はこの解決のあとに踏む刻み幅)。成員判定は、重力(`min(問い合わせ位置からの距離, ECI原点からの距離)` が `sqrt(2mu/GRAVITY_NEGLIGIBLE_ACCEL)` 以下 — 直達項と原点補正項の和が `2mu/min(d,D)²` を超えないことから来る)か表面到達(距離が半径以下)のいずれかに、刻み幅の `ARC_BODY_LEAD_STEPS` 歩ぶんの先読みを足した範囲。期限は同じ余裕を、保守的に見積もった接近速度(`(相対速さ + 天体自身の速さ) × ARC_BODY_CLOSING_SAFETY + ARC_BODY_CLOSING_MARGIN`)で割って出す — 外れても訪問が1回増えるだけで、短く見積もりすぎることだけが取りこぼしになるので精密である必要はない。最も重い解析天体(既定レジストリでは太陽)は寄与に依らず成員のまま(`pinned`)— 中心天体の解決(`strongestAttractor`)が空の一覧を引かないようにするため。コンストラクタの `excludeId` は候補から丸ごと外す — 弧の主が重力源(小惑星)のとき、自分自身を引力の相手にも表面到達の相手にもしないため。`lastResolved`/`lastRevisited` は直近の `resolve` で解決した天体数とそのうち期限到来で訪問した数 — `predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc` が `lastResolvedBodies`/`lastRevisitedBodies` としてそのまま転送し、`predictor.ts`'s `Predictor.grow` がフレーム集計へ積んで `perf-meter.ts`'s `PerfCounts.arcBodies`/`.arcRevisits` になる(below)。候補の顔ぶれは `sources.candidateRevision` が変わったときだけ組み直す(`syncCandidates`)— 生き残った候補の成員状態・期限は引き継ぎ、新しい候補はすぐ訪問する。

`src/game/camera/` — every camera holds a `viewpoint: Viewpoint` field (`physics/projection.ts` — `position`/`lookTarget`/`up`/`fovDeg`/`aspect`) and an `update` that computes the viewpoint in absolute ECI (THREE-free) and writes it into `viewpoint` — no camera returns its `Viewpoint` as a value, since a function that both mutates state and hands back a derived product is the anti-pattern CLAUDE.md's TypeScript rules ban. Pushing `viewpoint` into a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` through the floating origin (`sync`) and building the `ProjectFn` the rest of the game reads screen positions through (independent of both the THREE matrices and `fo`) are **`CameraSystem`'s job alone** — no sub-camera has its own `sync`/`projection`. Each sub-camera also exposes a `near: number` getter (a plain derived-value read, not a THREE write, so it doesn't cross that boundary) — `CameraSystem.sync` reads the active one and is the only place it reaches a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera.near`.
- `camera-system.ts` — `CameraSystem`: owns `CombatCameraSystem`/`MapCamera` and exposes the active one (`activeCamera`/`activeCameraPos`/`activeCameraProjection`); owns `overviewMode` (`zoomActive` is a forwarding getter — `!overviewMode && combatCamera.zoomActive` — since only `CombatCameraSystem` holds and updates that state). Module-private `syncCameraToViewpoint(camera, view, near, fo)` and `projectionFromViewpoint(view)` are the sole place any sub-camera's `viewpoint` becomes a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` update or a `ProjectFn` — `sync(fo)` calls the former on whichever of `combatCamera`/`mapCamera` is active, and `activeCameraProjection` calls the latter. `ScaleFn` (`(worldPos: Vec3) => number`, metres per screen pixel at that point) is the exact sibling of `ProjectFn` — module-private `scaleFromViewpoint(view)` binds `physics/projection.ts`'s `metersPerPixel` the same way, and `activeCameraScale` picks the active camera's viewpoint the same way, so a consumer that needs to size something in screen units receives a bound function as an argument rather than reaching for the camera. `update(player, simTime, input, dt, mapPickables)` also reads the roll keys (`K.cameraRollLeft`/`cameraRollRight`, mapped to the numeric-keypad `Numpad0`/`Numpad1` — distinct from the ship radiator toggles on the top-row `Digit9`/`Digit0`) and forwards `keyRoll` alongside `keyYaw`/`keyPitch` to whichever sub-camera is active. `mapPickables` (built fresh each frame by `MapPickables.refresh()`) is threaded through to `MapCamera.update` so its focus resolves against the same candidate set `MapContextActions.handleMapRightClick` picks against — `CameraSystem` itself holds none of that set. `setChaseCameraOverride(target: ChaseCameraTarget | null)` is held as inert data — a `_chaseCameraOverride` field this class threads straight into `combatCamera.update`'s trailing argument and otherwise knows no reason for; `docking.ts` is its only caller, setting it while an assembly session's camera should frame the edited target instead of the operated ship and clearing it (`null`) when the session ends, so `Game.update` keeps passing `followedVessel` every frame with no branch of its own for assembly. Also owns the map focus concern (= "where the overview camera looks"): the `focusMarkers` label set — resolving a right-click against the shared candidate set is `MapContextActions.handleMapRightClick`'s job via `map-pickable.ts`'s `pickNearest` directly, so this class no longer wraps that lookup itself — and the **表示 panel** (`hud/view-options-panel.ts`'s `ViewOptionsPanel`, exposed as the public readonly `viewOptionsPanel` — two toggle sections: 天体クラス別 (the per-class `BodyClassToggles` icon/label/orbit-line buttons, `BODY_CLASS_ROWS` then `ENTITY_ROWS`) and 天球グリッド (`Navball`'s `CelestialGridVisibility` icon/label/... rows, wired by `Navball`'s own constructor rather than by this class — see `navball.ts` below), so there is one "what does the map show" panel rather than two; foldable via a `hud/hud-root.ts` `buildCollapseToggle` on its own heading (`#hud-view-options-toggle`, the same collapse mechanism the object list/PREDICT bar use — see `hud/hud-root.ts` below); no camera-frame or focus control of its own — origin/rotation for both the camera and the planned trajectory are `hud/frame-controls.ts`'s job, and focus is chosen via `MapContextActions`'s always-shown object list, a double-click, or a property window's "move focus" item). Every control on the 天体クラス別 section writes `mapCamera`/`bodyClassToggles` state and nothing else, so the whole HUD wiring for that section stays inside this class; `sync(fo)` pushes the panel's visibility and highlights each frame. **That panel and the focus labels are the only DOM this class touches** — every other HUD panel's visibility belongs to that panel's own owner (`Hud`'s six panel classes — `vesselPanel` for `#hud-status`, `orbitPanel` for `#hud-orbit`, `enemiesPanel` for `#hud-enemies`, `targetPanel` for `#hud-target`, see `hud/hud.ts` below — `Stage`'s `StatusPanel` for `#hud-stagestatus`, CSS for what is purely view-driven), so no element ever has a second writer overwriting the first later in the same frame. `bodyClassToggles`' initial value is likewise read from `localStorage` (`tepui.bodyClassToggles`, `loadBodyClassToggles`) and written back on every toggle (`saveBodyClassToggles`), so it too survives a page reload alongside `Navball.gridVisibility`. The class-toggle section is one row per category (`BODY_CLASS_ROWS`: 惑星/衛星/準惑星/小天体/ラグランジュ点, then `ENTITY_ROWS`: 自艦/敵/弾薬/基地), each holding a `Button` per category's icon(●)/label(Aa)/orbit-line(⌒) `BodyClassToggles` field (the widget itself holds no on/off state — the row mirrors `BodyClassToggles` locally and calls `setOn` back, per `hud/widgets/`'s state-lives-with-the-caller rule) — orbit-line is omitted for 衛星 (no such toggle exists, see `body-visibility.ts` below) and for ラグランジュ点 (no orbit concept at all), so those two rows show only two buttons. `serialize()` mirrors `CombatCameraSystem`/`MapCamera`'s own `serialize()`, so a snapshot carries both sub-cameras' viewpoints regardless of which is active; the constructor's optional trailing `saved?: Pick<CameraSaveData, 'chase' | 'overview'>` argument forwards `saved?.chase`/`saved?.overview` straight into `new CombatCameraSystem(...)`/`new MapCamera(...)` — `game.ts` passes `initialSave?.camera` here, after `ViewManager`'s own initial view has already been resolved from that same `initialSave?.camera?.view`. `dispose()` takes the 表示 panel down and, less obviously, detaches the `pointerdown` handler this class binds to `#hud-chase-reset` — that button belongs to `buildHudDom` and outlives any one `CameraSystem`, so a handler left attached would still be there, and fire, alongside the next one's.
- `combat-camera-system.ts` — `CombatCameraSystem`: the combat view, holding its own `camera: THREE.PerspectiveCamera` and `viewpoint: Viewpoint` (the same `Viewpoint` shape `MapCamera` holds, so there's no second "camera view state" shape in the codebase). Owns the `[Z]` gunsight switch (`zoomActive`, read from `Input` each `update` — the key is only ever consulted while combat view is active, since `CameraSystem` doesn't call this class's `update` in map mode) and is the sole place FOV animates: each frame it picks `chase-camera.ts`'s `ChaseCamera` or `gunsight-camera.ts`'s `GunsightCamera` by `zoomActive`, calls that sub-camera's `update` (which writes its own `viewpoint`), then module-private `lerpViewpointFov` folds the picked `viewpoint` into this class's own — position/lookTarget/up/aspect snap to the target immediately, only `fovDeg` eases exponentially — so a sub-camera never animates its own FOV and a future third view only needs to expose the same `viewpoint: Viewpoint` field. `toggleFollowAttitude`/`camFollowAttitude` are thin forwards to `ChaseCamera` (the `[G]` key's receiver, same pattern as `reset()`) — the reference-frame decision itself lives there. `update`'s trailing optional `chaseTarget: ChaseCameraTarget | null` (default `null`) is `CameraSystem`'s own `_chaseCameraOverride` threaded through — supplied, `ChaseCamera` follows that pose instead of the operated ship's, and the gunsight is skipped outright (`useGunsight` requires `chaseTarget === null`), since a zoomed-in aim has no meaning while the camera is framing an assembly target rather than a ship in flight.
- `chase-camera.ts` — `ChaseCamera`: the third-person orbit view, owning only a unit quaternion `rot` and `dist` — no Euler yaw/pitch/up-vector state, so a long drag session can't gimbal-lock or drift into an inconsistent up vector. It holds **no target reference of its own, and no reference to `GameEntity` at all**: `update(mouse, keyYaw, keyPitch, dt, target: ChaseCameraTarget | null)` and `toggleFollowAttitude(target: ChaseCameraTarget | null)` both take a plain `{position, attitude}` pose as a per-call argument — the narrow structural shape `ChaseCameraTarget` states, the same "call taking a narrow interface rather than a stored reference" pattern `PlanExecutorShip`/`CapabilityVessel` already follow — rather than a stored reference or an entity, so switching the flown ship touches neither `rot` nor `dist` — the camera's current offset carries over instead of snapping to a default view, and only which pose the next call reads changes. Widening past `GameEntity` is what lets a caller point this camera at something with no entity of its own at all — a new-ship draft, or a docked vessel whose state stops advancing while parked — by handing it a pose it resolved some other way. `update` reads only `target.position`/`target.attitude` — never `alive` — and, with `target === null` (no pose supplied), returns immediately (`if (!target) return;`), leaving `viewpoint` exactly where it last was — the camera freezes at that moment rather than resetting or tracking anything else; `toggleFollowAttitude` likewise does nothing with `target === null`, since there is nothing to reinterpret `rot` against. `CHASE_DIST_MIN`/`CHASE_DIST_MAX` (12 m / 8000 m) are exported: `update`'s own wheel-zoom clamps `dist` into that range every frame, and any caller that assigns `dist` directly (`docking.ts`'s assembly camera framing) clamps into the same range rather than a second one. The togglable reference frame (`camFollowAttitude`, a read-only getter) decides what `rot` *means*: `true` — the default — makes it an offset relative to the target's own attitude (`target.att.q`); `false` makes it the camera's own absolute orientation in ECI, fixed in inertial space until dragged. `toggleFollowAttitude(target)` is the sole place that flips it, reinterpreting `rot` through `target.att.q` (multiply/divide) so the rendered view never jumps at the moment of the toggle. `update(...)` folds `target.att.q` into a working quaternion whenever `camFollowAttitude` holds, then applies key/drag rotations to that working quaternion before writing it back. Mouse drag rotates around `axis = normalize(cross(dragVector, viewVector))` (`viewVector` = the camera's current look direction, `dragVector` = the drag's screen delta expressed in the camera's own current right/up axes): the axis is always ⟂ the view direction by construction, so drag can never introduce roll around the view axis, and using the *current* basis rather than a fixed reference keeps the control gimbal-lock-free at any pitch. Arrow-key yaw/pitch rotate around the same current up/right axes; `keyRoll` (the numpad-0/1 keys) rotates around `view` itself (the camera's own look direction), the one axis arrow-key yaw/pitch never touch. `serialize()` writes `rot`/`dist`/`panEci`/`camFollowAttitude` into a `ChaseCameraSaveData`; the constructor's optional trailing `saved?: ChaseCameraSaveData` argument assigns `rot`/`_camFollowAttitude` directly rather than through `toggleFollowAttitude`, since that method's read-normalize is for a *live* frame switch and would reinterpret a value that is already correct for the saved frame.
- `gunsight-camera.ts` — `GunsightCamera`: the `[Z]` zoom view — stateless apart from `viewpoint`, just the ship's nose direction and position turned into a `Viewpoint` each `update`.
- `map-camera.ts` — `MapCamera`: the map Earth-centered orbit camera (near=`dist / OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_RATIO` clamped so it never grows past the star/celestial-grid shell at extreme zoom-out, so the near plane tightens as the camera zooms in rather than clipping a close focus at a fixed distance. The clamp is not a flat fraction of `CELESTIAL_SHELL_RADIUS`: a shell point at view-axis angle θ from screen center clips at view-space depth `R·cosθ`, so the cap has to be `R·cos(θ_diag)·OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_SHELL_MARGIN`, θ_diag being the screen's diagonal half-FOV derived from `fov`/`window.innerWidth/innerHeight` each call — a flat `R`-fraction cap only protects the narrow region near screen center and leaves the wide-aspect edges/corners clipped/far=`dist × OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_RATIO` clamped to `[OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MIN, OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MAX]`, so the far plane also tracks zoom instead of sitting at a fixed distance), exposing `camera`/`viewpoint` on the same footing as `CombatCameraSystem`. Each sub-camera (this one and `CombatCameraSystem`) exposes its own `far` getter — the combat camera's is the fixed `COMBAT_CAMERA_FAR` — and `CameraSystem.sync` writes the active one's `far` into the `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` every frame, the same way it already does for `near`. Its source of truth is three **`cameraFrame`-relative** vectors (`offset_r` = focus→camera, `pan_r` = focus→look-target, `up_r` = the camera's up direction), so switching to a rotating frame makes the viewpoint co-rotate for free; the ECI ⇄ frame round trip is delegated to `physics/frame.ts` and the class never brands values itself. `cameraFrame` is a read-only getter for **the frame the viewpoint is fixed to** — the frame the planned trajectory (and every other future display) is *drawn* in is `DisplayWindowManager.frame`, a separate value selected independently — and `setCameraRotation(rotatingWith: OrbitingId | null)` is the sole way to change it: it always resolves to `ephemeris.frameOf(ephemeris.originId, rotatingWith)`, keeping the frame's *center* pinned to `ephemeris.originId` regardless of `rotatingWith` (the three basis vectors are directions, not points, so only the rotation ever affects the viewpoint) and re-projects the current view into the new frame so the switch never jumps the visible camera. `update` rotates the viewpoint around the camera's *current* up/right axes (the same construction `ChaseCamera` uses) rather than around world Y, so a rolled map still yaws and pitches the way the drag looks on screen, and it re-orthogonalizes `up_r` against the just-changed view direction before applying `keyRoll` around it. Focus is a `camera/focus-target.ts` `FocusTarget` — either `{kind:'object', id}`, re-resolved every frame against the `candidates: readonly MapPickable[]` argument `update` receives (built by `MapPickables.refresh()` — Earth/Moon/Sun/Lagrange-point labels plus every living ship plus the navigation-target AN/DN icons), falling back to Earth once the id is missing for two consecutive frames; or `{kind:'point', frame, point}`, a `physics/frame.ts` `FramePoint` baked into a chosen `ReferenceFrame` and resolved back to ECI every frame via `frameTransformAt` — a rotating `frame` makes the fixed point ride that frame's own rotation instead of sitting still in ECI. `setFocusTarget(target, resetPan=true)` is the sole entry point for changing focus (replacing separate id/position setters), and `resolvedFocus` exposes the ECI position `resolveFocus` last settled on — `frame-controls.ts`'s "固定を解除" action reads it to bake a fresh `'point'` target in the star-centered inertial frame (or `ephemeris.inertialFrame` when the registry has no star) at the instant of release, so the camera keeps looking exactly where it was rather than jumping to Earth. `clearFocusIf(id)` only matches an `'object'` target, since a `'point'` target carries no id an object's disappearance could invalidate. The class holds no reference to `FocusMarkers` or any other pickable source itself. The future-ghost slider belongs to `DisplayWindowManager`. `serialize()` writes `offset_r`/`pan_r`/`up_r`, `cameraFrame.rotatingWith`, and `focus` into an `MapCameraSaveData` — a `'point'` focus is saved as its baking `ReferenceFrame`'s `center`/`rotatingWith` plus the frame-relative point; the constructor's optional trailing `saved?: MapCameraSaveData` argument resolves that frame (and `cameraFrame` itself) through `ephemeris.frameOf` rather than a literal, per `frame.ts`'s reference-identity rule, and with no `saved` builds the default look-down viewpoint at `ephemeris.inertialFrame` instead.
- `focus-markers.ts` — `FocusMarkers`: the focusable-target labels, each exposed as a `MapPickable` (`kind: 'body'`). The full label set is built once in the constructor from the active registry, **in hierarchical order** (a body, then its Lagrange labels, then its children — each carrying a `depth` so a consumer can indent without re-deriving the tree; `ObjectListPanel` uses exactly this) — one label per body, plus Lagrange-point labels for the bodies whose points are **dynamically meaningful**: `Ephemeris.hasUsableCollinearPoints` (L1/L2/L3, gated on the L1 clearance from the secondary's own surface exceeding `LAGRANGE_MIN_CLEARANCE_RATIO`) and `Ephemeris.hasStableTriangularPoints` (L4/L5, Routh's linear-stability mass ratio) are asked per body, so a system can legitimately get L4/L5 but not L1/L2/L3 — except for `body-class.ts`'s `'smallBody'`/`'dwarf'` classes, which cap out at L1/L2 regardless (too numerous for L3/L4/L5 to stay legible, and neither dynamically as interesting for these low-mass bodies). A Lagrange label carries two names, since the two readers want different forms: `name` (`地球-L1`, the secondary body's display name then the point) is what the object list and the property window read, so a point sorts and reads under its own body; `markerLabel` (`L1` over `地球`, drawn as two lines — `mk-lagrange` in `skeleton-style.ts` sizes/stacks them) is what the map marker draws, where the point itself is the thing being pointed at and the body is context. Both come from the module-private `lagrangeName`/`lagrangeMarkerLabel`, so registering a new body needs no change here. Every ordinary body's two names are the same string. Split across the two phases like `NavTarget` is: `update(t, focusId, toggles, cameraPos)` (`focusId: AttractorId | undefined` — `undefined` when the camera is fixed on a `'point'` `FocusTarget` rather than a body, in which case the policy falls back to its focus-independent rules) builds a `celestial/map-visibility.ts` `MapVisibilityPolicy` for this frame (from the registry, the toggles, the focus, and `systemMembersAt(cameraPos)` — the system the camera is physically in, so leaving a focus doesn't blank out the satellites right in front of it) and re-derives positions **only for the bodies whose `body(id).pickable` holds** — an invisible body costs no `positionOf`/`lagrangeAt` at all, which is what keeps the per-frame cost bounded as the registry grows — stamping each shown `FocusLabel`'s `showIcon`/`showLabel` from that same policy. `syncLabels(project, cameraPos)` places the markers off the subset `update()` just computed, passing `mk-poi`'s `sym`/`label` arguments as `''` for whichever of `showIcon`/`showLabel` is off (so the dot and the name can appear independently), hiding a label the previous frame showed but this one dropped, hiding one `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body, and resolving screen-space label crowding by `labelPriority` (planets/stars > dwarfs > satellites/small bodies > Lagrange points) over a uniform grid of `FOCUS_LABEL_PRIORITY_PX` cells, so label count doesn't cost O(N²). Crowding suppresses the **name only** — the icon stays, or the icon/label toggle split and the "this focus target exists" signal would both break. Each pass writes back the label's own `pickable`: an occluded label is unpickable outright, a name lost to crowding still is (its icon is on screen). `CameraSystem` calls the two back to back so the drawn markers are the same frame's coordinates. `allLabels` is exposed publicly too (the same array, id/isLagrange/name/depth) — `MapContextActions.sync()`'s parent-lookup construction reads it directly rather than the shown subset, since a row hidden on the map by a toggle must still nest correctly in the 軌道オブジェクト一覧's tree. `bodyPickables(t, visibility)` is the candidate-list half: it returns the bodies and dynamically-meaningful Lagrange points the *same* `MapVisibilityPolicy` admits (so a body the toggles have hidden is genuinely unselectable, not merely undrawn), each carrying the previous `syncLabels`' `pickable` flag — a target that was drawn nowhere this frame (occluded, or its name lost to crowding) is still returned, marked `pickable: false`, since it hasn't been turned off, it just isn't on screen to grab. An overview-camera-side concern, owned by `CameraSystem`.
- `focus-target.ts` — `FocusTarget`: `MapCamera`'s focus as a judged union, `{kind:'object', id}` (a body/ship/other `MapPickable`, re-resolved by id every frame) or `{kind:'point', frame, point}` (an ECI position baked once into a `physics/frame.ts` `FramePoint` within a chosen `ReferenceFrame`, re-resolved to ECI every frame via `frameTransformAt` so a rotating `frame` carries the point along). `focusTargetId(target)` is the sole way to ask "does this focus correspond to a body" — `undefined` for `'point'` — used everywhere a focus id feeds a body-keyed lookup (`MapVisibilityPolicy`, `EnvironmentScene`'s reference-line focus, `ObjectListPanel`'s row highlight). `focusPoint(ephemeris, frame, pos, t)` builds a `'point'` target from a live ECI position at time `t`; `frame-controls.ts`'s "固定を解除" action is its only caller.

Screen projection itself is **not** in `camera/`: `physics/projection.ts` holds the pure math (`Viewpoint`/`projectToNdc`/`ndcToScreen`/`Projected`) and each camera exposes a bound `ProjectFn` (`(worldPos: Vec3) => Projected`) that consumers receive as an argument rather than importing.

- `src/game/map-pickable.ts` — `MapPickable`/`MapPickKind` (`'body' | 'ship' | 'player' | 'apsis' | 'relnode' | 'ammo' | 'empty-space' | 'eqnode' | 'base'`) and `pickNearest<T extends { readonly pos: Vec3 }>`: the common shape for anything on the map (or in combat) that a click can hit, and the shared "project every candidate, take the screen-nearest one within a pixel-radius" search. The type parameter only requires a world position, not the full `MapPickable` shape, so a caller with its own payload (e.g. `Targeter.pickTargetAt`'s `{ pos, target }`) can pass it straight through without faking the rest of `MapPickable`. `'body'` is `FocusMarkers`' celestial labels, `'ship'` is every living `Vessel`, `'player'` is every living self-flown ship in `entities.ownShips()` — split from `'ship'` because a self-flown ship's menu offers activate/follow-plan/delete on top of what an enemy's does — `'relnode'` is `NavTarget`'s relative AN/DN icons, and `'apsis'` is `PlanDisplay`'s periapsis/apoapsis icons — each kind drives a different `MapContextActions.itemsFor` item set. Used by `map-pickables.ts`'s `MapPickables` (which builds the frame's candidate set) and `map-context-actions.ts`'s `MapContextActions` (the map's whole right-click path, and — via `handleCombatRightClick` — the combat-view one too) and `Targeter.pickTargetAt` (combat targets only, `[T]`-key selection), so target-selection and map-focus/property-window picking share one implementation instead of several near-identical screen-distance loops.
  The entity's own future trajectory is held behind a `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts` `PredictedArc` (`_predictedArc`, private) rather than a bare `DynamicTrajectory` — `predicted`/`predictedApsides`/`predictedImpact`/`predictionTruncated` are thin forwarding getters onto it (`null`/`false` while none exists), so every existing reader (`sync`, `TrajectoryLine`, `DisplayWindowManager`, `simulation/future-attractors.ts`) is unaffected by the split. `predictionBcInv` (defaulting to `bcInv`, overridden by `Vessel` to `aerodynamics.ts`'s orientation-averaged value) is what the prediction integrates with rather than `bcInv` itself: a prediction holds no future attitude, so pinning the current one would make it assume the player keeps that attitude — an assumption they never chose. The mean assumes nothing about attitude, and leaves the error one-directional: hold an attitude and the ship falls more slowly than predicted, tumble and it falls faster (§X-7). `ensurePredictedArc(sources: FutureAttractorProvider)` lazily builds the arc from `actual.state` — only for a `predictsFuture` entity, `null` otherwise at no cost — passing the entity's own `bcInv`/`srpCoeff` and, for a gravity-bearing entity (`mu !== 0`), its own `id` as the arc's `excludeId`, so a mass-bearing entity (an `Asteroid`) never attracts or collides with its own extrapolated stand-in; `game/simulation/predictor.ts`'s `Predictor` is the sole caller, once per budget slice. `invalidatePrediction()` is the one-line `_predictedArc = null` — sizing the step, resolving the window, tracking apsides, and detecting surface reach/burn-up are all the arc's own job (see `predicted-arc.ts` below), not this class's; `predictedApsides`/`predictedImpact` simply read whatever the arc has accumulated. `predicted.state.t` still ends up at or past `simTime + horizon` rather than short of it (the arc's own `needsGrowth` guard), which is what lets `at(t)` answer exactly `simTime + horizon` directly or by interpolation instead of needing a clamped final step. `discardPredictionIfDiverged(simTime, attractors)` is the cheap per-frame correctness check, returning whether it discarded: if `predicted.at(simTime)` is absent or has drifted from `actual.state.r` by more than the tolerance, it calls `invalidatePrediction()` (drops `predicted`, `predictedApsides` and `predictedImpact` together — a zero-cost operation; the list simply regrows through the normal budget next frame, which is why discards never spike frame time). The tolerance is **not** a flat `PREDICT_RESET_DIST`: `divergenceTolerance` widens it to whatever `at()`'s own cubic-Hermite interpolation error is at the list's current sample spacing (`PREDICT_SAMPLE_ERROR` scaled by the fourth power of how much that spacing has coarsened past `TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV`). Without that, a long display duration decimates the list far enough that a *correct* prediction reads as drifted — at the 28-day preset the interpolation alone is tens of km against a 500 m threshold — and every entity would discard and restart its list every single frame, so the prediction could never finish growing. **The spacing it scales by is the list's own recorded `DynamicTrajectory.sampleInterval`, not one re-derived from the current display duration**: the samples already in the list were decimated at whatever interval was in force when they were pushed, and nothing invalidates a list when the duration changes — deriving the threshold from the current setting instead would, on a switch from a long preset to a short one, shrink the threshold to metres against a list that is legitimately tens of km coarse and discard a correct list every frame forever. The `coarsening ** 4` term is capped against the local orbit scale (the semi-major axis implied by `localOrbitPeriod` at that position) rather than the raw distance to the strongest attractor, since out in interplanetary space that attractor is the star and a distance-based cap is effectively no cap at all. `hasFutureReader(canDisplayFuture)` is the single fold of **the reasons anything reads this entity's future**, and it is what `Predictor` narrows its growth target set by; `predictsFuture` — "could this entity ever hold a prediction", which is what `ensurePredictedArc`, the divergence pass and `future-attractors.ts`'s revision fold each want — is that same fold at its most permissive, `hasFutureReader(true)`. Neither is a field a class sets: **the reasons are separately declared per kind**, so which one applies to which kind is visible instead of collapsed into one opaque boolean: `predictedAsGravitySource` (a getter = `mu !== 0`, since an entity that pulls is summed by the prediction's and the plan's own integration at future times), `predictedAsPlanCollider` (a public `readonly` per kind — deliberately a different question from `collides`, which governs live rigid contact: this one asks whether a trajectory drawn minutes-to-days ahead should terminate on the object, which only a body of celestial scale is worth doing for, so `Asteroid` alone declares it; `simulation/future-attractors.ts`'s two `alive && predictedAsPlanCollider` filters read it directly, since deciding which dynamic entities a predicted or planned trajectory can end on is that module's own call, not this class's), and `predictedForGhost` (a `protected readonly` per kind, true for whatever is drawn as a mesh or marker at the future-ghost `displayTime` — `Vessel`/`Vessel`/`AmmoPickup` — and the sole reason `hasFutureReader` gates on its `canDisplayFuture` argument, since a display time pinned to the present makes the ghost read `actual` and never the prediction; it says nothing about the trajectory line, whose own contribution is simply `predictedLine !== null`). `Bullet`/`DebrisPiece` declare none of the three and so keep the base `false`. How far ahead is `horizon`, an argument threaded down from `DisplayWindowManager.durationSec(referencePeriod)` every frame, not a per-class constant, since the prediction horizon now tracks whatever span the future-ghost slider can reach. The payoff is `displayState(t, ephemeris?)`: without `ephemeris` it's the same two-way branch as ever (`t <= actual.state.t ? actual.at(t) : (predicted?.at(t) ?? null)`), which `sync` (below) and every marker-position lookup use, so "what does this entity look like at the currently-displayed time" has exactly one answer for meshes and markers alike. With `ephemeris` supplied, a `null` answer past the prediction's own tip falls through to `predicted.extrapolatedAt(t, ephemeris.stateOf(extrapolationCenter.id, t))` whenever `predicted` exists, isn't `truncated`, and carries a non-`null` `extrapolationCenter` — the same two-body Kepler extrapolation `TrajectoryLine` draws with (`kepler-extrapolation.ts`, below), turned into a single-state answer rather than a sampled tail. `simulation/future-attractors.ts`'s `FutureAttractors.bodyAt` (below) is the only caller that passes it, since a gravity source or collider needs to keep answering past where the prediction itself has grown to.
- `src/physics/trajectory-features.ts` — pure functions that find orbital features from an already-integrated trajectory, rather than from the osculating `OrbitalElements` at some evaluation epoch. It exists because osculating elements oscillate with the epoch under J2 — tens of km of periapsis/apoapsis wobble per revolution at LEO — so an analytically-placed icon jumps every time the evaluation epoch moves, e.g. when a zero-Δv maneuver node is added. `apsisCrossing(center, prev, next)` finds a periapsis/apoapsis directly on the physical condition that defines one — the radial velocity relative to `center` (`dot(r - center.r, v - center.v)`) changing sign — checked on a single pair of *consecutive* integration steps rather than scanned out of a decimated sample array; a negative-to-positive crossing is a periapsis, positive-to-negative is an apoapsis, refined with a fixed 20-iteration call into `physics/optimize.ts`'s `goldenSectionMin` over `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate` between the two steps — the same interpolation `DynamicTrajectory.at`/`game/trajectory-line.ts` already use, not a second one; the search itself is that module's, since `game/plan/plan-path.ts`'s `nearestSample` runs the identical bracket-narrowing over a different objective (screen distance rather than distance from a center). Working on the raw step pair rather than a coarse sample array is what makes a genuine collision course (radial velocity stays negative all the way to impact, never crossing zero) correctly report no periapsis at all, instead of a coarse sample array spuriously reading an earlier, non-final close approach as the periapsis. `ApsisTrack` accumulates `apsisCrossing`'s results across many `observe(prev, next)` calls, fixed on one center for its life and exposed back through its own `center` getter (so a caller reading a stored track can state which body its extrema were measured against without holding a second reference of its own) — `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc` holds exactly one, created on the first finite `step()` and centered on whichever body pulls hardest at that moment, and every `step()` after feeds it that step's pair; the same arc class backs both `game-entity/game-entity.ts`'s `GameEntity` (`predictedApsides`, a track spanning the whole predicted trajectory) and each owned segment of `plan/plan-path.ts`'s `PlanPath`, so there is one implementation rather than two. `dropBefore(t)` is what narrows a track down to a range's own front: `PlanPath.update` calls it on the *borrowed* segment (the node-less trailing segment reading the operated ship's own arc) every frame, at that segment's `state0.t`, since that arc's track spans the whole ship prediction and the segment only wants what's still ahead of its own start; an owned segment's arc never needs it, since its track was never fed anything earlier than its own fixed `state0.t` to begin with — so `periapsis`/`apoapsis` always answer the earliest extremum still ahead of the range's current front. `findEquatorCrossings(samples, center, pole)` finds the ascending/descending sign change of distance-from-center projected onto `pole` by scanning an already-integrated sample series (a bracketing triple by sign pattern), refined by a fixed 20-iteration bisection — a different feature with a different consumer (`game/marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts`), which has only a decimated sample series to work from rather than a live step pair. Both iteration counts are constants rather than convergence-driven, since a variable iteration count would let the result wobble frame to frame by itself. Pure functions, no game state.
- `src/physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts` — `DynamicTrajectory`: THREE/game-independent (`physics/`, alongside `StateQueue`/`Ephemeris` as "pure but stateful"), holding **one** decimated `StateQueue` (`_samples`) whose newest element *is* the tip — `state` is a thin getter (`_samples.newest!`) rather than a separate field, so the tip and the retained sample series can never disagree about what "now" is. `step(dt, bodies, bcInv, thrust, sampleInterval, keepDuration)` advances via `dynamics.ts`'s `stepDynamics` against the caller-supplied gravity-source array (`bodies` — the caller's own `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` result; which timestamp to sample it at, e.g. the step's midpoint, is the caller's policy, not this class's) — then, **before** pushing the new tip, decides whether the about-to-be-superseded tip is worth keeping as a retained sample: if `keepDuration > 0` and it sits at least `sampleInterval` seconds past the previously-retained sample (`_samples.newestGap >= sampleInterval`, or fewer than 2 samples are held yet), it stays and `_samples.cleanup(keepDuration, 2)` prunes the window's old end around it; otherwise `_samples.discardNewest()` drops it outright so the incoming tip simply replaces it (this is where the display-resolution decimation happens; `keepDuration = 0` always takes the discard branch, so casings/debris/ammo cost nothing). `prevState` (needed every step by `simulation/contact.ts`'s swept-sphere TOI test and `targeter.ts`'s board crossings, even for entities with `keepDuration = 0`) is tracked in its own field, separate from the retained sample series — it's simply "the state one `step`/`reset` ago". `reset(state)` is the discontinuous-overwrite counterpart to `step`: it just `push`es the new state, relying on `StateQueue.push`'s own correction contract (a push at-or-before the current newest discards everything from that time onward first) to drop whatever the discontinuity invalidated, and still advances `prevState`. `at(t)` is a one-line forward to `StateQueue.at(t)` — that class is the sole place a timed state series is binary-searched and Hermite-interpolated. `samplesOldestFirst()` flattens the retained span (`_samples.toArrayOldestFirst()`) into the oldest-first `readonly KinematicState[]` that `game/trajectory-line.ts` wants, and memoizes the result — repeated calls between a `step`/`reset` pair return the same array reference, which is what lets `TrajectoryLine.syncGeometry`'s reference-equality re-bake guard actually skip work; `step`/`reset` both invalidate the memo since both change what the array would contain. This class holds no orbital-elements memo of its own — a state's osculating elements around a chosen body are `game-entity/game-entity.ts`'s `GameEntity.orbitalElementsAround`, since which body to center on is a per-caller decision this class has no stake in. The class is deliberately direction-agnostic — the same step/at/record operations back a **past** span (`GameEntity.actual`), a **future** one (`GameEntity.predicted`, whose retained sample series spans "now → prediction horizon"), and a **planned** one (`game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc.trajectory`, for each of `plan/plan-path.ts`'s owned segments). There is no second implementation of "advance a timed state while recording decimated samples" anywhere in the codebase, and adding one would be a mistake. `step`'s optional trailing `extrapolationCenter: Attractor | null` (default `null`) is stored verbatim as `extrapolationCenter` and discarded by `reset` (a discontinuous overwrite changes the tip's own orbit) — this class never derives it, since judging which attractor is analytic needs an `Ephemeris` this class deliberately doesn't hold; the caller (`predictor.ts`, below) resolves and passes it. `extrapolatedAt(t, centerStateAtT)` is `at`'s counterpart for a time past the retained span: at or before `state.t`, or with no stored center, it defers to `at(t)` unchanged (existing callers that never pass a center see identical behavior); beyond it, it hands `state`/`extrapolationCenter` to `kepler-extrapolation.ts`'s `extrapolatedRelativeState` and adds the caller-supplied `centerStateAtT` — the center's own ECI state at `t`, since `physics/` holds no ephemeris lookup of its own — onto the center-relative result.
