- `src/physics/kepler-extrapolation.ts` — pure two-body Kepler extrapolation for whatever lies past a `DynamicTrajectory`'s recorded tip, reached only through `DynamicTrajectory.extrapolatedAt` — never an implicit fallback inside `at`. `extrapolatedRelativeState(tip, center, t)` treats `tip` as an osculating two-body orbit around `center` (`attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`) and returns the state at `t` relative to `center` (ECI-direction position/velocity with the origin at `center`, the same convention `elements.ts`'s `stateFromOrbitalElements` uses — absolute ECI is the caller's own addition); it answers `null` whenever the orbit isn't a well-posed ellipse — no orbital elements, `e >= 0.98` (the elliptic Kepler solver's own precondition), a non-finite or non-positive semi-major axis, or `center.mu <= 0`. `extrapolatedRelativeStates(tip, center, untilT, count)` is its multi-sample sibling, spacing `count` states evenly in eccentric anomaly `E` rather than in time (equal-time sampling bunches near periapsis and thins near apoapsis, the reason `game/orbit-line.ts`'s ellipse sampler is also E-uniform), with the last sample landing exactly on `untilT`. Spanning several revolutions needs the endpoint's `E` unwrapped past `eccentricAnomalyFromMean`'s own `[-π, π]` wrap before solving it — the one Newton solve the whole call makes; every other sample's `E → M → t → ν` is a direct formula, so the series stays monotonic without a second Kepler solve per point.

  The node-vs-context-menu split: in map mode `game.ts` calls `MapContextActions.handleMapRightClick`/`handleLeftClick`/`handleDoubleClick` before `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer`, so a right-click that lands on a marker (celestial label/ship/apsis/AN-DN icon) is claimed by `MapContextActions` first; only a right-click that misses every marker reaches the editor's own node hit test, and whatever that also misses falls through to `MapContextActions.handleEmptySpaceRightClick` last — the priority is expressed purely as call order, and the gizmos never reference each other.

`src/game/comms/` — the communication network and the coverage test (§13 of 第8版_2). Split so the judgment itself is a pure function and the per-frame assembly is separate:
- `coverage.ts` — **the sole implementation of "is this position in communication range", DOM- and THREE-free** (`tsconfig.test.json` compiles it directly for `test:unit`). `CommRelay` is `{id, pos, range, isGround}`; `activeRelays(relays, attractors)` closes forward from the ground stations (`isGround`), admitting a relay only once it is linked to an already-active one, so an isolated relay puts nobody in range however strong it is; `isInCommRange(pos, active, ownRange, attractors)` asks whether any active relay is reachable. Two points are linked when the line of sight clears every body (`physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded`, the same function the map's own icon visibility uses) **and** the distance is within `min` of the two sides' ranges — the reach is decided by the smaller of the two, so a small module near a powerful relay still only reaches its own grade. A relay standing on a body's own surface is a case the ray-versus-sphere test cannot answer — `isOccluded` drops a body the query point already sits on — so each endpoint is additionally checked against whatever body it is standing on, by whether the other end lies above that body's local horizon. Without it a lunar-surface station would reach straight through the Moon and cover its far side (§13-4). `CommOccluder` (`{radius, state}`) is the structural body shape `physics/attractor.ts`'s `Attractor` already satisfies.
- `comm-stations.ts` — `initialCommStations(ephemeris, t)`: the two ground stations that exist from the start (§13-4) — a lunar-surface base on the Earth-facing side, and the near-rectilinear-halo-orbit base, stood in for by Earth-Moon L2 (`Ephemeris.lagrangeAt('moon', t).L2`) since no halo is integrated. Both move with the Moon, so they are re-derived from the ephemeris rather than stored; a registry with no moon (the alternate-system debug stage) gets none, and everything is then out of coverage.
- `comm-network.ts` — `CommNetwork implements CoverageQuery`: this frame's network. `update(simTime, ephemeris, vessels, attractors)` collects the two ground stations plus every living vessel carrying a working communication part, runs `activeRelays`, and caches the result; **which of those vessels is itself a root of the network is `capabilities.ts`'s `isCommStation` — a base module and a communication module aboard the same vessel**, since §13-3 defines a communication station as 「基地モジュールの1つであり、大型の通信設備を持つ」 and no part type of its own states it; it rebuilds only once `COMM_REFRESH_SEC` (2 s) of simTime has passed since the last build (or time ran backwards, i.e. a snapshot load), since coverage changes on the scale of seconds and needs no per-frame precision. Owned by `Game`, which calls `update` in `advanceSimulation` **before** `activeStage.update` — the stage is what advances an unmanned vessel's plan, so the network it asks has to be this frame's — and hands the same instance to `Stage` as the twelfth `StageDeps` element.

`src/game/plan/` — maneuver planning and its future-trajectory *display*, independent of whether the map is open. "When and in what frame to look" (display duration, the future-ghost slider, the coordinate frame the future is drawn in) is a separate, `game/`-root concern (`DisplayWindowManager` below) from "what a plan looks like when you look" (this folder):
- `src/game/docking.ts` — `Docking`: the base cross-cut — physical docking between craft, the per-base operations windows, and the hull-assembly session — spanning `EntityManager`+`CameraSystem`+`ActiveVesselController`+`ViewManager`+the HUD, so no single one of those owns it and it gets its own module, the same shape as `MapContextActions`/`ViewManager`. **It holds no `Game` reference** — every collaborator arrives as its own reference (`ActiveVesselController`, `CameraSystem`, `ViewManager`, `EntityManager`, `MapContextActions`), and the one thing with nowhere else to come from — the pause state, which is `Game`'s own by the `/refactor-fixed` exception — arrives as two closures (`pauseGame`/`resumeGame`) instead of the whole object. **That is a deliberate, interim exception to this project's "inject references, not closures" rule**, and the reason is the imbalance: the two calls needed are tiny against everything `Game` exposes, and this module is still growing, so handing it `Game` would hand it reach it has no stated use for. Recording the exception here and in the constructor's own comment — rather than in `/refactor-fixed` — is itself the statement that it is a stopgap, not a settled boundary. **Nothing here switches which view is showing**: both the base's operations and the assembly session are windows layered over whichever world view is up, so a player never leaves the world to work on a ship. `selectBase(base)` only marks the base as the current one (`activeBase`). `openBaseOperations(base, clientX?, clientY?)` opens — or re-homes and brings to front — that base's own `hud/base-operations-window.ts` `BaseOperationsWindow`, held in a `Map<string, BaseOperationsWindow>` keyed by base id so one base never carries two, and asks for no proximity: a base is reached exactly the way any other object's property window is. `syncBaseWindows()` (run from `syncAssembly` each frame) closes the window of a base that has left the world, and `clearActiveBaseIf(base)` does the same at the moment a base is deleted, cancelling an assembly session on it first. **The assembly session is single** — `assembly: AssemblySession | null`, holding the base, its `vessel/dock-workbench.ts` `DockWorkbenchSession`, the `DockWorkbenchController` over it, the `hud/assembly-panel.ts` `AssemblyPanel`, which target is being edited, the current 3D `AssemblySelection` (`{kind:'node', nodeId} | {kind:'edge', edgeId} | null` — a picked part is a grab, not a selection, so this type carries no `part` variant) the panel's own section editor reads, and the chase camera's `savedChaseDist` from the moment the session opened, restored once it closes. `startAssembly(base, preferredTargetId?)` builds it from `assemblyTargets(base)` (the base itself, each docked vessel carrying an assembly, and each draft), opens the panel, frames the chase camera on the initial target (private `frameAssemblyCamera`, below) and calls `pauseGame()` — every edit lands on the session, and pausing is what keeps the world from moving under a construction that has not been decided yet. **`frameAssemblyCamera(entry)`** sets `cameraSystem.setChaseCameraOverride` to the selected target's own pose (`targetPose`, below) and its `dist` to that target's own circumscribing radius (`assemblyExtentRadius`, over both `collision-shape.ts`'s capsules and every node's own `circumradius` — the capsules alone under-measure a target reached only through a `decoupler` edge, which `deriveCapsules` skips) times `const.ts`'s `ASSEMBLY_CAMERA_DISTANCE_MARGIN`, clamped into `chase-camera.ts`'s `[CHASE_DIST_MIN, CHASE_DIST_MAX]`; a target whose pose can't be resolved (a draft whose `draftOffset` doesn't resolve) leaves the camera exactly where it was rather than doing nothing gracefully wrong. `panel.onTargetSelect` reruns it on every tab switch, and `endAssembly` clears the override (`setChaseCameraOverride(null)`) and restores `savedChaseDist` before resuming. `commitAssembly()` writes back through the private `applyTargetAssembly` per target and **applies nothing unless every target validates**, since a half-applied set would leave a base and the ships in its dock disagreeing about a shared dock port; it then replaces the base's inventory with the session's. `cancelAssembly()` simply drops the session — the real vessels were never touched, so there is nothing to undo. Both end through the private `endAssembly()`, the one place the held part is dropped, the panel closed and `resumeGame()` called. `assemblyInProgress` is what `launch` refuses on, so a ship cannot leave mid-edit. `updateAssembly(input)` is **the single place a grab, a release, or a 3D grab-up happens**: while a session is open it drains every queued click through `Input.takeClicks` unconditionally (nothing else reads this queue during a session, since `Game.handlePointerInput` returns early while paused), routing each through the private `handleAssemblyClick` — a click while holding a part releases it (`dragController.release`, then restores whatever real mesh `hideHeldOriginal` hid); a click while holding nothing instead raycasts the current target's own render root (`dragController.pickAt`) and, through `applyPick`, either begins a fresh drag on a picked part (`sourceInventory: false`, hiding its real mesh so the ghost doesn't double it) or replaces the selection with a picked node/edge, dropping it on a miss. **Consuming every click in one queue is what makes "drop, then immediately re-grab whatever is under the cursor" structurally impossible** — the click that ends a grab and the click that would start the next one can never be the same event. Only once the click queue is drained does it forward this frame's cursor to `AssemblyDragController.update` — camera matrices already at this frame's value, since it runs after `cameraSystem.update` — with the active camera, its ECI position and the frame's `AssemblyDragTarget` (the edited target's session-side assembly plus where that target's hull sits in ECI — the base's own state, a docked ship's slot pose, or a draft's offset ahead of the base). `syncAssembly(fo)` closes stale base windows (`syncBaseWindows`), calls `revealStructure()` on the current target's own `Vessel` (below), pushes the resolved pose into the ghost, and refreshes the panel (`panel.sync(session, selection)`) — self-gating on there being a session, so `Game` calls both unconditionally. `checkProximity()` (called once per frame) only keeps the `dockedPairs` map honest — capture is never automatic, and a ship enters a base through `storeInBase`, which pushes a `DockedVesselEntry` onto `base.baseState.dockedVessels`, attaches its mesh to the base's own render object and calls `EntityManager.parkVessel` (splices out of the roster without disposing — `removeVessel`'s `dispose()` would destroy the mesh/VFX permanently, which a ship that will fly again can't afford). **`alive` is never touched by any of `storeInBase`/`buildDraft`/`launch`** — a docked, freshly-built or freshly-restored ship stays `alive` throughout, and "docked" is defined purely as "outside `entities.ownShips()`". If the parked ship was the operated one, `storeInBase` clears its transient commands and silences `WorldSfx.setThrust`/`setRcs` itself (a parked ship's `updateControls` never runs again, so nothing else would), hands off to the next living ship, and moves to map view only when there is none left. `launch` reverses it: a fresh `KinematicState` off the dock slot's own position and normal, `entities.addVessel`, `activeVessels.set`, `viewManager.setView('combat')`. `createDraft(base)`/`buildDraft(base, targetId)` are the new-ship path, reached from the panel's own 新規船下書き/建造して格納 buttons — a draft is a `crewedAssembly` copy rendered as a child of the base and editable as an assembly target, and building it charges `economy/producibility.ts` against the base's own ledger before constructing the `Vessel` straight into a free dock slot — the charge is built from a separate blueprint holding only the placements the draft does not already own (`ownedPartIds`), so a part taken from the warehouse, whose materials were already spent producing it, is never charged twice. **A picked-up part is hidden on the real mesh for as long as it's held, tracked as `heldOriginal: {root, partId} | null`** — the ghost would otherwise double it — and `endAssembly`/every drop or release path restores it unconditionally, so a session that ends mid-grab never leaves a part invisible.
- `src/game/map-pickables.ts` — `MapPickables`: the frame's single candidate set of `MapPickable`s and the `MapVisibilityPolicy` they're judged against — "what may be picked", split from "what happens once it is" (`map-context-actions.ts`'s `MapContextActions`, below). `refresh(displayWindow)` returns immediately unless `cameraSystem.overviewMode` holds — `Game.update` calls it unconditionally every frame and leaves the map-view check to `refresh` itself — and otherwise re-derives `FocusMarkers`' celestial-label positions and `NavTarget`'s AN/DN (both are part of the set, so the pairing is kept inside rather than imposed on callers) and rebuilds `pickables`: `FocusMarkers`' celestial labels + every living self-flown ship (`'player'`), enemy (`'ship'`), ammo pickup and base, each at its `displayState(displayTime)` so a picked object sits at the same future-ghost position as its own mesh, + `navTarget.mapPickables()` + `PlanDisplay.apsisMarkers` + every entity's own `equatorNodes?.mapPickables()` (a loop over `entities.all()`, since each object owns its own EqAN/EqDN pair — see `marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts` below). **The candidate set stays one list** — splitting it per kind guarantees a consumer that only reads one slice, which is how "the menu opens but focus jumps to Earth" bugs happen; `CameraSystem.update` (focus resolution) and `MapContextActions.handleMapRightClick` (menu) both read this same `pickables` getter. `refresh` is also **the one place the frame's `MapVisibilityPolicy` is built**, exposed as the `visibilityPolicy` getter (`null` until the first `refresh`): settling it in the update phase and letting `Game.sync` read it back keeps "what may be picked" and "what is drawn" one judgment, rather than two structurally identical constructions that could be handed different focus ids or camera positions. Each candidate's `inFocusedSystem` is resolved for the non-`'body'` kinds only — a celestial body's row stands for a system rather than sitting in one, so nothing reads the value for it, and deriving it costs a `strongestAttractor` plus an ancestor walk per candidate. `lastSimTime` is the `simTime` the frame's `refresh` ran against, read back by `MapContextActions` so a time-dependent row (e.g. a passage countdown) is derived against the same instant the candidate set itself was. `perfCounts()` is the map-mode/candidate-count/label-count slice `Game.perfCounts()` merges in. Constructed by `Game` right after `PlanEditor` (`this.editor`), taking `Game`/`EntityManager`/`Ephemeris`/`NavTarget`/`CameraSystem`/`PlanEditor` as plain references — holding none of `MapContextActions`'s own state (`windows`/`ObjectListPanel`/`ContextMenu`) is what lets a caller that only needs the candidate set (`MapCamera.update`, `FrameControls.sync`) take a `MapPickables` reference alone.

- `src/game/map-context-actions.ts` — `MapContextActions`: everything that happens once a `MapPickable` is picked — the left/right/double-click hit test, the per-kind context menu and property-window item set, the property-window lifecycle, and the dispatch of the chosen action to its owner; the candidate set itself is `MapPickables` (above), taken as a constructor argument and read back through its `pickables`/`visibilityPolicy`/`lastSimTime` getters rather than rebuilt here. `handleMapRightClick(input, simTime)` picks via `map-pickable.ts`'s `pickNearest` over `this.pickables.pickables` and opens a `hud/property-window.ts` `PropertyWindow<MenuAction>` (below) built from `itemsFor`. Each open window's `entry.target` is refreshed every `sync()` from the current candidate set, and its `onSelect` reads through `entry.target` rather than the `target` captured at open time — a time-dependent action (warp/add-node) must run against the object's current position/crossing time, not a frame-old snapshot; `'empty-space'` is the one kind with nothing to show a property window about, so `handleEmptySpaceRightClick` keeps opening the plain `ContextMenu<MapPickable>` instead — via the shared private `openEmptySpaceMenu`, which is also what a combat-view right click that hits nothing falls through to (below), so the two views never carry two separate empty-space menu implementations. `handleCombatRightClick(input, targets, project, simTime)` is `handleMapRightClick`'s combat-view counterpart (the same object opens the same window regardless of view) — it resolves the click through `Targeter.pickTargetAt` rather than `this.pickables.pickables` (which only `MapPickables.refresh` — map-view-only — populates) and, on a hit, opens the identical `openPropertyWindow` after converting the `CombatTarget` to a `MapPickable` (`combatTargetPickable`, kind `'player'`/`'ship'` by `instanceof Vessel`); a miss falls through to `openEmptySpaceMenu`. The one thing that differs by view is item/row content, not window type: `combatTargetLockItems(entity)` (private) prepends `targetPrimary`/`targetSecondary` items to the `'ship'`/`'player'` handlers' `itemsFor` — reading/writing `game.targeter.target`/`.secondaryTarget` via `Targeter.setPrimaryTarget`/`.setSecondaryTarget` (`runTargetLock`) — and returns none of them while `cameraSystem.overviewMode` holds, since target-lock has no meaning on the map. `itemsFor`: `'body'`/`'ship'`/`'player'` all add the shared `navTargetItems` — set/unset nav-target, shown only when `NavTarget.canTarget` finds a resolvable orbital plane for that id, so an item is never offered and then silently refused; `'apsis'`/`'relnode'`/`'eqnode'` all add both "warp here" (`SimSpeedManager.startAutoWarpTo`) and "add node here" (`PlanEditor.addNodeAt`), routed through the shared `runApsisRelnode`; `'player'` additionally offers, at the top of its item list, a single 軌道計画の実行 toggle (shown only while `activeStage.executesPlans`, cycling OFF→瞬間移動→自動操縦→OFF on each click, showing the current mode in its own label — `PlanExecutionMode` above) followed by activate/deactivate (`game.activeVessels.set`/`setOrNull`, one label swapping for the other by whether this ship is already active) and delete, the last simply omitted for the active ship so the action is unreachable rather than rejected. `'player'`/`'ship'`/`'ammo'`/`'base'` also offer "duplicate" (`duplicateItems()`), gated — like `'empty-space'`'s "place object" item and `runDuplicate`'s dispatch — on `activeStage.authoring` being non-`null` (`stages/stage.ts`'s `ObjectAuthoring`, the placement/duplication entry point a stage that supports authoring returns for itself), so the item is never shown where there's nowhere to route it, matching the "don't offer what would be refused" rule the whole menu follows. Choosing it (`runDuplicate` → `duplicateSourceFor`, which maps `MapPickable.kind` to the matching `EntityManager` lookup and that entity's live `state`) hands the state to `activeStage.authoring.openObjectPlacerForDuplicate`, which converts it to a preset `ElementsForm` via `creative/duplicate-form.ts`'s `elementsFormFromState` and opens `ObjectPlacerPanel` pre-filled with it — a body/`'apsis'`/`'relnode'` target has no matching `duplicateSourceFor` case (nothing to duplicate), which is exactly why those kinds don't get the item in the first place. If the state can't convert (hyperbolic/parabolic) or the converted form's reference body fails `placement-validation.ts`'s `validateBaseReferenceFields` (a base whose current orbit isn't moon-centered), `openObjectPlacerForDuplicate` doesn't pass the mismatched form through at all — it hints why and opens with only the object type carried over (`ObjectPlacerPreset`'s `{kind: 'objectType'}`), since applying the values while silently rejecting just the reference body would leave the panel showing a different orbit than the one displayed. `handleRightClick` runs before `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer`, so a right-click on a marker is claimed here first; `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer` only sees the misses, consuming the ones that hit a node, so priority is expressed purely as call order and the two gizmos never reference each other. A right-click that hits neither falls through to `handleEmptySpaceRightClick`, which opens the `'empty-space'` menu whatever stage is running — only its "place object" item is gated on `activeStage.authoring`. `MapContextActions` owns a permanent `ObjectListPanel` (`hud/object-list-panel.ts`, docked top-right via `hudRail(root, 'right')`) shown for as long as `overviewMode` holds, with a `buildCollapseToggle` fold on its own heading (the list is always up and takes a wide strip of the right edge, so it needs to be foldable without being closable), listing the current `pickables`, grouped into per-kind accordion sections (天体/自艦/敵/弾薬/基地; `apsis`/`relnode`/`eqnode`/`empty-space` are markers, not objects, and are left out), each section's header toggling its own body open/closed — within 天体, a body's satellites and own Lagrange points nest under that body's row as a collapsible child list rather than a flat indent, and a satellite's own Lagrange points nest one level deeper still under the satellite's own row, mirroring the physical hierarchy (`ObjectListPanel`'s recursive `RowNode`, built from a `parentOf: ReadonlyMap<id, id>` `MapContextActions.sync` derives from `FocusMarkers.labels` — a Lagrange label's parent is `object-groups.ts`'s `lagrangeParentId`, an ordinary body's is `primaryOf`; a body with neither a parent nor children stays a flat row). Every such child list starts collapsed (`RowNode.expanded` defaults `false`) since the set nested under any one body is large and rarely needed at a glance, unlike the five top-level sections which start expanded. A row answers three separate gestures: a single click selects it (`onSelect`), a double click moves the camera focus there (`onFocus` → `game.frameControls.setFocus({kind:'object', id})`) — the same single-click-selects / double-click-commits split the map itself uses, so no single click moves the view out from under you — and a right-click (`onSelectRight`) opens the same `openPropertyWindow` a right-click on the object itself would — so the window is just another view onto the one candidate set, not a second source of what's on the map. `sync(simTime, attractors, player)` (called from `Game.sync`, alongside the other map-mode panel syncs, reading `cameraSystem.overviewMode` itself rather than taking it as an argument) is the only place `pickables` reaches the DOM — and it must not do that by rewriting `innerHTML`: `ObjectListPanel.sync` keeps one persistent row element per item id (added/removed by diffing against the previous frame's id set) precisely because a full rebuild would drop each row's click listener the instant a click lands mid-frame, the same reason `stage.ts`'s radiator buttons stay off the `StatusPanel`'s rebuilt-every-frame center column. **Which rows a section holds, in what order, and under which parent is settled per section as ids alone (`SectionOrder`) and rebuilt only when it can actually differ** — when the inputs that decide it change (the candidate ids, their display names, kinds and parents, whether each passes the filter, and the filter/sort selection, all compared against the previous frame's record), or when the order being held no longer satisfies the sort under this frame's values. That second condition is what the distance sort needs: nothing about the candidate set changes as ships drift, yet the correct order does, so a pure input comparison would freeze a stale order — checking the held order against the comparator instead costs one pass and rebuilds exactly on the frames the sort genuinely flips. Row values (distance, detail) and the per-section header counts are written every frame regardless, since those change continuously and cost nothing to push.

`MapContextActions` also owns the property-window lifecycle: `windows: Map<string, WindowEntry>` (`WindowEntry = {win: PropertyWindow<MenuAction>, target: MapPickable}`) keyed by `` `${kind}:${id}` `` (a `MapPickable.id` alone isn't unique across kinds) so an object holds at most one window. **The "at most one non-clipped window" exclusivity itself moved to `hud/overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager`** (施策5): `openPropertyWindow` passes a shared `PROPERTY_WINDOW_TEMP_GROUP` string as `PropertyWindow`'s `tempWindowGroup` constructor argument, and `PropertyWindow` itself declares/reconfigures its own `exclusiveGroup` membership (only while unclipped) on every clip toggle — `MapContextActions` no longer tracks a `tempWindowKey` field or wires `onOutsideClick`/`onClipChange` callbacks at all. `openPropertyWindow(clientX, clientY, target, simTime)` — called from `handleRightClick` (map view) and `handleCombatRightClick` (combat view, below) for every kind but `'empty-space'` — moves and `bringToFront()`s an existing window for that key instead of opening a second one; otherwise it builds one via `buildContent` (`itemsFor`'s `type: 'header'` item becomes the window's title/subtitle; the action items themselves come from `windowItems`, not a one-time snapshot — see below) with `rows: []` (filled by the next `sync`, so there's no separate row-building path at open time) and wires `onSelect`/`onClose`. `onSelect` calls `this.handlers[kind].run`, then closes the window unless it's clipped — except `'delete'`, which always closes it since the target is gone either way. `close()` (the same method `ViewManager` already called to fold the object-list panel on leaving the map view) now also closes every open window. `sync(overviewMode, simTime, bodies, player)` decides whether to close a window via `isTargetGone(target)` — for `'player'`/`'ship'`/`'ammo'`/`'base'` this asks `EntityManager`'s own finders (a `'player'` by whether `findOwnShip(id)` still resolves at all, since a lost self-flown ship leaves `entities.ownShips()` outright; the other three by the found entity's `alive`) rather than the frame's `pickables`, since a living ship can legitimately be absent from `pickables` for a frame when its `displayState(displayTime)` comes back `null` (future-ghost slider past its prediction horizon, or time-warp outrunning prediction growth) — closing on that would be wrong, since the entity hasn't died. The remaining kinds (celestial body/apsis/AN-DN icons) have no entity of their own, so they fall back to "still in this frame's `pickables`". A window that survives has its stored `target` refreshed from the current `pickables` entry when one exists (kept as-is otherwise, since every kind's row/item derivation reads the live entity's own state rather than `target.pos`) before `syncRows`/`syncItems` re-derive that kind's `PropertyRow[]`/`PropertyWindowItem[]` and push them through — row derivation is display-only and reuses `hud/orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo`/`relativeInfo` for every kind that shows orbital elements, never rederiving them, and action-item derivation (`windowItems`, shared by `buildContent` at open time and `sync` every frame after) re-runs `itemsFor` so a clipped window's items track state that changes after it opened (e.g. which ship is the active one). A row is omitted outright (not shown as `—`) wherever it depends on a live `player` that doesn't exist, e.g. on CREATIVE before any ship is placed. `buildContent` also passes `renameHandlerFor(target)` as `onRename` — non-`undefined` only for `'player'`/`'base'` (the two kinds with a mutable display name — `Vessel`/`Vessel``s own `name`), and re-resolved through `target.id` inside the closure (not the `target` object itself) so a rename always lands on the live entity regardless of how many frames have passed since the window opened.
- `src/game/hud/draggable-window.ts` — `DraggableWindow implements OverlayHandle`: the chrome every draggable HUD window is built from — a header (title/subtitle, an optional ✎ rename button, 📌 clip, ✕), header dragging (pointer capture, latched past the shared `CLICK_MOVE_THRESHOLD`), re-clamping into the viewport through `viewport.ts`'s `onViewportChange`, the `MQ_COMPACT` bottom-sheet layout (there `moveTo` only clears a stale non-compact `left`/`top` and dragging never starts at all), and its own `OverlayManager` registration. The optional `tempWindowGroup` constructor argument is the whole of "temporary": while unclipped the window declares `closeOnEscape`/`closeOnOutsideClick` and joins that exclusive group, and every clip toggle `reconfigure`s the declaration — a window built without one (the load-inspection window) closes only through its own ✕. It owns **the frame and nothing else**: `body` is handed back empty and what goes into it — property rows, a tab bar, a part shelf — belongs to whoever composed it, which is why `PropertyWindow`/`BaseOperationsWindow`/`AssemblyPanel` each hold one rather than each carrying their own drag/clip/close implementation. `onHandleShortcut` is where a caller plugs its own item shortcuts into `OverlayManager.dispatchShortcut`, refused while clipped so at most one window answers a key.
- `src/game/hud/base-operations-window.ts` — `BaseOperationsWindow`: one base's 格納艦艇 / 部品 / 生産, as a `DraggableWindow` over the world rather than a screen of its own — several bases can be clipped open side by side, and time keeps running while a list is being read (only the assembly session pauses, since only it changes a ship). Its own `#hud .bow-*` CSS is injected once through a module-local `ensureStyle()`, and it rebuilds its content on open/tab switch/action rather than per frame, since nothing here moves outside a click. **格納艦艇** lists the docked ships with their dock slot and HP and offers 発進 (`onLaunchVessel`, wired by `Docking`) and "部品を見る", which selects that ship and switches to the parts face. **部品** shows the selected ship's installed parts above the base's warehouse — the warehouse renders with no ship docked, since the inventory belongs to the base — and offers repair, refuelling and same-type swapping. **Everything is priced in resources, never currency**: repairing charges `production.ts`'s `repairBlueprintOf` (the part's own build materials scaled by the fraction of `maxHp` lost, the tank shell excluded since a damaged tank still has one), refuelling charges `refuelBlueprintOf` (the propellant that tank actually holds), and swapping charges nothing at all, since moving an already-built part manufactures nothing. One private `shortfall`/`canAfford`/`spend` trio backs every button, so what a button shows and what it charges cannot disagree. A repair writes the ship's own `Part.hp` and then re-derives the `DockedVesselEntry`'s `hp`/`maxHp` through `Vessel.refreshFromParts()`, which is what keeps the "hull or cockpit gone ⇒ hp 0" rule from being bypassed by a display-only snapshot. **生産** builds a single element into the warehouse — its palette is `default-blueprints.ts`'s `producibleParts()`, the elements the built-in designs actually carry, never a second spec table — and shows the base's own resource stock plus **a debug-only resource grant** (a resource picked from an `ObjectPicker`, a mass, added straight to that base's ledger), the foothold that lets production run at all until lunar mining and enemy drops land, and deleted once they do.
- `src/game/hud/assembly-panel.ts` — `AssemblyPanel`: the assembly session's own `DraggableWindow` — a `TabBar` over the session's targets, 元に戻す/やり直す (wired to `DockWorkbenchController.undo`/`redo`, whose enabled state follows the session's own history), 確定/取消, 新規船下書き/建造して格納 (`onCreateDraft`/`onBuildDraft`, the latter's label carrying the build cost the same way the base window's repair/production buttons already do, via `draftBuildStatus`), a selection row (the 3D pick `docking.ts` last resolved — `選択: ノード <id>` / `選択: エッジ <id>` / `選択: なし` — with its own `選択を削除` button, enabled only while something is selected), a section editor that appears only for a selected node (one control per existing cross-section primitive — a `SegmentedControl`/`ToggleSwitch` to pick which primitive when there is more than one, then its shape kind, its shape's own dimension fields, and its phase angle; **adding or removing a primitive is out of scope**, since the glued-face-length rule that would have to hold across such an edit isn't settled), a structural-member shelf (kind/length/radius, and a separation-impulse field shown only for `decoupler`, feeding a fresh `member.ts` `MemberSpec` straight into `AssemblyDragController.beginMemberDrag` on `部材を掴む`), a search field, the current target's validation errors, and the part shelf: the session's warehouse grouped by part type, one `Button` per part. **Pressing a part's button is what starts a drag** (`AssemblyDragController.beginDrag(workbench, part, currentTargetId, true)`) — the shelf is one of two places a drag begins, the other being picking an already-mounted part off the hull in 3D (`docking.ts`'s `applyPick`, which calls `beginDrag` with `sourceInventory: false`). Node/edge removal and every primitive edit are built with `assembly-editor.ts`'s `removeNode`/`removeEdge`/`editSection` and applied through `DockWorkbenchController.applyAssemblyEdit`, so each confirmed edit is one undo-history entry regardless of which control produced it. It is a persistent panel, refreshed by `sync(session, selection)` every frame, and it keeps its own last-seen keys for the target list, the draft-action status, the selection, the section editor's own inputs, the inventory and the error list so the DOM is only rebuilt when one of them actually changed — a shelf rebuilt under a half-finished click would drop the very button being pressed. The filter only hides buttons rather than rebuilding the shelf, for the same reason.
- `src/game/hud/inventory-labels.ts` — `PART_TYPE_LABELS`/`formatPartMeta`/`formatResourceAmount`: the display names and number formats for what a base holds. Shared by `BaseOperationsWindow` (the existing lists) and `AssemblyPanel` (the shelf), so the same part and the same resource read identically wherever they appear.
- `src/game/nav-target.ts` — `NavTarget`: the map's *navigation* target — any `MapPickable`, not just an `Vessel`. It is deliberately a different class from `Targeter`'s combat target: the two differ both in what they can point at (`Vessel` only vs. any `MapPickable`) and in what the designation is *for* (gunnery vs. maneuver planning), so neither one's constraints — e.g. `Vessel`-specific fire-assist logic — get to narrow the other's target range. They may happen to point at the same enemy; that is a coincidence, not a shared id. Holds only a target id (`toggleTarget`/`id`); `update(player, entities, ephemeris, displayWindow)` re-derives the relative AN/DN (where the player's orbit crosses the target's orbital plane) from scratch each frame — `resolvePlaneNormal` reads the target's own orbit normal for a ship or base (`orbitalElementsOf(strongestAttractor(...)).hHat`, found by name in `entities.hostileVessels()` first, then `entities.ownShips()`, then `entities.baseVessels()` — a base has a real orbit via `GameEntity` like any other entity, so it resolves the same way), the lunar orbit normal for the moon (`Ephemeris.orbitNormalAt('moon', …)`), or the relevant rotating frame's ẑ for a Lagrange point (`orbitFrameRotationAt('moon'|'earth', …)`) — and stores nothing if the target's plane can't be resolved or is ~coincident with the player's own. It also drives the target's own EqAN/EqDN pair (`marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts`) off the private `resolveEntity(id, entities)` that `resolvePlaneNormal` shares, before the player's own elements are consulted at all — those icons describe the target's orbit, not the relation between the two. `nodeAnomalies` returns the node true anomalies in the player's own center-relative basis, so `anPos`/`dnPos` are un-baked back to absolute ECI via `frameOfAttractor(playerCenter)` + `toInertialState` before being stored — `positionOnOrbit` alone returns a center-relative position, which only happens to read as ECI while the player orbits Earth (center at the ECI origin). `canTarget(id, entities, ephemeris, t)` runs the same `resolvePlaneNormal` just to answer whether a plane resolves at all, which is what lets `MapContextActions.itemsFor` show the "set as nav-target" item on any `MapPickable.kind` whose id can actually work — including the moon and every Lagrange point, not just ships. `passTimeOf('nav-an'|'nav-dn')` answers the AN/DN crossing time (`tofBetween` from the player's current true anomaly), which `MapContextActions` feeds to `SimSpeedManager.startAutoWarpTo`/`PlanEditor.addNodeAt`. `mapPickables()`/`sync(project, overviewMode, cameraPos)` expose/draw the △/▽ icons themselves — `MapPickables.refresh()` folds them into the shared candidate set alongside `FocusMarkers`' labels and every living ship. `sync` hides an icon `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body when `overviewMode`, off the `Attractor[]` `update` cached at `simTime`. Setting the nav-target itself is otherwise map-only (`MapContextActions`'s right-click), with one exception: `handleCombatBaseClick(entities, input, project, overviewMode)` is `NavTarget`'s own combat-view right-click handler, holding a private `ContextMenu<Vessel, MenuAction>` (offering only `MenuCommon.navTarget`/`cancel`, since a base isn't a weapons target). It takes `overviewMode` as a plain argument and self-gates (`NavTarget` holds no `CameraSystem` reference, so it can't read the view itself) rather than trusting the caller to have already checked it. `Game.handlePointerInput` calls it *before* `MapContextActions.handleCombatRightClick` — its callback returns `false` on a miss (`Input.takeRightClicks`' fall-through convention), so a click that doesn't land on a base is left for `MapContextActions` to try against enemies/self-flown ships, exactly as if this handler weren't there. This is why the split stays clean: `Targeter` never learns about bases, and `NavTarget` never learns about enemies.
- `src/game/view-manager.ts` — `ViewManager`: **the source of truth for which view is showing** and the sole place a transition happens (`setView`). Two views draw the 3D world — `WorldViewId` = `'combat'` (Operations) | `'map'` — and everything else the player opens over them (a base's operations, an assembly session, a property window) is an overlay window rather than a view, so there is nothing to enter and leave. `ViewId` keeps a third value, `'dock'`, purely so an older snapshot's `camera.view` still parses: `setView('dock')` returns immediately, `canEnter('dock')` is false, and the constructor resolves a requested view through the same `canEnter` rule anyway, so a saved `'dock'` lands in `'map'` — the same save-compatibility-only shape `'combat'`'s own internal id already has. **`setView` calls the private `applyChrome` unconditionally, even when `next` already equals `current`** — so every call, transition or not, leaves the guarantee "the camera/plan/future-display flags and HUD chrome match the current view" holding. The constructor takes `ActiveVesselController` and `TouchControls | null` alongside an optional trailing `requestedView?: WorldViewId`, resolves `worldView` through `canEnter`, and calls `applyChrome()` itself, so a launch that falls back to `'map'` starts with its chrome already in sync rather than needing a first transition to earn it. `applyChrome` co-switches the camera-side flag (`cameraSystem.setMapMode`), the plan-side editing flag (`editor.setMapMode`), the future-display gate (`displayWindow.forceCurrent`), the HUD's own world-view class and the touch UI, so they move together while staying logically independent; leaving map additionally runs `editor.onMapClosed()`/`closeMenu()`/`mapActions.close()` and entering it clears the node selection. `handleInput(input)` takes `[M]` and, when leaving map for Operations, refuses with a hint whenever `canEnter('combat')` is false rather than silently no-op'ing; leaving Operations for map is never refused. **`canEnter('combat')` asks whether there is anything to look at**, not only anything to fly: an operated vessel, a base the controlled-base provider names, or any living base at all. `selectableViews()`/`getSelectableMenuItems()` are what `ViewBadge` lists, filtering out the current view and anything unenterable. `ViewManager` knows nothing about `Stage` or the win/lose `phase` — a decided stage's result screen is a full-screen overlay in the `system` layer above every view. `Docking` registers itself after construction (`setDocking`, the same late-injection shape `MapContextActions.setDocking` uses) because it is built later; `TouchControls` is a plain constructor argument, since nothing forces those two to be built in the other order. Rendering/viewpoint code checks `overviewMode`; input/behavior code checks `editMode`. `serializeView()` exposes `worldView` — the one piece of view state a snapshot saves — and it re-enters through the constructor's `requestedView` on the next launch, never through a method called on a live instance.
- `src/game/hud/frame-controls.ts` — `FrameControls`: the map's **座標系** panel, a cross-cut over two owners the same shape as `MapContextActions`/`ViewManager` — `MapCamera` (camera focus/rotation) and `DisplayWindowManager.frame` (the planned polyline's — and every other future display's — origin/rotation) each keep their own state, and this class only writes into them from one panel so the two don't need separate UIs. Two labelled sections, one per coordinate system, each split into the same center × rotation pair so the two read alike: `カメラ(視点)` (`基準にする天体` `AnchorZone` → this class's own `setFocus`, `視点を一緒に回す` `RotationZone` → `mapCamera.setCameraRotation`) and `軌道計画(描画基準)` (`基準にする天体`/`線を一緒に回す`, both writing `displayWindow.frame` via `ephemeris.frameOf(center, rotatingWith)` and keeping whichever half the other zone chose). A `カメラの基準に追随` `ToggleSwitch` under the plan section decides whether a focus move also drags the plan's center along, and a one-line summary under both states what is currently selected (`カメラ: 地球・慣性系 / 計画: 地球・月回転系`) — the panel's four controls answer four separate questions, so what they add up to has to be readable without recomposing them by eye. **`setFocus(target: FocusTarget)` is the sole entry point for moving the camera's focus, and every focus-changing UI in the game routes through it** — `Game.frameControls` is `public readonly`, and `MapContextActions`'s object-list row double-click/property-window "move focus" item call `frameControls.setFocus(...)` on the reference they hold rather than reaching `mapCamera.setFocusTarget` directly; `PlanEditor` holds this same instance as its own constructor argument (built right after `CameraSystem`, ahead of `PlanEditor` — see `game.ts` above) and calls `this.frameControls.setFocus(...)` directly for its node-focus menu action, rather than being handed a focus callback. It forwards to `mapCamera.setFocusTarget` unconditionally, then — only while the `カメラの基準に追随` toggle is on, and only when `target.kind === 'object'` and `target.id` is a body registered in `ephemeris.registry` — also re-centers `displayWindow.frame` on that same body, keeping `rotatingWith` as-is; an unregistered id (a self-flown ship, an enemy, a base, an ammo pickup) or a `'point'` focus leaves `frame` untouched, since `Ephemeris`'s frame resolution has no ECI origin to fall back to for a body it doesn't carry. The toggle exists because moving the focus is a *viewing* action while the plan frame decides the drawn line's shape: with it off, the two stay independent and the line never changes shape as a side effect of looking somewhere else. The coupling is one-way either way — re-centering the plan section never moves the camera focus back. Only the camera section's center zone offers "固定を解除" (`releaseLabel` — see `AnchorZone` below): releasing bakes the camera's current ECI position into a `camera/focus-target.ts` `FocusTarget` fixed in the star-centered inertial frame (`ephemeris.frameOf(starId, null)`, or `ephemeris.inertialFrame` with no star), via `focus-target.ts`'s `focusPoint`, using `mapCamera.resolvedFocus` and the simTime `sync` last saw (`lastTime` — `onSelect` fires from a DOM event outside the frame flow, so it can't take simTime as an argument). The plan section's center zone never offers a release: a drawn polyline is always baked into *some* coordinate system, so "fix to nothing" has no meaning there — fixing to solar-system space at all is what selecting the star itself in the pulldown already means. `Game` constructs it once and calls `sync(pickables, cameraPos, attractors, simTime, visible)` from `Game.sync`; it has no `update` of its own, since every zone's choices are re-derived fresh each `sync` from the current pickables/attractors rather than advanced over time.

  **Every way of editing Δv funnels through one method, `applyDv(axis, sign, amount)`**, which is the sole place `plan.applyNodeDv` is called from user input — so a drag, a key hold, a button hold, and a latched drag can't each compute their own rounding or sign convention, and a zero `amount` is a no-op (skipping the call, rather than letting `Plan.applyNodeDv`'s unconditional downstream-discard fire on a no-op edit). The three input paths differ only in how they arrive at `amount`: `applyAxisDrag` (short arm drags, below the latch threshold) scales pixel delta by the existing per-drag rate; `applyHeldDv` (WASDQE keys or a `dvButtons` press, checked together via `||` in `updateEditing`) accumulates a per-direction hold time in `dvHoldTime` (indexed `axis*2 + (sign<0?1:0)`) and looks it up through `rampedDvRate` — an exponential ramp from `DV_RATE_MIN` to `DV_RATE_MAX` over `DV_RATE_RAMP_SEC`, so a tap stays fine while holding sweeps up to coarse; a released direction resets its hold time to 0, so ramping always restarts from the beginning. The arm drag itself only free-drags (via `applyAxisDrag`) up to `NodeGizmo`'s own `DV_DRAG_LATCH_PX` — beyond that `NodeGizmo` stops reporting deltas and instead exposes `latch: AxisLatchState | null` (the axis/sign and the pixel excess past the threshold), which `updateEditing` reads every frame and feeds through `applyDv` at a rate proportional to the excess (capped at `DV_RATE_MAX`) — so a large, held drag keeps adding Δv even while the pointer stops moving, without the user needing to keep dragging further.
- `plan-display.ts` — `PlanDisplay`: the plan's trajectory display. `update(planData, displayWindow, attractorProvider, ship)` takes the operated `Vessel` directly rather than just its name — `ship?.name` still feeds the apsis icons' label prefix, but `ship` itself passes straight through to `plan-path.ts`'s `PlanPath.update`, which needs it to answer the plan's node-less trailing segment by borrowing the ship's own predicted arc rather than integrating a second one (see `plan-path.ts` above). It keeps **no copy of the `PlanData` it was updated with** — the only thing `sync` needs to know about it afterwards is how many nodes the drawn polyline has, and `PlanPath.nodeCount` (set by that same `update` call) already answers it, so a second field mirroring the count would only be a value that could go stale against the line actually drawn. What it does hold is the ⬢ `plannedPlayer` ghost marker showing where the plan puts the ship at `displayTime` (present or future alike, so it reads as "is the ship actually flying the plan" the instant the map closes — its label is `計画位置 高度 …` at or before `simTime`, `T+…h…m 高度 …` beyond it, altitude measured from whichever body pulls hardest at that position via `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`, not fixed to Earth), the ◇ Pe/Ap periapsis/apoapsis icons, the ルーラー calendar-tick marks along the polyline (below), so it disappears once `displayTime` runs past the trailing arc's one-revolution end. The apsis icons describe **the orbit the ship is on after its last planned burn**, found directly on the actually-drawn integration — not the analytic ellipse — because osculating `OrbitalElements` oscillate with the evaluation epoch under J2 (tens of km of periapsis/apoapsis wobble per revolution at LEO), which would otherwise make the icons visibly jump the instant a zero-Δv node changed which epoch they were evaluated at. `apsisIconsOf` reads `path.finalSegment()`'s `periapsis`/`apoapsis` (`PlanPath`'s exposure of the trailing segment's own arc's periapsis/apoapsis — see `plan-path.ts` above for how those are found by the radial-velocity sign crossing on the live integration step pair, not by scanning the drawn sample array) directly rather than deriving them itself, so a collision-course segment's `periapsis === null` simply shows no periapsis icon rather than a spurious one. The center both points are measured against is `FinalSegment.apsisCenter` — **the same body the crossing was detected against**, not one re-resolved at the extremum's own time: the icon's altitude reading has to be taken against the body the physics that found it used, or a segment whose strongest attractor changes partway through would print a distance measured from a body the detection never considered. Only that body's *position* is re-evaluated at the extremum's time (`ephemeris.positionOf(center.id, point.t)`), which is the one thing that genuinely moves. The circular guard compares `(apDist - peDist) / (apDist + peDist)` from those two precise center-distances against `APSIS_MIN_ECC`, hiding both icons below it since the apsis direction is then unreliable; a lone point (e.g. a hyperbolic orbit's periapsis with no apoapsis) is shown ungated, since the guard only makes sense once both are available to compare. `apsisTimeOf(id)` reads the found point's own `t` directly (no re-derivation), so `PlanEditor.addNodeAt` always places a node at a time that actually exists on the drawn line, and `apsisMarkers` exposes the currently-shown icons as `MapPickable`s (`kind: 'apsis'`) for that same right-click to hit. In the map view, `syncApsisMarkers` also hides an icon `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body from the camera's current position (`sync`'s `overviewMode`/`cameraPos` arguments) — combat view never gates on this, since a body can't occlude anything from the chase camera's close-in vantage the way it can on the map. The ruler-style calendar ticks are split the same way: `tickIconsOf(mode, simTime)` (update, its two arguments taken from the frame's `DisplayWindow.tickLabelMode`/`simTime` and used only for the label text) reads `PlanPath.timeRange()` and `hud/calendar-ticks.ts`'s `calendarBoundaries` to build the candidate set, tagging each with the coarsest calendar rank it satisfies (an ordinary midnight is a day tick, a month-start midnight is a month tick) rather than deciding the whole displayed span's unit at once — on an eccentric orbit the drawn samples bunch near periapsis and spread near apoapsis, so the screen-space density (and thus which unit reads as legible) varies along a single polyline, and a caller can't pick one interval for the whole thing without either flooding the dense end or leaving the sparse end blank. `syncTickMarkers` (sync) does the actual thinning, walking ranks coarsest-first and keeping a candidate only when it's at least `PLAN_TICK_MIN_PX` from the nearest already-kept tick on either side — a plain per-rank pass would let a fine-rank tick crowd out its own coarse-rank neighbor, so ties are broken toward keeping the coarser marks first. A tick's radius is drawn relative to whichever rank is actually the finest surviving one this frame (`PLAN_TICK_RADIUS_PX`, indexed by that offset rather than by the absolute rank), so the average tick size stays constant as the unit switches along the line and only the switch point itself reads as a size step; its label only shows past the wider `PLAN_TICK_LABEL_MIN_PX` threshold, since a legible tick spacing is still too dense for a label at every one. Each tick is drawn as an inline SVG dot (`tickSvg`, `mk-plantick`) — a dot carries no orientation, so it reads the same when the drawn orbit is viewed from any angle, unlike a line segment aligned to the trajectory's tangent. This is a distinct concern from `hud/tick-scale.ts`'s PREDICT-slider ticks: that module places ticks at even fractions of an *elapsed*-time span with a `T+` label, which has no notion of a UTC calendar boundary at all.
- `plan-path.ts` — `PlanPath`: splits the plan's corners into segments (anchor → node₁ → … → nodeₙ, plus a trailing one), and owns two parallel index-keyed pools — one `SegmentSource` record per segment (`{arc: PredictedArc | null; from; to; owned}`, `arc` the same unified `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts` `PredictedArc` `GameEntity`'s own prediction uses — see that entry above) — and a pooled `game/trajectory-line.ts` `TrajectoryLine` (grey before the first burn, white after it, orange beyond). `owned` is the whole distinction the two kinds of segment need: every segment but the sole trailing one of a node-less plan builds and keeps its own arc (`owned: true`), while that one segment instead borrows the operated ship's own arc outright (`owned: false`, `ship.predictedArc` — `GameEntity`'s own raw getter, not `ensurePredictedArc`, since `PlanPath` only ever reads it and never creates or grows it), so the ship's own free-fall trajectory is never integrated a second time. **Growing an owned arc is not this class's job at all** — `PlanPath` only ever constructs one (empty, no synchronous integration) and writes `requiredEnd`/`retainFrom` onto it; `growableArcs()` hands the owned arcs back out, in segment order, for `Predictor`'s budget pass to actually `step()` (via `PlanEditor.growableArcs()`, see `plan-editor.ts`/`predictor.ts` above) — which is what turns a freshly-placed or freshly-edited node from a same-frame synchronous integration into a line that visibly grows outward, a few steps a frame, from wherever the budget reaches it. The line pool is a different lifetime from the arc pool: a line is never discarded — only hidden — once built, since the color a line was constructed with (`arcColor(idx)`) has to stay tied to its screen position (pre-burn/post-burn/trailing) rather than to whichever segment happens to occupy that index this frame; `lineAt(i)` lazily grows the line pool but never shrinks it. `PlanPath` is also the **single place display-frame transforms and screen picking are done** (`toDisplay`/`toDisplayDir`/`projectPoint`/`nearestSample`) so the drawn polyline and click hit-testing can't drift apart. The two transforms are split by what kind of quantity is moved, mirroring `physics/frame.ts`'s own point/direction split: `toDisplay` is affine (a sample position), `toDisplayDir` is rotation-only (an orbital basis vector, e.g. the Δv gizmo's axes) — putting a direction through the point path would offset it by the display frame's own origin the moment that frame is centered on a moving body. `update(planData, ship, ephemeris, frame, currentTime, ...)` (`planData` being `plan.ts`'s `PlanData`, whose anchor is always settled — a caller that has already resolved "is there a plan to draw" passes the one value through instead of destructuring it) rebuilds the segments. The borrowed segment's record is rebuilt fresh every frame (`{arc: ship.predictedArc, from, to, owned: false}` — `null` for a frame the ship's own arc hasn't grown yet, in which case that segment simply answers nothing until the next frame), and it also calls `arc?.apsides?.dropBefore(seg.state0.t)` every frame, since the borrowed track spans the ship's whole prediction and this segment only wants what's still ahead of its own start. Every owned index instead asks its existing arc (if any) whether `represents(state0, end, sourceRevision)` holds for this frame's segment — if not, it builds a fresh (empty) `PredictedArc(state0, attractorProvider, planBcInv, planSrpCoeff, /* keplerTail */ false)` — plan-owned arcs always integrate with the operated vessel's own ballistic coefficient, taken at its orientation-averaged projected area since a plan spanning many revolutions cannot know the attitude they will be flown at, since the planner only ever plans the player's own orbit, and never carry a Kepler tail, since a node placed on an extrapolated stand-in would disagree with the next real re-integration of that span — and replaces the pooled entry outright; if so, the existing arc is kept as-is. Either way `requiredEnd`/`retainFrom` are (re)written to the segment's `state0.t`/`end` before the record is stored — a live segment's answered range tracks the display window's far edge with no growth of its own happening here. `sync(fo, project)` reads each owned arc's `trajectory`/`to` and pushes them through that index's own pooled line's `setDash`/`syncGeometry`/`syncTransform`/`sync`; a borrowed index instead only hides its line, since the operated ship's own `predictedLine` already draws that trajectory. **Two different "how far does the plan reach" values are exposed, and mixing them up is what made the plan rebuild every frame**: `timeRange()` is the drawn span, clipped to `[currentTime, currentTime + duration]` and therefore tracking `simTime` — `PlanDisplay`'s calendar ticks want exactly that; `plannedEnd` is the segment list's own terminus (`buildSegments`' last `end`), un-clipped and a pure function of the anchor/nodes/ephemeris/duration preset, which is what `simulation/future-attractors.ts`'s revision must fold (see that entry). The display context is split along the same line — the frame and un-bake time are settled in `update` (screen picking from a DOM pointer event has to work between frames), the `ProjectFn` arrives in `sync`; the `ProjectFn` is deliberately *not* captured at construction, since `CameraSystem.activeCameraProjection` returns a snapshot of whichever camera is active *now* (capturing it once would pin picking to the chase camera's startup viewpoint). `finalSegment()` returns the trailing segment as one `FinalSegment` value (`null` only before `update()` has ever run — a trailing segment always exists once it has, since `buildSegments` always appends one past the last node): `state0` (its start state), `samples` (that segment's own `[from, to]`-clipped read of its arc's `trajectory.samplesOldestFirst()`, memoized per index against `(that array's own reference, to)` in a private `samplesCache` so a read that needs no clipping keeps returning the exact same array reference — re-exposing it under a new frame-local array would defeat `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s reference-identity re-bake guard the moment a consumer draws it), `periapsis`/`apoapsis` (the same arc's `apsides?.periapsis`/`.apoapsis`, clipped through `arc-range.ts`'s `withinEnd`, each still `null` on its own when the segment is truncated before reaching that extremum), and `apsisCenter` (`arc.apsides?.center ?? null` — the body the arc's own `ApsisTrack` was centered on at its first finite step, so a consumer can state a distance without re-resolving a center that might disagree with the detection's own). `state0`/`samples` are what `PlanEditor` feeds the operated ship's own `marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts` `EquatorNodeMarkerPair` (see that module below), so it doesn't have to know how segments are split; `periapsis`/`apoapsis` are what `PlanDisplay`'s apsis icons read directly rather than deriving from `samples` themselves. Owned by `PlanDisplay`; `PlanEditor` shares the same instance (`planDisplay.path`) for its own screen picking. `sync` caches its caller-supplied camera position — `nearestSample` fires off a DOM pointer event, outside the frame flow — and drops any sample `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body before ranking candidates, so a node can't be placed on a stretch of the line that isn't actually visible. **How far ahead the plan is drawn is the PREDICT panel's call**: every segment ends at the next node's `t`, and the trailing one runs `plan.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom(state0, attractors, displayDuration)` from its own start state — the constructor takes a `DisplayDurationSource` (`plan.ts`'s narrow `{durationSec(referencePeriod): number}` interface, threaded from `Game`'s concrete `DisplayWindowManager` through `PlanEditor`/`PlanDisplay` — see `display-window-manager.ts` above) precisely so this length tracks the PREDICT panel's selection instead of a fixed span, with no import of `display-window-manager.ts` itself. The default `'orbit'` preset reproduces one orbital period around whichever body pulls hardest at that state (`attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`'s `OrbitalElements.period` — not `localOrbitPeriod`'s circular approximation, which would return the short period of the periapsis radius for a node freshly placed on an eccentric orbit); picking a longer preset draws further, and `Plan.nodeTimeRange` (the range a node may be dragged across) grows the same way since it calls the same `segmentDurationFrom`. Beyond one revolution the polyline overlaps itself, so a click can no longer be disambiguated by screen distance alone — see `nearestSample` below. `nearestSample`'s optional `TimeRange` is how a caller restricts picking to part of the polyline; `PlanEditor` passes `Plan.nodeTimeRange` there so a node drag can only ever land on a legal time. Its tie-break is two-stage: first the screen-nearest candidate decides *which segment wins* — segment 0 (pre-burn) always has smaller `t` than the post-burn segments, so comparing `t` across segments at the point where two segments' lines visually cross would silently pick the wrong one — then, restricted to that segment's candidates within `NEAREST_SAMPLE_TIE_PX` of the screen-nearest distance, it picks whichever is closest to the caller-supplied `referenceT` (ties broken toward the earlier `t`). That winning sample is a starting point, not the answer: `nearestSample` then narrows to the interval between its two neighboring samples (clamped to `range`, or collapsed to the sample itself at a range/list edge) and runs `physics/optimize.ts`'s `goldenSectionMin` against the screen distance to the segment's own interpolated curve there, so the returned point sits on the drawn curve — Hermite-interpolated between samples, the same as the line itself — rather than snapped to whichever recorded sample happened to be nearest; every point this can return already sits inside the segment's own integrated range, since the candidate list itself is the clipped `samples` — a node can never be dropped on a stretch the plan hasn't actually integrated yet. Both call sites in `plan-editor.ts` route through this one rule rather than branching: new-node placement (`handleMapClick`/`handleNodeRightClick`) passes `referenceT = -Infinity`, which — since every candidate is then equally "infinitely far" — degenerates to the earliest-`t` tie-break and reproduces "the first time the line reaches here"; a node drag (`dragNodeToNearestSample`) passes the node's own current time, so a click near where a later revolution's polyline overlaps an earlier one keeps the node on the revolution it was actually dragged from instead of snapping back to revolution 0. Every line is drawn at the same `PLAN_ARC_OPACITY`, so the only thing distinguishing the pre-burn segment's line from the post-burn ones is `SEGMENT_COLORS`. `sync` also converts the dash pattern from screen pixels to world metres before handing it to each line (`TrajectoryLine.setDash`): it takes the segment's middle sample, puts it through its own `toDisplay`, and asks the caller-supplied `ScaleFn` for the metres-per-pixel there, so the dashes keep the same on-screen spacing across the map's four orders of magnitude of zoom.

- `arc-range.ts` — the range-clipped read `plan-path.ts`'s `PlanPath` builds every per-segment answer from, restricting a `DynamicTrajectory` (an owned segment's own arc, or a borrowed segment's ship-owned one) to `[from, to]`. `EPOCH_EPS` (1e-6s) is the rounding slack that lets an integration's own tip — landing just short of a requested time — still read as exactly that time; `stateAt(trajectory, t, end)` answers `t`'s state off `trajectory`, folding the tip back in within `EPOCH_EPS`, `null` past `end` or outside the retained span; `withinEnd(t, end)` is the same tolerance for a caller that already has a `t` and only wants to know whether it survives the clip (an arc's own `impact`/`apsides` extrema); `clipSamplesTo(source, end)` trims a time-ascending sample array's trailing entries past `end`, returning `source` itself when nothing needs trimming — the reference-identity contract `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `TrajectoryLine` needs is `PlanPath`'s own to keep on top of, via its private per-segment `samplesCache` (below), not this module's.
- `node-gizmo.ts` — `NodeGizmo`: the maneuver-node DOM pointer-events layer — node handles + Δv axis arms + the node context menu (warp/delete). A plan concern; uses the shared `hud/context-menu.ts`. An axis-arm drag accumulates its total screen-space projection (`totalProj`) from pointerdown; once `|totalProj|` exceeds `DV_DRAG_LATCH_PX` the drag latches — `onAxisDrag` stops firing per-move deltas and the public `latch: AxisLatchState | null` field instead exposes the axis/sign and the pixel excess past the threshold, for `PlanEditor.updateEditing` to read and integrate every frame at its own rate (see `plan-editor.ts` above). `latch` is cleared and the latched flag reset on pointerup/pointercancel.
- `plan-gizmo-3d.ts` — `PlanGizmo3D`: `NodeGizmo`'s 3D counterpart — six world-space arrow meshes (PRO/RET blue, NRM/ANM green, OUT/IN red), one `THREE.Group` at `renderOrder = 999` so the gizmo always draws on top of the scene it's pointing into. It holds no pointer/drag logic of its own and never references `NodeGizmo`; the two stay decoupled the same way the DOM/3D split works everywhere else in `plan/` — `PlanEditor.syncGizmo` is what reads `NodeGizmo`'s `latch`/`activeAxis` state each frame and translates it into this class's own `setStretch(axis, sign, factor)` call, so a drag on the 2D arm and the matching 3D arrow's stretch are two independent pushes from one shared frame of truth rather than one gizmo driving the other. `setPositionAndRotation(pos, pro, nrm, radOut, scale)` places the whole group at the selected node and aligns its local axes (X=RAD, Y=PRO, Z=NRM) to the orbital basis at that node, with `scale` supplied by the caller from the current camera distance so the gizmo holds a constant apparent size regardless of zoom. `setVisible`/`setPositionAndRotation`/`setStretch` are plain setters pushing already-computed values into mesh transforms — sync-shaped by what they do, even though (unlike most of this codebase) they aren't named with the `sync` verb.

- `plan.ts` — `Plan`: the node list plus the `anchor` (the plan's starting state). **Both are plain `KinematicState`s: a node *is* its frozen post-burn absolute state, not a relative Δv** — the burn time is the state's own `t`, and Δv is the derived value (node `v` − arrival velocity). The node list is a single `KinematicState[]`, always kept in execution-time order by construction: `addNode(postState, from)` finds the insertion point (the count of existing nodes whose `t` is earlier), truncates from there, then pushes — so the added node always lands at the end and its returned index is stable. **The anchor and the node list are one value, `{anchor, nodes} | null`, and `null` is exactly "no nodes"** — a plan with no node has no start of its own, since that start is the live ship's own state, so `Plan` refuses to hold it. **That `null` never surfaces as an anchor a caller has to substitute for**: every way of reading the start takes the state to borrow when nothing is frozen (`anchorOr(fallback)`, the single statement of the rule, which `displayData(shipState)`/`addNode`/`nodeTimeRange` all route through), so no caller ever writes `?? ship.state` itself — `displayData` returns the folded-line material `PlanData`, whose `anchor` is therefore never `null`, and `nodeTimeRange` likewise always answers a range. The one method that does report the absence is `frozenData(): PlanData | null`, whose `null` means "there is no plan to persist" — the save path's own question, not a rule to re-implement. `addNode`'s `from` is what freezes the anchor, and only when there is no node yet; from then on prediction is a pure function of anchor + nodes and never reads `player` state. **`consumeNodesUpTo(t, actualState)` is the one way a node is ever consumed** (`PlanGuide.update` at `simTime - C.NODE_EXPIRE_GRACE`, `PlanExecutor.finish` at cutoff, `CreativeStage.applySimulationEvents` for an `'instant'` ship): it removes every node whose execution time is at or before `t`, returns how many it removed, and — **whenever any node is still queued behind it** — **re-anchors the plan on `actualState`** — where the ship actually ended up, never the node's ideal value. With nothing left queued the anchor goes with the nodes, since there is no longer a plan for it to start. A powered burn does not achieve its planned Δv exactly, and folding that residual away would make the divergence between plan and reality unreadable on screen; `'instant'` execution passes the consumed node itself, since a teleport has no error to keep. The remaining plan is still drawn from a valid start and the already-flown segment of `PlanPath` disappears on its own. `addNode` refuses a state at or before the anchor's own time (returning `-1` rather than inserting), since a node there would break the "a node is never earlier than the state it follows" invariant at the very first node — `PlanEditor` turns that `-1` into a hint instead of a silent no-op. Editing a node discards downstream nodes (`removeNode` trims from `idx`, and `replaceNode` — which `applyNodeDv` delegates to — trims everything past it), which is what keeps the module's one invariant true: **a node is never placed later than its predecessor's drawn segment reaches** (node *i*−1, or the anchor for node 0). `nodeTimeRange(idx, from, ephemeris, displayDuration)` is the single statement of that rule and `replaceNode`'s precondition — with it holding, node times are already ordered, so `replaceNode` neither sorts nor moves the node's index. `applyNodeDv` discards downstream nodes unconditionally, with no zero-Δv guard — a caller that wants to skip a no-op edit (`PlanEditor.applyDv`) checks for that itself before calling, rather than `Plan` special-casing a magnitude it has no reason to inspect. The module-level `orbitPeriodOf(state, attractors)` (`attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`'s `OrbitalElements.period`, around whichever body pulls hardest at `state` — never a plan-level setting — falling back to `APERIODIC_ARC_DURATION` when no finite period exists, e.g. hyperbolic orbits) feeds `segmentDurationFrom(state0, attractors, displayDuration)` — the shared definition of "how long a segment starting at this state may run," which `nodeTimeRange` and `plan-path.ts`'s `buildSegments` both call rather than each deriving it separately, so the legal node range and the drawn polyline can never disagree. `displayDuration` is typed as the narrow `DisplayDurationSource` interface (`{ durationSec(referencePeriod): number }`) rather than the concrete `DisplayWindowManager`, so this module — and the no-DOM `tsconfig.test.json` that compiles it directly for `test:physics` — never has to resolve `DisplayWindowManager`'s DOM-heavy dependency chain. No prediction cache lives here. `revision` is the plan's own edit counter — bumped by every call that actually changes the anchor or the node list (`addNode`/`removeNode`/`replaceNode`/`consumeNodesUpTo`/`clear`, each only on the path where it really did change something, and `applyNodeDv` only through the `replaceNode` it delegates to), and it is held outside the `{anchor, nodes}` value so that emptying and refilling the plan still leaves it monotonically increasing (a counter that reset would collide as a cache key); a nodeless plan's start following the live ship is not an edit and bumps nothing — the divergence check on the ship's own prediction (`GameEntity.discardPredictionIfDiverged`) is what judges that case instead. **Each `Vessel` owns its own `Plan`** (`readonly plan = new Plan()`), which is what lets a multi-ship stage like CREATIVE keep each ship's plan independent of the others.
- `plan-executor.ts`/`plan-executor-math.ts` — `PlanExecutor`: drives a `'powered'` ship's maneuver node through a state machine (idle → slew → armed → burn → trim → idle), owned one-per-`Vessel` (`readonly planExecutor: PlanExecutor`) the same way `plan: Plan` is. It receives the ship/HUD/sim-speed it acts on as `PlanExecutorShip`/`PlanExecutorHud`/`PlanExecutorSimSpeed` — narrow structural types covering only the members it actually reads/writes, **not** `Vessel`/`Hud`/`SimSpeedManager` themselves (the same reason `plan.ts` has `DisplayDurationSource` instead of importing `DisplayWindowManager`): `Vessel` drags in `three/webgpu` and DOM types transitively, and importing it — even `import type` — would still force the whole reachable graph through `tsc -p tsconfig.test.json`'s DOM-less, `three`-less compiler options, breaking `test:physics`'s ability to compile this file at all. `Vessel` itself is passed at every real call site without a cast, since it's a structural superset of `PlanExecutorShip`. `PlanExecutionMode` (`'off' | 'instant' | 'powered'`) is defined here, not on `Vessel`, for the same reason — `vessel/vessel.ts` re-exports it (`export type { PlanExecutionMode }`) rather than defining its own copy. **Node identity is tracked by reference (`targetNode: KinematicState | null`), never by `node.t`**: `Plan.applyNodeDv`/`replaceNode` replace a node with a new `KinematicState` at the *same* `t` (Δv is applied to the existing node, not appended as a new one), so comparing `t` alone would read a mid-burn Δv edit as "still the same node" and let the burn keep going in its old, now-stale direction. Every entry point (`update`/`applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime`) re-checks `node !== this.targetNode` — a stale reference stops `applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime` outright (they no-op until `update` has re-synced `targetNode` to the new node next frame), and `update` itself routes an identity change through the same `reset()`/`clearState()` every other "stop what's in flight" path uses, so `ship.thrust`/`ship.torque` can never survive across a node swap into the new node's (unrelated) burn.

  **`commandLinked` is the trailing argument of all three entry points (`update`/`applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime`), and it is "is a command reaching this vessel right now", not "is it in coverage"** — `CreativeStage` resolves it as `isOperable(ship, commNetwork)`, so a crewed ship answers `true` wherever it is and only an unmanned one depends on the network. While it is false the executor **never starts anything**: no alignment torque, no ignition (`applyIgnitionAndCutoff` returns before the `armed` branch) and `nextEventTime` answers `null` for an `armed` node, so `Simulator` doesn't subdivide toward an ignition that cannot happen. **A burn already running is finished regardless** (§13-6: a burn sequence is driven by an on-board clock and needs neither observation nor judgment), which is why the `burn`/`trim` branch is reached before the gate and why the cutoff check keeps running. A node whose execution time passes while the link is down is *not* consumed — it is remembered (`missedWhileDark`), and the first frame the link returns discards every node already in the past in one `consumeNodesUpTo`, hints how many were dropped, and resumes from the rest; the vessel is therefore on an unintended orbit rather than on a silently-shifted plan, which is the whole point of §13-6.

  **Attitude is held inertial-fixed, not chased**: while idle/slew/armed, the target forward is `norm(node.v − ship.state.v)`, re-derived every `update()` call so it tracks the moving target right up to ignition; at ignition it's frozen into `burnDirWorld` (and a matching `burnUpWorld`) and never recomputed for the rest of the burn — a real high-thrust/short-duration burn is closer to an impulsive shot than a continuously-corrected one. The up reference defaults to the ship's own radial-out `state.r`, but `plan-executor-math.ts`'s `burnUpReference` falls back to the orbit normal (`kinematic-state.ts`'s `orbitAxes(state).nrm`) whenever the burn direction is itself ~radial — `r` and the orbit normal are orthogonal by construction, so a burn direction can never be singular against both at once, which is what keeps a pure radial-in/out node from deadlocking `qFromForwardUp` (angle stuck at 180°, torque stuck at zero, `slew` never exits) the way using `r` alone would. `updateBurnOutput` (the `burn`/`trim` half of `update`) keeps torquing toward the *frozen* `burnDirWorld`/`burnUpWorld` for the whole burn — attitude authority is never silently dropped mid-burn — separately from whether thrust is currently flowing.
