A zero-length `dir` means "the orientation isn't known" and falls through to `meanProjectedArea` — the orientation-averaged value `(A_x+A_y+A_z)/2`, since the mean of `|d̂·x̂|` over the unit sphere is ½ — which is what `radiationPressureCoeff` (`C_R·A/m`, `RADIATION_PRESSURE_COEFF` = 1.3) always uses, since the sun direction is not reachable where the coefficient is resolved, and what a maneuver plan's own arcs use, since a plan spanning many revolutions cannot know the attitude those revolutions will be flown at. Pure, THREE/DOM-independent.
- `src/game/hud/` — the DOM-overlay HUD, split by concern: `hud.ts` (`Hud` — just the shell: `root`/`layers`/`svgOverlay`/`overlayManager`/`helpPanel` for others to attach to or read, toast/hint/help, and the six always-on panel classes (`vesselPanel`/`orbitPanel`/`targetPanel`/`enemiesPanel`/`simulationStatusBar`/`mapScaleBadge`, built from `hud-root.ts`'s `els` map — see `vessel-panel.ts` et al. below), each a public readonly field so `Game.sync` calls its `sync` directly), `overlay-layer.ts` (`buildOverlayLayers`/`bringToFront` — **the single definition of the HUD's stacking order, and the only module allowed to assign a `z-index`.** Eight fixed `<div>`s are appended to `#hud` once, back to front: `marker` (world-space markers + the SVG overlay) / `panel` (the always-on docked/status/navball/plan/creative panels) / `window` (`PropertyWindow`) / `popup` (`ContextMenu`/`ObjectPicker` popups) / `gate` (`#hud-overlay-shield`, the input-gating scrim — sits above those four so a gated modal blocks the game world/panels/windows/popups beneath it, but below `view`/`system` so a gating modal itself is never blocked by its own scrim, wherever it lives) / `view` (`ResourceTransferDialog`, the one full-screen dialog that must cover the panels/windows beneath it) / `notify` (toast/hint/the view badge) / `system` (help/end-screen/pause-menu/save-browser) — `z-index: 10` through `17` in that order. Every other DOM owner picks which layer it's a child of (via a constructor argument threaded down from `Hud.layers`, e.g. `panelRoot`/`popupRoot`) and never sets a `z-index` of its own; ordering *within* a layer is plain DOM append order, and `bringToFront(el)` — re-`appendChild`ing `el` to its own parent — is the sole way to move something to the front of its layer. A popup that opens from inside another popup (an `ObjectPicker` inside a `PropertyWindow`, a `ContextMenu` inside a placement panel) calls `bringToFront` on itself at open time, so "opened from X draws above X" falls out of the layer split alone, with no per-widget z-index bookkeeping that could get the ordering wrong), `overlay-manager.ts` (`OverlayManager` — the single registry every overlay (modal/popup/window) joins, and the sole source of the *logical* stacking order among them (the DOM order within a layer is still `overlay-layer.ts`'s). Registration declares `kind` (`'modal' | 'popup' | 'window'`), `closeOnEscape`, `closeOnOutsideClick`, `gatesInput`, and an optional `exclusiveGroup` (opening a member closes any other open member of the same group — `'system-modal'` holds `help`/`pause-menu`/`save-browser`; `map-context-actions.ts`'s unclipped `PropertyWindow`s share their own `'property-window-temp'` group so at most one stays unclipped at a time). It owns **no** `window`/`document` listener of its own for ESC or for item shortcuts: `Game.handleInput` takes `[Esc]` off `Input`'s edge queue and calls `closeTopmostOnEscape()`, which closes the highest still-open `closeOnEscape` entry, falling back to opening the pause menu only when nothing closed; the same call also runs `Input.takeKeys` over the frame's remaining pressed codes and hands each to `dispatchShortcut(code)`, `closeTopmostOnEscape`'s sibling — top-down through the stack, calling `handle.handleShortcut?.(code)` on each entry and stopping at the first one that returns `true` (an entry with no `handleShortcut`, or whose own items don't match, is transparent and falls through to the one below — this is what lets a clipped `PropertyWindow` sitting above an unclipped one in the stack still let the unclipped one's shortcuts reach it), returning `false` while `document.activeElement` is a text input so a keystroke typed into a `ValueInput` field can never double as a shortcut. It owns the **one** capture-phase `document` `pointerdown` listener for outside-click closing, iterating every `closeOnOutsideClick` entry and closing any whose `handle.contains(target)` is false — several independently-registered overlays can each close on the same click if it lands outside all of them. `gatesInput` (true only for full-screen modals — `help`/`save-browser`/`result`/`settings`/the resource-transfer dialog; `pause-menu` leaves it false so background camera/map input still passes while paused) drives the `#hud-overlay-shield`'s `pointer-events`; **`isInputGated(): boolean`** is the same predicate exposed for a caller that needs to ask "is *some* gating overlay open" without naming one — `Game.handleMapPointerInput` is its sole caller, gating the map-edit/combat-targeting pointer dispatch generically rather than against one hardcoded id; **any** `kind: 'modal'` entry being open (`gatesInput` or not) drives the `body.hud-overlay-modal-open` class (hides `#touch-ui`) and fires `tepui-release-touch-inputs` — but only on that flag's own false→true transition, not on every registration change, which is what stops a second modal opening (or one of two closing) from spuriously re-releasing already-held touches. Every overlay-owning class (`ContextMenu`/`ObjectPicker`/`PropertyWindow`/`PauseMenu`/`SaveBrowser`/`ObjectPlacerPanel`/`HelpPanel`, plus `ResultScreen`'s own handle and `DraggableWindow`, which registers on behalf of every window composed from it) implements `OverlayHandle` (`contains`/`close`, plus an optional `handleShortcut` on the three that carry item shortcuts — `ContextMenu`/`PropertyWindow`/`ObjectPlacerPanel`) and calls `open`/`close`/`reconfigure` on itself — `OverlayManager` never reaches into a caller to decide how it closes. `help-panel.ts` (`HelpPanel` — owns the `[H]`-toggled help panel's open/closed state, its `OverlayManager` registration (`kind: 'modal'`, `exclusiveGroup: 'system-modal'`), and its own `#hud-help` DOM (`buildHelpTableHtml()`, built once in the constructor) — the operation-reference table's touch-gesture rows (長押し=右クリック相当/二本指ドラッグ=パン/ピンチ=ズーム/ダブルタップ=フォーカス移動) sit first, ahead of the keyboard rows, so a touch player reaches every mapped gesture from the same table without a second one), `hud-root.ts` (`buildHudDom` — static DOM/style construction for the panels it builds directly (SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS, the global status bar, chase-reset badge); the help panel and result screen build their own DOM instead (`HelpPanel`/`ResultScreen`, below) — `buildHudDom` only instantiates/appends them. Calls `buildOverlayLayers` once and is the one place that decides, per element it builds, which layer it becomes a child of, and calls `viewport.ts`'s `startViewportTracking()` once. Anything painted at HUD level must be given a layer, because markers are created at runtime and would otherwise land on top of everything by DOM order. `input/touch.ts`'s virtual pad sits below `#hud` entirely for the same reason — it drives the game world, so it must not cover a system window. `breakpoints.ts` is the sole definition of the responsive vocabulary the other three files (and `input/touch.ts`, `hud/base-operations-window.ts`, `hud/save-browser.ts`, `hud/object-picker.ts`, `hud/property-window.ts`, `stage-select.ts`) all reference rather than re-deriving: three named width classes — `compact` (<700px, `MQ_COMPACT`), `medium` (700–1100px), `wide` (>1100px) — plus two orthogonal axes, `coarse` (`pointer:coarse`, `MQ_COARSE`) and `short` (<500px tall, `MQ_SHORT`, deliberately not paired with `orientation:landscape` — a short viewport gets the short-height treatment whichever way it's held). `MQ_MEDIUM_DOWN` (`(max-width:1100px), (pointer:coarse)`) is what most "shrink for mobile" rules key on — coarse forces the medium-or-narrower treatment even on a wide touch display — and `MQ_COARSE_SHORT` is the compound most relevant to a phone held sideways. `isCompactViewport()` re-evaluates `MQ_COMPACT` on each call (viewport width changes far more often over a session than pointer coarseness does, unlike `pointer-precision.ts`'s once-at-startup `isCoarsePointer`) and is read at the discrete moments a layout decision is made once — `hud-root.ts` picking a panel/rail's default collapse state, `object-picker.ts`/`property-window.ts` deciding whether to anchor near a trigger or open as a bottom sheet — never per frame. Its own `STYLE` is the concatenation of five place-specific files (`WIDGET_STYLE` is not one of them — it joins separately, only at injection time, below), each importing `breakpoints.ts`'s query strings rather than spelling out a threshold — `layout-tokens.ts` (`LAYOUT_TOKENS_STYLE`, the `--rail-w-left`/`--rail-w-right`/`--combat-panel-max-h` custom properties, defined once at `:root` and reassigned per breakpoint in self-contained `@media` blocks so a rail width or the combat panels' height cap is written in exactly one place per breakpoint; every consumer reads `var(--rail-w-left)` etc. rather than repeating the underlying `min(...)`/`calc(...)` expression), `skeleton-style.ts` (`SKELETON_STYLE`, concatenating `overlay-layer.ts`'s `OVERLAY_LAYER_STYLE` — the four置き場 that aren't a caller-built panel: `#hud` root/reset, the marker layer's z-index and glyph appearance, the shared `.panel`/`.panel-shell-*`/`.row` shell every panel builds on, the left/right rails (`.hud-rail`, width from the rail vars; `#hud .hud-map-root.active .hud-rail` alone gets `pointer-events:auto; touch-action:pan-y` so a player can grab the rail's empty space to scroll it, without an empty rail swallowing camera-drag input outside map mode) — `buildWorldRoot` mints one left/right rail pair per world root, so the combat root's own pair (populated by `buildInfoPanels` with SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS) is a separate DOM instance from the map root's, each foldable independently through its own `rail-toggle` — and the screen-fixed badges (view badge/global status/map scale/chase-reset/hint/toast), `panel-content-style.ts` (`PANEL_CONTENT_STYLE`, every caller-specific panel's own content: the four info-panel rows, the object list, 軌道計画/表示設定/表示時刻/座標系/艦艇配置/ナビボール/ステージステータス/設定・ヘルプ・終了画面), `combat-view-style.ts` (`COMBAT_VIEW_STYLE`, `.hud-combat-root.active`-scoped — the SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS panels' and the global-status-bar/chase-reset badge's Quiet Glass background/blur/radius, with every saturated color confined to the small areas marking the primary target, proximity to it, the secondary target, or danger; also the no-`backdrop-filter` fallback and the `MQ_MEDIUM_DOWN`/`MQ_COMPACT`/`MQ_COARSE`/`prefers-reduced-motion` responsive rules), and `map-view-style.ts` (`MAP_VIEW_STYLE`, `.hud-map-root.active`-scoped — hides the combat-only `#hud-target`, gives the always-on object-list/view-options panels a Quiet Glass and the decision-time PREDICT/frame-controls/plan panels a denser Focus Glass, and holds every map-only semantic color (the object list's selected/related-orbit rows, the view-options body-class on-state dot, PREDICT's future-vs-past accent split) plus its own `MQ_MEDIUM_DOWN`/`MQ_COMPACT` rules). `#hud .bow-*`/`#save-browser`'s own CSS lives in `base-operations-window.ts`/`save-browser.ts` themselves, each injected once via its own module-local `ensureStyle()`, not in this file. `hud/panel-shell.ts`'s `PanelShell` (see below) is what `buildInfoPanels` wraps SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS in, giving each an individual collapse toggle on top of the two-tier rail-collapse that already existed; a panel's programmatic show/hide (as opposed to the player's own collapse choice) is the `.hidden` class (`PanelShell.setHidden`/the four info panels' own `classList.toggle('hidden', …)`), never `style.display` — the `#hud .hud-rail > .panel[style*="display: none"]` inline-style-matching selector this replaced is gone. `hud/widgets/collapse-toggle.ts`'s `buildCollapseToggle(root, id, className, target, labels)`, re-exported here for `hud-root.ts`'s own callers, is the one implementation of "a button that toggles `target`'s `.collapsed` class and keeps its own glyph/`aria-expanded`/`title` in sync with it," parameterized by a `CollapseToggleLabels` (glyph/title pairs) so a caller only supplies which labels it wants — `buildRailToggle` builds the left/right map-panel rail toggles this way, and `predict-panel.ts`'s `PredictPanel` builds the PREDICT bar's own fold toggle and `hud/object-list-panel.ts`'s `ObjectListPanel` its own, both the same way. Nothing resets these: a collapsed panel stays collapsed until the player reopens it. `COLLAPSE_EXPANDED_GLYPH` (`▾`) / `COLLAPSE_COLLAPSED_GLYPH` (`▸`) are exported from here so every vertical fold in the HUD — rail toggles, the PREDICT bar, the object list, `PanelShell`'s own toggle, `PropertyWindow`'s row groups and its 詳細 toggle — points the same way for the same state; the map's own markers deliberately draw from a different glyph family (`marker/marker-glyphs.ts`) so a fold arrow can never be misread as a direction marker), `panel-shell.ts` (`PanelShell` — the common "見出し+`CollapseToggle`+本文" outer shell every individually-collapsible panel builds on: `el` (the `.panel.panel-shell` root, still carrying the plain `.panel` class so every existing `.panel`-scoped rule keeps matching), `titleEl` (the `<h3>`, exposed publicly so a caller can append embedded markup — CONTACTS' count badge, TARGET's `data-id`), and `body` (where the caller's own content goes; `.collapsed` folds it). Collapse state is per-panel-id and persists to `localStorage` under `tepui.panelCollapsed` via the exported `loadPanelCollapsed`/`savePanelCollapsed` (a stored value always wins; with none, the constructor's optional trailing `defaultCollapsed` argument decides the very first render), independent of the pre-existing rail-level `.hud-rail.collapsed`, whose hiding effect is scoped to `#hud.map-mode` (`#hud.map-mode .hud-rail.collapsed > .panel`) since the right rail also carries `TARGET`, a combat-view-permanent panel a map-session collapse must never be able to hide — the two nest (a whole rail can be folded, and a panel within it can independently be folded too), but share the *same* persisted store: `hud-root.ts`'s `buildRailToggle` reads/writes a rail's own `hud-rail-${side}` entry through the same two functions rather than inventing a second mechanism, so a rail's fold survives a reload exactly like a `PanelShell`'s does. compact 縦の既定表示(戦闘ビューの ORBIT/CONTACTS、マップビューの左右レール — PREDICT バー自体は畳まない)はどちらも `defaultCollapsed`/レールの既定値が `breakpoints.ts`'s `isCompactViewport()` を `buildHudDom()` 構築時に一度だけ読んで決まる — 初回起動が compact なら畳んだ状態で始まり、以後どちらの幅でも一度でも手で開閉すればその状態が永続する。 `setHidden(hidden)` is the *other* axis — a `.hidden` class for state the game decides (has a target, is in map mode, …), never confused with the player's own collapse choice), `viewport.ts` (`startViewportTracking`/`onViewportChange` — the sole subscriber of `visualViewport`'s `resize`/`scroll` events (falling back to `window` where `visualViewport` doesn't exist), called once from `buildHudDom`. Each event recomputes `--vvh` (the visual viewport's own height, distinct from `dvh` under an on-screen keyboard or pinch-zoom) and `--safe-*` (`max()`'d against the static `env(safe-area-inset-*)` `theme.ts` already injects, so a transient visual-viewport inset — keyboard, pinch-zoom pan — is honored without discarding the notch/home-indicator floor). `onViewportChange(listener)` is what every other module that used to hold its own `window` `resize` listener (`property-window.ts`'s reclamp, `context-menu.ts`'s reposition) subscribes to instead — `render/scene.ts`'s renderer resize is the one exception, kept as its own `window` listener since it's a renderer concern, not a layout one), `vessel-panel.ts` (`VesselPanel`), `orbit-panel.ts` (`OrbitPanel`), `target-panel.ts` (`TargetPanel`), `enemies-panel.ts` (`EnemiesPanel`), `simulation-status-bar.ts` (`SimulationStatusBar`), `map-scale-badge.ts` (`MapScaleBadge`) — the six always-on info panels, one sync class per panel rather than one god object: each takes the `Game` instance directly (cherry-picking from the whole game state rather than a narrow ctx) and the shared `els: Map<string, HTMLElement>` `hud-root.ts`'s `buildHudDom` already built the panel's DOM into, and is **display-only** — it writes its own panel and touches no other module's state or DOM. Each self-throttles its own row-value refresh to a `SYNC_INTERVAL_MS` (100ms for `VesselPanel`/`OrbitPanel`/`TargetPanel`/`SimulationStatusBar`, 250ms for `EnemiesPanel`), reading `performance.now()` against a private `nextSyncAt` rather than depending on a shared cadence, while the panel's own visibility (`.hidden`) is still checked every frame regardless — a panel that should disappear this frame must not wait out a stale throttle window. `VesselPanel` (`#hud-status`: RCS制動/並進出力/微調整/進行方向ホールド/視点のRCS追従/弾薬 — plus two placeholder rows `hud-root.ts` leaves for it, `status-actions`/`status-throttle-touch`, which its own constructor fills with `Button`s/a throttle `SegmentedControl` built against `Input.tapKey`, the same synthetic-press mechanism the touch pad uses: R/F/G/T (reload/prograde-reset/follow-attitude-toggle/target-select) are always shown, while the スロットル 1-4 control is shown only while `document.body` carries `touch-ui-active` — the class `input/touch.ts`'s `TouchControls.setPointerKind` already toggles, read here through CSS alone rather than a second "is touch active" query. `Input` isn't available at construction (`Hud` predates `Game`), so `setInput(input)` is a late-injection setter `Game`'s constructor calls once these buttons' `onClick`s can actually reach) and `OrbitPanel` (`#hud-orbit`: 自艦の基準天体/高度/速度/AP/PE/INC/PRD/動圧/機体温度, deriving each row via `orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo` and only formatting/writing them) both hide outright with no ship and fold in map view — an object's orbital elements are read through its own `property-window.ts` window's `'軌道'` group there instead, not duplicated in these two. `TargetPanel` (`#hud-target`) shows only name/装甲/距離/接近速度/相対速度 for the locked combat target (`orbit-info.ts`'s `relativeInfo`) plus a trailing hint row pointing at the right-click property window — orbital elements and relative inclination are **not** shown here, so a target's full orbit reads from exactly one place (the property window), not two competing ones. `EnemiesPanel` (`#hud-enemies`) lists living enemies distance-sorted, collapsing every enemy sharing a `waveId` (`vessel/vessel.ts`'s `Vessel.waveId`, stage00's wave spawns only) into a single `第N波 ×count` row at that wave's nearest member's distance, so a large stage00 wave doesn't flood the list with one row per ship, and also owns the title's `count` badge (spawned/killed). `SimulationStatusBar` (`#hud-globalstatus`: MET/時間加速/NODE WARP) is independent of whether a ship exists and always syncs. `MapScaleBadge` (`#hud-map-scale`) is a thin DOM-writing wrapper around the pre-existing pure functions in `map-scale.ts` (`mapScaleFor`/`formatMapScaleDistance`, still DOM-free so `tests/physics/map-scale.test.ts` can compile it under the no-DOM `tsconfig.test.json`), deriving the ruler from the meters-per-pixel at the camera's current focus depth rather than screen center, so panning without zooming leaves the reading unchanged as long as the same body stays focused. `Hud` (`hud.ts`, above) owns all six as public readonly fields, constructed in its own constructor from the `els` map `buildHudDom` returns; `Game.sync` calls each one's `sync` directly (`hud.vesselPanel.sync(this)`/`hud.orbitPanel.sync(this, attractors)`/`hud.targetPanel.sync(this, attractors)`/`hud.enemiesPanel.sync(this)`/`hud.simulationStatusBar.sync(this)`/`hud.mapScaleBadge.sync(this)`)), `orbit-info.ts` (`orbitInfo(entity, bodies)`/`relativeInfo(self, other, bodies)` — the DOM-free derivation of an entity's resolved center/altitude/speed/apsides/inclination/period, and of one entity's distance/closing-speed/relative-speed/relative-inclination against another; both route the center resolution through `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor` and the shape through `elements.ts`'s `apsisAltitudes`, so `OrbitPanel`/`TargetPanel`/`MapContextActions`'s row builders and any other consumer needing the same numbers share one implementation rather than rederiving them), `widgets/` (`button.ts`/`toggle-switch.ts`/`segmented-control.ts`/`hold-button.ts`/`close-button.ts`/`value-input.ts`/`meter.ts`/`tab-bar.ts`/`collapse-toggle.ts`/`slider.ts`, one widget per file, plus `widget-base.ts`/`widget-style.ts`/`index.ts` — the HUD's one operable-DOM vocabulary; every pressable/toggleable/typeable element in the HUD is one of these, not a hand-rolled `<button>`/`<input>`. `Button` (単発クリック; `on`/`disabled` の表示は呼び出し側が `setOn`/`setEnabled` で与え、自分では反転しない — 点灯型トグルはこの `setOn` を外から呼ぶ形で表現し、別ウィジェットを持たない), `ToggleSwitch` (見出し+トラック/ノブ型の ON/OFF; `setOn` は表示のみで `onChange` は呼ばない), `SegmentedControl<T>` (3択以上の排他選択専用 — 2択の ON/OFF には使わない; `setItems` は内容が同一なら作り直さない、押しかけのボタンでクリックを落とさないため), `HoldButton` (押している間 `isHeld` が真になり、呼び出し側が毎フレーム読む), `CloseButton` (✕), `ValueInput` (数値/文字/検索入力の唯一の実装 — Enter・blur=確定、Escape=破棄; `escapeBehavior: 'revert'`(既定、確定値へ戻す)/`'clear'`(検索欄限定、空にする)を型で明示させる), `Meter` (HP/温度/電力バー; `setRatio(0..1)` は常に左から右へ満ち、`setDanger` が `--danger` 色へ切り替える), `TabBar<T>` (パネルの表示面を切り替えるタブ列), `buildCollapseToggle` (縦方向の開閉トグル)。状態語彙は `.on`(点灯)/`.pressed`(押下中、タッチと `:active` の差を吸収するため JS で付与)/`.disabled` に統一し、`widget-style.ts` の `WIDGET_STYLE`(`hud-root.ts` の `STYLE` へ連結して注入)がこの3状態の見た目を一箇所で定義する — 個別ウィジェットや呼び出し側で上書きしない。セレクタは `#hud` に閉じない(`.w-btn` であって `#hud .w-btn` ではない)— `#hud` の外(`stage-select.ts`・`input/touch.ts` の `#touch-ui`)でもウィジェットを組めるようにするためで、他ファイルの `#何か span.クラス名` 形の上書きは元から ID を持つぶん `.w-btn` より詳細度で確実に勝つ。`box-sizing: border-box` を要る要素(`.w-close`/`.w-toggle-track` — 固定寸法と border/padding を両方持つ)はここで自前に持ち、`#hud, #hud * { box-sizing: border-box; … }` の一括リセットに依存しない。`widget-base.ts` の `stopDragPropagation`(カメラドラッグへの伝播を止める)と `expandHitTarget`(`pointer:coarse` のときだけ要素自身の寸法で `--hit-target-min` まで広げる)は全対話要素が経由する共通の下地。`widgets/` (`Button`/`ToggleSwitch`/`SegmentedControl`/`HoldButton`/`CloseButton`/`ValueInput`/`Meter`/`TabBar`/`buildCollapseToggle`/`Slider`、1ウィジェット1ファイル、plus `widget-base.ts`/`widget-style.ts` — HUD 内のあらゆる押す/切り替える/択一/長押し/閉じる/文字数値検索入力/バー/タブ/折りたたみ/スライダーの唯一の実装。フォルダの外で組む生 `<button>`/`<input>` は、切り出し漏れのウィジェットと見なす。`SegmentedControl.setItems` は見出しを保ったまま選択肢だけを差し替える(内容が前回と同じなら何もしない — 毎フレーム同じ選択肢を渡す呼び出し側で、押しかけのボタンを消さないため)。姉妹の `TabBar` は値でなくパネルの表示面そのものを切り替える用途向けで、`role="tab"`/`aria-selected` を持つ。`HoldButton.isHeld` は毎フレーム呼び出し側が読む(`setInterval` に頼らない、他の継続入力と同じ毎フレーム参照の流儀)。`ValueInput` は文字/数値/検索入力の唯一の実装 — Enter・blur で確定、Escape は確定済みの値へ破棄(`escapeBehavior: 'clear'` は空にする、検索欄限定)、数値欄で非有限な確定は破棄扱い。コンストラクタ第3引数の任意 `onCancel` は「確定」と「破棄」を呼び出し側が区別できるようにするためのもの(`predict-panel.ts` の `DurationValueInput` がこれで内側の編集UIの開閉を判断する)。`Button`/`CloseButton`/`ToggleSwitch` はクリックで自分の `on` を反転しない — クリックは呼び出し側の `onClick` を呼ぶだけで、状態(既に持っているか、`setBodyClassToggles` のように毎フレーム鏡映しに保つ必要があるか)を持つ呼び出し側が `setOn` を呼び返す。ある画面の「on の意味」を決める場所は常に1箇所になる。`widget-style.ts` は hover・pressed(`:active` でなく JS が付ける `.pressed` クラス)・on・disabled の視覚規約を定義する唯一の場所 — ウィジェット側にも呼び出し側にも上書きは禁止で、レイアウト(位置・幅・余白)だけを別クラスで足す。`widget-base.ts` の `stopDragPropagation`/`expandHitTarget` は、ウィジェットのクリックがカメラドラッグを起動しないための pointerdown 停止と、`--hit-target-min` までのタップ領域拡張を、各ウィジェットが構築時に一度だけ適用する共通処理。Anything here that takes a click needs `pointer-events: auto` in `hud-root.ts`'s STYLE, since `#hud` itself is `pointer-events: none`, **`pointer-events` is an inherited property**, and a click that falls through reaches the canvas and fires the gun. The same reset (`#hud, #hud * { margin: 0; padding: 0 }`) outranks bare class selectors by ID specificity, so any rule that sets margin/padding/width on a HUD child must be scoped by an id — see the `#hud .bow-… ` rules), `frame-labels.ts` (`celestialBodyName(id)` — the `AttractorId` → display-name lookup, reading `celestial-registry.ts`'s `CELESTIAL_BODIES` for a real solar-system body and falling back to the bare id for one a custom registry invented, shared by `OrbitPanel`/`TargetPanel`'s center-body rows, `camera/focus-markers.ts`'s celestial labels, `hud/object-picker.ts`'s rows and `creative/object-placer-panel.ts`'s reference-body control, so no caller spells out "地球"/"月"/"太陽" itself), `object-picker.ts` (`ObjectPicker<T>` — the trigger button + popup used wherever the choices number in the dozens: a filter input, then the rest grouped and laid out as a multi-column grid (`.op-grid`, `repeat(auto-fill, minmax(140px, 1fr))`) so a hundred-item candidate set doesn't need a page-length scroll. `setGroups` skips the rebuild when the incoming groups are contentwise identical to what's already shown (label + each item's `[value, label]` pair, value by reference) — callers such as `AnchorZone`/`ObjectPlacerPanel` pass the same groups every frame, and an unconditional rebuild would fight an open popup's own filter/hover state. **`SegmentedControl` stays for anything with a handful of fixed choices** — it lays its items out in one flex row with no wrap/scroll/truncate handling, which is exactly why a registry-sized list needs a different widget rather than a wider row. Registers itself with `hud/overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager` on open/close (`kind: 'popup'`, `closeOnEscape: true`, `closeOnOutsideClick: true`) — the same registration `ContextMenu` makes, so both close through the manager's one capture-phase outside-pointerdown listener rather than a listener of their own; the popup is a child of the `popupRoot` its constructor is given — always the `overlay-layer.ts` popup layer — and calls `bringToFront` on itself when opened. Under `MQ_COMPACT` the popup is a bottom sheet (CSS `right:0; bottom:0; width:100%`) rather than anchored under the trigger — `open()` checks `isCompactViewport()` and skips the anchor-clamp math entirely there (clearing any `left`/`top` a prior non-compact open left behind, since an inline style would otherwise override the sheet's `right`/`bottom`)), `object-groups.ts` (`groupPickables(registry, items)` — the pure `MapPickable[]` → ジャンル別 `ObjectPickerGroup<string>[]` classifier `AnchorZone`'s object-picker builds on: `'body'` items split into ラグランジュ点(id が `-l1`〜`-l5` で終わるもの、`LAGRANGE_ID`/`lagrangeParentId` もここに置く)か `body-class.ts`'s `bodyClassOf` の5分類、`'player'`/`'ship'`/`'base'`/`'ammo'` はそのままジャンル、マーカー由来の kind(`apsis`/`relnode`/`eqnode`/`empty-space`)は出さない — 空のグループは返さない), `anchor-zone.ts` (`AnchorZone` — マップの座標系UIのうち「何に固定/追随するか」を選ばせる1ゾーン: 上段は登録天体・自艦・敵・基地・弾薬まで含む全候補から選ぶ `ObjectPicker`、下段は呼び出し側(`FrameControls`)が `celestial/body-visibility.ts`'s `systemMembersAt` で絞った天体列を `setNearby(members, pickables)` で受け取って並べるだけのクイック `SegmentedControl` — 押せてから拒否されることのないよう、`pickables` に無い id はクイックボタンに出さない。コンストラクタの `releaseLabel` が `null` なら「固定を解除」の選択肢自体を出さない(並進ゾーンの用途)。値は `string | null` — `null` が解除で、`onSelect` はどちらのゾーンでも同じ値を受け取る)、`rotation-zone.ts` (`RotationZone` — 同じUIのうち「何の公転に合わせて回すか」を選ばせるゾーン。`setNearby(members)` で受け取った天体列のうち `primaryOf` が非 `null` を返すもの(恒星以外)だけを `OrbitingId | null` の `SegmentedControl` として並べる — 恒星の除外はこのクラス自身の判断で、渡ってくる `members` 自体は恒星を含む。原点込みの座標系そのものは呼び出し側が `Ephemeris.frameOf` で組む — このクラスは回転対象の id だけを返す), `pause-menu.ts` (`PauseMenu` — the `[Esc]` pause menu, a single pane with no tabs: BGM volume/mute, スナップショット/負荷を表示/設定ビューを開く buttons, and the quit-to-title button. It owns no drawing-quality state of its own — that whole concern (the former 描画 tab's `GraphicsPanel`) lives in `settings-view.ts`'s `SettingsView` instead, reached through its own 設定ビューを開く button. `main.ts` owns the instance and wires its `onOpenSnapshots` to close itself then `SaveBrowser.open()`, its `onOpenPerfWindow` to close itself then `PerfMeter.open()`, its `onBgmVolumeChange` to `Bgm.setVolume`, its `onQuitToTitle` to `launcher.returnToTitle()`, its `onOpenSettings` to close itself then `SettingsView.toggle(true)`, and its `onPauseMenuOpenChange` to `launcher.current?.pause()`/`.resume()`. It owns no `handleInput` — ESC is `Game.handleInput`'s sole business (see `game.ts` above): `toggle` registers itself with `overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager` under id `'pause-menu'` (`kind: 'modal'`, `gatesInput: false` so background map/camera input still passes while paused, `exclusiveGroup: 'system-modal'` so opening `SaveBrowser`/the help panel/`SettingsView` evicts it and vice versa), and `Game.handleInput` closes whichever registered overlay is topmost, falling back to `toggle(true)` only once nothing was open to close), `settings-view.ts` (`SettingsView` — the tabbed system-settings view opened both from the title screen and from `PauseMenu.onOpenSettings` in-game; unlike `PauseMenu` it is not itself a pause-triggering overlay's only reach — `main.ts` still pauses/resumes the running `Game` through its own `onOpenChange`. Two `TabBar`-switched faces (the same `.hidden`-toggling shape `PauseMenu` used to use for its tabs): 音楽 (the view's original content — a BGM volume `Slider`, muted into audition via `Bgm.beginAudition()`/`endAudition()` for as long as the view is open, and a per-track 試聴/試聴を停止 list built from `audio/bgm/tracks/tracks.ts`'s `BGM_TRACKS`) and 描画 (`hud/graphics-panel.ts`'s `GraphicsPanel`, moved here from `PauseMenu` — the same quality-preset/resolution/detail-level controls, per-element toggles (including 艦のワイヤーフレーム) and デバッグ表示 selector, reading and writing the same `render/graphics-settings.ts`'s `GraphicsSettings` and `DebugTargetHost` (the `RenderPipeline`) its constructor takes) It also holds the 配色 theme `<select>` (`theme.ts`'s `THEME_PRESETS`/`applyThemePalette`/`getThemePalette`, each option previewed as a swatch row), moved here from `PauseMenu` alongside it since both are display settings rather than pause-menu actions.) Registers with `OverlayManager` under `'settings-view'` (`kind: 'modal'`, `gatesInput: true`, `exclusiveGroup: 'system-modal'`), `context-menu.ts` (`ContextMenu<T>` — a generic screen-positioned popup that holds the target `T` it was opened against, so `onSelect(act, target)` always reports back which item was chosen for which target without the caller tracking that itself; shared by `MapContextActions` (`ContextMenu<MapPickable>`, the `'empty-space'` right-click menu only — every other `MapPickable` kind, including combat-view enemies/self-flown ships, opens a `property-window.ts` `PropertyWindow` instead, see `map-context-actions.ts` above), `NavTarget` (`ContextMenu<Vessel>`, the combat-view base right-click menu), `NodeGizmo` (`ContextMenu<number>`, over a node index), and `ViewBadge` (`ContextMenu<true, ViewId>` — no real target, so `true` stands in for it; `ContextMenu` only fires `onSelect` when its target is non-`null`, which is the one thing a target-less menu has to route around). DOM/event handling only. Each item's `shortcut` (a `KeyboardEvent.code`, shown via `shortcutKeyLabel`) is also stored on its row's `dataset` so `handleShortcut(code)` — the `OverlayHandle` method `OverlayManager.dispatchShortcut` calls — can find and select it through the same close→`onSelect` path a click takes; `ContextMenu` itself owns neither a `window` keydown nor a `document` pointerdown listener, since it registers with `OverlayManager` on `open()`/`close()` (`kind: 'popup'`, `closeOnEscape: true`, `closeOnOutsideClick: true`) for both that and ESC/outside-click closing), `property-window.ts` (`PropertyWindow<A>` — the property/action window, composing `draggable-window.ts`'s `DraggableWindow` for its whole frame (drag, 📌 clip, ✕, `OverlayManager` registration, viewport re-clamping, the compact bottom sheet) and adding only the two stacked sections that are its own — property rows and action items, opened per selected map object by `MapContextActions` and as the load-inspection window by `perf-meter.ts`'s `PerfMeter` (`rows` alone, `items` empty); unlike `ContextMenu` it is instantiated fresh per window rather than reused, since several can be on screen at once, and it is appended as a child of the `window` overlay layer (its root argument, from `overlay-layer.ts` via `Hud.layers.window`) rather than `document.body` — every one of its own CSS selectors is scoped with a leading `#hud` so it outranks the `#hud, #hud * { margin: 0; padding: 0 }` reset by ID specificity. Display-only: the constructor takes a `PropertyWindowContent` (title/subtitle/rows/items) and builds the header DOM once; `syncHeader(title, subtitle)`, `syncRows(rows)` and `syncItems(items)` are the per-frame entry points, each diffing (by row `key` for rows, by a serialized `act`/`label`/`shortcut` string for items) so an unchanged value never touches the DOM and an unchanged shape never rebuilds it (same reasoning as `ObjectListPanel`/`StatusPanel` — a full rebuild would drop the item-click/header-drag/clip-button listeners). A `PropertyRow`'s `group` sorts it into a named, collapsed-by-default section drawn first in the row list (`map-context-actions.ts`'s `orbitRows` puts every ship/base/ammo/celestial-body orbital-element row under `'軌道'`, which is how map view shows an object's orbital elements — the ORBIT panel is combat-view-only, see `panel.ts` above), and its `collapsible` flag instead sorts a group-less row under a single "▸ 詳細を表示" toggle at the end of the row list — the expand/collapse state of both is this class's own (`groupExpanded`/`collapsibleExpanded`), not passed in, since it's pure display state no caller needs to know about; `map-context-actions.ts`'s row builders leave the combat-relevant fields (HP, distance, temperature, power, ammo) always visible — `syncItems` exists because the action set itself is state-dependent (e.g. a ship's "操作対象にする"/"削除" presence, or a follow-toggle's label) and `MapContextActions.sync` calls it every frame alongside `syncRows`, not just once at open time. Either sync reclamps the window into the viewport (`reclamp()`, a private wrapper around `moveTo` using the window's own current position) whenever its shape changed, since content growing after the window opened at a viewport edge would otherwise push it off-screen. Header-drag and the 📌 clip toggle belong to the composed `DraggableWindow`, leaving the `onSelect`/`onClose`/`onClipChange` callbacks as this class's own interactive surface. The constructor forwards an `overlay-manager.ts` `OverlayManager` and an optional `tempWindowGroup` string straight into it, and that window registers/reconfigures the `OverlaySpec` on open and on every clip toggle (`kind: 'window'`, `closeOnEscape`/`closeOnOutsideClick`/`exclusiveGroup` all `!clipped && tempWindowGroup !== undefined` — a clipped window, or one built with no `tempWindowGroup` at all (`perf-meter.ts`'s load-inspection window), never auto-closes and never joins an exclusivity group) — so "at most one temp window per group" and ESC/outside-click closing are OverlayManager's job, not a caller's callback. `close()` always does what the ✕ button does (`dispose()` then `onClose?.()`), so every closing path — ✕, ESC, outside click, a caller's own explicit `.close()` — notifies the same way. `shortcut` on a `PropertyWindowItem` is a `KeyboardEvent.code`, shown via `shortcutKeyLabel` and also matched by `handleShortcut(code)` — the `OverlayHandle` method `OverlayManager.dispatchShortcut` calls — which refuses (returns `false`) while `_clipped`, the same rule `currentSpec()`'s `isTemp` already applies to ESC/outside-click: a clipped window neither auto-closes nor answers a shortcut, so with at most one window ever unclipped there's no ambiguity about which one a keypress reaches. Under `MQ_COMPACT` the window is a bottom sheet instead of a draggable small window — CSS caps it to the bottom 40% of the viewport (`right:0; bottom:0; max-height:40dvh`), `moveTo` becomes a no-op that only clears any stale non-compact `left`/`top`, and the header's own pointerdown returns immediately (`isCompactViewport()`) so dragging never starts — clipping (📌) still works exactly as before, so several clipped sheets can still exist, just stacked rather than scattered. `moveTo(clientX, clientY)` is public so a caller can re-home an existing window (`MapContextActions`'s "already open, move to click position" path) as well as re-clamp on its own `viewport.ts` `onViewportChange` subscription; `bringToFront()` just re-`appendChild`s to its root, since siblings at the same fixed `z-index` stack by DOM order — no per-window z-index counter. When `content.onRename` is supplied, an extra ✎ button appears beside 📌/✕ and swaps `titleMainEl` for an `<input>` (`startRename`); Enter or blur commits (empty/unchanged input is a no-op) and calls `onRename(name)`, Escape cancels — either way the input is swapped back for `titleMainEl` without waiting for the next `syncHeader`. The input's own `keydown` stops propagation, the element-level exception `widgets/value-input.ts`'s `ValueInput` also follows, so a rename-in-progress keystroke never reaches `Input`'s window-level edge queue), `layout.ts` (`clampOverlayPosition`/`Point2` — the pure viewport-clamping arithmetic shared by `PropertyWindow`'s drag/resize and `ContextMenu`'s open-position clamp, so the two popups can't drift to different clamping rules), `shortcut-hint.ts` (`shortcutKeyLabel` — the pure `Escape`→`ESC`/`Delete`→`DEL`/else-uppercase conversion behind both popups' `[KEY]` item hints), `view-badge.ts` (`ViewBadge` — the top-right badge showing the game title/version, `Mode: <selectLabel, title-cased>`, and a `View: <Combat|Map|Dock> ▾` dropdown built on the shared `ContextMenu`; owned and `sync`'d by `Game` rather than by `Hud` itself, since it needs a live reference to `ViewManager` that `Hud` is constructed too early to hold — see `game.ts` above for why `Hud` predates `Game`. It holds **no view state of its own**: the label reads `ViewManager.current` and the menu is exactly `ViewManager.selectableViews()`, which already excludes the current view and anything unreachable, so a listed item can never be refused after being picked), `result-screen.ts` (win/lose/score-attack end-screen assembly; `ResultScreen implements OverlayHandle` holds the `#hud-result` DOM and a `show(result: StageResult)` method that rewrites it and registers with `OverlayManager` under id `'result'` — `kind: 'modal'`, `gatesInput: true`, but `closeOnEscape`/`closeOnOutsideClick` both `false` since the only ways off this screen are the 再出撃/タイトルへ actions already on it (`RunTransitions.restart`/`returnToTitle`, both satisfied by `Launcher`); `close()` hides the DOM and deregisters it, idempotent, called by `Launcher.endRun()` at the start of every run transition so a decided screen never survives into the next one — constructed once by `Launcher` itself (`new ResultScreen(hud, this)`), not a module-level singleton), `tick-scale.ts` (`chooseTickInterval`/`buildTicks` — the 表示時刻 slider's tick marks: pick the smallest interval from a fixed 10min→1year ladder whose tick count fits the panel, then place ticks at that interval's multiples; pure, DOM-free), `calendar-ticks.ts` (`calendarBoundaries`/`tickLabel` — the planned trajectory's ruler-style tick marks (`plan-display.ts` above): a fixed rank ladder of calendar units (1時間/3時間/6時間/12時間/1日/1月/1年), `calendarBoundaries(fromUnix, toUnix, maxCount, hourFamilyMaxCount)` picking the finest rank whose boundary count over that span still fits its own budget and enumerating that rank's boundaries, each tagged with the *coarsest* rank it actually satisfies (an ordinary midnight is a day boundary, but a month-start midnight is also a month boundary, so it's tagged the coarser way) — the budget is `maxCount` (`const.ts`'s `PLAN_TICK_MAX_COUNT`) for the day/month/year ranks but the separately, more generously tuned `hourFamilyMaxCount` (`PLAN_TICK_HOUR_FAMILY_MAX_COUNT`) for the hour ranks (1/3/6/12時間), since each hour-family step's boundary set is already a superset of every coarser hour-family step's (1時間ごとの列挙は3/6/12時間ごとの境界を全て含む) — without the separate, larger budget, a segment long enough to force a coarser hour-family step would lose the finer one's candidates outright for its whole length rather than the two coexisting and thinning gradually against each other the way `syncTickMarkers`'s screen-distance pass already does once both are present in the candidate list. month/year boundaries step through `Date.UTC`'s own month/year index rather than a fixed second count, since neither unit is a fixed duration. `tickLabel(unix, rank, mode, referenceUnix)` formats the matching string: `mode: TickLabelMode` = `'absolute'` gives the calendar form per rank (`HH:00`/`M/D`/`M月`/year), `'relative'` the signed elapsed time from `referenceUnix` (`T+3h`/`T-2d`), built on `hud/utils.ts`'s own `fmtDuration` so there is no second duration format. Which mode is in force comes in as an argument (`DisplayWindow.tickLabelMode`'s value) rather than being read here, keeping this module free of any `display-window-manager.ts` import; `calendarBoundaries`'s candidate set is the same either way, since only the label text differs. This is a separate module from `tick-scale.ts` rather than a second mode of it: that one places ticks at even fractions of an *elapsed* span with a relative `T+` label, which has no notion of a calendar-absolute month or year boundary — the two modules answer genuinely different questions, not the same question for two callers), `utils.ts` (shared formatters `fmtDist`/`fmtSpeed`/`fmtTime`/`fmtDuration`/`fmtMarkerDist`/`fmtDateTime`, plus `SIM_EPOCH_SEC` — `fmtDateTime` renders a Unix timestamp as ISO 8601 UTC (`2026-08-08T14:16:00`) by reading the date's components rather than `toISOString()`, and `SIM_EPOCH_SEC` parses `const.ts`'s `SIM_EPOCH_TDB` with its own regex rather than `Date.parse`; both exist because the sim epoch sits in a far-future year whose 5-digit form `Date.parse` rejects outright and `toISOString()` renders in the signed extended-year notation. The pair turns `SIM_EPOCH_TDB` + `simTime` into the global status bar's absolute-date readout and `plan-display.ts`'s UTC day-boundary ticks; it is deliberately independent of `Ephemeris`'s phase offsets, since those are random per-game initial phases and `SIM_EPOCH_TDB` is a fixed display anchor with no bearing on where the sun/moon actually are) — **flat dark theme: monotone grays on near-black panels (1px light edge, no shadows) with a saturated orange primary accent** (`theme.ts`'s `ACCENT` = `#ff6a00`, plus its `ACCENT_FILL_*`/`ACCENT_EDGE_*` ramps for selected/hovered/pressed states); markers and orbit lines follow the same rule — monotone base (player orbit light gray, planned orbit white, prograde/boresight light gray) with orange reserved for attention targets (target marker+orbit, lead, maneuver node/BURN, ammo pickups). The one deliberate second color is cyan (`ACCENT_SECONDARY` = `#00c8ff`, `theme.ts`) for the secondary target only — chosen specifically to be unmistakable against orange, since the two can appear on screen together (secondary orbit line, enemy-list row, marker glyph). `DANGER` (`theme.ts`) is the HUD's one warning/error color, shared by both a critically-low readout and the target-lock highlight rather than carrying two separate reds.
- `src/game/marker/` — the screen-projected marker **mechanism**, plus the few marker sets that no single object can own. `marker-glyphs.ts` holds the glyph vocabulary, split into three families by **the question a reader asks first of any symbol on the map — is it a thing, a direction, or a point on an orbit?**: `ENTITY_GLYPH` (filled: ship `▲`, body `●`, plan ghost `⬢`, placement preview `▷`), `DIRECTION_GLYPH` (arrows: prograde/retrograde `⊙`/`⊗`, normal/antinormal `⇧`/`⇩`, radial out/in `◎`/`◉`, target/anti-target `⇨`/`⇦`, off-screen bearing `↑`, grid axis `⇕`) and `ORBIT_POINT_GLYPH` (hollow: apsis `◇`, AN/DN `△`/`▽`, maneuver node `◈`, burn point `⬡`, impact `✕`). It holds **only the choices** — which glyph a given marker uses stays with that marker's owner, since nothing here knows about marker keys — so the vocabulary can be kept consistent without centralizing the decisions. The HUD's vertical fold arrows (`hud/hud-root.ts`'s `▾`/`▸`) are deliberately outside all three families, so a fold can't read as a direction marker. `marker-manager.ts` (`MarkerManager` — element pooling keyed by id, `set`/`setPosition`/`setDirection`/`setBearing`/`headingRotationDeg`/`hide`/`remove` (`hide` keeps the pooled element for keys drawn from a fixed set; `remove` drops the DOM node for keys that grow per target — enemy/LEAD markers, which would otherwise accumulate forever), label-collision relaxation and the SVG leader lines; `setBearing` is the shared "off-screen target → arrow on a ring at the screen edge" placement, used by `GroupedMarkers` (enemies), `EntityMarker` (ammo pickups and bases) and `PlanGuide` (burn direction), all with the one `DIRECTION_GLYPH.bearing` `↑` — the caller supplies an *up-pointing* glyph because the ring angle is applied as a rotation, and an off-screen hint is a direction rather than the object itself whatever the object is; **owned by `Game`, not `Hud`**, and shared by reference with every module that emits markers; `dispose()` empties the pools rather than removing `layers.marker`/`svgOverlay` themselves, which are `Hud`'s, and cancels every pending occlusion-fade `setTimeout` first — a timer left armed would fire against an element that is no longer there). `headingRotationDeg(worldPos, vel, project, scale)` is the sole place the "rotate an up-pointing glyph to face `vel`" assembly lives — it projects `worldPos` and a point `MARKER_HEADING_PROBE_PX` pixels ahead along `vel`, turns the atan2 angle between them into the +90°-corrected rotation `set`'s `rotationDeg` expects, and returns `undefined` — meaning "don't rotate" — when the projected difference falls under `MARKER_HEADING_DEGENERATE_PX`, i.e. the velocity is nearly along the view axis and the direction is numerically unreliable. `set` treats `rotationDeg === undefined` as "leave the glyph's current rotation alone" rather than resetting it to 0°, so a marker passing through that degenerate moment holds its last heading instead of visibly snapping to unrotated and back; a marker that never rotates (the combat-view direction markers, the boresight) simply never receives a defined `rotationDeg` and stays unrotated forever. **The rule is that each object syncs its own markers** — a module must not expose both `sync` and a separate `syncMarker`; the marker update belongs inside its `sync`. So the orbital-direction/boresight/`self` markers live in `vessel/pilot-markers.ts` (owned by `Vessel`, driven from `syncVessel`), the target bearing + board-crossing marks in `targeter.ts`, the relative AN/DN markers in `nav-target.ts`, an object's own position marker in `marker/entity-marker.ts` (held by the object as `GameEntity.marker`), the celestial labels in `camera/focus-markers.ts`, NODE/BURN in `plan/plan-guide.ts`, and the periapsis/apoapsis icons + the planned-position ghost in `plan/plan-display.ts`. Two sets stay in `marker/` because no single object can decide them, and `MarkerManager` constructs and owns both itself as public readonly fields (`combatMarkers`/`leadMarkers`, built in `MarkerManager`'s own constructor) rather than `Game` constructing them separately. A `TODO` on those fields flags this as a staging point, not their final home: the two are a different classification from `MarkerManager`'s own "display mechanism" responsibility, and belong under a proper owner once one is found.
  - `grouped-markers.ts` (`GroupedMarkers`) [`MarkerManager`-owned as `combatMarkers`] — takes a set of `GroupedMarkerItem`s (key/class/symbol/world position/velocity/priority/label parts/bearing color) and does the things that need the whole set: collapsing screen-adjacent markers into one representative (highest `priority` wins; the rest lose their label), in combat view only, replacing off-screen ones with a screen-edge bearing arrow (`sync`'s `overviewMode` argument hides it on the map, same as `Logistics`/`CreativeStage`'s own bearing arrows — a map-mode object is already visible in the overview, so a bearing hint is redundant there), and, in map view only, rotating each marker to face its own `vel` via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg` (`sync`'s `scale` argument — a `ScaleFn` — is what that conversion needs; combat view leaves `rotationDeg` unset, since there the mesh itself already shows attitude). Target-agnostic and reusable; `targeter.ts`'s `Targeter.syncTargetMarkers` (below) is what supplies the set's content, from `Vessel.markerItem()`/`Vessel.markerItem()`.
  - `lead-markers.ts` (`LeadMarkers`) [`MarkerManager`-owned] — the ✛ LEAD markers, which depend on the player *and* the enemy and so belong to neither. Shows one marker per currently-locked target (primary and secondary), each at `physics/intercept.ts`'s `leadPoint` solution, and holds only the keys it drew last frame so it can `remove` the ones it no longer draws — a key is minted per target and would otherwise accumulate. Hidden entirely in map mode. Driven by `Targeter.syncTargetMarkers` too, alongside `combatMarkers`.
  - `entity-marker.ts` (`EntityMarker`) [one per `GameEntity`, held as `GameEntity.marker`] — the marker that points at **the object itself**, plus its screen-edge bearing arrow. The owner builds it in its own constructor with the DOM class, the combat-view glyph it wants, and whether the map-view label carries a distance (`AmmoPickup`: `mk-ammo` + `▣`, no map-view distance — a pickup is found by its position on the orbit, not by chasing a shrinking number; `Vessel`: `mk-base` + `▲`, distance shown in both views) and everything else is one shared rule: **map view draws `ENTITY_GLYPH.ship` `▲` rotated to face the object's own ECI velocity** (a heading is only readable on an up-pointing glyph, and the overview already shows where things are, so no bearing arrow there), **combat view draws the owner's own glyph plus a `↑` `setBearing` arrow while the object is off screen**. The label is the owner's `name`, followed by the distance from `viewerPos` in combat view always and in map view only when the owner opted in. `sync` reads `displayState(displayTime)`, so the marker sits at the same future-ghost position as the object's own mesh and hides once that is `null` beyond its prediction horizon; a `MapVisibility` whose `pickable` is false hides it outright, and `icon`/`label` blank the two halves independently. `EntityManager.syncMarkers(project, scale, displayTime, overviewMode, viewerPos, visibilityPolicy)` is the single driver, walking `ammos` and `bases` with each kind's own visibility.
  - `equator-node-marker-pair.ts` (`EquatorNodeMarkerPair`) [one per `GameEntity`, held as `GameEntity.equatorNodes`] — the △▽ EqAN/EqDN icons showing where **one** object's orbit crosses its center body's equatorial plane. `GameEntity.ensureEquatorNodes(markerManager)` lazily creates the pair and returns it, so whichever module decides an object needs the icons is the one that brings it into being; `Vessel` instead builds its own in its constructor, since a base always shows them. `GameEntity.dispose()` disposes it, and `EntityManager.parkVessel` (docking) disposes and drops it, since a ship out of `players` no longer reaches the per-frame sync. **`update(frame, displayTime, ephemeris, state = owner.state, samples = null)` produces the frame's two icons and `sync(project, show, cameraPos)` consumes them** — placing the markers and then dropping the icons, so a pair nobody updated this frame hides itself. That is what removes the need for a layer that collects "which objects show them" this frame: each selector just calls `update` on its own selection, and the four that do are `PlanEditor.update` (the operated ship), `Targeter.updateEquatorNodes` (the combat target), `EntityManager.updateBaseEquatorNodes` (every living base — a base is a permanent orbital fixture and approaching/docking to it is itself a plane-matching maneuver, so it shows them regardless of selection) and `NavTarget.update` (whatever entity its own `resolveEntity` finds for the nav-target id; celestial bodies and Lagrange points resolve to nothing, since they aren't themselves an orbiting "object"). `update` resolves the state's own strongest attractor and produces nothing when that body has no `degree2.pole` (sun/jupiter — no modeled equator) or the orbit plane is ~coincident with it. **`samples` decides how the crossings are found, and the split is deliberate rather than uniform**: only `PlanEditor` passes them (the plan's trailing `FinalSegment`'s `state0`/`samples`), so only the operated ship's crossings are found by scanning the integrated series (`physics/trajectory-features.ts`'s `findEquatorCrossings`) — the same reasoning `plan-display.ts`'s apsis icons follow, since the ship's planned orbit is drawn as `plan/plan-path.ts`'s owned-segment arc's integrated polyline and an analytically-derived icon would drift off it under J2's epoch-dependent osculating elements. Every other object keeps the analytic `nodeAnomalies(el, eqNormal)` path, because those orbits are drawn as `OrbitLine`'s analytic ellipse — an icon derived any other way would be the one not on the line. Positions are baked/un-baked into the display `frame` self-contained (no `plan-path.ts` dependency, since the object need not be on the plan at all). `mapPickables()` (`kind: 'eqnode'`) is what `MapPickables.refresh` gathers off every entity into its candidate set. Marker keys are `eqan-${owner.id}`/`eqdn-${owner.id}`, and a point `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body (off the `Attractor[]` `update` cached at `displayTime`) is `hide`d, since the pair itself hasn't disappeared. `EntityManager.syncEquatorNodes(project, show, cameraPos)` is the single sync driver, looping `all()` so every pair is reached whichever module created it.

  `markerManager.resolveCollisions()` is the one call that must see every marker, so `Game.sync` calls it last.
- `src/game/navball/navball.ts` — `Navball`: owned by `Game`, holds `gridVisibility: CelestialGridVisibility` (the celestial-grid toggle state, loaded from/saved to `localStorage` under `tepui.gridVisibility`) and nothing else — its DOM is `hud/view-options-panel.ts`'s `ViewOptionsPanel` (the 表示 panel's 天球グリッド section, alongside the per-class icon/label/orbit-line toggles — see `camera-system.ts` above), so there is one "what does the map show" panel rather than a second one just for the grid. The constructor takes that already-built panel (`CameraSystem.viewOptionsPanel`, constructed before `Navball` — see `game.ts` above) and only wires `onGridToggle`/calls `setGridVisibility`, split on the usual build/sync line the same way every other panel-backed state owner is.

  **`gridVisibility: CelestialGridVisibility` is the source of truth for the six celestial-grid toggles** — `Navball` holds it (flipped by its own panel's `Button` rows) and `Game.sync` reads it once, passing it as `EnvironmentScene.sync`'s own `gridVisibility` argument for `render/celestial-grid.ts`'s `CelestialGrid.sync` to consume; `CelestialGrid` itself holds no visibility state of its own (see `game/celestial/environment-scene.ts` below). Grouping the grid toggles inside the 表示 panel rather than a separate window is a spatial choice only. Its initial value is read from `localStorage` (`tepui.gridVisibility`, `loadGridVisibility`) and written back on every toggle (`saveGridVisibility`), so it survives a page reload.

`src/game/economy/` — 資源と設備の定義。**DOM にも THREE にも依存しない純データと純関数**（型として `physics/attractor.ts` の `AttractorId` だけを読む）なので、機体側の実装を待たずに書け、`npm run test:unit` から node で直接動く。通貨は存在せず、生産が要求するのは資源・設備・電力の3つだけである。
- `resource.ts` — `ResourceDef`(id/表示名/記号/密度[kg/m³]/貯蔵の性格 `bulk|cryogenic|pressurized|hazardous`) と、元素・鉱石・中間素材・部材を通した資源表 `RESOURCES`。`solar-system.ts` の `SOLAR_SYSTEM` と同じく `satisfies Record<string, ResourceDef>` で受けるので、リテラルなキー集合が `ResourceId` としてそのまま残る — 資源を1つ足すと、それを `Record` のキーや union として参照する側で欠落がコンパイルエラーになる。
- `resource-ledger.ts` — `ResourceLedger`: 資源 id ごとの質量[kg]の帳簿。`amountOf`/`add`/`take`/`clear` だけを持ち、`take` は足りなければ**何も減らさず** false を返す(在庫が 0 未満になる経路が無い)。負や非有限の質量は例外にする。
- `facility.ts` — `FacilityDef`(入力/出力の `kg/s`、消費電力 `powerDraw` と発電量 `powerOutput` の2つの `W`、建設費`kg`、`requiresFacility`)と設備表 `FACILITIES`(`FacilityId` も同じ `keyof typeof` で導出)、および月面基地が地球から運ばれた最初の一組 `INITIAL_FACILITY_IDS` 10基。入力の1枠は `{anyOf, rate}` で、同じ役を果たす資源(原子炉のウランまたはトリウム、電子機器工場の銅またはアルミ)のうち1つを消費すれば足りることを表す。**電力は資源ではない** — 貯蔵も輸送も資源とは違う規則に従うため `ResourceLedger` には入らず、発電設備は `powerOutput` を持ち `outputs` は空になる。送電網は発電も変換もしないので両方 0 である。`requiresFacility` は技術の前提ではなく物理的な前提だけを表す。塩素・過酸化水素・超伝導材は、それぞれ溶融塩電解炉の陽極ガス(媒体の塩化カルシウムが電解のたびに出す。アパタイトの塩素を単体で取り出せる唯一の口)、過酸化水素合成炉(水素と酸素をパラジウム触媒の上で直接反応させる)、超伝導線材製造炉(希土類バリウム銅酸化物の線材。バリウムは KREEP 岩の希土類に随伴するので資源としては別建てにしない)から出る — この3つが無いと固体推進剤とヘリウム3が閉包から漏れる。
- `propellant-compatibility.ts` — `TANK_MATERIALS`: 推進剤ごとに、タンク材として使える資源(いずれか1つで足りる `allowedMaterials`)と、それとは別に必ず要る資源(`requiredResources` — ヒドラジンの触媒床の白金族)。推進剤の選択は、その推進剤に耐える金属を持っているかどうかに縛られる。
- `actuator-materials.ts` — `ACTUATOR_MATERIALS`: 姿勢制御アクチュエータごとの要求資源。磁気トルカとフライホイールで要る資源が違い、どちらを先に作れるようになるかが進行の形を決めるため、§17-4 の正本はここであって `FACILITIES` の巻線工場の入力欄ではない(`FacilityDef` は製品ごとに異なる入力を表せない)。
- `deposit.ts` — `DEPOSITS`(天体 id × 資源 id × 産出係数 × 採取手段 `surface|atmosphere|regolith`)と、天体に紐づかない `ENEMY_DROPS`。`access` が `'atmosphere'` の産地は着陸を要さず大気捕集機で採る。火星と木星の大気にはキセノンが微量に含まれ、電気推進の推進剤はここからしか採れない。
- `build-cost.ts` — **搭載要素1つを作るのに要る資源(§6 B6 の T-4)。33種すべてが行を持ち、行を持たない種別は型として存在しない**(`PART_BUILD_MATERIALS` が `PartType` 全体を鍵に取る)。表が持つのは質量ではなく**内訳の比**である — 質量は `第9版_搭載要素の性能値.md` が定め、実装では `Part.weight` がその値を持つので、比だけを置けば性能値が動いたときに建造費が追従する(値を2箇所に書けば必ず片方が古くなる)。内訳は「その要素が実物として何でできているか」で決め、加工済みの部材(電子機器・機械部品・太陽電池パドルなど)が資源として登録されている種別はその部材へ一本化し、素材から直に組む種別(装甲・タンク・構造物)だけを素材へ割り振る。1種別ぶんの合計は 1 になる — **ただし推進剤タンク3種(`oxidizer_tank`/`reductant_tank`/`rcs_tank`)だけは合計が `1 - TANK_SHELL_FRACTION` になる。**殻をどの金属で作れるかは積む推進剤が決める(§17-2)ので、殻の質量はこの表ではなく生産の `tanks` の枠が課金する。`catalystMassFor(thrust)` はヒドラジンの触媒床の質量 [kg](推力 25 N までは `1.09e-3 × F`、それを超えると `5.45e-3 × √F`。境界で連続し、いずれも 27.3 g)で、`needsCatalystBed(propellant)` が真になるのは単推進剤のヒドラジンだけである — **二液推進剤は自己着火性であり触媒を要さない。**`partBuildCost(part)` はその2つを合わせた、要素1つぶんの資源。触媒床は質量の内訳ではなく推力から別に決まる追加の要求なので、`catalyst-bed` はここで初めて足される。
- `producibility.ts` — `producibility(bp, ledger, facilities: readonly FacilityId[], powerAvailable)`: 与えられた在庫・所持設備・利用可能電力に対して、設計を生産できるかどうかを判定し、足りないものを `Requirement[]` として返す(空配列なら生産できる)。**`Requirement.kind` は `'resource' | 'facility' | 'power'` の3つに閉じており、4つ目は作らない** — 生産の可否はこの3つだけで決まる。`needed`/`available` は真偽値に潰さない実際の値(資源は kg、電力は W、設備は基数)で、資源の要求は id ごとに1件へ積み上げる。`needed` が 0 の資源要求は「量ではなく所持そのものを要求する」もので、要る質量がこの層では決まらないもの(ヒドラジンの触媒床の白金族)に限る。**判定は `ledger` を一切変更しない** — 生産の実行(在庫の消費)は第2巻の側にある。設計の形は `ProducibilityBlueprint`(`parts`/`tanks`/`structure`/`requiresFacility`)という**この関数が読むフィールドだけの狭い構造的インターフェース**で受ける — 第2巻 §4-1 の `VesselBlueprint` が後からこれを構造的に満たす形にするためで、`plan.ts` の `DisplayDurationSource`・`plan-executor.ts` の `PlanExecutorShip`・`debug-target.ts` の `DebugTargetHost` と同じ流儀である。搭載要素ごとの建造費は `BlueprintPart.buildCost` として設計側が申告する — §6-1 は性能値だけを定めていて建造費の表を持たないため、その表は F5(`parts.ts` の置き換え)または第2巻に委ねてある。要る電力は所持の有無に依らず要求設備すべての `powerDraw` の和で、不足すればちょうど1件の `'power'` 要求になる — **同じ設備を複数の搭載要素が要求しても、実物は1基なので電力も建造費も1回しか数えない。**所持していない要求設備は `'facility'` 要求に加えて、その設備の `buildCost` と `inputs` の不足も資源要求として挙げる — `anyOf` の枠は選択肢のうち1つでも在庫にあれば満たされ、全滅したときにだけ**先頭の id を代表として1件だけ**挙げる(同じ不足が選択肢の数だけ並ぶのを避けるため)。タンクを持つ設計には §17-2 の `TANK_MATERIALS` を引き、適合する材料のどれか1つで殻の質量を賄えるかを見る — 四酸化二窒素はアルミしか許さないので、チタンだけの在庫では拒否される。

`src/game/celestial/` — the meshes/lights/labels for each individual celestial body (THREE-dependent), as opposed to `physics/ephemeris.ts`'s "where is it" (THREE-free). Moved here from `render/` because it already depended on `game/camera/`, `game/floating-origin` and `game/const` — a `render/` regulation violation this move corrects.
- `celestial-body.ts` — the abstract `CelestialBody`: `id: AttractorId`, `build(scene)` (one-time mesh construction), `sync(fo, displayTime, cameraSystem, ephemeris)` (per-frame placement). Holds no position/velocity of its own — `Ephemeris` is the only source of truth, read fresh every `sync`.
- `src/game/celestial/point-field.ts` — **THREE-free** generation and evaluation of the solar system's diffuse point clouds (main belt, Jupiter's L4/L5 Trojans, the Hilda group, the Kuiper belt's cold/hot populations, the scattered disk) as plain orbital elements, so it's a `physics/`-style pure module covered by `test:physics` (`tests/physics/point-field.test.ts`) despite living under `game/celestial/` (display-only — these points are never a gravity source, pick target, or focus target, so they don't belong in `physics/attractor.ts`'s `AttractorId` registry). Each group is one `PointFieldDef` in the `POINT_FIELD_DEFS` array — a size distribution (`'semiMajor'` with a range plus optional Kirkwood-style Gaussian-rejected gaps, or `'perihelion'` for the scattered disk, whose q clusters at 30–40 AU rather than its a) crossed with an eccentricity/inclination range, plus an optional `ResonanceDistribution` for a group locked to a Jupiter mean-motion resonance. `generatePointField(seed = ASTEROID_SEED)` is deterministic (`mulberry32`, not `Math.random`, precisely so a save/replay reproduces the same field) and returns one `PointFieldGroup` per def; `allPoints` flattens them. A resonant group's `l0`/`lonPeri`/`meanMotion` are solved from the resonant angle σ = p·λ_J − q·λ_H − (p−q)·ϖ_H staying near one of `librationCentersDeg`: the 1:1 Trojans (p=q) have no ϖ_H term, so σ fixes `l0` directly and `meanMotion` is Jupiter's own `lRate`; the 3:2 Hilda group (p=3, q=2) instead draws `l0` freely and solves for `lonPeri`, with three libration centers (0°/120°/−120°) reproducing the real Hilda group's three-lobed clustering relative to Jupiter — a single center only reproduces one lobe, since nothing else about the model forces the other two to be occupied. `PointElements` carries a precomputed `meanMotion` alongside the usual elements specifically because `pointPositionAt(el, t)` runs across the whole field every frame update and a `sqrt` per point per call would be wasted work. `pointPositionAt` returns a **sun-centered** position (via `elements.ts`'s Kepler solve + `Q_ECLY_TO_ECI`) — ECI-izing by adding the sun's own position is the caller's job, same split `elements.ts` itself follows elsewhere.
- `src/game/celestial/point-field-view.ts` — `PointFieldView`: the `THREE.InstancedMesh` draw side, one `InstancedMesh` **per group** (a `GROUP_VIEW` table of draw radius/color keyed by `PointFieldDef.id`, since the Kuiper belt/scattered disk sit an order of magnitude farther out than the main belt/Trojans/Hildas and need a larger draw radius to stay visible at that zoom — this table is this module's own, since `point-field.ts` stays THREE-free), owned by `environment-scene.ts`'s `EnvironmentScene`. Geometry is `TetrahedronGeometry`, not a flat billboard/plane — a flat shape has a normal, and at some camera angle every instance would go edge-on and the whole field would flicker out at once; a tetrahedron always presents some visible face. `update(t, overviewMode, ephemeris)` is a no-op while `!overviewMode` (never drawn in combat view, so there's nothing to evaluate) and otherwise re-evaluates only **1/8 of the points per frame** (`UPDATE_FRACTION`, round-robin via a persistent `cursor` per group) — justified because the fastest possible orbital period in the field is several years, so a few frames of position lag is sub-pixel at any map zoom. Positions are cached sun-centered (`positions: Vec3[]`, mirroring `pointPositionAt`'s own convention) because the sun's own ECI position moves every frame regardless of whether a given point's orbital position was just refreshed. `sync(fo, overviewMode)` rewrites every instance's `instanceMatrix` unconditionally each frame (not just the ones `update` refreshed) — the floating origin itself moves every frame, so even an unrefreshed point's on-screen position must be re-baked.
- `earth-body.ts` — `EarthBody`: wraps `render/earth.ts`'s `Earth` (self-rotation angle, sun direction, aurora/atmosphere `tick`). `phase0` (the spin's initial phase, `0` until set — the random draw itself lives in `main.ts`, the sole `Math.random()` call for it) is mutable and exposed via `spinPhase0()`/`setSpinPhase0()` so it can round-trip through a save the same way `Ephemeris`'s own phase offsets do — see `GameSaveData.earthSpinPhase0` below.
- `sun-body.ts` — `SunBody`: the sun's two views (`render/stars.ts`'s `Sun`, holding both). Combat view keeps the camera-relative glow billboard (a flat sprite reads fine at that scale); the overview camera instead places the real `Sun.mesh` — a self-lit `MeshBasicMaterial` sphere, since the sun is a light source and must not shade under the scene's own lighting — at its true ECI position and `R_SUN` radius, the same "real position/radius on the map" rule `SphereBody` follows for the moon/Jupiter; whichever mode isn't active hides its own visual. It owns no light: the scene's one `DirectionalLight` illuminates only what is drawn near the render origin, so it belongs to `EnvironmentScene` alongside the ambient light rather than to the star's own view.
- `sphere-body.ts` — `SphereBody`: the shared "textured sphere at a compressed display distance" view for any orbiting body that needs nothing more (the moon and Jupiter). The one place the `visDist * radius / trueDist` scaling formula lives, so every body drawn this way keeps its true angular size — a shaped body scales all 3 axes by the same factor, so the property holds unchanged. **The sphere mesh rides `screen-lod.ts`'s `SPHERE_LOD_LADDER`, the same as `render/earth.ts`**: the constructor calls `buildSurface(level)` once per ladder tier, collecting the resulting `CelestialSurface`s' meshes (initially hidden) into a `THREE.Group` — since WebGPU never allows swapping a live mesh's own geometry, tessellation is instead varied by building every tier's mesh once and toggling `visible` (private `syncLod`, mirroring `Earth.syncSurfaceLod`); `build(scene)` adds that one group to the scene, and every per-frame transform (`position`/`scale`/`quaternion`) is written once on the group rather than on an individual mesh, propagating to whichever tier is currently visible. `sync` derives the apparent diameter from the body's **true** ECI position (`Ephemeris.positionOf`, not the compressed draw position) and `CameraSystem.activeCameraScale`, so the LOD/visibility choice reflects true angular size in both views alike; below `screen-lod.ts`'s `showsPhysicalSphere` threshold the whole group (and any ring) is hidden outright — the threshold compares against `outerRadius` (the ring's own outer edge when the body has one, so a small body's still-visible ring doesn't vanish with it), while the LOD tier itself is chosen from the body's own `radius` alone. The constructor resolves `solar-system.ts`'s `shapeAxes(radius, shape)` once into a local `THREE.Vector3 axes` (`shape` an optional `ShapeDef` argument, `undefined` = a sphere of all 3 axes = `radius`); `sync` writes the group's `scale` from `axes` (map view) or `axes` scaled by `visDist/dist` (combat view) instead of `setScalar`. Because THREE composes a matrix from position/quaternion/scale independent of assignment order, `axes` is always interpreted in the body's own (pre-spin) local frame regardless of which of `quaternion`/`scale` is written first — a shape is a fact about the body, not about ECI, so it must not rotate with the spin write. Orientation is not `Object3D.lookAt` — `sync` reads `ephemeris.poleAt(this.id, displayTime)` and, when non-`null`, sets the group's quaternion straight from `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinOrientation(axis, spinAngle)`; a body with no `pole` model simply keeps whatever orientation it already had. The Moon's tidal lock therefore falls out of its synchronous spin phase rather than a per-frame "face the parent" override (verified over a full sidereal-month sweep: the modeled meridian tracks the true Earth direction to within ~10.1°, the size of lunar libration). An optional constructor argument `rings?: RingSystemDef` builds a `game/celestial/ring-view.ts` `RingView` in `build()`, added to the scene **as a sibling of the body group, not a child** — see `ring-view.ts` below for why. `sync` scales the group by `axes × (scaleFactor/radius)` (3 axes, so a shaped body's flattening survives the map/combat distance switch) but hands the ring only the uniform `scaleFactor` itself (a ring is a circle in the equatorial plane, so it takes no flattening of its own even when the body it orbits does), and passes the resolved pole axis (`null` for a body with no `pole` model, in which case the ring is left unrotated by this class — `RingView.sync` itself no-ops on a `null` axis).
- `body-class.ts` — `BodyClass` (`'star' | 'planet' | 'dwarf' | 'satellite' | 'smallBody'`) and `BODY_CLASSES: Record<SolarSystemId, BodyClass>`. **A display-importance judgment, deliberately separate from `solar-system.ts`'s `kind`** — `kind` says what a body orbits (a dynamical fact), this says whether the map shows it by default, which is what splits dwarf planets and comet nuclei out of the `kind: 'planet'` crowd. Kept THREE-free (unlike `celestial-registry.ts`) so the visibility rules below can be evaluated — and tested — with no renderer. `bodyClassOf(registry, id)` falls back to the dynamical `kind` for an id a custom registry invented.
- `body-visibility.ts` — `BodyClassToggles`/`DEFAULT_BODY_CLASS_TOGGLES`/`bodyClassVisible(cls, toggles)`/`alwaysFullyVisibleIds(registry, focusId, nearbyIds, toggles)`/`bodyIconLabel(registry, toggles, id)`: the toggle table and the per-class/per-id predicates `map-visibility.ts`'s `MapVisibilityPolicy` (below) composes into the actual map answer. `focusId: AttractorId | undefined` — `undefined` is the camera fixed on a `FocusTarget` with no corresponding body (a `'point'` target baked into a coordinate system rather than following a specific object), in which case `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` falls back to the star plus whatever `nearbyIds` the caller resolved from the camera's own position. `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` is the toggle-independent part: every star, plus the camera's current system (`nearbyIds`), plus (once `focusId` is defined) the focused body's ancestor, child, or **planetary-system sibling** (sibling only below the star, since every heliocentric body is a sibling of every other and the star already covers that layer) — so focusing Jupiter surfaces the Galilean moons and leaving it puts them away again; each of those still has to clear its own class's category toggle, so an always-visible exception can't resurrect a category the player switched off. **Icon (the marker dot) and Label (the name) are independent per class** (`planetIcon`/`planetLabel`/`dwarfIcon`/`dwarfLabel`/`satelliteIcon`/`satelliteLabel`/`smallBodyIcon`/`smallBodyLabel`/`lagrangeIcon`/`lagrangeLabel`) — wanting a body's position without its name (or the reverse) is a real request, and `bodyIconLabel(registry, toggles, id)` is the per-id answer a caller already inside `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` must not call (that case is always icon:true/label:true, decided by the caller before falling back to this function). `planetOrbit`/`dwarfOrbit`/`smallBodyOrbit`/`satelliteOrbit` are a third, orbit-line axis independent of both — `bodyIconLabel` never reads them; `MapVisibilityPolicy.body(id).orbit` does, and `environment-scene.ts`'s reference lines read that. `satelliteOrbit` is additionally gated (in `map-visibility.ts`'s own `orbitForBody`, not in `bodyIconLabel`) on the camera/focus being in that satellite's own planetary system — Earth's moon excepted, always shown — since every satellite's ellipse drawn at once buries the inner solar system; `satelliteIcon`/`satelliteLabel` carry no such gate, since `FocusMarkers`' crowding relaxation (screen-space label thinning, not a per-system toggle) already keeps a map full of satellite markers legible. `lagrange` has no orbit flag at all (a Lagrange point has no orbit to draw). Defaults: among the body-class `Orbit` flags, every one is off except `planetOrbit` (drawing every dwarf's/small body's/satellite's ellipse at once buries the inner solar system; planets are few and form its skeleton); the four entity-side `Orbit` flags (`playerOrbit`/`shipOrbit`/`ammoOrbit`/`baseOrbit`) default on instead, since a stage's self-flown ships/enemies/pickups/bases are few enough that their orbit lines don't crowd the map the way every small body's would — and every `Icon`/`Label` flag on, since crowding relaxation keeps a full marker set legible regardless of count. **Proximity is deliberately not an axis**: camera distance changes continuously, so a distance threshold would make labels and list rows flicker mid-zoom; the focus parent/child relation is the discrete stand-in that only changes when the player asks it to. `systemChainAt(registry, cameraPos, attractors)` is the one exception, and deliberately so: it walks from whichever body most strongly pulls at `cameraPos` (`attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`) up through its ancestors — a continuous quantity, camera position, driving a discrete result — because it answers "what system is the camera physically in" rather than gating what's shown; a body not in the active registry (a living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`) returns a single-element chain rather than an ancestor walk, since it has none. `systemMembersAt(registry, cameraPos, attractors)` builds on it for the UI's actual need: the chain plus each chain body's own children (skipping a star's children, or a Jupiter-chain list would pull in every other planet) — a fixed near-to-far, body-then-its-children order so the caller's choices don't reorder frame to frame. `frame-controls.ts`'s `AnchorZone`/`RotationZone` use it to build their quick-pick/rotation-target lists for "the system I'm currently near."
- `map-visibility.ts` — `MapVisibilityPolicy`: **the single answer to "what does the map show, and what may be selected", for celestial bodies and for game entities alike.** Constructed from `(registry, toggles, focusId, nearbyIds)` — a plain per-frame value, not stored state — **once per frame, by `MapPickables.refresh` in the update phase** (`MapPickables.visibilityPolicy`), and handed from there to everything else that judges the same frame: `FocusMarkers.update` takes it as an argument, and `Game.sync` reads it back off `MapPickables` and passes it to `EnvironmentScene.sync`/its reference lines and on to `Stage`/`Logistics`/`CreativeStage`/`Targeter` for their own markers and orbit lines. Each of those still accepts an absent policy and builds its own from the same four inputs, which is what keeps them callable outside the map path rather than a second live judgment — one shared instance is what guarantees the pick set and the drawn set can't be answered from different focus ids or camera positions. It answers one struct, `MapVisibility {category, icon, label, orbit, pickable}`: `category` is the class's own on/off (the thing a hidden class fails first, short-circuiting everything else to false), `icon`/`label` are the marker dot and the name independently, `orbit` is the orbit/reference line, and `pickable` is `icon || label` — **an object nobody can see must not be clickable**, which is exactly what a separate hit-test filter would let drift. `body(id)` resolves a Lagrange point (id suffix `-l1`…`-l5`) off the `lagrange*` toggles, an unregistered id to all-false, and anything else through `bodyClassOf` → the class toggles, with `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` forcing `icon`/`label` true for the star, the focused body's immediate family, and the camera's current system; `orbit` additionally applies the satellite/focus-system rule above. `entity(kind, isActivePlayer)` does the same for `'player' | 'ship' | 'ammo' | 'base'` off their own four toggles apiece — with one exception: the **operated** ship keeps `category`/`icon` true even with its category switched off, since losing your own position on the map is never a display preference; its label and orbit line still follow the toggles, so the exception can't quietly re-enable what was turned off.
- `celestial-registry.ts` — `CELESTIAL_BODIES: Record<SolarSystemId, { name: string; create(): CelestialBody }>` — keyed by `solar-system.ts`'s `SolarSystemId` (not the general `AttractorId`), so this table stays exhaustive over `SOLAR_SYSTEM`'s full body set (101 as of this writing — 1 star, 49 `kind: 'planet'` bodies, 51 `kind: 'satellite'` bodies) exactly as before the id type opened up: registering another body in `SOLAR_SYSTEM` still makes this table fail to compile until it too gets an entry. **The single definition of each real solar-system body's display name** — `hud/frame-labels.ts`'s `celestialBodyName`, `focus-markers.ts`, and `creative/object-placer-panel.ts` all read it rather than spelling the name out again. Japanese display strings never appear under `physics/`. `fallbackCelestialView(registry, id)` covers the complementary case — a body a caller's active registry actually has but this table doesn't (a custom-registry stage's invented body) — returning a `SunBody`-equivalent for `registry[id].isStar`(-classified bodies) and a plain solid sphere otherwise, sized from the registry's own `radius`; it's a runtime fallback, not a second `Record`, so it carries no exhaustiveness guarantee and none is needed — an id a caller reaches this way is by definition outside `SOLAR_SYSTEM`. Every planet other than Earth is a real-photography textured sphere: `CelestialSurface.textured(textureUrl, widthSegments, heightSegments)` (`render/celestial-surface.ts`) loads one of the `src/assets/2k_*.jpg` textures (Solar System Scope, CC BY 4.0 — credited in `README.md`; Venus's is its cloud-deck atmosphere texture, since the surface itself is never visible), built via the shared `planetEntry(id, name, textureUrl, pointBrightness?)` registry helper — it reads `rings: RingSystemDef | undefined` straight off `bodyDef(SOLAR_SYSTEM, id)` (only present when `def.kind === 'planet'`) and forwards it to whichever of `SphereBody`/`PointBody` it constructs. Every `buildSurface` factory in this file is `(level: SphereLodLevel) => CelestialSurface`, called once per `screen-lod.ts` `SPHERE_LOD_LADDER` tier by `SphereBody`/`PointBody`'s own constructor (see those files below) — passing `level.widthSegments`/`level.heightSegments` straight through is what lets `CelestialSurface`'s internal geometry pool share one set of unit-sphere tiers across the whole registry instead of building one per body. **`pointBrightness` (`undefined` by default) is what switches a planet's `create()` from `SphereBody` to `point-body.ts`'s `PointBody`** — venus/jupiter=`'bright'`, mercury/mars/saturn=`'medium'`, uranus=`'faint'`; neptune and every dwarf-planet/large-asteroid/comet-nucleus body stay `SphereBody`, since past +6 apparent magnitude a combat-view point sprite would be dishonest about what's actually visible to the eye. Every moon (this file's satellites) stays a flat-shaded, single-colored sphere (`CelestialSurface.solid(color, widthSegments, heightSegments)`, no texture, via `satelliteEntry(id, name, color)`, displayed at the same `MOON_VIS_DIST` compressed distance the Moon uses) — their look is intentionally unfinished, since registering them exists to prove a body can be added, not to art-direct each one.
