The 41 non-planet heliocentric bodies — the original 9 (ケレス/ベスタ/パラス/冥王星/ハウメア/マケマケ/エリス/ハレー彗星/エンケ彗星) plus 32 further dwarf planets/large asteroids/comet nuclei/NEOs registered later — are likewise flat-shaded solid spheres, via the sibling helper `solidPlanetEntry(id, name, color)` — same look, but displayed at `PLANET_VIS_DIST` (the textured-planet distance) rather than `MOON_VIS_DIST`, since they orbit the star directly rather than a planet. Ring data itself is registered per-body in `solar-system.ts`'s `SOLAR_SYSTEM` (jupiter/saturn/uranus/neptune/quaoar/chariklo — see that file's entry below), not here. 衛星は `texturedSatelliteEntry`(実写の全球モザイクが手に入った6体 — フォボス/イオ/エウロパ/ガニメデ/カリスト/タイタン、いずれも USGS Astrogeology のパブリックドメイン画像)と、それ以外の全衛星(トリトン/ダイモスに加え、木星以遠の不規則衛星や準惑星・小天体の衛星まで)が使う `satelliteEntry`(単色)に分かれる。**実写が無い天体に想像図を貼らない**ので、この分岐は「作り込みの手間」ではなく「実データの有無」で決まる。
- `point-body.ts` — `PointBody`: the combat-view stand-in for a planet too small on screen for `SphereBody`'s visual-distance compression to read as anything but a sub-pixel speck. It is a **wholesale model swap by view, not a mode branch inside `SphereBody`**: map view (`cameraSystem.overviewMode`) draws the exact same real-position/real-radius(-or-shape)/self-rotating group `SphereBody` would (spin via `Ephemeris.poleAt`; the constructor resolves `shapeAxes(radius, shape)` the same way `SphereBody` does, so a shaped body's dwarf-planet-scale point still reads flattened once zoomed into map view; an optional `RingView`, built and scaled the same sibling-not-child way `SphereBody` builds one, is shown here and hidden in combat view — a point sprite has no room to also show a ring); combat view instead hides that group (and the ring group, if any) and places a `render/billboard.ts` `Billboard` at `camera.position + direction-to-body × STAR_SHELL_RADIUS` — the same camera-following shell the star field sits on, so the point never drifts relative to the stars as the camera moves. `PointBrightness` (`'bright' | 'medium' | 'faint'`) is a fixed 3-step table of billboard scale/opacity fed in at construction by `celestial-registry.ts`'s `pointBrightness` argument — this module holds no magnitude physics of its own, just the three preset levels. **The physical (non-billboard) side rides the same `screen-lod.ts` `SPHERE_LOD_LADDER`/`showsPhysicalSphere` machinery as `SphereBody`**, constructed and toggled identically (a `THREE.Group` of per-tier meshes, transform written once on the group). The billboard fallback is combat-view-only, gated on `showsPhysicalSphere` returning false for the body's true (uncompressed) apparent diameter; map view instead just hides the group outright below that threshold (a billboard sprite would misrepresent the exact-position display map view otherwise guarantees).
- `environment-scene.ts` — `EnvironmentScene` (moved from `render/`, see above): owns the star shell, `CelestialGrid`, ambient light, the `CelestialBody[]` built from `CELESTIAL_BODIES` (one per registry entry, in declaration order), and `point-field-view.ts`'s `PointFieldView` (the main-belt/Trojan/Hilda/Kuiper-belt/scattered-disk point clouds — a map-only display built from the ephemeris, so it belongs here alongside the other celestial views rather than under `physics/`). The map-only reference `OrbitLine`s are **data-driven, not one field per body**: a `ReadonlyMap<OrbitingId, OrbitLine>` is built once, one entry per orbiting body registered in `SOLAR_SYSTEM` (a satellite's line colored the old moon-line gray `0xaab3c0`, a planet's white), and the shared `orbitElementsFor(id, simTime)` resolves each one's center — a satellite's own planet, a planet the sun (a fresh sun `Attractor` from `ephemeris.stateOf('sun', t)` each call, since the sun itself moves in ECI) — so registering a body needs no new field or branch here. The one exception is the geostationary reference line (`geoLine`), which isn't a celestial body at all and stays its own field. `syncReferenceLines(simTime, fo, overviewMode, focusId, toggles, sharedVisibilityPolicy, nearbyIds, camera, cameraPos)` decides *which* reference lines actually draw, via `MapVisibilityPolicy.body(id).orbit` — a star's line always shows (while `overviewMode`); a planet's, dwarf-planet's or small-body's line follows that class's **orbit** flag in `body-visibility.ts`'s `BodyClassToggles` (`toggles.planetOrbit`/`toggles.dwarfOrbit`/`toggles.smallBodyOrbit`, from `cameraSystem.bodyClassToggles`, forwarded in by `sync`) — independent of the icon/label flags the same body's map marker reads, so a body can show its icon and/or name without its ellipse or the reverse; a satellite's line follows `MapVisibilityPolicy`'s own focus-system rule the same way. A shown line is drawn even where it passes through the body it belongs to (the body's own opaque, depth-writing mesh occludes it there via the ordinary depth test — see `render/curve.ts` below for why no further trick is needed), but `private referenceLineOpacityAt(id, dist)` fades a planet's/satellite's line out below `const.ts`'s `PLANET_ORBIT_LINE_FADE_NEAR_DIST`/`SATELLITE_ORBIT_LINE_FADE_NEAR_DIST` and up to full `REFERENCE_LINE_OPACITY` at `..._FADE_FAR_DIST`, `dist` being the camera's distance (`cameraPos`, the caller's already-resolved `CameraSystem.activeCameraPos`) to the body's true ECI position — a reference line close to a small, unzoomed body would otherwise read as visual noise around it. **`update(t, overviewMode)`** re-derives the point field's positions at the display time (a no-op while `!overviewMode`, since the field is never drawn in combat view) — called from `Game.update`, ahead of everything else that block updates, so the field's coordinates are as fresh as the candidate set/camera built from the same frame (see `game.ts` below). `sync(playerPos, floatingOrigin, displayTime, cameraSystem, gridVisibility, sharedVisibilityPolicy?)` — `playerPos: Vec3 | null`, `null` with no ship flown, in which case the sunlit-fraction lookup is skipped and lighting stays undimmed — computes the sunlit fraction once, feeds it to the `SunBody` instance it picks out of the array, forwards `cameraSystem.mapCamera.focus` into `syncReferenceLines`, syncs `pointFieldView` (transform-only — it holds no per-frame physics of its own), then just loops `for (const body of this.bodies) body.sync(...)` — adding a body to the registry needs no change here. The same sunlit fraction feeds two lighting consumers from one place: the scene's own `THREE.DirectionalLight`/`THREE.AmbientLight` (`game/const.ts`'s `SUN_INTENSITY`/`AMBIENT_INTENSITY`/`SHADOW_MIN_SUN`/`SHADOW_MIN_AMBIENT` floored in directly) and, via the constructor-injected `render/pipeline/sun-light.ts` `SunLight.set(...)`, the deferred lighting pass — this class is `SunLight`'s sole writer, pushing the *raw* `SUN_INTENSITY`/`AMBIENT_INTENSITY`/occlusion scalar and leaving the `SHADOW_MIN_*` floor to `SunLight`'s own `sunVisibility()`/`ambientIntensity` (see that module's entry for why the floor moves there rather than being pre-applied here). **Neither light ever shades a lit-opaque mesh directly any more** — every `MeshStandardMaterial` ship/debris/casing stands on `render/pipeline/lit-layer.ts`'s `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` alone (see that module below), invisible to the world pass's own default-channel camera, so the two lights' intensity/position only feed `SunLight` and the deferred material pass now. They still each carry `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` too (`.layers.enable(LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER)`, set once at construction, on top of their default channel) for a reason that has nothing to do with shading anything directly: three.js drops a light from a camera's render list whenever `light.layers.test(camera.layers)` fails, and both `GBufferPass` and `MaterialPass` narrow the camera to that one channel for the duration of their own `render()` call — with the lights absent from that restricted view, `NodeMaterial.setupLighting` would never find a light to invoke a lit-opaque mesh's custom `setupLightingModel()` with (three.js only calls it once some light clears that same per-camera test), and every ship would render flat black (`vec3(0)`) however correct the G-buffer/light-prepass output is. `dispose()` takes the whole environment back down — the geostationary reference ring and every built reference line, the two lights, the star shell, `CelestialGrid`/`SpatialGrid` (each of which also detaches the scale labels it appended outside `#hud`), every `CelestialBody` (`CelestialBody.dispose()` is abstract, so a newly registered body has to answer for its own resources before it compiles), and the point field if the map was ever opened.
- `src/audio/` — WebAudio, all synthesized at runtime (no audio-file assets), split by concern into the shared substrate at the root plus a `bgm/` and an `sfx/` folder: `audio-engine.ts`'s `AudioEngine` owns the AudioContext lifecycle (`unlock()` needs a real user gesture — `Launcher.startRun` wires it, together with `Bgm.ensureStarted()`, to `Input.onUserGesture`; also resumes a browser-suspended context) plus the shared white-noise buffer and the `tone`/`noiseBurst` one-shot primitive voices; `sfx/world-sfx.ts`'s `WorldSfx` builds the sounds emitted by objects and events in the game world — the CIWS-motif gun one-shots (motor-whine spin-up, heavy discrete reports), hits/clanks/explosions, the ship's altitude-alarm klaxon, and the thrust/RCS loop channels (composed lazily on the first `setThrust`/`setRcs` call once the context exists) — on that substrate, while `sfx/ui-sfx.ts`'s `UiSfx` holds the position-less operation/notification blips (`warp`); every consumer is injected with only the class it actually sounds (`Logistics` alone takes both); the BGM is split three ways along the boundary between *when to play*, *what to play*, and *the values that decide it*. **`bgm/bgm.ts`'s `Bgm` is the app-facing window** — the user volume as a **master gain** that outlives everything below it, the one `LOOKAHEAD_SEC` scheduler pump, and the musical lines it advances with it; **`bgm/conductor.ts`'s `Conductor` is one such line** — a continuous musical program that decides which track it plays and when it gives way to the next (rotation every `TRACK_ROTATION_SEC`, fixed per line rather than toggled), holding the piece currently sounding. The role is a class rather than something `Bgm` merely performs because it is **plural**: auditioning a track in the settings view and the gameplay music are two independent lines that must not perturb each other, which is what the adaptive-music work needs; **`bgm/track-playback.ts`'s `TrackPlayback` is one sounding piece** — its own gain (that piece's fade envelope, feeding the master), its `Composer`, the track's instruments built once and looked up by id (an unknown or duplicated id throws rather than silently going quiet), its step position, `scheduleUntil(deadline)`/`playNote`, and — for the owner to retire it with — `soundingUntil` (the time its last scheduled note goes quiet, the instrument's own release included) plus a `dispose()` that disconnects its instruments and then its gain. **The three gain layers are deliberately separate**: the master (user volume), one per `Conductor` (ducking that line — `pause()`/`resume()`), and one per `TrackPlayback` (that piece's own fade). One node doing two of those jobs would have two writers ramping the same `AudioParam`, where the later call cancels the earlier one's shape — and a line ducked through the piece's gain would come back up un-ducked the moment rotation opened a new piece. **A discarded playback is disconnected, not merely faded**: a node that is still connected is still part of the audio graph and is never collected, so leaving one behind strands its gain and every instrument's panner on the master for the rest of the session. `Conductor.retire` is the single path — taken by both `stop()` and `openPlayback()` — and it waits until that playback's own `soundingUntil`, **not** until its fade completes: rotation deliberately does not fade at all, and once a piece's oscillators have stopped nothing flows through its gain whatever the fade is doing. Rotation opens the next playback at the outgoing one's `nextStepTime`, so the beat does not break across a track change, and applies only to a line constructed with rotation on — the audition line never rotates, since a sound test that wandered off after five minutes would not be one. A `Conductor` holds `TrackPlayback | null` rather than a list because nothing plays two pieces at once yet; a crossfade is what would make that field plural, which is the reason the unit was extracted at all. `Bgm.ensureStarted()` begins playback once on the first unlocked gesture — guarded by a one-shot `autoStartUsed`, since that gesture hook fires on every keypress and a decided run's `stop()` must not be undone by the next one — while `playAudition`/`stopAudition` drive the settings view's preview — opening that view calls `Bgm.beginAudition()` (ducking the ambient line) and closing it calls `endAudition()` (disposing the audition line and un-ducking the ambient one), so the view carries no audio state of its own — a line that was silent at open is ducked and un-ducked back to silence, which is what makes a run-end fade-out impossible to resurrect without a flag; a decided run fades it out via `Launcher`. **`bgm/composer.ts`'s `Composer` is the note-generation seam**: `stepDurSec` plus `notesAt(step): readonly ComposerNote[]`, where a `ComposerNote` names the `instrument` that sounds it, its `velocity` (0..1 musical intent, not a gain), and its onset as an offset from the step rather than an absolute time. **How a note actually sounds is `bgm/instrument.ts`'s `Instrument`** — `play(freq, when, durationSec, velocity)` plus `dispose()` (required, since an instrument holds its persistent nodes from construction and they must be disconnected when the piece is retired), implemented under `bgm/instruments/` and selected per track by `bgm/instrument-factory.ts`, the same union-plus-factory shape the composers use. The split is that a composer says *what to play and how hard* while an instrument says *what that sounds like*, so DSP can grow without a composer ever naming a WebAudio type. `bgm/instruments/tone-instrument.ts`'s `ToneInstrument` is the plainest implementation (one oscillator, linear attack into exponential decay, plus a persistent `StereoPannerNode` — **pan is a per-instrument parameter**, which is how the BGM gets stereo); `bgm/instruments/unison-instrument.ts`'s `UnisonInstrument` stacks `voices` oscillators spread symmetrically across ±`detuneCents` through one shared `BiquadFilterNode`, dividing the peak by the voice count so `level` keeps its meaning as voices are added. Instruments hold their persistent nodes from construction and build only per-note nodes in `play`, and **must derive every time from `when` rather than reading `ctx.currentTime`**, since the scheduler runs `LOOKAHEAD_SEC` ahead. It names no WebAudio type at all, so an algorithm's output can be read and compared without an `AudioContext` — which is how a change to one is verified. **`bgm/composers/phasing-composer.ts`'s `PhasingComposer` is the algorithm the shipped tracks use** — the Steve-Reich-style ambient minimal piece (modal quartal pitch set, two pulse patterns of coprime lengths phasing against each other, pads/drone/sparkle over the top, sine/triangle only) — and **`bgm/tracks/tracks.ts`** holds the values it consumes. `BgmTrack` is a **discriminated union over `kind`** — `{kind, name, params}`, where `name` is the only field common to every kind (it is what a consumer reads without caring which algorithm is behind it, e.g. `SettingsView`'s preview list) and `params`' shape is that kind's own. `'phasing'`'s `PhasingParams` describes a track in full: its scale and step length, the two pulse voices (pattern, waveform, level, optional detuned harmonic), the pad/drone/sparkle layers with their own cadences, and the two `PhaseCycle`s (transposition and octave) whose periods multiply out into the track's super-cycle. A second algorithm therefore arrives as another `Composer` implementation with its own params type and one more union member, without `Bgm` changing: **`bgm/composer-factory.ts`'s `createComposer(track)` is the single place the union is switched on**, and its `default` branch assigns to `never`, so adding a `kind` without a factory branch is a compile error rather than a runtime fallthrough. It lives in its own file rather than in `composer.ts` because it must import every implementation, and the implementations import the seam — putting it there would be an import cycle, and keeping the seam dependency-free is what lets a new composer be written without touching it. `bgm/composers/antipode-composer.ts`'s `AntipodeComposer` is the second implementation, still a sketch: one stab layer that strikes a chord every `everySteps`, its members given as `scale` indices. **Its transposition and its chord progression advance on separate cadences** — the stab fires every `everySteps`, but the chord itself only advances after `repeatFor` firings (so the same chord is struck `repeatFor` times running before the next one), while `transpose` turns over on its own `everySteps` with no relation to either. The three drift against each other, so a repeated chord is not heard identically each time whenever transpose happens to turn over mid-repeat, and the whole combination only repeats at their least common multiple. Any number of further layers — `arps: AntipodeArpLayer[]` — each step through their own `notes` list once per their own `everySteps`, one index at a time rather than a chord's worth at once, the same shared `transpose` applied to all; the layers don't coordinate with each other or with `stab`, so several sharing the same `everySteps` but a different `notes` length still drift apart. None carries a `durationSec` of its own; each note is given `stepDur * everySteps` (fills exactly to the next one, an unbroken hand-off) rather than adding a field a track author hasn't asked for. `bgm/composers/utils.ts` holds what the composers share: `phaseValue` (the value a `PhaseCycle` holds at a step), `cycleAt` (the element a list holds at a step, for a cadence independent of that list's length), and `scaleFreq` (index→frequency, octave wrap-around included). The pitch sets and the cycles themselves stay per-track data.
- `src/physics/kinematic-state.ts` — the `KinematicState` type (`{t, r, v}`, built only via `kinematicState(t, r, v)`) plus the geometry derivable from a state vector alone: the orbital basis `orbitAxes(state)` → `{pro, nrm, radOut}` plus `fromOrbitAxes(state, x)`, the one place that basis is built (the Δv edit keys, the Δv arm screen directions, the PROGRADE/NORMAL/RADIAL markers and every `norm(cross(r, v))` spelling all route through it — note `radOut` is the in-plane direction perpendicular to the velocity, which equals r̂ only when r⊥v; the name follows the HUD's RADIAL OUT/IN labels, not the true radial), and `hermiteInterpolate` (cubic Hermite between two `KinematicState`s, using their velocities as tangents; always refuses a `t` outside the two epochs, since the cubic diverges fast outside the span). Surface contact with *any* attractor is `attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody`, which is what every `checkLoss` uses; altitude above Earth specifically (for the atmosphere-only callers — `atmosphere.ts`'s density table, `thermal.ts`'s drag/heating, the descent-warning EMA) is `solar-system.ts`'s `earthAltitudeOf`. Pure functions, no Earth constants — those live in `solar-system.ts` alongside the registry that names Earth.
- `src/physics/elements.ts` — the classical orbital `OrbitalElements` type and the state↔elements conversions. **`OrbitalElements.center: Attractor` replaces a bare `mu`**: an orbit's shape alone doesn't say what it's an orbit *around*, and comparing/displaying elements needs that answer (which body's radius the apsis altitudes are measured from, which body's name a HUD readout shows) — so the element set carries its own center rather than leaving the caller to remember it separately. `orbitalElementsFromState(rel, center)` takes a **center-relative** state (not absolute ECI — the argument name and its own comment say so) and is called from exactly one place; the sole entry point every other module uses is `attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf(s, center)`, which converts the absolute ECI state itself before calling in. `keplerPeriod(a, mu)`/`semiMajorFromPeriod(T, mu)` keep a plain `mu` argument (they only ever need the number, not a full `Attractor`, and are used before any `Attractor` exists yet — e.g. `CreativeStage`'s size-from-period input mode); `keplerPeriod` is the sole place a semi-major axis becomes an orbital period, and `OrbitalElements.period` routes through it. `OrbitalElements` carries no altitude of its own — periapsis/apoapsis depend on a body radius — so `apsisAltitudes(el)` reads it off `el.center.radius`. Kepler helpers for maneuver planning (`trueAnomalyAt`, `tofBetween`, `positionOnOrbit`/`velocityOnOrbit`, `stateFromOrbitalElements` — all round-trip-verified to machine precision; `stateFromOrbitalElements` returns a center-relative state too, absolute ECI conversion is the caller's job). `nodeAnomalies(el, planeNormal)` is the single implementation of "where does this orbit cross a given plane" — the true anomalies of the ascending/descending nodes (`planeNormal × el.hHat` for the line of nodes, so it points at the ascending one), `null` when the two planes nearly coincide; `plan/plan-display.ts`'s equatorial-node icons and `nav-target.ts`'s relative AN/DN both call it rather than each rederiving the line-of-nodes cross product. Pure functions, no Earth constants. `eccentricAnomalyFromMean(m, e)`'s Newton-method initial guess switches at `e > 0.8`: below that it starts from `E = M`, but at high eccentricity `M`'s own near-0 region makes that starting point converge slowly or oscillate, so it instead starts from `±π` on the same side as `M`.
- `src/physics/attractor.ts` — the shape every gravity source shares. `AttractorId`/`OrbitingId` are plain `string` (not a closed union) because the set of registered bodies is a runtime choice (`solar-system.ts`'s `CelestialRegistry`, injected per `Ephemeris` instance) — the exhaustiveness that keeps a body's display name from being forgotten lives one level down, in each concrete registry's own `keyof` (`solar-system.ts`'s `SolarSystemId`), not here. `Attractor` (`{id, mu, radius, state: KinematicState, accel: Vec3, degree2: Degree2Gravity | null, isStar}`) holds position/velocity as one timed `KinematicState` rather than separate `r`/`v` fields — so a position and a velocity from different instants can never be paired by mistake; Earth pinned at the origin under the default registry. `accel` is the body's own ECI acceleration at `state.t`; `attractorPositionAt(a, t)` is the sole place it's used to extrapolate the body's position ballistically (`r + v·s + ½·accel·s²`) to a time other than `state.t`, so an RK4 stage evaluated away from a step's midpoint can still ask each body where it was at that stage's own time rather than treating every body as fixed for the whole step. `Degree2Gravity` (`{j2, refRadius, pole, tesseral}`) is the body's own degree-2 gravity field, resolved to concrete ECI vectors at the lookup time; `null` means "treat as a point mass". `tesseral` (`{c22, longAxis}`) is likewise `null` for an axisymmetric field. **`refRadius` is the radius the coefficients were defined against and is deliberately separate from `radius`** (the Moon's is 1738.0 km against a 1737.4 km surface radius), so updating the surface radius can never silently rescale the gravity field. `isStar` is what `dynamics.ts` sums solar radiation pressure over (every star, not "the" one), so a registry with zero or several stars degrades safely instead of hard-coding `id === 'sun'`. The pure functions built on `Attractor`: `attractorAccel(r, attractor, t)` (one body's own contribution to the ECI equation of motion — `μ_b[(r_b−r)/|r_b−r|³ − r_b/|r_b|³]`, `r_b` being `attractorPositionAt(attractor, t)` rather than `attractor.state.r` directly, the second term correcting for ECI itself free-falling toward that body, and each term skipped at zero distance so a body coincident with the query point — or the origin body itself — can't blow up); `strongestAttractor(r, attractors, excludeId?)` (the body with the largest `attractorAccel` magnitude — deliberately not the largest raw μ/d², which hands the sun a false win ~2.6×10⁵ km from Earth by ignoring that the ECI origin is comoving with it; the optional `excludeId` drops one body from consideration by id, for a caller whose own position would otherwise win at zero distance); `localOrbitPeriod(r, attractors)` (the circular period around whichever body that is); `frameOfAttractor(center)` (the identity-rotation `FrameTransform` centered on an already-looked-up `Attractor`, so `orbitalElementsOf` doesn't need to re-query the ephemeris for a snapshot it already has); `orbitalElementsOf(s, center)` (osculating `OrbitalElements` around a caller-chosen body, via `frame.ts`'s `toFrameState`); and `reachedBody(prev, next, attractors, margin)` (a `BodyImpact` — the body whose `radius + margin` surface the step from `prev` to `next` reached, earliest first, paired with the state at the moment of that reach (`hermiteInterpolate` at the swept crossing's toi, or `prev`/`next` itself on the discrete fallback) — `null` for none — the path is swept as a Hermite curve via `sphere-contact.ts`'s `sweptHermiteSphereToi`, so a coarse step cannot tunnel through a body between its two endpoints, with a `containingBody` point test as the fallback for the two cases the sweep leaves to a discrete solver: a state already inside a body, and a zero-length interval. Each body is held fixed at the one snapshot position it was passed, since a body moves far less within a step than the entity does relative to it). This module holds no position-dependent narrowing of its own — **the only thing that ever reduces which bodies enter a gravity sum is `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s spatial-grid classification** (`classifyAttractors`/`attractorsNearInto`, over `physics/spatial-grid.ts`'s `SpatialGrid`), which is what actually lowers the summation's order; a position-dependent filter evaluating `attractorAccel` itself (as a straight distance/magnitude cutoff once did) costs the same O(N·M) the sum it's supposed to cheapen already costs, so it was removed rather than kept as a second, redundant narrowing. `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` is the sole place all bodies' current `r`/`v` are assembled into an `attractors` array, in registry declaration order — used both for gravity (after `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s `gravityBodiesAt` narrows it to `mu !== 0` and merges in any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`) and for every other "which bodies are there" question (occlusion, surface contact, center-body resolution, integration/sampling-interval step sizing), since there is exactly one window, not two.

  This module intentionally joins only two things — summing gravity, and handing out a body to center an ellipse on — and nothing more. Five once-conflated concerns stay structurally separate, with no umbrella concept binding them: **which gravity sources the integrator sums** (always all three, decided nowhere — `attractorAccel`/`dynamics.ts`); **which body an analytic ellipse (`OrbitalElements`) is centered on** (chosen per call site, typically `strongestAttractor` → `orbitalElementsOf`, and re-derived every frame as a point-in-time display rather than cached as if it belonged to the entity); **which frame a *sampled* trajectory is baked into for display** (the existing `Frame`/`DisplayWindowManager.frame` machinery, untouched by this module — a sampled polyline must stay in one frame for its whole length, so it is never derived from the per-frame ellipse-center choice); **where the overview camera looks and which frame it's fixed to** (`MapCamera.focus`/`cameraFrame`, likewise untouched); and **the time scale sizing integration/sampling steps** (`localOrbitPeriod`, independent of the ellipse-center choice). No function here derives one of these from another, and there is no GUI to pick an ellipse's center — it is always resolved via `strongestAttractor`.
- `src/physics/ecliptic.ts` — the ecliptic basis and its relation to ECI (Y = north): `Q_ECL_TO_ECI` (Z-up ecliptic → ECI, used by rotation-basis code), `Q_ECLY_TO_ECI` (Y-up ecliptic → ECI, used wherever a Kepler element set built in ecliptic coordinates is turned into an ECI state via `stateFromOrbitalElements`, which assumes Y = pole), `ECL_POLE`/`ECL_POLE_ECI`/`ECL_VERNAL`, `eclToEci`/`eciToEcl`, plus `ECI_POLE` (the ECI polar axis, Y — the one constant every "is this body's spin axis the one that defines ECI" check reads) and the exported `stdToEci(xs, ys, zs)`/`raDecToEci(raDeg, decDeg)` (standard equatorial X=vernal-equinox/Z=north-pole coordinates, and IAU right-ascension/declination pairs, both converted straight to ECI — `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinPhaseRef` reads `ECI_POLE` and `ephemeris.ts`'s `'iau'` pole resolution reads `raDecToEci`, so a body's ECI-frame pole direction is never derived a second way). The obliquity (23.439291°) is a fixed J2000-equivalent constant — equinox precession (25,772-year cycle, ~5.6° of equatorial drift per 1000 years) is out of scope, since it's a question of what ECI itself means (J2 axis, atmosphere co-rotation, Earth mesh spin axis, celestial grid all assume it fixed), not an orbital detail.
- `src/physics/kepler-orbit.ts` — the orbit math shared by every classification: `KeplerOrbit` (Kepler elements plus each angle's secular rate, fixed to a caller-chosen reference plane — see `basisToEci` below) and its evaluators `keplerOrbitState`/`keplerOrbitRotation`/`keplerOrbitNormal`. Angles are composed mean longitude L (one full turn per orbital period) → mean anomaly M = L − ϖ → true anomaly ν, then u = ν + (ϖ − Ω) for the in-plane angle — computing u directly from L would let precession speed up or slow down the apparent revolution. **`KeplerOrbit.basisToEci: Quat` is the reference plane the elements (inc/raan/argp) are measured against** (z = the plane's normal, x = the elements' own origin direction) → ECI; every evaluator — position/velocity, `normalFromAngles`, `directionFromAngles`, `rotationFromAngles` — routes its basis-relative vector through the single module-private `toEci(orbit, x, y, z)`, so the reference plane can never be applied inconsistently between an orbit's position and its rotation basis. This module holds no fixed plane of its own: `ECLIPTIC_BASIS` (= `ecliptic.ts`'s `Q_ECL_TO_ECI`) is exported as the default a caller passes in, not read internally — `planet-orbit.ts`'s `planetOrbit` always uses it (a planet's orbit is always measured from the ecliptic), while `satellite-orbit.ts`'s `satelliteOrbit` takes an optional override for a satellite whose elements are given against its planet's equator instead (see below). `keplerOrbitState` reuses `elements.ts`'s `stateFromOrbitalElements` (via `basisToEci` composed with the module's own `Q_ZUP_TO_YUP`, since that function assumes Y = pole) rather than a second position/velocity implementation; velocity adds the rotating frame's own angular velocity (ω×r, precession's contribution) on top of the radial rate. `keplerOrbitMeanDirection` returns the direction the **mean** longitude points (in-plane angle `u = L − Ω`, mean anomaly rather than true), which is what a synchronously rotating body's prime meridian tracks — a uniformly spinning body cannot follow the non-uniform true anomaly, and the two differ by the equation of center (up to 6.3° at the Moon's eccentricity). Knows nothing about stars/planets/satellites — that classification is `planet-orbit.ts`/`satellite-orbit.ts`'s.
- `src/physics/planet-orbit.ts` — a planet's orbit is its **planet-satellite system barycenter**'s heliocentric Kepler orbit, not the planet body's own (Earth is not light enough relative to the Moon, 1:81, to skip the ~4,673 km barycenter offset) — `PlanetOrbit = KeplerOrbit` plus secular element rates in deg/Julian-century (`planetOrbit`, which takes `lRateDegPerCentury` — Standish's mean-longitude rate L̇ passed straight through — rather than an orbital period, so no caller has to derive one from the other). `planetAngles` derives the sun-direction angles a satellite model needs (mean anomaly/longitude and their rates) straight from this barycenter orbit, so satellite evaluation never depends on the satellite's own state to find the sun. `AU` (the astronomical unit in meters) is exported from here too.
- `src/physics/satellite-orbit.ts` — a satellite's orbit is a two-body Kepler ellipse around its planet **plus, optionally, solar perturbation** (secular node regression/perigee advance via `raanRate`/`lonPeriRate` — the sign is this module's own, never spelled out at a call site — and periodic longitude/latitude/distance correction terms, `PerturbationTerm[]`, summed as sine/cosine series in the four fundamental arguments D/M/M′/F). `satelliteOrbit`'s `basisToEci?: Quat` picks the reference plane the two-body elements are measured against (`kepler-orbit.ts`'s `KeplerOrbit.basisToEci`), defaulting to the ecliptic (`ECLIPTIC_BASIS`) when omitted — the Moon's own call site leaves it at that default; `solar-system.ts`'s `equatorialSatelliteOrbit` overrides it to the parent planet's equatorial plane instead (see below). `satelliteState`'s periodic-term decomposition works in ecliptic (λ, β, r) coordinates and diverges (`rho0 → 0`) for an orbit whose plane passes through the ecliptic pole, which every equator-referenced satellite's does not rule out; since a satellite with **no periodic terms at all has nothing for that decomposition to add**, `satelliteState` checks `lonTerms`/`latTerms`/`distTerms` are all empty and returns the two-body `keplerOrbitState` result directly in that case, skipping the decomposition rather than routing zero-amplitude corrections through a coordinate system that can blow up. The Moon's own perturbation table (only body with one) precesses at the *measured* periods (18.612958y node / 8.85y perigee), not first-order theory's (which under-predicts perigee precession by ~2×, the historical problem Clairaut resolved with second-order terms), and its two-body part alone must never duplicate the periodic table's central-difference-equivalent terms (arguments that are `m`-only or `f`-only) — that's double counting. `orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt` are built from the mean (two-body) elements only, so the Moon's true position can drift up to ~2.5° off that basis's x̂ (the sum of the periodic-term amplitudes); a perturbation-free satellite has no such drift, since its two-body elements are its entire model.
- `src/physics/solar-system.ts` — `CelestialBodyDef` (the star/planet/satellite discriminated union — this `kind` switch is the one exhaustive switch over a registered id's classification that's allowed, since it's a physical claim, not a registry lookup) and `CelestialRegistry` (`Readonly<Record<AttractorId, CelestialBodyDef>>`), the shape any `Ephemeris` reads. `SOLAR_SYSTEM` is one instance of that shape — the real solar system, and every `Ephemeris`'s default — declared with `satisfies CelestialRegistry` rather than a type annotation so `SolarSystemId = keyof typeof SOLAR_SYSTEM` derives its literal union (101 members as of this writing — 1 star, 49 `kind: 'planet'`, 51 `kind: 'satellite'`) straight from the data: registering a body means adding it to `SOLAR_SYSTEM` alone, and `game/celestial/celestial-registry.ts`'s `Record<SolarSystemId, …>` goes red until that id gets a display view too. `bodyDef(registry, id)`/`primaryOf(registry, id)`/`starOf(registry)` all take the registry as an explicit argument rather than reading `SOLAR_SYSTEM` directly. `starOf` is the sole place a registry is scanned for `kind: 'star'`, returning `null` for zero stars and throwing for more than one — this codebase supports a single primary star per registry only; genuinely comparable-mass bodies orbiting each other are `Asteroid`'s numerical n-body, not a registry concept (see `game/game-entity/asteroid.ts` below). `primaryOf` maps a body to its displayed parent (a satellite's planet, a planet's `starOf` result, and **`null` for a star itself** as well as for a registry with none — a star is never its own parent, so `null` is a usable "this is the root" signal for anything walking the hierarchy) and is the single implementation of that lookup, replacing five call sites that used to each spell out `def.kind === 'planet' ? 'sun' : def.planet`. `GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT` [m³/(kg·s²)] is the one physical constant this file adds beyond registry data — `Asteroid` is its sole reader, deriving `mu = G·mass` from a single mass rather than the registry's convention of a directly-measured `mu`. `MU_SUN`/`R_SUN`/`MU_MOON`/`R_MOON`/`MU_EARTH`/`R_EARTH`/`R_EARTH_EQ`/`SIDEREAL_DAY` are `SOLAR_SYSTEM`'s own numeric facts, read by callers elsewhere in `physics/` (`ephemeris.ts`'s Earth `Attractor`, `dynamics.ts`'s `degree2Accel`, `atmosphere.ts`'s co-rotating-atmosphere rate) and in `game/`. `earthAltitudeOf(r)` (position vector → altitude above Earth) sits here rather than in `kinematic-state.ts` since it's the only function that reads `R_EARTH`. The degree-2 gravity coefficients live here too — `J2_EARTH`, and `J2_MOON`/`C22_MOON`/`R_MOON_GRAVITY`/`MOON_OBLIQUITY` — all **unnormalized**. Each body's `degree2?: Degree2GravityDef` states only its own coefficients (j2/c22/refRadius) — omitting it makes a body a point mass. A body's spin axis/phase is a separate, optional field one level up: `CelestialBodyDef.pole?: PoleModel` (`'eciPole'` for the body that defines ECI; `'cassini'` for a synchronously rotating satellite, from its equatorial tilt and orbit normal; `'iau'` for a body whose pole right ascension/declination and rotation phase W are each given as a linear function of time — every `'iau'` body here is one of the seven planets, and every coefficient is NAIF `pck00011.tpc`'s `BODY_POLE_RA`/`BODY_POLE_DEC`/`BODY_PM`, WGCCRE 2015-compliant). Spin axis and gravity-field coefficients are split apart on purpose: which way a body's mesh is oriented is a fact about the body, not about its gravity field. `J2_EARTH`'s outside reader is `object-placer-panel.ts`'s sun-synchronous-inclination preset. **There is no static per-body flag deciding what counts as a gravity source.** `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s `gravityBodiesAt` is the sole gate, and it is a plain `mu !== 0` filter over `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)`'s full window (a body whose mass has never been measured is registered with `mu: 0` and drops out) — no separate "is this a gravity source" flag exists to keep in sync with `mu` itself. `CelestialBodyDef.lagrangeLabels?: boolean` (only earth/moon/jupiter/saturn are `true`) separately gates whether `game/camera/focus-markers.ts` mints that body's five L1–L5 focus labels. The seven planets' (and Earth's/the Moon's) orbital elements come from JPL's Standish Table 1 (mean ecliptic/equinox of J2000, valid 1800–2050) plus their own secular per-Julian-century rates; Mercury's ϖ̇ (577.7″/Cy) already includes the 42.98″/Cy general-relativistic contribution. `MU_MARS`/`MU_JUPITER`/`MU_SATURN`/`MU_NEPTUNE` are exported so a satellite's mean orbit shares the exact μ its planet's own `CelestialBodyDef` uses. Eight major moons (Phobos/Deimos around Mars, Io/Europa/Ganymede/Callisto around Jupiter, Titan around Saturn, Triton — retrograde, `incDeg` > 90° — around Neptune) are two-body-only satellites (`equatorialSatelliteOrbit`, no perturbation terms) whose elements are measured against their **parent's own equatorial plane** rather than the ecliptic: `equatorBasis(pole)` builds that plane's `basisToEci` from `body-orientation.ts`'s `equatorBasisToEci` off the same IAU pole (`MARS_POLE`/`JUPITER_POLE`/`SATURN_POLE`/`NEPTUNE_POLE`) the planet's rendered spin axis uses, fixed at its J2000 epoch value. Each satellite's mean motion is `elements.ts`'s `keplerPeriod(a, planetMu)`. 41 more heliocentric bodies round `SOLAR_SYSTEM` out to its full non-satellite population: the original nine — five dwarf planets (Pluto/Haumea/Makemake/Eris/Ceres), two large asteroids (Vesta/Pallas), two comet nuclei (1P/Halley, 2P/Encke) — plus 32 further dwarf planets/TNOs/main-belt asteroids/NEOs/comet nuclei registered later (three of which, Quaoar/Orcus/Didymos, carry their own `kind: 'satellite'` moon — Weywot/Vanth/Dimorphos). All are two-body-only heliocentric ellipses with every secular rate held at 0 — their elements come from JPL's Small-Body Database (`sbdb.api`, `full-prec=true`) at that query's own epoch, extrapolated to J2000 by mean motion (Pluto has no SBDB entry and uses known literature elements instead); comet epochs carry no non-gravitational (outgassing) correction, so position accuracy degrades with distance from that epoch even though period/shape stay exact — the 32 later bodies mostly share one query epoch (JD2461200.5) but five (churyumov/tempel1/wild2/hartley2/bennu) were queried at their own distinct epochs, all alike baked to `simTime = 0` regardless, so none of this reproduces a single real instant of the sky.

  `CelestialBodyDef.shape?: ShapeDef` is a body's static distortion — `{kind:'spheroid', equatorRadius, polarRadius}` for the eight planets (Venus omitted: its pck00011.tpc equatorial/polar radii are equal) or `{kind:'triaxial', a, b, c}` (`a >= b >= c`, `a`/`b` equatorial, `c` the spin-axis semi-axis) for haumea/phobos/deimos/vesta/ceres/io; every other registered body, including pallas (whose two published triaxial fits don't converge — NF-2 forbids picking one), stays a sphere by omitting `shape`. Source is NAIF `pck00011.tpc`'s `BODY_RADII` (a diameter-valued triple for the triaxial bodies, halved on the way in — the field itself is a radius). `shapeAxes(radius, shape)` is the sole place `ShapeDef` becomes a 3-axis scale: `undefined` → `(radius, radius, radius)`, `'spheroid'` → `(equatorRadius, polarRadius, equatorRadius)`, `'triaxial'` → `(a, c, b)` — Y is always the spin axis, matching ECI's own Y-pole convention, since `game/celestial/sphere-body.ts`/`point-body.ts` apply this scale inside the body's own pre-spin local frame. **`radius` (the scalar `Attractor.radius` used by `hitCelestialBody`'s collision sphere and `apsisAltitudes`'s altitude reference) is deliberately left un-ellipsoidized** and instead raised to the shape's circumscribing value (the longest axis: `equatorRadius` for a spheroid, `a` for a triaxial body) wherever a `shape` is added — a collision/altitude sphere sized to the *shortest* axis would let a ship pass through a pole that's actually there. `tests/physics/shape.test.ts` checks this invariant (`radius >= max(axes)`) mechanically across every shaped body, plus Saturn's/Jupiter's `Rp/Re` ratio and two triaxial axis-ratio spot checks against the source table.

  `CelestialBodyDef.rings?: RingSystemDef` is a planet's ring system — `{bands: readonly RingBandDef[]}`, each `RingBandDef` a physical description: `innerRadius`/`outerRadius`/`thickness` in metres, an optional `arcs?: readonly RingArcDef[]` for a longitude-limited bright structure like Neptune's Adams ring, and `optics: RingOpticsDef` (`normalOpticalDepth`/`singleScatteringAlbedo`/`phaseG`/`color`, built via the file-local `ringOptics(...)` helper) — the Beer-Lambert/single-scattering parameters `render/ring.ts`'s TSL shader graph evaluates per fragment, not a flat opacity. `thickness > 0` marks a diffuse, non-flat structure (Jupiter's halo/gossamer rings, Saturn's E ring) drawn as a flattened torus rather than a flat annulus; `thickness === 0` is flat, and whether that band is built as a filled annulus or as a hairline circle is decided once, at construction, from the band's own real width (`game/celestial/ring-view.ts`, below) — this module only states the physical width and optics. jupiter/saturn/uranus/neptune/quaoar/chariklo carry `rings` today — jupiter (4 bands: halo + main + two gossamer rings), saturn (5: the D–A band, F, G, the diffuse E ring, and the far-out Phoebe ring), uranus (13, one per named ring, all flat and thin enough to read as lines at any sane zoom), neptune (5, including the arc-bearing Adams ring), quaoar (2: Q1R/Q2R, from Morgado et al. 2023), chariklo (2: C1R/C2R) — sourced from Wikipedia's per-planet ring articles or the cited paper (in the module's own comments) via the file-local `ringBand(innerKm, outerKm, thicknessKm?, arcs?)` km→m helper.
- `src/physics/lagrange.ts` — the restricted three-body Lagrange points as pure geometry: `collinearGamma(mu, point)` (Newton's method for the collinear-point distance ratio from the quintic balance equation, seeded from the Hill radius) and `lagrangePoints(mu, place)` (the five points' dimensionless rotating-frame coordinates, mapped to a caller-supplied `place(x, y)`). Mass-ratio-only — no body identity, no ECI.
- `src/physics/shadow.ts` — `sunlitFactor(r, star, attractors)`: the日照率 at a position, generalized over every registered body rather than Earth alone — split out of `ephemeris.ts` because it's geometry from a position and each body's current state, not "where is a body at time t". `r` is treated as a point and the star/each `attractors` entry as a sphere; the fraction of the star's disk (as seen from `r`) not covered by any occluding disk is the closed-form circle-overlap-area formula, which is what makes umbra (full overlap → 0), annular (occluder disk inside the star's disk → `1 − (r_occ/r_sun)²`), penumbra, and full daylight fall out of one continuous expression with no case split. An occluder must sit between `r` and the star along the sun direction — one on the far side of `r` from the star cannot shadow it, however the angle alone might read. Several simultaneous occluders combine by multiplying their individual lit fractions, since two bodies transiting the star's disk at once from the same point never happens in practice and the exact union of their shadowed regions isn't worth the added complexity.
- `src/physics/srp.ts` — `srpAccel(r, sun, srpCoeff, sunlit)`: solar radiation pressure as the standard cannonball model — the body is a sphere of constant reflectivity, so the force is along the sun→body line regardless of attitude and the coefficient and cross-section only ever appear as the product `srpCoeff = C_R·A/m` [m²/kg] (the same shape as drag's `bcInv = Cd·A/m`, and stored the same way: one constant per entity class). `SOLAR_PRESSURE_1AU` = 4.56e-6 N/m² and `ASTRONOMICAL_UNIT` are this module's own — the latter is the inverse-square reference distance, deliberately not read from `solar-system.ts`'s Earth semi-major axis, which is a different quantity that merely has a similar value. The shadow factor is supplied by the caller rather than computed here, so `shadow.ts`'s `sunlitFactor` stays the single answer to "is this point in sunlight" shared with the lighting, radiator and power systems. **Near Earth this is the smallest perturbation modeled** (~7e-8 m/s² for the player ship, about a quarter of the drag at 420 km), but drag vanishes with altitude while this does not, so out at the Lagrange points it is the only non-gravitational force left.
- `src/physics/body-orientation.ts` — a body's own orientation: not just its spin axis, but its spin *phase* about that axis (which way the prime meridian points) — as opposed to where the body is (`ephemeris.ts`). `cassiniSpinAxis(eclipticPole, orbitNormal, obliquity)` builds a synchronously rotating satellite's spin axis from Cassini's laws (spin axis, orbit normal and ecliptic pole stay coplanar, with the spin axis on the far side of the ecliptic pole from the orbit normal — so feeding it a precessing orbit normal makes the spin axis precess on the same 18.6-year period for free). `orthogonalizedTo(pole, reference)` orthogonalizes a reference direction against the pole — a general-purpose projection, used both for the `C22` long axis and for a meridian direction. Phase is anchored the way IAU's rotation angle `W` is: `spinPhaseRef(axis)` is the direction phase 0 points at — the ascending node of the body's equator on the ECI equator (`ECI_POLE × axis`, falling back to the vernal-equinox direction `orthogonalizedTo(axis, (1,0,0))` when the axis is ~parallel to `ECI_POLE`, i.e. the node is undefined) — `spinPhaseOf(axis, meridian)` reads the phase [rad] of an arbitrary meridian direction against that reference, `meridianDirection(axis, spinAngle)` is its inverse, and `spinOrientation(axis, spinAngle)` composes the two into a full quaternion (model +Y = spin axis, +Z = prime meridian, via `attitude.ts`'s `qFromForwardUp`) — the one function `game/celestial/sphere-body.ts` needs to orient a body's mesh. `equatorBasisToEci(axis)` builds the *reference-plane* counterpart for orbital elements rather than a mesh orientation: z = `axis` (the body's spin axis), x = `spinPhaseRef(axis)` (the same phase-0 direction `meridianDirection`/`spinOrientation` use) — so a satellite's orbit and its parent's rendered prime meridian share one IAU-ascending-node convention. It is `kepler-orbit.ts`'s `KeplerOrbit.basisToEci`'s sole non-ecliptic source, built by `solar-system.ts`'s `equatorBasis` for every equator-referenced satellite. **The orbit normal is not the spin axis** — for the Moon the two are 6.688° apart, and substituting one for the other tilts the `C22`/`J2` perturbation by that much without changing its magnitude, which is why `body-orientation.test.ts` checks the angles rather than the resulting acceleration. Pure functions, no body identity.
- `src/physics/ephemeris.ts` — `Ephemeris`: composes star → planet-satellite-system barycenter → planet/satellite (the three-stage pipeline: a planet's barycenter follows its own heliocentric Kepler orbit via `planetOrbit`/`keplerOrbitState`; each satellite's planet-relative state comes from `satelliteState` fed the planet's own `planetAngles`; the planet body itself is then the barycenter minus Σ(μ_satellite/Σμ)·(satellite's planet-relative state), applied to both position *and* velocity, and each satellite is that planet position plus its own planet-relative state), then ECI-izes by subtracting the origin body's own heliocentric state. Holds **no name-specific branch** — every step dispatches on the registry's `kind`, so adding a body needs no change here. Every evaluation adds `epochOffsetSec` (defaulting to the exported `EPOCH_T_OFFSET`, a fixed 6.9721972e6 s — the value that anchors `simTime = 0` to "the sun sits on +X" under `SOLAR_SYSTEM`; a custom registry would need its own value if that anchor is meaningful there) to `t` before consulting a body's Standish elements. **The constructor takes `registry`/`originId`/`epochOffsetSec`, each defaulted to today's values (`SOLAR_SYSTEM`/`'earth'`/`EPOCH_T_OFFSET`)** — every existing `new Ephemeris()` call site is therefore unaffected; only a stage that overrides the static `createEphemeris` (`StageDebugAltSystem`, see `stage.ts`/`stage-debug-alt-system.ts` above) supplies different ones directly to its own `new Ephemeris(...)` call. **`frameOf(center, rotatingWith)`** is the sole mint point for a `ReferenceFrame`: it caches by the `(center, rotatingWith)` pair (a `Map<AttractorId, Map<OrbitingId | null, ReferenceFrame>>`) and always returns the same reference for the same pair, whether or not either id belongs to the active registry — a living gravity-bearing `GameEntity` used as a rotation-system center works the same way as a registered body, since `frameTransformAt` (below) already resolves both. From `registry`/`originId` the constructor derives, once, through this cache: `ids` (the registry's keys, in declaration order — the same order `attractorsAt` returns); `starId` (`solar-system.ts`'s `starOf(registry)`, `null` for a registry with no star); `inertialFrame` = `frameOf(originId, null)`; and `frames` (one inertial `ReferenceFrame` per registered body plus one rotating frame per orbiting body) — **this is what `frame.ts`'s former module-level `FRAMES`/`INERTIAL_FRAME` became**, since which frames exist is a property of the active registry, not a global constant. `frameFor(id)` is `frameOf(id, null)` under a name that reads as "the inertial frame at this body" at call sites that only ever want that. Because every one of these goes through the same cache, `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `frame === lastFrame` re-bake guard holds regardless of which of `frameOf`/`frameFor`/`inertialFrame`/`frames` produced the value being compared. Public surface: `stateOf`/`positionOf(id, t)`; `orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt(id: OrbitingId, t)`; `lagrangeAt(secondary, t)` (primary resolved via `primaryOf(registry, secondary)`, throwing if `secondary` has no resolvable primary); `sunDirFrom(r, t)` (the unit direction from an ECI point to the star — the reference point is an argument because the star sits in a different direction from each point, so no privileged origin can stand in for it once the player leaves Earth's neighborhood; `starId === null` falls back to a fixed +X direction, since shadow/SRP have nothing to compute against); `frameTransformAt(frame, t, attractors)` — the sole place a `ReferenceFrame`'s origin/rotation is resolved into a concrete `FrameTransform`, now taking the frame's-worth of live `Attractor`s as a third argument so it can resolve **two ways**: `frame.rotatingWith` **registered** in the active registry uses the existing analytic `orbitFrameRotationAt` (unchanged behavior under `SOLAR_SYSTEM`); **unregistered** (a living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`) builds a frame from that body's live relative state in `attractors` (x̂ = center→body, ẑ = relative angular momentum direction) — the only model available for a body with no stored orbital elements, and deliberately not treated as an approximation of one, since a free multi-body system has no long-term analytic basis to approximate; `poleAt(id, t)` (a body's spin axis and spin phase — `{axis, spinAngle} | null`, `null` for a star or a body with no `pole` model — resolved by the private `orientationOf`, which dispatches purely on `PoleModel.kind`); `game/celestial/sphere-body.ts`'s `SphereBody.sync` is `poleAt`'s sole consumer. `attractorAt(id, t)` fills `Attractor.accel` from a private `eciAccelOf` — each body's heliocentric acceleration as a two-body term about its own primary (recursing star→planet→satellite), minus the origin body's own, so the value is the second derivative of the ECI position it sits beside and the origin body reads exactly zero. It is an approximation of the analytic model's own second derivative rather than its exact differentiation, and stays that approximation under the precise pack too, because its only consumer is `attractorPositionAt`'s second-order term — measured against a central difference of `stateOf`'s velocity it agrees to ~1%, and the extrapolation error that leaves is three orders of magnitude below the first-order term it removes. `attractorsAt(t)` (registry declaration order) is the **all-bodies** window, answering every "which bodies are there" question (occlusion checks, surface-contact/`hitCelestialBody` checks, center-body resolution via `strongestAttractor`, integration/sampling-interval step sizing). `gravityAttractorsAt(t)` is its **gravity-source** sibling — same shape, same declaration order, same "same `t` returns the same immutable array reference" contract, but assembled from only the `mu !== 0` bodies (64 of `SOLAR_SYSTEM`'s 101). They are two windows rather than one plus a filter because the gravity integration is evaluated once per substep at a time that is unique on every call (a substep midpoint), so each call is a full evaluation and narrowing afterwards would still pay for the 37 bodies it then discards. Which ids are gravity sources is settled once in the constructor (`gravityIds`), `mu` being independent of `t`. Both windows go through the same per-body `attractorAt(id, t)` and share the intermediate `planetHelioCache`/`satelliteRelCache`, so a body's `Attractor` is identical between them at the same `t`. `attractorAt` is itself public — the one-body window `simulation/future-attractors.ts`'s `FutureAttractors.bodyAt` reads directly for an analytic candidate, rather than pulling the whole-registry `attractorsAt`/`gravityAttractorsAt` array just to pick one entry back out. `attractorsAt`'s per-body `Degree2Gravity` resolution also calls `orientationOf`, so a body's gravity-field pole and its rendered spin axis can never disagree. `attractorsAt`'s result, plus the intermediate `planetHelioState`/`satelliteRelState` composition steps, are each backed by a **fixed-length ring cache (`TIME_CACHE_SLOTS` = 32) keyed on exact-match `t`**: a cache hit returns the same array reference every time (callers must not mutate it), a miss always recomputes from scratch, and because a miss never depends on what was looked up before, there is no ordering constraint between callers. The slot count is sized so the frame's main-path times (`simTime`, `displayTime`) are not evicted by the single-shot times that also flow through the same frame (an apsis or node crossing, a prediction tip, a substep midpoint); a hit costs that many number comparisons, which is nothing against the cost of one miss. `gravityAttractorsAt` holds its own ring, separate from `attractorsAt`'s, since the two return different contents for the same `t`. `attractorsCacheStats`/`timeCacheStats` expose the cumulative hit/miss counts of the `attractorsAt` ring alone and of every ring combined (the gravity ring included in the latter). The phase offsets each ring's composition reads (`phaseOffsets`, a constructor argument defaulting to `{}`) are fixed at construction and never change afterward, so nothing ever needs to invalidate these caches mid-game. `getPhaseOffsets()` is the sole save entry point — loading one passes the saved `phaseOffsets` back into a fresh `new Ephemeris(...)` rather than writing into a live instance, since a snapshot load discards the running `Game` and constructs a fresh one (see `save/` below). THREE-independent; the single instance is **built by `main.ts`** (via `stageClass.createEphemeris`, before `Game` exists — see `main.ts` above) and held by `Game`, shared by reference from there with `EnvironmentScene`, `Simulator`, `OverviewCamera`, `PlanEditor`/`PlanDisplay`/`PlanPath`, `FocusMarkers`, `Player.behave`, and `Enemy.behave`/`Stage.behaveAllEnemies`. **The constructor's optional `absoluteSource`/`epochJdTdb` arguments are the analytic model's high-precision upgrade path** (see `src/physics/ephemeris-pack/` below): when supplied, they build `private readonly precise = new OriginCenteredEphemeris(absoluteSource, originId, epochJdTdb)`, and `stateOf`/`orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt`/`attractorsAt`'s per-body lookups all check `this.precise?.hasBody(id)` first, falling back to the Standish-elements pipeline described above only for a body the precise source doesn't carry — a satellite absent from the pack falls back by adding its existing planet-relative model on top of the *precise* parent state rather than the analytic parent, so precision degrades gracefully body-by-body instead of all-or-nothing. `main.ts` supplies both by default, via `stageClass.createEphemeris` (`Stage`'s own default implementation, see `stage.ts` above) — `StageDebugAltSystem`'s override skips the pack fetch entirely, since an invented registry has no real astronomical data to correct with — so the analytic pipeline above is the fallback path, not the primary one, for every stage that plays in the real solar system.
- `src/physics/time/index.ts` — `Date`-free astronomical time-scale conversion: `CalendarDate<S>`/`JulianDate<S>` are branded by `TimeScale` (`'UTC' | 'TT' | 'TDB'`) so a UTC value can never be silently fed where TDB is expected, and calendar↔Julian-date conversion (`calendarDateToJulianDate`/`julianDateToCalendarDate`, `parseCalendarDate`) is hand-rolled rather than routed through JS `Date` because `Date` can hold neither a fractional Julian day nor the five-digit years `far-future-20000` reaches. `TT`↔`TDB` differ only by a sub-millisecond periodic term this module ignores (`ttToTdb`/`tdbToTt` are near-identity here); `UTC`↔`TT` needs the accumulated leap-second/ΔT offset, which this module does not hardcode — `UtcOffsetProvider`/`TdbOffsetProvider` are injected by the caller, keeping this module free of a leap-second table it would otherwise have to keep updating. THREE-independent, no game-state dependency; `ephemeris-pack/absolute-adapter.ts` is its main consumer (a Chebyshev pack's native time axis is JD_TDB).
- `src/physics/ephemeris-pack/` — the binary Chebyshev-polynomial ephemeris format (JPL DE440/DE441-derived, the same representation SPICE/BSP files use) that gives `Ephemeris` its optional high-precision path, split by what each layer is allowed to know:
  - `types.ts` — the in-memory pack shapes (`ChebyshevManifest`/`ChebyshevSegment`/`ChebyshevBodyPack`/`ChebyshevEphemerisPack`), deliberately binary-, JD-, and frame-independent: times are plain SI seconds on a caller-chosen axis and positions are metres, so this shape (and everything built on it below) has no notion of TDB or ICRF until an explicit adapter says so.
  - `evaluator.ts` — `ChebyshevEphemeris`: the pure interpolation engine, knowing nothing about JD_TDB or ICRF. `segmentOf`/`evaluate` binary-search the body's segment list for the one covering a given `time` (`findChebyshevSegmentIndex`) and evaluate that segment's Chebyshev series (`evaluateChebyshevWithDerivative`) into a `KinematicState` — position from the series value, velocity from its analytic derivative rescaled by the segment's own `[start, end]`→`[-1, 1]` affine mapping, so no finite-difference velocity estimate is needed. The constructor snapshots every input coefficient array (`copyBody`) and deep-freezes the result, so a caller mutating the array it originally passed in can't retroactively corrupt an already-constructed evaluator. `ChebyshevBodyNotFoundError`/`ChebyshevTimeOutOfRangeError` are explicit rather than silently clamping or extrapolating outside a segment's covered span.
  - `absolute-adapter.ts` — `ChebyshevAbsoluteEphemeris`: the one place a `ChebyshevEphemeris`'s plain-seconds axis becomes JD_TDB, implementing `AbsoluteEphemeris` (below) directly over an evaluator plus a chosen `epochJdTdb`. It refuses a manifest that doesn't declare `coordinateFrame: 'ICRF-J2000'`/`timeScale: 'TDB'` (when the manifest states either field at all — `toEvaluatorEphemerisPack` already validated the canonical binary manifest and keeps only the core evaluator fields, so an *absent* declaration is accepted as "not yet checked" rather than rejected) — the runtime pack-loading path (`packed-absolute-ephemeris.ts`) performs the same JD↔seconds conversion inline rather than going through this class, so this adapter's own primary consumer today is the evaluator-level test suite exercising the JD_TDB boundary in isolation from the rest of the loading pipeline.
  - `format.ts` — the on-disk `.epk` binary layout: an 8-byte magic (`TEPUIEPK`) + version/size header, a canonical-JSON manifest (`canonicalJson` sorts object keys recursively so the same data always encodes to the same bytes, which is what makes a payload SHA-256 meaningful), then a contiguous little-endian Float64 payload of X/Y/Z Chebyshev coefficients per series. Deliberately boring and dependency-free (a Node CLI tool can write it, a browser can read it with nothing but `DataView`/`TextDecoder`) rather than reusing an existing ephemeris container format, since the game only ever needs a handful of bodies' worth of coefficients, not a general-purpose SPK reader. `decodeEphemerisPack`/`validateManifest` reject a truncated, corrupt-header, or non-finite payload outright — every decoded value is checked for finiteness whichever way the bytes were read, since a little-endian host reads them as one bulk copy into the `Float64Array`'s own (8-byte-aligned) buffer and only a big-endian one walks them through a `DataView`. **`DecodedEphemerisPack.payloadBytes` is a view onto the caller's input, not a copy** (the `payload` `Float64Array` beside it is the copy), so the input buffer must not be mutated while a decoded pack is still in use — `verifyEphemerisPayload` hashes exactly those bytes, and a mutation between decode and verify would be hashed rather than caught. `toEvaluatorEphemerisPack` adapts a decoded pack into the shape `evaluator. `toEvaluatorEphemerisPack` adapts a decoded pack into the shape `evaluator.ts` expects.
  - `index.ts` — barrel re-export of the three files above.
- `src/physics/absolute-ephemeris.ts` — the abstraction every precise ephemeris source implements: `AbsoluteEphemeris` (`hasBody`/`barycentricStateOf(id, jdTdb)`, returning a `BarycentricState` in raw ICRF/J2000 metres) plus its own `validStartJdTdb`/`validEndJdTdb`, so a source's coverage is a property of the interface, not read off a concrete class. `icrfToGameEci` is the one place ICRF's (vernal-equinox, equatorial-plane, north-pole) axes are remapped into the game's ECI (vernal-equinox, north-pole, equatorial-plane) convention — a right-handed axis relabeling, not a rotation, since both frames share the same equinox and equator. `OriginCenteredEphemeris` is what `physics/ephemeris.ts`'s `Ephemeris` actually holds as `precise`: it takes a raw barycentric `AbsoluteEphemeris`, an origin body, and the JD_TDB epoch `simTime = 0` corresponds to, and its `stateOf(id, simTime)` converts `simTime` to JD_TDB, looks up both `id` and the origin body's own barycentric state, subtracts them, and axis-remaps the result — so the barycenter never needs to be a body the rest of the game reasons about. The origin body's own barycentric lookup is cached in a private one-slot memo (`originStateAt`, keyed on the last-seen `jdTdb`), so evaluating several bodies at the same `simTime` — the common case, since a substep or an arc's midpoint resolves every candidate at one instant — pays for that lookup once rather than once per body. `MissingEphemerisBodyError` fires rather than silently falling back for a body the source doesn't carry, since a silent fallback here would defeat the whole point of asking for the precise source in the first place — `Ephemeris.stateOf`'s own `precise?.hasBody(id)` check is what makes the *fallback-to-analytic* decision instead, one layer up.
- `src/physics/packed-absolute-ephemeris.ts` — `PackedAbsoluteEphemeris implements AbsoluteEphemeris`: the concrete, file-backed precise source, composing `ephemeris-pack/format.ts`'s decoder with `ephemeris-pack/evaluator.ts`'s `ChebyshevEphemeris` (converting JD_TDB↔J2000-ET-seconds itself, the same conversion `absolute-adapter.ts` offers as a reusable class — see above for why this path doesn't go through it). `verifyEphemerisPayload` is a separate, explicitly async step (SHA-256 over the payload bytes alone, via `crypto.subtle` — deliberately not folded into the synchronous `decodeEphemerisPack`, since decoding a pack that's merely *malformed* is a synchronous, offline question, while asking whether the bytes are the *expected* bytes needs the browser's crypto API and is meaningful only for data that arrived over the network) — `loadPackedAbsoluteEphemeris` composes decode+verify+construct as the one path a network-fetched pack should go through; `fromTrustedBytes` skips verification for bytes a caller already trusts (e.g. re-decoding within a session).
- `src/physics/ephemeris-profile.ts` — **the near-future/far-future split itself**: a pure declaration of which pack covers which era, with no data or game-mode logic. `EPHEMERIS_PROFILES` holds exactly two non-overlapping-by-design entries — `'modern-de440'` (JPL DE440, valid for a 10-Julian-year window starting 2026-01-01) and `'far-future-20000'` (`sourceModel: 'Tepui long-term integration anchored to JPL DE441'`, valid for a 10-year window starting in the invented year 20115) — because a single Chebyshev fit can't stay accurate across millennia, and the far-future era has no real JPL solution to draw from at all: it's Tepui's own long-term numerical integration, anchored to DE441 at its start, packaged in the exact same `.epk`/Chebyshev representation so `Ephemeris`/`OriginCenteredEphemeris` need no branch to consume either one. Each profile's `packId` embeds the expected pack's own payload hash, so `ephemeris-catalog.ts` (below) can catch a stale or mismatched binary asset before it's ever decoded. `profileAt(jdTdb, requestedProfile?)` is the sole selector — given just a JD_TDB it picks whichever profile's range covers it (`UnsupportedEphemerisEpochError` if none does), or validates a caller-named profile explicitly; the "no implicit priority between overlapping profiles" comment in the source is a guardrail for a third profile someone adds later, not a case that exists today.
- `src/physics/ephemeris-catalog.ts` — `loadAbsoluteEphemeris(profileId, epochJdTdb, requiredEndJdTdb)`: the only module that knows the `.epk` binary assets exist (`assets/ephemeris/modern-2026-10y.epk`/`far-future-20115-10y.epk`, webpack-imported as URLs via `PACK_URLS`). Fetches the profile's own file, decodes+verifies it (`loadPackedAbsoluteEphemeris`), then checks the *decoded* payload's SHA-256 against the *profile's declared* `packId` — a second, independent check beyond the loader's own tamper check, since a byte-valid pack that simply isn't the one this profile expects (a stale build artifact, a mismatched deploy) must still be caught — and finally range-checks that the requested `[epochJdTdb, requiredEndJdTdb]` window actually falls inside the loaded pack's own validity span. `Stage`'s own default `createEphemeris` (called from `main.ts`, before `Game` exists) calls `profileAt(SIM_EPOCH_JD_TDB)` then this function to build the `AbsoluteEphemeris` it passes straight into `new Ephemeris(...)`; `StageDebugAltSystem`'s override skips both calls entirely.
- `src/physics/frame.ts` — a display coordinate system is **an origin body × an optional rotation**, `ReferenceFrame = {center: AttractorId, rotatingWith: OrbitingId | null}` — independent choices, so the type is their direct product rather than a single enumerated frame name. `rotatingWith: null` is an inertial frame at `center`; otherwise the frame spins with whichever orbiting body `rotatingWith` names — for a satellite that's its planet (so `{center:'earth', rotatingWith:'moon'}` holds the moon still), for a planet that's itself (`{center:'earth', rotatingWith:'earth'}` holds the sun's apparent motion still without moving the origin off Earth). **This module holds no registry and mints no `ReferenceFrame` values of its own** — `Ephemeris.frameOf`/`frameFor`/`inertialFrame`/`frames` are the only source, since which frames exist and which body a rotating frame's origin resolves to are properties of *which registry is active*, not facts this module could hold on its own; every UI/display consumer must reference one of `Ephemeris`'s elements rather than construct a `ReferenceFrame` literal — a fresh object would still be `==`-equal in content but not in reference, and `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `frame === lastFrame` re-bake guard is a reference check (silently degrading to "re-bake every frame" on a mismatch, not a crash, which is why the discipline has to be enforced by convention rather than caught by a type).

  A `ReferenceFrame`'s actual motion at a moment — `FrameTransform = {origin, originVel, q, omega}` — is supplied by `Ephemeris.frameTransformAt`, which this module does not import (avoiding a `frame.ts` ⇄ `ephemeris.ts` cycle); `frame.ts` itself is pure conversion arithmetic over an already-resolved `FrameTransform`. Six conversions split by what kind of quantity is being moved: **points** (`toFramePoint`/`toInertialPoint`, affine — origin translation *and* rotation, for a position) and **directions** (`toFrameDir`/`toInertialDir`, linear — rotation only, for a displacement/velocity-difference/up-vector); their outputs are the branded `FramePoint`/`FrameDir` types so a caller can't feed a direction through the point path (or vice versa) and get a silently wrong offset the moment a frame's origin starts moving. `toFrameState`/`toInertialState` do the same split within one `KinematicState`: `v_rel = R⁻¹(v − ȯ − ω×r_rel)`, the Coriolis-like `ω×r_rel` term existing because a rotating frame's own spin contributes to the relative velocity, not just its translation. `toFrameState` bakes at the state's own epoch (`s.t`) and needs no time argument; `toInertialState` still takes one, because un-baking happens at the *display* time, not the sample time. Used only by display concerns (`game/trajectory-line.ts` bake/un-bake, `MapCamera`'s viewpoint offset/pan/up, `PlanPath`'s picking) — the simulation itself is always plain ECI.
- `src/physics/projection.ts` — the pure screen-projection math (`Viewpoint` → `projectToNdc` → `ndcToScreen`, yielding `Projected {x, y, front}`). **It lives under `physics/` deliberately**: a pinhole projection is the geometric map from Cartesian to projective coordinates, exact and free of any look-and-feel tuning — the kind of thing `physics/` is for. What would not belong here is anything that bends that map for legibility (marker nudging, distance compression, fades); those stay in `game/`. Independent of THREE.js and of the floating origin; each camera in `game/camera/` binds it into a `ProjectFn`. `metersPerPixelAtDepth(fovDeg, depth, viewportHeight)` (built on `metersPerPixelFromTanHalfFov`, for a caller that already holds `tan(fovDeg/2)`) is the pinhole-camera core — how much world distance one screen pixel spans at a given forward-axis depth (`2·depth·tan(fov/2)/viewportHeight`, floored at `MIN_DEPTH`); `metersPerPixel(view, worldPos, viewportHeight)` derives `depth` from a `Viewpoint` and a world position and calls it. A camera that already knows its own distance to the look target (`ChaseCamera`'s `dist`, `MapCamera`'s `dist`) calls `metersPerPixelAtDepth` directly with that distance rather than reconstructing a `Viewpoint` just to re-derive the same depth.
- `src/physics/intercept.ts` — `solveLeadTime` (the lead/interception time for a relative position+velocity and a muzzle speed, used by the enemy AI's aim) and `leadPoint` (the world position to aim at, given two `KinematicState`s and a cut-off time; `null` when there's no usable solution). `leadPoint` is what keeps `LeadMarkers` from rolling its own lead math.
- `src/physics/occlusion.ts` — `isOccluded(cameraPos, point, attractors)`: pure ray-vs-sphere geometry answering whether a camera's line of sight to a point is blocked by any `Attractor`'s sphere before reaching it (the near intersection of the ray with a body must fall strictly between the camera and the point). The map view's icon visibility (`plan-display.ts`'s apsis icons, `nav-target.ts`'s relative AN/DN, `marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts`'s EqAN/EqDN) and its pick candidates (`map-pickables.ts`'s `refresh`, `plan/plan-path.ts`'s `nearestSample`) all call this one function, so "hidden behind a body" and "unpickable" can never disagree.
- `src/physics/halo.ts` — CREATIVE-only: initial `KinematicState`s for halo/Lissajous orbits about the collinear Earth-Moon/Sun-Earth L1/L2 points. The rotating local frame (`collinearFrame`) is built entirely from existing `ephemeris.ts` API — `lagrangeAt` for the point's position, `orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt` for its rotation and plane normal — so this module adds no orbital elements or rotation axes of its own; from the point's distance ratio γ it derives Richardson's `cn` coefficients and the linear in-plane/out-of-plane frequencies λ/ωz (verified against the published values: Sun-Earth L1 λ=2.0864, ωz=2.0152, k=3.2293; Earth-Moon L1 λ=2.3344, ωz=2.2688). **Halo orbits do not exist in the linear solution** — λ ≠ ωz, so the two oscillations never close into a loop. They appear at third order, where Richardson (1980)'s amplitude constraint `l1·Ax² + l2·Az² + Δ = 0` makes the frequencies coincide; `haloState` therefore takes only the out-of-plane amplitude `Az`, solves that constraint for `Ax` (reproducing the ISEE-3 halo — Az = 110,000 km → Ax ≈ 205,000 km, Ay ≈ 662,000 km, both within 1% of the published figures) and only then drives the linear solution at the single shared frequency. `lissajousState` imposes no constraint and takes both amplitudes, leaving the two frequencies independent (an open Lissajous figure). The state itself is the first-order solution — the third-order series expansion is not included. And **the game's integrator is not a restricted three-body problem** (it's full multi-body gravity + degree-2 fields + drag + SRP, same as every other entity via `dynamics.ts`'s `stepDynamics`), so a ship placed at one of these states does **not** stay on a halo/Lissajous orbit once the simulation runs — it drifts, same as it would drift off any other idealized orbit under real perturbations. `CreativeStage.buildLagrangeState` is the only caller.
- `src/physics/dynamics.ts` — the RK4 integration core, in two layers. `stepRK4(s, dt, accel)` is the bare stepper, driven entirely by a caller-supplied acceleration callback (`AccelFn`: `(t, rx, ry, rz, vx, vy, vz) => Vec3`, evaluated once per RK4 stage at **that stage's own absolute time** — `s.t` for k1, `s.t + dt/2` for k2/k3, `s.t + dt` for k4. This is what makes the method fourth-order at all: RK4's order conditions hold for a non-autonomous `y' = f(t, y)` only when each stage is evaluated at its own time, and freezing `t` at a single value per step instead solves an autonomous system whose quadrature is the midpoint rule, collapsing the global error to O(h²). It is not a theoretical concern — in low lunar orbit, where the dominant attractor moves several km per step through ECI, freezing costs ~660 m of position error per revolution at a 20 s step against ~0.03 m when the stages are timed correctly) and returning the advanced `KinematicState` — epoch included — without touching its input; it holds **no gravity of its own**, so a caller wanting a bare two-body step supplies its own gravity callback (as `dynamics.test.ts` does when it checks `stepDynamics` against a hand-written legacy composition). `stepDynamics(state, dt, attractors, bcInv, srpCoeff, thrust)` is the single RK4 step behind `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory.step` — the one place "full multi-body gravity + degree-2 fields + drag + SRP + thrust" is composed, whether the caller is `GameEntity.stepActual` (drag/SRP on via each entity's own `bcInv`/`srpCoeff`, its own `thrust`) or `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc.step` (`thrust = null` always, whether the arc backs an entity's own predicted future or an owned plan segment — neither ever thrusts).
