Its module-private `totalAccel(t, r, v, attractors, bcInv, srpCoeff)` — whose `t` is that stage's own time, at which every gravity-bearing term reads the body's position through `attractor.ts`'s `attractorPositionAt` rather than off `state.r` directly — sums `attractor.ts`'s `attractorAccel(r, attractor)` over every body (each term already carrying its own ECI non-inertial correction), this module's own `degree2Accel` for every body that carries a `degree2` field, `atmosphere.ts`'s `dragAccel` (`bcInv = 0` skips drag), and `srp.ts`'s `srpAccel` gated by `shadow.ts`'s `sunlitFactor(r, attractor, attractors)` — the same `attractors` array doubles as the shadow calculation's set of possible occluders — summed over every `isStar` body (`srpCoeff = 0` or zero registered stars both skip it cleanly) — `stepDynamics` adds an optional `thrust` on top and drives it all through `stepRK4`. **`totalAccel` holds no body-specific branch**: which bodies are oblate, how oblate, which are stars, and which way their poles point all ride on the `Attractor` itself, so adding an oblate body or a second star needs no change here. `degree2Accel(rRel, mu, g)` takes a *body-centered relative* position (unlike the point-mass term, which is built in ECI) and evaluates both the zonal `J2` term — `k[(1 − 5u²)r + 2(r·p̂)p̂]`, `k = −(3/2)J2·μ·R²/|r|⁵`, for an arbitrary pole `p̂` — and, when the field is not axisymmetric, the tesseral `C22` term as the gradient of `3·C22·μ·R²·(x² − y²)/|r|⁵` in the principal-axis frame. Earth uses it with the pole on Y (verified ≈ −5°/day RAAN regression at 420 km/51.6°); the Moon uses it with a Cassini-state pole and a non-zero `C22`. No out-parameter variant — see the immutability note above.
- `src/physics/attitude.ts` — rigid-body attitude: quaternion + body-frame ω via Euler's equations. ω integrated with RK4 + kinetic-energy projection (naive explicit integration diverges — this is what makes the Dzhanibekov effect on debris stable long-term); `stepAttitude` carries the sub-step ω/q as scalar locals and returns one new `Attitude`. `qFromForwardUp` (nose +Z / up +Y basis → quaternion, re-orthogonalizing, null at the fwd∥up singularity) is the shared attitude-basis builder used by `player/player.ts` (`progradeAttitude`), `attitudeAlignError`/`attitudeAlignTorque` (below), and `stages/spawner/enemy-generator.ts` (`generateApproachingEnemy`). `attitudeAlignError(desiredFwd, desiredUp, q)` reduces a target forward/up pair and the current attitude to a signed error angle and body-frame axis (`null` at the fwd∥up singularity); `attitudeAlignTorque` adds a PD torque on top (gains passed in by the caller, since `physics/` doesn't read `game/const.ts`) — the one implementation of "torque a rigid body toward a target facing" shared by `player/player-throttle.ts`'s prograde-hold autopilot and `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s burn-direction alignment. Pure functions.
- `src/physics/optimize.ts` — `goldenSectionMin(lo, hi, f, iterations)`: a 1-variable golden-section search minimizing a caller-supplied unimodal `f` over `[lo, hi]`, with no notion of what `f` measures. `iterations` is fixed by the caller rather than convergence-driven, for the same reason `trajectory-features.ts`'s `REFINE_ITERATIONS` is — a variable count would let the result wobble frame to frame by itself. `trajectory-features.ts`'s `refineExtremum` and `game/plan/plan-path.ts`'s `nearestSample` are its two callers, each supplying its own objective (distance from a center, screen distance to a projected point); `refineExtremum`'s apoapsis case negates `f` to search for a maximum instead.
- `src/physics/section-moments.ts` — the area, centroid and second moments of area (`Ix`/`Iy`/`Ixy`) of a hull cross-section (`CrossSection`, one or more `SectionPrimitive`s glued face-to-face into a tree), computed **entirely in closed form with no numerical integration anywhere**. THREE/DOM-independent and not yet wired to anything in `game/`. The whole point of the module is that two properties make the closed form reachable: primitives that do not overlap, so a composite's geometry is exactly the sum of its parts — gluing is limited to plane-against-plane butt joints, but a chain of butt joints can still close a ring and fold back onto itself, so **whether the parts are actually disjoint is verified rather than assumed**: every placement runs a pairwise convex-overlap area test and a section whose primitives share area throws instead of answering an inflated sum; and every primitive is either a simple polygon (the shoelace formulas — one general expression covering regular polygons, notched ones and every composite alike, rather than a formula per shape) or a circle/ellipse (its own analytic constants). The shape types (`PrimitiveShape`/`PrimitiveAttachment`/`SectionPrimitive`/`CrossSection`) live here rather than in a `game/` module, since nothing outside this file consumes them yet. Internally every quantity is accumulated as one raw moment set about the coordinate origin (`{area, ∫x dA, ∫y dA, ∫x² dA, ∫y² dA, ∫xy dA}`), which is what makes subtracting a circle's chord-cut segment and adding a glued-on primitive the *same* operation — the parallel-axis shift onto the centroid then happens exactly once, at the end. `polygonArea`/`polygonCentroid`/`polygonSecondMoments` (centroidal)/`polygonSecondMomentsAboutOrigin` are the polygon side, with `polygonMomentsAboutOrigin` handing out that same raw moment set (`PolygonMoments`) whole — the form that stays defined for a zero-area vertex list, which is what a loft's degenerate apex section needs; `regularPolygonVertices(sides, radius, phaseAngle)` and `notchedPolygonVertices(sides, radius, phaseAngle)` build the two polygonal primitives' outlines counter-clockwise, the notched one as its own base polygon (a triangle for 6 sides, a square for 8) with every corner truncated by the single cut depth that makes the long edges exactly twice the short ones, then scaled so its vertices land on the requested circumradius. `circleSegmentMoments(radius, halfAngle)` is the circular segment a side port cuts away, stated about the circle's own center with the segment facing +x, and `portHalfAngle()` is that segment's own half-angle — exported, since `hull-loft.ts` has to cut the same segment out of the outline it samples and a second spelling of `asin(PORT_WIDTH_RATIO/2)` would let the subtracted area and the drawn outline disagree; `PORT_WIDTH_RATIO` (=1.0) fixes the chord at one radius, which is exactly the widest port that keeps six of them from overlapping — and at that value a 6-branch circle reduces to precisely the regular hexagon, which is what cross-checks the analytic subtraction against the shoelace path. `sectionMoments(section)` walks the tree from its single root, placing each child by the rigid transform that lays the child's own face onto the parent's face running the opposite way (which is what puts the child *outside* the parent, and which absorbs a child's own `phaseAngle` entirely), and sums; `placeSectionPrimitives(section)` hands that same placement out as `PlacedSectionPrimitive`s (each primitive's shape, its phase, and — for the polygonal ones — its placed outline), the one way another module can reach the composite's geometry rather than only its integrals. **`Ixy` is not generally 0** — a composite whose symmetry axis doesn't lie along a coordinate axis has principal axes rotated away from them, and the inertia tensor has to carry the term. A structurally invalid section (no single root, an unknown parent, an unreachable cycle, a circle or ellipse used as a child or as a parent — neither has an edge to glue to — or two glued faces of unequal length) or one whose primitives overlap throws rather than answering a wrong number, since those are authoring errors rather than degenerate numeric cases.
- `src/physics/hull-loft.ts` — the volume, centre of mass, inertia tensor, lateral surface area and projected area of a **loft**: the solid swept between two cross-sections whose outlines vary linearly along an axis (a generalized frustum). THREE/DOM-independent and not yet wired to anything in `game/`, and **computed with no numerical approximation of the axial integral**. The *sections themselves* are another matter: a loft works from outlines, so a polygonal section is reproduced exactly while a curved one (circle, ellipse) is the inscribed `LOFT_SAMPLE_COUNT`-point polyline and every quantity is exact for that polyline rather than for the curve — at 120 points a circle's area comes out 4.1e-4 low against `section-moments.ts`'s own closed form, and `hull-loft.test.ts` pins that gap so changing `LOFT_SAMPLE_COUNT` cannot move the two paths apart unnoticed (a 6-branch circle degenerates to a regular hexagon and the two agree to machine precision). The local frame is x/y in the section plane, z along the axis, with section A at `z = 0` and section B at `z = length`. **The closed form comes from one observation about degree**: interpolating two outlines linearly makes each intermediate vertex a degree-1 polynomial in the axial fraction, so the intermediate section's raw moments about the local origin are polynomials too — the area is quadratic, `∫x dA`/`∫y dA` are cubic, and `∫x² dA`/`∫y² dA`/`∫xy dA` are quartic — and every integrand the mass properties need (those, plus `z·A`, `z²·A` and `z·∫x dA`) therefore tops out at degree 4. A fixed five-station Boole's rule is exact through degree 5, so the weighted sum this module evaluates *is* the integral rather than an estimate of it; §10-2's own `V = (h/6)(A₁ + 4Am + A₂)` is the same argument one degree down, Simpson's rule being exact on the quadratic area. **Every quantity is assembled from raw moments about a fixed origin and shifted onto the centroid exactly once, at the end** — a section's *centroidal* moments would not do, since the section centroid `C(t)` is a ratio of two polynomials and not a polynomial itself, while the parallel-axis expansion `I + A·|C − C_ref|²` collapses right back onto the raw moments that are. `loftVolume`/`loftCentroid` (the centre of mass's axial position, measured from section A)/`loftCenterOfMass`/`loftInertia`/`loftLateralArea`/`loftProjectedArea` are the public quantities, each taking its two ends as a `LoftEnd` — either a `CrossSection`, or an outline handed over directly, which is how a caller expresses a cone's apex (a section of zero area, which `CrossSection` cannot describe) and how one that already holds outlines avoids re-deriving them. `loftInertia` answers an `inertia-tensor.ts` `InertiaTensor` (re-exported from here). `loftLateralArea` is the lateral band alone — each quad between corresponding vertices is generally non-planar and is split into two triangles — and **adding an exposed end face's own area is the caller's job**, since only the tree of lofts above knows which ends are covered by a neighbouring segment. `loftProjectedArea(…, axis)` is `½∮|n̂·axis| dA` over the closed boundary (lateral bands plus both caps), which is exact wherever a line of sight crosses the solid twice and over-counts by the extra crossings where it does not.

  `sectionOutline(section, sampleCount = LOFT_SAMPLE_COUNT)` lives here rather than beside the section's own integrals because the point count, the arc-length equalization and the vertex correspondence all exist solely to make two sections loftable — a section's area and second moments are reached without ever forming an outline. `LOFT_SAMPLE_COUNT` is 120 because 3, 4, 5, 6 and 8 all divide it, so sections of different edge counts still correspond vertex-for-vertex. **Equalization is by arc length, not by angle**: a circle carrying side ports is an outline of arcs and chords, and equal angles space the chord's points by `1/cos²` of the angle off its midpoint, thinning the flats and making the loft's bands uneven. The outer boundary itself is found without any coordinate matching between primitives — an attachment names exactly the two faces it hides, so the boundary is the remaining faces chained into one loop (the chaining picks the most clockwise turn at a junction, which is what hugs the outside when three primitives meet at a vertex), and a circle or ellipse root, which can carry no children, contributes its own analytic curve refined into a polyline. `alignOutlines(reference, outline)` is the anti-twist rule: it cycles the second outline to whichever offset minimizes the summed vertex-to-vertex distance, so two ends at different rotational phases still connect along the shortest bands.
- `src/physics/random.ts` — coordinate-free random number generation, split out of `vec3.ts` because a random number and a `Vec3` are different kinds of thing: the exported deterministic PRNG `mulberry32(seed)` (a `Math.random`-free `() => number` generator — the seed itself decides the sequence, which is what lets a debug stage or a save/load path reproduce the exact same layout) and `randSym(amp, rand?)` (a `[-amp, amp]` uniform scalar, `rand` defaulting to `Math.random`). Both are pure numeric functions with no `Vec3` involved.
- `src/physics/vec3.ts` — plain-object Vec3 math helpers (all pure, all returning new immutable `Vec3`s), plus `randVec`/`randPerp` — the two random helpers that actually build a `Vec3` (a `[-amp, amp]` cube-uniform vector, and a unit vector ⟂ a given direction) rather than just a scalar, so they stay here rather than in `random.ts`; both take the same optional `rand: () => number` (default `Math.random`) `randSym` does, so a caller that needs determinism (a debug stage, `game/celestial/point-field.ts`) passes its own `random.ts` `mulberry32` instance through.
- `src/physics/spatial-grid.ts` — `SpatialGrid<T>`: a general-purpose uniform grid over anything with a position — `insert(item, pos)` buckets by `floor(pos / cellSize)` per axis, `neighbors(pos)` returns everything in `pos`'s own cell plus its 26 neighbors (27 cells total). Pure, no game-state dependency; cell size is a constructor argument, so the same class backs both `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s gravity-source classification (cell size derived per call from the heaviest gridded body's `μ`) and `game/simulation/contact.ts`'s contact-pair enumeration (cell size derived per call from that frame's own radii/movement) without either caller needing to know the other exists.
- `src/physics/deque.ts` — `Deque<T>`: a generic ring-buffer double-ended queue (`pushLeft`/`pushRight`/`popLeft`/`popRight`/`at`/`peekLeft`/`peekRight`/`deleteLeftN`/`deleteRightN`/`clear`), capacity doubling on overflow. No game-state dependency; a general-purpose data structure, not physics per se, but grouped under `physics/` since it's equally pure/DOM-free and covered by the same `test:physics` runner.
- `src/physics/state-queue.ts` — `StateQueue`: a `Deque<KinematicState>`-backed time series kept newest-first (`peekLeft`/`newest` = newest, `peekRight`/`oldest` = oldest — `oldest` is `newest`'s exact symmetric counterpart, `null` on an empty queue; `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `TrajectoryLine` reads both to clamp the span it draws). `newestGap`/`oldestGap` are the matching pair of adjacent-sample time gaps [s] (0 below 2 samples): `oldestGap` is the decimation coarseness at the queue's old end — `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory.sampleInterval` getter reads it directly, and it's the basis the interpolation-error estimate at that end is sized against — while `newestGap` is the gap between the two newest samples, which is what `DynamicTrajectory.step` checks against a caller-supplied interval to decide whether its outgoing tip is worth keeping as a retained sample at all. `push` adds the latest sample; a push whose time doesn't exceed the current newest is treated as a correction and discards everything at-or-after its own time before inserting (the same "editing invalidates downstream" rule `Plan` node edits follow), so the pushed sample always ends up as the new head — `DynamicTrajectory.reset`'s discontinuous overwrite relies on exactly this to drop whatever samples the discontinuity invalidated, with no separate discard call of its own. `cleanup(maxAge, minCount)` is the age-based sibling — this is what backs `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory`'s own retained-sample window (`DynamicTrajectory.step` calls it with `keepDuration` whenever it keeps the outgoing tip): prunes samples older than `maxAge` relative to the newest one, never below `minCount`, and (when `maxAge` is nonzero) always leaves one sample older than the cutoff in place so `at(newest.t - maxAge)` still has a bracketing pair to interpolate against instead of falling outside the retained span. `discardNewest()` drops just the newest sample — `DynamicTrajectory.step` calls this instead of `cleanup` when the outgoing tip isn't worth retaining, so the incoming tip simply replaces it rather than being kept alongside it. `at(t)` binary-searches the bracketing pair; when `t` lands exactly on a stored sample's own time it returns that sample by reference with no interpolation at all, and otherwise reuses `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate` between the bracketing pair — **the sole implementation of "binary-search a timed state series and Hermite-interpolate it" in the codebase**, returning `null` outside the retained span rather than extrapolating; `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory.at` is a one-line forward to it, and `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `TrajectoryLine` sampler delegates here too rather than re-implementing the search itself. The private `bisect` (lower-bound over the descending series) is the single search primitive `push`/`cleanup`/`at` all share. `toArrayOldestFirst()` reverses the internal newest-first order into the oldest-first array `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory.samplesOldestFirst()` exposes, which `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `TrajectoryLine` reads directly from the `DynamicTrajectory` it's handed — the one place this class's storage order leaks out.
- `src/render/earth.ts` — realistic-style Earth at true radius: real photography (`assets/earth.jpg` + `assets/8k_clouds.jpg`) shaded with TSL nodes over an indexed `SphereGeometry`. **The atmosphere is analytic, not geometric** — the stacked shells this file used to draw are gone. At near=2m with a 24-bit non-logarithmic depth buffer, any geometry floating tens-to-hundreds of km above the surface z-fights with it at grazing view angles (δz ≈ z²/near/2²⁴ grows with the square of distance), and neither shell spacing nor shell count fixes the quantization itself. So the invariant is **exactly one opaque depth-tested body below ~400 km**: clouds are baked into the surface albedo, and both the near haze and the limb glow are computed per-pixel from the view direction (`ATMO_HAZE_TAU0`, `ATMO_RIM_MIN_H`/`ATMO_RIM_MAX_H` = 340 km/`ATMO_RIM_SCALE_H`), with occlusion by the Earth resolved by analytic ray-sphere intersection rather than the hardware depth test. **The surface geometry has 3 fixed LOD tiers** (64×48 / 160×120 / 384×288, all 4:3) rather than one `SphereGeometry` drawn always at full detail: `WebGPURenderer` caches a `Mesh`'s geometry when its `RenderObject` is first built, so reassigning `mesh.geometry` on a live mesh keeps drawing the old vertices — the only way to vary tessellation per frame is to build every tier's mesh once and toggle `visible`, never swap geometry. Every tier's silhouette sag stays within 1px of the true sphere at the apparent diameter it is chosen for, which is what makes the switch invisible; the top tier holds that bound out to ~60,000px, well past a body filling the screen. All 3 meshes share one material and sit in the spin group; `Earth.syncSurfaceLod(apparentDiameterPx)` (called from `game/celestial/earth-body.ts`'s `sync`, which resolves `apparentDiameterPx` via `CameraSystem.activeCameraScale` and `render/screen-lod.ts`'s `apparentSizePx`) picks the tier via `screen-lod.ts`'s `sphereLodLevel` and shows only that one. Aurora curtains are real geometry on the spin group — 4 `render/aurora.ts` `Aurora` instances, two per pole, the two layers of a pole sharing a `geomSeed` so they stay parallel — each advanced by `Earth.tick(simTime)`, which also refreshes the atmosphere shader's earth-center uniform.
- `src/render/aurora.ts` — `Aurora`: one wavy additive curtain ringing a pole at ~67° latitude, built as vertex-colored geometry whose breaks and color flicker come from periodic (loop-closing) noise functions. The constructor takes the pole sign plus the shape/color/radius/latitude/flicker offsets that distinguish one layer from another; `mesh` is the built curtain and `sync(phase)` moves its waving and flicker to that phase.
- `src/render/ships.ts` — low-poly meshes, **loaded rather than built**: the primitive-assembly code lives in `tools/export-models.mjs`, which serializes each mesh with `Object3D.toJSON()` into `src/assets/models/*.json`; at runtime this file parses those with `THREE.ObjectLoader` and `clone(true)`s them, so startup never re-assembles geometry. Before serializing the magazine (`buildMagazineMesh`, which the ammo pickup nests four of), `export-models.mjs`'s `mergeStaticChildren` collapses each `Group`'s direct mesh children sharing one material into a single baked-geometry mesh — a draw-call reduction, not a modeling choice, so it must stay confined to siblings that never move independently at runtime: it recurses into child `Group`s but never merges across a `Group` boundary, which is exactly what keeps the belt-magazine's per-round `role: 'round'` children (still individually removable by `buildMagazineFrame`, now as two merged meshes instead of 64) and the solar-panel/radiator accordion's per-fold `Fold{i}` groups (still individually resolvable by `getObjectByName` and independently rotated by `power.ts`/`radiator.ts`) intact — only what's *inside* one fold gets merged. The modeled shapes are: magazine belt links (`buildMagazineMesh`: caged 4×8 = 32 visible brass rounds — 1:1 with `MAG_ROUNDS`) and orbital ammo pickups (`buildAmmo`), enemy variants (`buildEnemyShip`/`buildStage0EnemyShip`), shared-geometry tracer bullets (`buildBulletMesh`/`buildPlasmaMesh`; `bulletBodyResources`/`bulletHaloResources`/`plasmaBodyResources`/`casingBodyResources` expose the same shared geometry/material pairs for `game/simulation/entity-manager.ts`'s `InstancedPool`s, so the instanced draw path never re-derives them), CIWS-motif bottleneck casings (`LatheGeometry` profile: rim + extractor groove + tapered body + shoulder + neck), debris fragments (`debrisFragmentResources`: `DEBRIS_FRAGMENT_VARIANT_COUNT` unit-scale geometries — outer-hull chunks/panels/rods built from `debrisChunk`/`debrisPanel`/`debrisRod`'s parsed assets plus displaced octahedra/thin boxes/rods — baked once at module load from a fixed-seed `mulberry32` stream, and one shared white `MeshStandardMaterial`; a fragment's size/aspect variation and which of the two accent-vs-`COLOR_SHIP_DARK_HULL` colors it gets are therefore properties of the *variant* and of the per-instance color `game/simulation/entity-manager.ts`'s `InstancedPool`s carry, not of a per-instance geometry or material), ejected barrels/magazine frames, and the base station (`buildBaseModel`: central hub + 4 radial trusses/docking modules + 2 solar wings + an accent beacon, all primitive-built rather than loaded — the one shape here assembled directly with `THREE.*Geometry` calls at construction time instead of parsed from `src/assets/models/*.json`, the same way debris/barrels are). `cloneIndependent` (the JSON-template clone/material-independence helper above) also calls `render/pipeline/lit-layer.ts`'s `markLitOpaque` on every clone it returns, so every ship/enemy/magazine/casing/debris-panel mesh built through the JSON pipeline joins the G-buffer pass's lit-opaque layer for free; the handful of meshes this file builds directly rather than through that helper (`buildAsteroidMesh`, `buildBaseModel`, `buildBarrelMesh`) call `markLitOpaque` themselves.
- `src/render/hull/hull-triangles.ts` — the hull's triangulation: `HullShape`(`HullBand`/`HullBeam`)から頂点座標と面の索引だけを返す純関数で、**THREE には触れない** — 生成した面の面積を `physics/hull-loft.ts` の `loftLateralArea` と突き合わせる検証を、描画器を持たない `tsconfig.test.json` で行えるようにするためである。帯の頂点の対応は、両端の輪郭を**進行方向に垂直な共通の平面へ落としてから**取る:断面の座標系はノードの持ち物であり、軸が逆を向くノード同士では基底が鏡像になるので、断面座標のまま `alignOutlines` を掛けると帯がねじれる。`alignOutlines` は入力の頂点そのものを巡回させて返すので、先頭がどこから来たかで巡回量が分かり、世界座標の環を同じだけずらせる。露出した端面は重心からの扇で塞ぎ、トラスと分離機構は縦通材と横枠の直方体の集まり(`lattice` / `collar`)として張る。`trianglesArea` は面積の総和で、検証だけが読む。
- `src/render/hull/loft-mesh.ts` — `buildLoftGeometry(shape)`:上の三角形を `THREE.BufferGeometry` へ詰め替えるだけの器。
- `src/render/hull/part-meshes.ts` — 外装の搭載要素の造形。放熱板と太陽電池パドルは蛇腹の折り目 Group を入れ子にして返し、その名前は `vessel/radiator.ts` / `vessel/power.ts` が `getObjectByName` で引くものと一致する(毎フレーム `rotation.y` を1つ書くだけで蛇腹が伸縮する構造)。それ以外の外装要素は `buildFitting` が種別ごとの単純な形(ノズル・砲身・スラスタ・アンテナ・熱シールド・箱)を、取り付け位置の座標系(+Z が外向き)で作る。
- `src/render/vessel-wireframe.ts` — `buildVesselWireframe(tree, capsules)`: the tree's edges drawn as straight lines and each capsule as its axis plus a ring at either end, as one `THREE.Group` of two `LineSegments`. It exists because the lofted hull skin does not, so this is how a design's derived shape and its contact capsules are checked by eye; `Vessel`'s constructor adds it to `renderObject` for any design carrying an assembly, once — it rides the ship's own transform from there. Its own `.visible` follows `render/graphics-settings.ts`'s `GraphicsSettings.current.wireframe` (off by default), set at build time and re-read every `Vessel.syncVessel` so the 設定ビューの描画タブ toggle takes effect live on an already-built ship, not just on the next one placed.
- `src/render/instanced-pool.ts` — `InstancedPool`: a general-purpose (no `game`/`physics` imports) wrapper around a set of individually-transformed instances sharing one geometry/material — `beginFrame()`/`push(obj)`/`endFrame()` per frame, writing transforms into a single `THREE.InstancedMesh` of the caller-given capacity rather than rebuilding geometry (WebGPU gotcha, below). `push` reads `obj.matrixWorld` (calling `obj.updateMatrixWorld()` itself), not the local position/quaternion/scale directly, so a caller can push either a standalone `Object3D` or a child of a `Group` that was updated separately — `game/simulation/entity-manager.ts`'s bullet pools need the latter (see below). **`mesh.count` stays pinned at the full capacity and is never moved**: three.js's `InstanceNode` decides how `instanceMatrix` reaches the shader from `InstancedMesh.count`, and settles both that decision and the buffer length once, at the first draw — so a slot with nothing in it is retired by writing the all-zero `PARKED` matrix into it, not by shrinking the drawn count. `endFrame` parks only the slots between this frame's push count and the previous frame's, so that cost tracks how far the count dropped rather than the capacity; the whole `instanceMatrix` (and `instanceColor`, when the pool was built with one) is still re-uploaded every frame, and the vertex shader still runs for every slot including the parked ones. The constructor calls `render/pipeline/lit-layer.ts`'s `markLitOpaque(this.mesh)` — an `InstancedMesh` is itself a `Mesh`, so the casing/debris-fragment pools' `MeshStandardMaterial` joins the G-buffer pass's lit-opaque layer the same way any other standard-material mesh does, while the bullet/plasma pools' basic materials are left untouched. `dispose()` detaches the mesh from its parent and calls `THREE.InstancedMesh.dispose()`, which releases the instance buffers and touches neither geometry nor material — **the pool never disposes those**, since they arrive from the caller and only their owner may destroy them; for the bullet/plasma/casing/debris-fragment pools that owner is `render/ships.ts`'s module-scope cache, which outlives any one pool, so disposing them here would leave a later pool drawing from a freed buffer.
- `src/render/graphics-settings.ts` — `GraphicsSettings`: the source of truth for the drawing-quality settings, persisted to `localStorage` under `tepui.settings.graphics`. **Only elements that genuinely disappear when switched off are listed** — a toggle whose picture doesn't change is indistinguishable from a bug, so an element gets an entry only once it exists as its own mesh or draw. Today: 小天体の点群 (`PointFieldView`), 惑星の環 (`RingView`, whose shadow on the parent body's own surface goes with it — otherwise the band would vanish while its shade stayed), オーロラ and 大気リム (`render/earth.ts`'s own objects), 艦のワイヤーフレーム (`render/vessel-wireframe.ts`'s design-tree/collision-capsule debug geometry, off by default in every preset — `Vessel` reads it live every `syncVessel` off the `GraphicsSettings` reference its `VesselDeps` carries), plus two scalars — the resolution multiplier and `lodBias`. **`lodBias` is applied to the apparent diameter, not to the ladder**: each celestial body's `sync` runs its `apparentSizePx` through `scaleApparentSize`, so one number moves the LOD tier and the `showsPhysicalSphere` cutoff together and `render/screen-lod.ts` stays a pure function of what is on screen. `antialias` is here too but can only take effect at renderer construction, so its row says 次回起動から rather than pretending to apply live. Owned by `main.ts`, built before `initScene` (the renderer needs `antialias`) and shared by reference with `createGameScene`, `SettingsView`/`GraphicsPanel`, every `VesselDeps` (`EntityManager`/`Docking`/`Stage.vesselDeps`) and — through `Game` — `EnvironmentScene`, which hands it to each `CelestialBody.sync`.
- `src/render/scene.ts` — `createGameScene(canvas, graphics)`: canvas → `WebGPURenderer` + `THREE.Scene`, the only place the renderer is created. It takes the `GraphicsSettings` because two renderer options can only be passed at construction: `antialias` (which is why the setting is labelled 次回起動から) and `trackTimestamp: true` (the WebGPU device's `timestamp-query` feature, which `gpu-timings.ts` needs). `trackTimestamp` is therefore always on rather than following the load-inspection window — the cost of that was measured against the window closed and stayed inside the run-to-run spread of the frame interval. `GameScene.setResolutionScale(scale)` is the resolution multiplier's one entry point, registered with `GraphicsSettings.bindResolutionTarget`; the scale is kept in a local and re-applied by the `resize` handler, so it survives a window resize and a move to a display with a different `devicePixelRatio`. `EnvironmentScene` itself now lives at `game/celestial/environment-scene.ts` (see above) — everything left under `render/` is THREE builders, taking `game/`-owned state as plain arguments rather than importing `game/` modules, with one narrow exception: `ships.ts` reads `game/const.ts` for gameplay-tuning values (colors, geometry sizes) that must match the rest of the game's tuning, the same read-only relationship every constant-consuming module has to that file.
- `src/render/pipeline/lit-layer.ts` — `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` (a `THREE.Layers` channel) and `markLitOpaque(root)`: the marker for "consumes scene lighting, belongs in the G-buffer/material passes" — everything else (celestial bodies' self-shaded `MeshBasicNodeMaterial`, lines, billboards, the star shell) draws no G-buffer output and must not corrupt its depth attachment (the star shell in particular has `depthTest: false`). `markLitOpaque` traverses every `Mesh` whose material — or any entry of a material array — has `isMeshStandardMaterial` and *moves* it onto the layer with `mesh.layers.set(LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER)` (not `.enable`, which would leave it on the default channel 0 too) — a marked mesh therefore leaves channel 0 outright, and since the world pass draws with the camera's default mask (channel 0), that single move is what keeps the world pass from ever drawing a lit-opaque mesh a second time on top of what the material pass already drew: **a marked object is drawn by the G-buffer and material passes and by nothing else.** It is a no-op on anything else, so calling it on an already-built object of unknown material mix is always safe, and under-calling it is the only real failure mode (a mesh silently missing from the G-buffer/material passes, and therefore left undrawn everywhere, since it's also absent from channel 0). Membership is decided under `render/` alone (never `game/`, per the material/renderOrder/LOD ownership rule) — `ships.ts`'s `cloneIndependent` and `instanced-pool.ts`'s `InstancedPool` constructor are its call sites (see both above).
- `src/render/pipeline/gbuffer.ts` — `GBufferPass`: the frame's first pass, drawing depth/normal/roughness for every lit-opaque mesh into a 2-target MRT `RenderTarget` (`samples: 0` — interpolating a normal across a multisampled triangle edge produces a direction that belongs to neither surface, so the G-buffer is never multisampled) plus an attached `DepthTexture`. `render(scene, camera, width, height)` narrows the camera to `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` for the call (saving/restoring `camera.layers.mask`, since `RenderPipeline` hands it the same camera object the world pass then uses), sets the renderer's global MRT node and render target, calls `gpu.beginPass(GPU_PASS.gbuffer)` immediately before its own `renderer.render()`, and restores both. Setting a renderer-global MRT node (`renderer.setMRT(...)`) rather than an `overrideMaterial` is what lets the pass draw the real scene materials unmodified: three.js's `NodeMaterial.setupOutput` still runs `setupVariants` (so per-material `roughness` and normal-mapped normals resolve correctly) but substitutes the MRT node for the lit-color result, so the lighting graph itself is never generated. The `normal` output is view-space `normalView` (the post-normal-map shading normal) run through the module's own `octEncodeNormal` — an octahedral encoding into a 0..1 `vec2`, with its own `signNotZero` helper standing in for TSL's `sign()` (which returns 0 at 0 and breaks the fold) — chosen so the buffer reads as a picture once a debug viewer decodes it; the `roughness` output is the TSL `roughness` node as-is. Both MRT output keys must match a `target.textures[i].name` for `MRTNode.setup` to route them at all, which is why the two textures are explicitly named `'normal'`/`'roughness'` after construction. Exposes the three textures (`normalTexture`/`roughnessTexture`/`depthTexture`) as read-only getters; `render/pipeline/render-pipeline.ts`'s debug-target composite materials (below) are their first reader. The module also exports `octDecodeNormal`, `octEncodeNormal`'s exact inverse and defined right beside it (a change to the encoding must be mirrored in the decoding), which is what lets the 法線 debug view turn the buffer back into a picture instead of a raw rg pair.
- `src/render/pipeline/debug-target.ts` — `DebugTargetId` (`'off' | 'normal' | 'roughness' | 'depth' | 'diffuse' | 'specular' | 'material'`), `DEBUG_TARGETS` (the id/label pairs in display order the 描画 tab's `SegmentedControl` is built from), and `DebugTargetHost` (`{debugTarget: DebugTargetId}`) — the narrow structural interface `hud/graphics-panel.ts`'s `GraphicsPanel` writes through so `game/hud/` never imports `RenderPipeline` itself, the same shape `plan.ts`'s `DisplayDurationSource`/`graphics-settings.ts`'s `ResolutionTarget` already follow. Imports nothing, so both sides of that boundary can depend on it with no cycle. Deliberately no `'world'` entry: the composite pass's own normal (`'off'`) output already puts the world colour target on screen unmodified, so a `'world'` entry would show the identical picture. `diffuse`/`specular` read `LightPrepass`'s own two output textures; `material` reads `MaterialPass`'s own isolated debug target (populated only while this id is selected, so choosing anything else costs no extra geometry draw).
- `src/render/pipeline/render-pipeline.ts` — `RenderPipeline implements DebugTargetHost`: owns the frame's render-pass sequence, kept distinct from the per-object `render/**` modules that only build/sync individual meshes. Currently five passes: a **G-buffer pass** (`GBufferPass`, above — built and owned by this class, taking the same `renderer`/`gpu` this class itself holds), a **lighting pass** (`LightPrepass`, below — built and owned by this class too, reading only the G-buffer and `SunLight` (below) to write diffuse/specular irradiance into its own 2-target MRT), a **material pass** (`MaterialPass`, below — the sole consumer of that MRT: it draws every `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` mesh into the same offscreen HDR target the world pass shares, clearing it as the first write there), a **world pass** (the scene, drawn into that same offscreen HDR `RenderTarget` — `HalfFloatType`/`RGBAFormat`, `depthBuffer: true`, `samples` set to 4 when `graphics.current.antialias` holds and 0 otherwise, read once at construction since an offscreen target doesn't inherit the canvas's own multisampling and `antialias` itself only takes effect at renderer construction — with `renderer.autoClear` forced `false` for this one render call and restored to `true` immediately after, so it composites on top of what the material pass already wrote rather than erasing it; the material pass has to run first for exactly this reason — the world pass draws transparent objects (orbit lines, plumes, billboards) last within its own sort, so the opaque ships' depth must already be in the shared depth buffer before it runs, or a transparent object sitting in front of a ship would be overwritten by it), and a **composite pass** (a `QuadMesh` whose material writes to the canvas — the one place the linear→sRGB conversion happens, since every offscreen texture it might sample stays linear/`NoColorSpace`). `EXPOSURE` = 0.72, the frame's one brightness multiplier applied uniformly to every HDR value the composite pass samples: a physically-derived exposure is a later concern, and today's value is chosen as the level `render/ring.ts`'s single-scattering radiance (whose own hand-tuned coefficient moved here) stays unsaturated at in LDR. The composite pass no longer has one fixed material — it holds one `MeshBasicNodeMaterial` per `DebugTargetId`, built once in the constructor (`off` samples the HDR world texture × `exposure`; `normal` samples `GBufferPass.normalTexture` through `octDecodeNormal` and displays `n*0.5+0.5`; `roughness` samples `GBufferPass.roughnessTexture` as greyscale; `depth` samples `GBufferPass.depthTexture` and maps it onto `[0,1]` **logarithmically** between the camera's own near/far via the module-private `viewDistanceFromDepth` — a flat linear map is a white screen at this near=2m/far=6e7m ratio, and three.js's own `perspectiveDepthToViewZ` cannot be used at that same ratio: it computes `far.sub(near)` directly, which float32 rounds to exactly `far` once `near` falls below that magnitude's own ULP, driving the formula's denominator to exactly 0 right at `depth=1` (the far plane — background, never-drawn regions) and producing NaN there specifically, while leaving already-drawn geometry (whose depth sits well below 1) unaffected — `viewDistanceFromDepth` reorders the identical algebra into `near*far / (far*(1-depth) + near*depth)`, exact and finite at both endpoints since neither term needs the two magnitudes subtracted directly; `diffuse`/`specular` sample `LightPrepass.diffuseTexture`/`.specularTexture` × `exposure`, since irradiance is an HDR value that can exceed 1; `material` samples `MaterialPass.texture` × `exposure`, the material pass's own isolated debug target) — and `render()` swaps `quad.material` to whichever one `debugTarget` (the public field this class exposes as `DebugTargetHost`) currently names, immediately before the pass draws. Building one material per target rather than one branching on a uniform is deliberate: a uniform branch would bind and sample every G-buffer/lighting texture on every frame of ordinary play, not just while a developer is looking. **`debugTarget` is session state only, reset to `'off'` on every reload** — a debug view silently surviving past a page refresh is a trap for whoever opens the game next. A debug target's shader compiles lazily on its first draw, so switching may hitch a frame; acceptable for a developer tool. Selecting a target never skips any pass — the point is to show what an ordinary frame actually produced, not a special-cased one; selecting `'material'` specifically is what tells `MaterialPass.render` to also draw into its own isolated target that frame (`this.debugTarget === 'material'`, passed as that call's trailing argument), the one pass whose own debug draw is otherwise skipped to avoid a third geometry pass nobody is looking at. `render(scene, camera)` resolves the renderer's current drawing-buffer size once and hands it to `GBufferPass.render`/`LightPrepass.render`/`MaterialPass.render` (each resizes its own target(s) to match) before resizing the HDR target itself, so every offscreen target tracks window/resolution-scale changes the same way the canvas does. Each pass is bracketed by its own `gpu.beginPass(GPU_PASS.<pass>)` immediately before the `renderer.render()` call that pass issues (`GBufferPass.render`'s own `beginPass(GPU_PASS.gbuffer)`, `LightPrepass.render`'s own `beginPass(GPU_PASS.lighting)`, `MaterialPass.render`'s own `beginPass(GPU_PASS.material)` — called once before each of its own render calls, so both its optional debug-target draw and its always-on shared-target draw carry the same GPU-time attribution when both run in one frame — and this class's own `beginPass(GPU_PASS.world)`/`beginPass(GPU_PASS.composite)` around the world render and the composite `QuadMesh.render`'s internal call) — see `gpu-timings.ts` above for how each turns into `GpuTimings.msOf(GPU_PASS.<pass>)`. `main.ts` builds `gpu`/`pipeline` immediately after `initScene` (both need only `gs.renderer`/`graphics`) — ahead of `Hud`/`PauseMenu`, since `initHud` now threads `pipeline` into `PauseMenu`'s constructor as the `DebugTargetHost` its 描画 tab's `GraphicsPanel` writes `debugTarget` through — and hands the same instance to `Game`'s constructor; `Game.render()` only calls `pipeline.render(this._scene, this.cameraSystem.activeCamera)` — `Game` holds no render-target or pass state of its own. Also owns the `SunLight` (below) as the public `sunLight` getter, constructed alongside `LightPrepass`; `game/celestial/environment-scene.ts`'s `EnvironmentScene` is the sole writer (`Game`'s constructor threads `pipeline.sunLight` into it), and disposal follows `GBufferPass`'s own — `dispose()` tears down `LightPrepass`/`MaterialPass` too.
- `src/render/pipeline/sun-light.ts` — `SunLight`: the value object answering "how much light arrives at a shaded point, and from where" — direction (the unit direction from the render origin toward the star)/colour/intensity (the irradiance a receiver gets, inverse-square already folded in)/ambient intensity/the CPU-computed occlusion scalar (`physics/shadow.ts`'s `sunlitFactor`, unfloored) — written once a frame via `set()`, with no knowledge of any material's own reflectance, which is `LightPrepass`'s side of the boundary. `angularRadius` (rad, `0` today, currently unread) is shaped for a future sphere-light swap without disturbing this interface. `sunVisibility(): FloatNode` is the occlusion-query seam a shadow map replaces later — today its body just applies `game/const.ts`'s `SHADOW_MIN_SUN` floor to the raw occlusion scalar; `ambientIntensity`'s getter applies `SHADOW_MIN_AMBIENT` the same way rather than through a twin hook method, since ambient occlusion has no shadow-map future to seam against. This module reads `SHADOW_MIN_SUN`/`SHADOW_MIN_AMBIENT` directly from `game/const.ts` — the same narrow constant-reading exception `render/ships.ts` already has — rather than have `EnvironmentScene` pre-apply the floor, so the seam itself lives on the `render/` side of the boundary it's meant to replace.
- `src/render/pipeline/light-prepass.ts` — `LightPrepass`: the frame's lighting pass, reading only the G-buffer's normal/roughness/depth and `SunLight` to write diffuse/specular irradiance into its own 2-target MRT `RenderTarget` (`depthBuffer: false`, `samples: 0`, both textures `RGBAFormat`/`HalfFloatType` — the design roadmap's "rgb16float" has no WebGPU counterpart, so the unused alpha channel is the cost of reusing the 4-channel format). Reconstructs the shaded point's view-space position via TSL's own `getViewPosition(screenUV, rawDepth, projMatrixInverse)` (matrix-based, not `perspectiveDepthToViewZ`'s direct near/far subtraction — see `render-pipeline.ts`'s own entry for why that formula is unusable at this near/far ratio; `getViewPosition` sidesteps it by keeping the ill-conditioned arithmetic inside `camera.projectionMatrixInverse`'s own CPU-side construction rather than recomputing it per pixel), decodes the normal via `gbuffer.ts`'s shared `octDecodeNormal`, and combines Lambertian diffuse (`sunColour × sunIntensity × saturate(dot(N,L)) × sunVisibility()`, plus a fixed `0x8899bb` ambient tint × `ambientIntensity` — independent of `EnvironmentScene`'s own copy of that literal) with a GGX specular term evaluated at `F0=1` (`D_GGX`/`V_GGX_SmithCorrelated`/`F_Schlick`, three's own lower-level BRDF primitives rather than the higher-level `BRDF_GGX` wrapper, whose N/V/L come from global per-fragment accessor nodes meaningless on a fullscreen quad pass — this pass supplies its own reconstructed N/V/L directly); the true material F0 (a colour) is left for `material-pass.ts` to multiply in afterward, since this pass has no albedo/metalness to read. `projMatrixInverse`/`sunDirectionView` (the world-space `SunLight.direction` rotated into the *scene* camera's view space, since `L` and the G-buffer's `N` must share a space and `QuadMesh`'s own fixed camera isn't the scene one) are both written once per `render(camera, width, height)` call — the latter via a scratch `THREE.Vector3.transformDirection(camera.matrixWorldInverse)` rather than a shader-side matrix, keeping the shading graph itself matrix-free, the same "own uniform fed from the real camera" pattern `render-pipeline.ts`'s `depthDebugNear`/`Far` already follow. `render()` brackets its own `renderer.render()` call with `gpu.beginPass(GPU_PASS.lighting)`, the same shape `GBufferPass.render` uses.
- `src/render/pipeline/material-pass.ts` — `MaterialPass`: the frame's material pass, the one place `LightPrepass`'s two irradiance textures get multiplied by an actual surface's albedo/metalness/F0 and turned into a final colour. It does this by upgrading each `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` mesh's `MeshStandardMaterial` to a `MeshStandardNodeMaterial` carrying a custom `MaterialPassLightingModel extends PhysicalLightingModel` — `direct()` is a no-op (the scene's own `DirectionalLight`/`AmbientLight` contribute nothing directly; see `EnvironmentScene`'s entry above for why those lights still have to exist and stay visible to this pass's own camera even so) and `indirect()` reads `diffuseIrradiance`/`specularIrradiance` (the two `LightPrepass` textures, sampled once at construction) and adds `diffuseIrradiance × diffuseColor.rgb` to `reflectedLight.indirectDiffuse` and `specularIrradiance × specularColorBlended` (F0=0.04 mixed with the albedo by `metalness`, since `MeshStandardNodeMaterial`'s own blended F0 isn't exposed) to `reflectedLight.indirectSpecular` — everything else (BRDF, maps, roughness/metalness handling) stays three's own `MeshStandardNodeMaterial` machinery untouched. `reflectedLight.indirectDiffuse`/`.indirectSpecular` are typed as bare `Node` in `@types/three` (no type argument, so none of the `addAssign` extension methods attach) even though they're the same TSL node type as everywhere else in this file — the same `as unknown as` gap `light-prepass.ts`'s `D_GGX`/`V_GGX_SmithCorrelated`/`F_Schlick` results already have to cross, read here through the same cast to `Vec3Node`. The upgrade (private `upgrade(mesh)`) happens inside `render()` itself, via `scene.traverse` gated to `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` meshes, every frame — not at mesh-build time in `render/ships.ts`, since the lighting model needs `diffuseNode`/`specularNode` bound to `LightPrepass`'s textures, which don't exist yet when `ships.ts` builds a mesh — a `WeakSet<THREE.Material>` makes every visit after the first a no-op (checked before the `isMeshStandardMaterial` test, so an already-upgraded mesh costs one set lookup and nothing else), and a mesh whose material isn't standard in the first place is skipped without ever allocating anything. `standardMaterialParams` copies exactly the fields `MeshStandardNodeMaterial`'s constructor accepts off the original material (color/maps/roughness/metalness/normal/emissive/alpha/transparency/side) before the original is disposed. `render(scene, camera, sharedTarget, width, height, showDebugTarget)` narrows `camera.layers` to `LIT_OPAQUE_LAYER` for the call (saved/restored, the same pattern `GBufferPass.render` uses) and always draws once into `sharedTarget` — the HDR target the world pass shares — clearing it (`autoClear = true`), since this is the first write there that frame; when `showDebugTarget` holds (the `'material'` debug view is selected) it draws the identical geometry a second time into its own private `target` first, so a developer can see the material pass's output in isolation against a cleared black background rather than the composited scene — this second draw, and the resize it needs, are skipped entirely otherwise, so ordinary play never pays for a third geometry pass. `dispose()` only tears down that private target; the upgraded materials themselves are owned by the meshes they replaced on, same as any other material.
- `src/render/celestial-grid.ts` — `CelestialGrid`: reference-only equatorial and ecliptic grids (a basis circle, latitude/longitude intersection crosses at 15° steps, and a pole marker per plane), each independently toggleable across all three parts (`CelestialGridVisibility`'s six flags — plane/pole/grid × equator/ecliptic). Each plane draws as exactly three objects — one `THREE.Line` for the basis circle, and one `THREE.LineSegments` apiece batching every grid-cross segment and every pole-marker segment into a single vertex buffer — so a toggle's draw cost is one draw call regardless of how many crosses/markers it contains, capping the whole grid at 6 draw calls. Vertices are built once at construction, fixed points on a unit sphere at `render/stars.ts`'s `STAR_SHELL_RADIUS`; `sync(visibility, cam, scale)` only toggles visibility and repositions/rescales the whole shell (`scale` relative to `STAR_SHELL_RADIUS` — `EnvironmentScene.sync` computes it from `overviewMode`, so the grid still reads at map zoom without this module reading `CameraSystem`/`game/const.ts` itself) — it never rebuilds geometry. The equatorial basis is the game's ECI basis directly (Y = pole); the ecliptic basis is ECI rotated through `physics/ecliptic.ts`'s exported `Q_ECL_TO_ECI`, so the 23.44° tilt is never hand-written here. All six toggles default to false — nothing from this module draws until something turns them on; the toggle state itself is `Navball.gridVisibility` (see `src/game/navball/` above).
- `src/render/celestial-surface.ts` — `CelestialSurface`: a celestial body's surface mesh together with its day/night shading. **Celestial bodies never take part in scene lighting.** A body's drawn position is not its true one (combat view compresses it onto a fixed distance from the camera, `point-body.ts` puts it on the star shell), so a shared parallel light would put the terminator in the wrong place — up to 179° off for a planet, since the direction to the star from Earth and from that planet are unrelated. Instead each body holds a `sunDirection` uniform, set every `sync` from `Ephemeris.sunDirFrom(truePosition, t)`, and shades itself (`albedo × (NIGHT_AMBIENT + lambert × (1 − NIGHT_AMBIENT))` on an unlit `MeshBasicNodeMaterial`). `textured(url, widthSegments, heightSegments)`/`solid(color, widthSegments, heightSegments)` are the two surfaces bodies are built from, each wrapping one `mesh: THREE.Mesh` at a caller-chosen tessellation; `render/earth.ts` follows the same rule with its own richer node graph, reusing the exported `NIGHT_AMBIENT` so every body's night side sits at one brightness. **The unit-sphere geometry is shared across every body requesting a dimension pair that matches one of `screen-lod.ts`'s `SPHERE_LOD_LADDER` tiers** (module-private `unitSphereGeometry`, a `Map<SphereLodLevel, THREE.BufferGeometry>` keyed by the matching ladder entry) — `SphereBody`/`PointBody` build one `CelestialSurface` per ladder tier per body, so without sharing, ~100 bodies × 3 tiers would mint that many redundant unit spheres. A dimension pair that doesn't match any ladder tier (`render/stars.ts`'s `createMoon`, which needs `mesh.geometry.rotateY(...)` once at construction to align the moon texture's prime meridian) falls through to a private, per-call geometry instead — mutating a *shared* geometry there would corrupt every other body drawn at that tier. `unitSphereGeometry` reports which of the two it handed back, and the surface keeps that answer so `dispose()` frees a private geometry and leaves a ladder-shared one standing. `dispose()` also frees the `textured()` map explicitly: the texture is only ever referenced from inside the TSL node graph, and `THREE.Material.dispose()` dispatches an event without ever reaching a material's textures, so nothing else would. The scene's single `DirectionalLight` (`EnvironmentScene`) is left for the real-scale objects drawn near the render origin — ships, debris, casings — where one direction, taken from the render origin, is right for all of them. `setRingShadowSystem(rings, bodyCenter, bodyRadius, displayScale, axis)` is the body's own half of a ring casting shade on its planet: it holds a fixed-size array of 32 uniform slots (comfortably above any registered `RingSystemDef`'s band count) and, per surface fragment, intersects the sun ray with each active band's ring plane and darkens the direct (lambert) term alone — never the night-side ambient floor — by that band's own Beer–Lambert transmittance at the sun's grazing angle through the ring, so a planet's evening terminator can fall inside a band's shadow while the band itself still reads correctly lit from other angles. Whichever `SphereBody`/`PointBody` owns both a surface and a `RingView` calls this once per `sync` alongside the ring's own `sync`, from the same resolved position/scale/axis, so the two can never disagree about where the ring plane sits.
- `src/render/{stars,billboard,glow-texture}.ts` — stars as a textured `SphereGeometry` shell (`createStars`, `BackSide`, depth-tested off since the shell always draws first at `renderOrder = -10`) painted with a real starfield photo, not per-star geometry — WebGPU points are still 1px and `THREE.Points` sizing still doesn't work, so any *procedural* point-style star field would still need small triangles the way the ring/asteroid point clouds already use, but a baked sky photo sidesteps the question entirely; `createSun`/`createMoon` build the sun billboard and moon mesh THREE objects that `game/celestial/{sun-body,sphere-body}.ts` place and scale each frame (`SUN_DISTANCE`=4.2e7 / `MOON_VIS_DIST`=4.5e7 m, inside the 6e7 far plane); camera-facing `Billboard` sprites (flashes, plume, RCS puffs) over a shared radial-`glow-texture`. `stars.ts` exports `STAR_SHELL_RADIUS` — the star shell's fixed radius — so `CelestialGrid` can share the exact same camera-following shell radius instead of picking its own. `createStars`/`createSun` return a `Stars`/`Sun` carrying its own `dispose()` alongside the meshes, rather than the bare mesh: the star shell's `TextureLoader` image and the sun billboard's plane are per-call resources with no other reference, and `THREE.Material.dispose()` never chains to a `map`, so the only way to free them is for the builder to hand back the means to.
- `src/render/screen-lod.ts` — the pure functions that turn a screen-space scale (metersPerPixel) into a display detail level, with no THREE or game/physics coordinate-type dependency (scalar arguments only): `apparentSizePx(worldSize, metersPerPixel)` (a world-space size's on-screen extent in px), `sphereLodLevel(apparentDiameterPx)` (picks the coarsest of a fixed 3-tier `SPHERE_LOD_LADDER` whose polygon-silhouette sagitta — `≈ R·(π/N)²/2`, converted to px via the diameter/metersPerPixel relation without needing metersPerPixel itself — stays within 1px; `render/earth.ts`'s surface-mesh LOD and every `game/celestial/sphere-body.ts`'s `SphereBody`/`point-body.ts`'s `PointBody` instance are its consumers), `showsPhysicalSphere(apparentDiameterPx)` (below `PHYSICAL_DIAMETER_THRESHOLD_PX` = 2px a body isn't worth drawing as a sphere at all — `SphereBody` simply hides it, `PointBody` falls back to its billboard in combat view — the single threshold every celestial body's sphere mesh is gated on), and `ringPixelCoverage(widthMeters, metersPerPixel)` (a ring band's fractional screen coverage below 1px, the same "what does this look like on screen" judgment as the sphere LOD, not a ring-specific optical quantity — `game/celestial/ring-view.ts`'s `sync` is its consumer, feeding a line-classified band's `RingVisual` a dimming `coverage` as it shrinks toward and past sub-pixel). This is also where a future screen-space shadow-cascade/depth-cascade selection (§1-6-1/§1-3) belongs.
- `src/render/line-style.ts` — `LineStyle` (color/opacity/renderOrder/dash) and its `LineDash`: the values that decide how a folded line looks, held apart from the drawing mechanism below so a module that only carries style values — `game/const.ts`'s `LINE_STYLE` table above most of all — reads the type without pulling `render/curve.ts` and THREE in behind it. That separation is load-bearing: `tsconfig.test.json` compiles `game/const.ts` for `test:physics` under a DOM-less `lib`, which `curve.ts` (it reads `window.innerHeight`) cannot satisfy.
- `src/render/curve.ts` — `Curve`: the sole mechanism for drawing a folded-line curve in THREE, shared by `game/orbit-line.ts`'s `OrbitLine` and `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `TrajectoryLine`, and the single place that decides **how densely to sample a curve**. `setCurve(sample: CurveSampler, {revision, camera})` takes an opaque `(t: number, out: THREE.Vector3) => void` over `t∈[0,1]` — it has no idea whether that's an ellipse or a Hermite-interpolated point series, and a sampler whose `sample(1)` reproduces `sample(0)` simply closes the drawn loop with no special case needed — and adaptively bisects it: starting from `INITIAL_SEGMENTS` coarse chords, each candidate segment's midpoint is checked against a screen-space sagitta target (`MAX_EDGE_SAG_PX`, converted to world metres from the caller-supplied `camera`'s own position/fov/aspect, cached once per `setCurve` call rather than re-read per subdivision test) and a turn-angle cap (`MAX_EDGE_TURN`, a floor so a segment can't grow arbitrarily coarse once zoomed out far enough that the sagitta target alone stops binding), recursing until both are satisfied or the `maxVertices` budget / `MAX_SUBDIVIDE_DEPTH` recursion cap is hit. **The `maxVertices` budget is split evenly across the `INITIAL_SEGMENTS` initial chords before each is subdivided** (each chord's share is "remaining budget ÷ remaining chords," so an unused remainder rolls forward to the chords after it) — subdivision is depth-first and a vertex that doesn't fit the budget is simply dropped, so without this split the first chords would exhaust the budget and leave the rest of the curve entirely undrawn; the even split instead means a tight budget coarsens the whole curve uniformly. A candidate segment's screen-space sagitta is converted to world metres via `scaleAtLocal`, whose forward-axis depth is floored at the camera's own near plane and, for a point *behind* that plane (depth < near, unprojectable — nowhere on screen), falls back to straight camera-to-point distance instead — reading raw depth there would collapse to `projection.ts`'s `MIN_DEPTH` floor and demand near-plane resolution for a stretch of curve that never appears on screen, which in combat view can burn the whole vertex budget on the half of an orbit passing behind the camera. Re-sampling is skipped whenever `revision` (compared by `!==`) and a representative screen scale (compared with a `SCALE_REBAKE_RATIO` band) are both unchanged since the previous call — the representative value is the **minimum** m/px over `INITIAL_SEGMENTS + 1` coarse sample points, not just `t=0`, since the point that decides how finely to subdivide is whichever one sits closest to the camera, and a single `t=0` reading oscillates every frame on a curve whose `t=0` end crosses the view plane. **The baked vertices' source of truth (`bakedLocal`) is a `Float64Array` in the sampler's own coordinate system, but what reaches the GPU (`positions`, f32) is always that value minus a `pivot` kept near the camera** — a sampler's coordinates can sit tens of thousands of km from its own origin (a LEO orbit is ~6.8e6 m out), and f32's ~7-digit relative precision at that magnitude quantizes to roughly half a meter, which at the combat chase camera's close range (~0.026 m/px) reads as ~19px of jitter; writing camera-relative differences instead keeps the magnitude — and so the quantization step — near zero regardless of where the sampled curve itself sits. `pivot` is re-seeded (and the baked positions re-written to reflect it) whenever the curve rebakes, and otherwise only once the camera has drifted far enough from the current `pivot` that the resulting f32 quantization would exceed `PIVOT_MAX_ERROR_PX` (well under `MAX_EDGE_SAG_PX`, so the residual quantization stays smaller than the subdivision's own sagitta target and is never visible on its own) — `setTransform`'s requested position/quaternion are kept separately as `reqPosition`/`reqQuaternion` and are what `line.position` is actually written from (pivot-corrected) and what `scaleAtLocal`/subdivision read to map sampler space to world, so pivot bookkeeping never leaks into the geometric judgments. Below that, `Curve` owns the `THREE.LineSegments` geometry/material and the fixed-capacity vertex/index/`lineDistance` buffers (`maxVertices`, allocated once at construction per the WebGPU gotcha below), plus `setDash`/`setOpacity`/`setTransform`/`setVisible` (hiding whenever fewer than 2 vertices are baked, regardless of the caller's request), `clear` (drops the baked curve back to nothing, hiding it regardless of `setVisible`'s request) and `dispose`. `dash` (a `LineDashedMaterial` reading `lineDistance`, baked as cumulative distance along the baked vertices) is the one constructor option beyond a plain `LineBasicMaterial`; either way `setOpacity` writes `material.opacity` directly, since occlusion by an opaque body already falls out of the depth test and this class carries no per-vertex fade of its own. It knows nothing about `Vec3`/`KinematicState`/`OrbitalElements`/`ReferenceFrame`/`Ephemeris`/`FloatingOrigin` — only plain numbers and `THREE.Vector3`/`THREE.Quaternion`/`THREE.Camera` — so the two callers only ever supply a sampler function and the coordinate frame it operates in.
- `src/render/ring.ts` — builds one `RingVisual` (`{object, sync}`) per `RingBandDef`, from `RingOpticsDef`'s physical parameters (`normalOpticalDepth`/`singleScatteringAlbedo`/`phaseG`/`color`) rather than a fixed opacity. `ringOpticsNodes(baseColor, optics)` is the one TSL node graph behind every band — extinction from `exp(-tauNormal/muView)`, scattered radiance from the sun-facing optical depth times the escape transmittance times the Henyey-Greenstein phase, and a ray-vs-sphere shadow term (`inBodyShadow`) that darkens a band wherever the parent body sits between it and the sun — returning `{colorNode, opacityNode, sync}` rather than a material, so `physicalMaterial` (a `MeshBasicNodeMaterial`, for `createAnnulusRing`/`createTorusRing`) and `lineOpticsMaterial` (a `LineBasicNodeMaterial`, for `createRingLine`) both wrap the identical graph instead of each shading a band its own way — a 1px-wide band gets exactly the same per-fragment shadowing and phase function a wide one does, evaluated at that vertex's own interpolated world position. `createRingLine` is `createAnnulusRing`'s sibling for a band whose real width `game/celestial/ring-view.ts` has judged too narrow to trust as a filled shape — a closed `THREE.Line` circle (repeating the first vertex as the last, since WebGPU's renderer has no `LineLoop`); its `coverage` uniform (via `RingVisualState`) dims it in proportion to its actual screen-space width, so a ring narrower than a pixel does not read brighter than one that fills a pixel. `sectorParts(arcs)` splits a band with `RingArcDef`s into non-overlapping longitude sectors, each built as its own mesh/line at a scaled optical depth, so an arc's brightening composites exactly once rather than stacking with its band — both `createAnnulusRing` and `createRingLine` route through it. All builders share `transparent: true, depthWrite: false` (`MeshBasicNodeMaterial` additionally `side: DoubleSide`), and the `rotation.x = -π/2` tilt pointing the geometry's flat/circle normal along model +Y (the spin axis).
- `src/game/celestial/ring-view.ts` — `RingView` turns one `RingSystemDef` into the `THREE.Object3D`s `render/ring.ts` builds and drives their per-frame `RingVisualState` (body center/radius, sun direction, camera position, ring axis, coverage). **Deliberately not a child of the body's own mesh**: a body's mesh quaternion bakes in its full spin phase (`spinOrientation(axis, spinAngle)`), and while that's invisible on an axisymmetric ring, Neptune's Adams-ring arcs are not axisymmetric — inheriting spin phase would sweep them around the planet at its ~16h rotation period, when the arcs are meant to hold static. So `sync` takes the body's resolved position/scale (matching `SphereBody`/`PointBody`'s own each frame) and the pole axis alone, and rebuilds its own orientation via the same `spinOrientation` function called a second time with the angle zeroed — reusing the one existing attitude function rather than writing a new one. A `thickness === 0` band is exactly one `RingVisual` — `buildBand` decides once, from the band's own real width against `RING_LINE_WIDTH_THRESHOLD_M` (1500 km), whether it becomes `createAnnulusRing` (Saturn's/Jupiter's main-disk-scale bands, Uranus's dusty ζ/ν/μ bands — wide enough to stay a filled shape at any zoom the game's overview camera reaches) or `createRingLine` (every band whose real width sits at the km-to-few-thousand-km scale of a named ringlet — Uranus's 9 narrow rings, Neptune's arc-bearing Adams ring and its neighbors, Saturn's F ring, Quaoar's/Chariklo's rings — narrow enough that a filled annulus would risk vanishing between rasterized samples as it thins toward sub-pixel on screen). Each such band's `sync` always feeds its single `RingVisual` a `coverage` of `ringPixelCoverage(bandWidthMeters, metersPerPixelAt(bodyPos))`, evaluated at the ring's **true** ECI position (not the `SphereBody` combat-view compressed one) — since `SphereBody`'s `visDist` compression is defined to preserve true angular size exactly, the true-position/true-width answer and the compressed-render answer always agree. `metersPerPixelAt` clamping this to 1 once the band spans more than a pixel is what keeps the annulus-classified bands reading exactly as before at ordinary viewing distance, and what keeps the line-classified bands from over-brightening as they shrink toward and past sub-pixel — the two share one formula, so there is nothing to keep in step between them.
  - `src/game/orbit-line.ts`'s `OrbitLine` draws an **analytic ellipse from `OrbitalElements`**: its `CurveSampler` maps eccentric anomaly `E = t·2π` to a position in the (`pHat`, `qHat`) orbital plane, so `Curve` samples the ellipse directly rather than reading a fixed vertex array — resolution near the ship falls out for free, since a point closer to the camera has a smaller m/px and so demands more subdivision there. Vertices stay relative to whichever body the passed-in `OrbitalElements` are centered on, moved per frame only via `Curve.setTransform` (through `fo`, offset by `OrbitalElements.center.state.r` — that body's own ECI position); `Curve`'s own `revision` is only replaced when osculating elements drift past thresholds (or on `sync`'s trailing `force`, which `creative-stage.ts`'s placement-preview ellipse passes while the panel is open so an edit smaller than a threshold still redraws) — rewriting vertices in ship-relative coords every frame made the ellipse visibly jitter under J2/third-body element wobble. **`sync` takes no center argument at all**: the ellipse and its translation both come off the `OrbitalElements`, so a caller cannot draw an ellipse at a body it isn't centered on. The ellipse passes through the body's own current position, but `Curve`'s line material is depth-tested and depth-writing bodies (`SphereBody`/`PointBody`/`celestial-surface.ts`'s opaque, depth-writing `MeshBasicNodeMaterial`) are opaque and always render before any transparent line, so the body simply occludes the line wherever the two coincide — no `excludeSphere`-style vertex exclusion is needed, and `setOpacity` (forwarding to `Curve.setOpacity`) is the only per-frame lever a caller (`environment-scene.ts`'s reference lines) has for fading a line out near a body it hasn't zoomed in on. `renderOrder` is a constructor argument (third, defaulting to 0) forwarded straight to `Curve` — a line's draw order is fixed at the one place it is created. **Every value comes from `const.ts`'s `LINE_RENDER_ORDER` table** — draw order only means anything as a relation between lines (two transparent lines at the same value stack unpredictably), so no line picks its own number: `reference` 0 / `shipOrbit` 1 / `secondaryTarget` 2 / `target` 3 / `plan` 4 / `predicted` 5, ordered least-to-most likely to need to win a coincident-orbit tie — a rendezvous target's orbit is often nearly coincident with the player's, the plan is what the player is actively editing, and the integrated prediction has to sit above the analytic ellipse it stands in for on the frame where both are drawn. Line colors: secondary target cyan, primary target orange, enemies `COLOR_ENEMY_ORBIT_LINE` (a dim gray, so a target/lead highlight actually stands out). The planned trajectory is not an `OrbitLine` — it's each owned segment's own pooled `TrajectoryLine` (`plan/plan-path.ts`, above), drawn white, since a multi-node plan is a chain of arcs rather than one closed ellipse.
  - `src/game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `TrajectoryLine` is its sibling for **a `DynamicTrajectory`'s own held span, continued past the tip by a two-body Kepler extrapolation whenever asked to draw further than the trajectory has actually integrated** — it takes the `DynamicTrajectory` itself (or `null` to draw nothing) rather than a caller-assembled point array, so predicted arcs (`GameEntity.predicted`) and owned plan-segment arcs (`game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts`'s `PredictedArc.trajectory` — the same class either way) both feed it unchanged. `syncGeometry(trajectory, from, to, frame, ephemeris, attractors)` reads `trajectory?.samplesOldestFirst()` (a module-shared empty array when `trajectory` is `null`); whenever `to` reaches past the held span's own tip and the trajectory carries a non-`null` `extrapolationCenter`, it appends a tail of `kepler-extrapolation.ts`'s `extrapolatedRelativeStates(tip, center, to, count)` — converted to absolute ECI by adding `ephemeris.stateOf(center.id, ·)` at each sample's own time, since that function's own return is center-relative — before baking the combined series into `Frame`-relative `KinematicState`s pushed oldest-first into a fresh `physics/state-queue.ts` `StateQueue` (private `baked`); a trajectory with no `extrapolationCenter` (an owned plan segment's own arc, built with `keplerTail: false` so it never receives one — see `game/simulation/predicted-arc.ts` above) or a `to` the held span already reaches simply bakes the held samples alone. `count` targets the trajectory's own `sampleInterval` (or the extrapolated span divided by 64 with no history to measure one from), capped at a fixed per-module sample budget (`MAX_EXTRAPOLATED_SAMPLES`). Baking (the `frameTransformAt`/`toFrameState` pass over every sample, the expensive part) only re-runs when the held samples' array reference (`samplesOldestFirst()` itself only mints a new array when `DynamicTrajectory.step`/`reset` actually ran) or `frame` changes — **or**, while a tail is in play, when `to` has moved by at least one extrapolated sample's own interval since the last bake; a smaller move is left stale, since the drawn tail then lags by less than the adaptive subdivision downstream could ever tell from the correct answer, and `to` otherwise moves every frame (`simTime` advancing) which would defeat the whole point of skipping the bake. `from`/`to` are the drawn span's lower/upper bounds — either `null` leaves that side unbounded (drawing to the held-or-extrapolated span's own edge) — each clamped to `baked`'s own `oldest`/`newest` time (nothing outside is interpolable) and held as `startTime`/`endTime`; changing `from`/`to` alone still only remints the `Curve` revision token without re-baking whenever the paragraph above doesn't call for one, since it only moves where the sampler's `t=0`/`t=1` ends of `[startTime, endTime]` map to — its `CurveSampler` maps `t∈[0,1]` linearly onto that range, clamps the result into the retained span, and delegates to `StateQueue.at(time)` for the interpolation itself, so the leading/trailing vertices are the trajectory interpolated at `from`/`to` rather than manufactured samples; this file holds no binary search or `hermiteInterpolate` call of its own — `StateQueue.at` is the one implementation both share. `toFrameState`'s frame-relative velocities are the interpolation's tangents. The separate `sync(camera)` call is what actually asks `Curve` to (re)subdivide, so a `TrajectoryLine` whose bake and `from`/`to` haven't moved still tracks camera zoom without redoing the bake; `sync` also clears the curve outright whenever the drawn span collapses (fewer than 2 baked vertices, or `startTime` at or past `endTime` — e.g. a plan segment's `end` has been dragged behind its own start). `syncTransform` does the per-frame O(1) work — rigid un-bake rotation plus the floating-origin translation, both pushed into `Curve.setTransform`. `setDash(dashSize, gapSize)` forwards to `Curve.setDash`, which lets a caller hold the on-screen dash spacing constant while the camera zooms without touching the baked geometry (`Curve` bakes the cumulative `lineDistance` itself whenever its constructing `LineStyle` carried a `dash`).

  **Solid means it will happen, dashed means it is being asked for.** `GameEntity.predictedLine` (integrated future) and `OrbitLine` (analytic ellipse) are solid; the maneuver plan's own polyline (`plan/plan-path.ts`'s pooled `TrajectoryLine`s) is dashed. Nothing else may take a dash pattern without a reason to read as unrealized.
  - Both use `THREE.Line` — **`LineLoop` is unsupported by the WebGPU renderer**; close loops manually.
- `src/render/tsl-types.ts` — the TSL node type vocabulary every shader-building module shares (`FloatNode`/`Vec3Node` for graph intermediates, `FloatUniform`/`Vec3Uniform` for the nodes the CPU writes each frame). `@types/three` types a node as `Node<T>` and `uniform()` as an overload set, so `ReturnType<typeof uniform>`/`ReturnType<typeof vec3>` resolve to one arbitrary overload and don't describe what a call site actually holds — these aliases name the handful of combinations the shaders use instead.
- `src/types/{png-shims,svg-shims,ephemeris-pack-shims}.d.ts` — asset-import typings for the webpack loaders (`.png`/`.jpg`, `.svg`, `.epk`), which `tsc` has no way to know about. THREE itself needs no shim: `@types/three` covers `three/webgpu` and `three/tsl` in full. Import THREE **only** from `'three/webgpu'` (mixing with `'three'` would duplicate classes at runtime).

### WebGPU renderer gotchas (three.js 0.185)
- `THREE.LineLoop` and `THREE.Points` sizing are not supported; use `THREE.Line` / triangle-based sprites.
- Additive-blended materials need explicit `transparent: true`.
- **Vertex data must be updated in place, never by swapping the geometry or the attribute.** `RenderObject` caches both the geometry and the resolved attribute list when it is first built, so assigning a new `BufferGeometry` to `mesh.geometry` — or a new `BufferAttribute` onto a live geometry — keeps drawing the original vertices. Allocate the `Float32Array` once, write into it, set `attribute.needsUpdate = true`, and vary the drawn length with `geometry.setDrawRange`. `render/curve.ts`'s `Curve` — the drawing mechanism shared by `game/orbit-line.ts`'s `OrbitLine` and `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s `TrajectoryLine` — is built this way, which is why its vertex capacity is a fixed constant declared by the caller. The same rule applies to `THREE.InstancedMesh`: `render/instanced-pool.ts`'s `InstancedPool` writes into a fixed-capacity `instanceMatrix` in place (`setMatrixAt`/`needsUpdate = true`), retires an unused slot with an all-zero matrix rather than moving `mesh.count`, and never replaces the mesh or its geometry.
- **A failed `CreateBindGroup` blacks out the entire scene with no JS exception.** It invalidates the whole frame's command buffer, and the error surfaces only as a `GPUDevice` validation message on the browser's stderr. When a change makes the screen go black with a clean console, check stderr for `Invalid BindGroup` before looking anywhere else. One way to reach it is oversizing a uniform-buffer-backed binding past WebGPU's baseline `maxUniformBufferBindingSize` of 65536 bytes — for `InstancedMesh.instanceMatrix` that would be 1024 mat4s — but the current three.js version and `render/instanced-pool.ts`'s single-mesh construction do not hit it: the bullet pools run at `MAX_BULLETS * 3` = 1200 instances and draw correctly, so the backend is not routing `instanceMatrix` through a uniform buffer at that size. Treat 1024 as a threshold worth re-checking on a driver or three.js change, not as a cap the code must design around.
- Headless-Chrome screenshots work with `--headless=new --enable-gpu --enable-unsafe-webgpu --disable-gpu-sandbox --no-sandbox` (flaky; retry a few times).
- **`frustumCulled = false` is not a default-safe habit — most objects here don't need it.** An object that only ever moves via `position`/`scale`/`quaternion` on a geometry that is never mutated after construction (`SphereBody`/`PointBody`'s bodies, `RingView`'s bands, `Billboard`) gets a correct world-space bounding sphere for free, the same as any ordinary three.js scene. Turning it off is legitimate only for a camera-following shell whose bounding sphere is therefore never outside the frustum (`stars.ts`'s star shell, `celestial-grid.ts`'s grid/pole lines), a line whose vertex buffer is rewritten in place without a matching `computeBoundingSphere()` call (`curve.ts`, shared by both `orbit-line.ts` and `trajectory-line.ts`), or an `InstancedMesh` whose instances scatter across a region far larger than the shared geometry's own bounding sphere (`instanced-pool.ts`, `point-field-view.ts`) — each of these carries a comment saying which.

Not yet implemented: enemy *maneuvering* AI (enemies shoot back — `EnemyAi.behave` burst-fires lead-aimed plasma — but never thrust), solar-storm/combat auroras, ECS, full N-body cislunar phase.
